Rigorous methods for computational number theory
KOEN DE BOER 1 , ALICE PELLET-MARY 2 , AND BENJAMIN WESOLOWSKI 3 1 Leiden University, Mathematical Institute, Leiden, The Netherlands 2 Univ. Bordeaux, CNRS, Inria, Bordeaux INP, IMB, UMR 5251, F-33400, Talence, France 3 ENS de Lyon, CNRS, UMPA, UMR 5669, Lyon, France

Abstract
. We present the first algorithm for computing class groups and unit groups of arbitrary number fields that provably runs in probabilistic subexponential time, assuming the Extended Riemann Hypothesis (ERH). Previous subexponential algorithms were either restricted to imaginary quadratic fields, or relied on several heuristic assumptions that have long resisted rigorous analysis. The heart of our method is a new general strategy to provably solve a recurring computational problem in number theory (assuming ERH): given an ideal class [ 𝔞 ] [\mathfrak{a}] of a number field K K , sample an ideal 𝔟 ∈ [ 𝔞 ] \mathfrak{b}\in[\mathfrak{a}] belonging to a particular family of ideals (e.g., the family of smooth ideals, or near-prime ideals). More precisely, let 𝒮 \mathcal{S} be an arbitrary family of ideals, and 𝒮 B \mathcal{S}_{B} the family of B B -smooth ideals. We describe an efficient algorithm that samples ideals 𝔟 ∈ [ 𝔞 ] \mathfrak{b}\in[\mathfrak{a}] such that 𝔟 ∈ 𝒮 ⋅ 𝒮 B \mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B} with probability proportional to the density of 𝒮 \mathcal{S} within the set of all ideals. The case where 𝒮 \mathcal{S} is the set of prime ideals yields the family 𝒮 ⋅ 𝒮 B \mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B} of near-prime ideals, of particular interest in that it constitutes a dense family of efficiently factorable ideals. The case of smooth ideals 𝒮 = 𝒮 B \mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{B} regularly comes up in index-calculus algorithms (notably to compute class groups and unit groups), where it has long constituted a theoretical obstacle overcome only by heuristic arguments.



1.
Introduction

Many number theoretic algorithms resort to heuristic assumptions for their analysis.
This issue concerns even the most fundamental problems of the field, such as the computation of class groups in subexponential time.
This persistent need for heuristic assumptions often stems from a step of this form: given an ideal class
[\mathfrak{a}]
of a number field
K
, find a representative
\mathfrak{b}\in[\mathfrak{a}]
belonging to a particular family
\mathcal{S}
of ideals (for instance, the family of smooth ideals).
It is relatively simple to design an algorithm for this task: sample a random representative
\mathfrak{b}\in[\mathfrak{a}]
, and hope that it belongs to the desired family
\mathcal{S}
. One then heuristically argues that the probability that
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}
should be proportional to the density of
\mathcal{S}
. For instance, the subexponential density of smooth ideals heuristically implies that one can find smooth representatives in subexponential time. This is the heart of state-of-the-art algorithms to compute class groups, unit groups, or generators of principal ideals in number fields
[
10
,
17
,
51
]
, and has long constituted a theoretical obstacle overcome only by heuristic arguments (with the exception of quadratic fields
[
38
]
).

In the first part of this paper, we propose a general strategy to solve these ideal sampling tasks rigorously and efficiently, assuming only the extended Riemann hypothesis (henceforth, ERH).
In the second part, we apply this new technique to present the first algorithm for computing class groups and unit groups of arbitrary number fields that provably runs in probabilistic subexponential time.
These two parts of the paper can be read essentially independently.

Part
21.5
consists of roughly two subjects: an slightly extended analysis and an application of a known provable variant of the BKZ algorithm for ideal lattices
[
39
]
, which is used in Part
3
for lattice basis reduction; and an extended analysis of the algorithm of Buchmann, Pohst and Kessler
[
19
,
18
]
which is required for the post-processing part in the class group and unit group computation in Part
11
. These extra analyses are necessary to apply these known results to the specific use-cases of the present work.

Main result of Part
3
: sampling ideals

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an arbitrary family of ideals, and
\mathcal{S}_{B}
the family of
B
-smooth ideals (i.e., products of prime ideals of norm at most
B
). In the first part of the paper, we describe an efficient algorithm that samples
\mathfrak{b}\in[\mathfrak{a}]
such that
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
with probability proportional to the density of
\mathcal{S}
.
The set
\mathcal{S}_{B}
is used to randomize the input, and
B
can be chosen as small as
(\log|\Delta_{K}|)^{O(1)}
, where
\Delta_{K}
is the discriminant of the field
K
.
This result is formalized in
Theorem

9.5
, page
9.5
, and allows to work with arbitrary ray class groups, and to restrict
\mathcal{S}_{B}
to ideals whose prime factors fall in a prescribed subgroup.

For concreteness,
Theorem

1.1
below is a specialization of
Theorem

9.5
to the simplest case, without ray nor subgroups. Here,
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]
is the local density of
\mathcal{S}
(
Definition

2.11
), i.e., the proportion of ideals of norm about
r^{n}
that belong to
\mathcal{S}
.

Theorem 1.1

(ERH)
.

Assuming ERH, there is a randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}
such that the following holds.
Let
K
be a number field, with degree
n
, discriminant
\Delta_{K}
, and
let an LLL-reduced basis of the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
be given.
Let
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
be an integral ideal. Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, let
\mathsf{b}\geq 2
be an integer, and
let
r\geq 48\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{a})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

Given the above data, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
outputs
\beta\in\mathfrak{a}
such that

(\beta)\cdot{\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}

with probability at least
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]/3-\varepsilon
, for some smoothness bound
B=(\log|\Delta_{K}|+\log\log(1/\varepsilon))^{O(1)}
and for any set
\mathcal{S}
of integral ideals. Furthermore, the algorithm runs in expected polynomial time in

\log|\Delta_{K}|
, in
\log(\norm(\mathfrak{a}))
, in

\allowbreak\log(1/\varepsilon)
, in
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
, and in the length of the input.

Remark 1.2
.

Note that the algorithm is described in a slightly different way than the above discussion: given
\mathfrak{a}
, we find
\beta\in\mathfrak{a}
such that
\beta\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
. The ideal
\beta\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
is in the inverse class of
\mathfrak{a}
, so up to an inversion, this problem is equivalent to the ideal sampling problem discussed above.

Technique.

The folklore strategy to solve ideal sampling tasks is the following. The input ideal
\mathfrak{a}
can be seen as a lattice, via the Minkowski embedding. One may find a reasonably short basis of
\mathfrak{a}
(for instance, by means of LLL
[
52
]
), which then allows one to repeatedly sample reasonably short random elements
\beta\in\mathfrak{a}
, until the ideal
\mathfrak{b}=\beta\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
belongs to the desired family
\mathcal{S}
.
One then typically argues (heuristically!) that the probability of success is proportional to the density of
\mathcal{S}
.

To obtain a rigorous sampling algorithm, we proceed in two steps. First, we prove that a fairly straightforward strategy as above indeed has the desired probability of success
when the input
\mathfrak{a}
is treated as a random ideal lattice with uniformly random Arakelov class
. More precisely, we prove in
Theorem

7.1
that there is a reasonably small box
\mathcal{B}
(in the embedding space) such that the
expected
density (over the randomness of
\mathfrak{a}
) of elements
\beta\in\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathcal{B}
such that
\beta\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}
is proportional to the density of
\mathcal{S}
.

Second, we deal with arbitrary input
\mathfrak{a}
by randomizing its Arakelov class via a generalization of the random walks introduced in
[
14
]
. Concretely, the input
\mathfrak{a}
is multiplied by random ideals of small prime norm (the
discrete
part of the random walk), and is randomly distorted according to some Gaussian distribution (the
continuous
part of the random walk).
We prove in
Corollary

6.5
that the result is close to uniformly distributed in the Arakelov ray class group. The discrete part of the random walk introduces small prime factors, hence our method samples ideals in
\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
instead of
\mathcal{S}
. In all applications we are aware of,
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
.

Main result of Part
11
: computing class groups and unit groups

The case of smooth ideals
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{B}
regularly comes up in index-calculus algorithms, such as the aforementioned algorithms for computing class groups or unit groups. In these cases, this sampling task is not the only source of heuristics, so significantly more work is required to obtain a rigorous algorithm. This is the object of the second part of the article.

Let
K
be a number field of degree
n
and discriminant
\Delta_{K}
. The determination of the structure of its class group
\Cl(K)
, together with a system of fundamental units, is one of the main problems of computational number theory
[
22
, p. 217]
.
It has long been believed that this task can be solved in probabilistic subexponential time. Such algorithms have been described and analyzed under a variety of heuristic assumptions
[
17
,
10
]
.
Despite decades of investigation, only imaginary quadratic fields have been amenable to a rigorous analysis
[
38
]
, assuming ERH.
The history of class group computation is discussed in further detail in
Section

12.2
.
In Part
11
of this paper, we present the first general algorithm for this problem that provably runs in probabilistic subexponential time, assuming ERH.
We use the classical
L
-notation

L_{x}(\alpha,c)=\exp\left((c+o(1))(\log x)^{\alpha}(\log\log x)^{1-\alpha}\right),

and
L_{x}(\alpha)=L_{x}(\alpha,O(1))
. We prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3

(ERH)
.

There is a probabilistic algorithm which, on input a number field
K
of degree
n
and discriminant
\Delta_{K}

and an LLL-reduced basis of its ring of integers,
computes its ideal class group and a compact representation of a fundamental system of units, and runs in expected time polynomial in the length of the input, in
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2)
, in
L_{n^{n}}(2/3)
, and in
\min(\rho_{K},L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(2/3+o(1)))
,
where
\rho_{K}
is the residue at
1
of the Dedekind zeta function
\zeta_{K}
.

It has been conjectured since Buchmann’s 1988 heuristic algorithm
[
17
]
that this problem can be solved in subexponential time
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2)
for any family of fields of fixed degree.

Theorem

1.3
, together with the upper bound
\rho_{K}=(\log|\Delta_{K}|)^{O(n)}
(see Equation (
5
) below), implies this conjecture, assuming ERH.

Then, it was conjectured by Biasse and Fieker’s 2014 algorithm
[
10
]
that this problem can be solved in subexponential time even for varying degree.
Again,
Theorem

1.3
implies this conjecture, assuming ERH. However, Biasse and Fieker conjectured a complexity as in
Theorem

1.3
where the quantity
\rho_{K}
is replaced with
L_{n^{n}}(2/3)
.
In our analysis, the quantity
\rho_{K}
arises from the best known estimates on the density of bounded smooth ideals. It seems
\rho_{K}
should appear in the same way in the heuristic complexity of
[
10
]
, unless one expects a better bound on the density of smooth ideals.

Computing
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

While we stated our main result as an algorithm for computing units and class groups in
Theorem

1.3
, our algorithm actually does slightly more than that: it computes the so-called Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice for any set
{\mathbb{S}}
of prime ideals.
It is well known that such an algorithm for
{\mathbb{S}}
-units can be used to compute the class group and the unit group. Combined with
Theorem

17.7
, which allows to decompose any integral ideal as a product of prime ideals in a sufficiently large set
{\mathbb{S}}
, this can also be used to solve other algorithmic problems, such as the principal ideal problem, or the class group discrete logarithm problem.

Main results of Part
21.5
: Detailed analyses of provable lattice techniques

Part
21.5
consists of an extended analysis of two already known
results in lattice theory; namely the existence of a BKZ-algorithm variant
[
39
]
that
has a provable running time, and the existence of an LLL-algorithm variant (called the Buchmann-Pohst-Kessler algorithm
[
19
,
18
]
) that
has a relatively well numerical stability, so that it can be used on ‘approximated’ bases.
The extended analysis consists, in the case of the BKZ-algorithm, mainly of making explicit the techniques described in
[
39
, Section 3, ‘Cost of BKZ”]
and applying this BKZ-variant to ideal lattices. In the case of the Buchmann-Pohst-Kessler algorithm
[
19
,
18
]
, the present work required an extended analysis because in our use-case (in contrast to theirs) some lattice invariants like the rank and the determinant are unknown, thus requiring a slight extension to their algorithm.

The BKZ-variant a with provable running time is used in Part
3
for lattice basis reduction of ideal lattices, and the extended algorithm of Buchmann, Pohst and Kessler is required for the post-processing part in the class group and unit group computation in Part
11
. Note that both of these algorithms being non-heuristic and having a provable running time is essential for the main results of the present work: a rigorous analysis of a common number-theoretic technique (Part
3
) and a rigorous algorithm for computing the unit group and the class group that has a provable upper bound on the run time (Part
11
).

Further applications

Sampling smooth ideals is a task that regularly arises in computational number theory. In Part
11
, we focus on the problem of class group and unit group computation, but it is more generally a common component of index-calculus algorithms, like the general number field sieve for integer factorization or the computation of discrete logarithms in finite fields. We do not investigate this direction further in the present paper.

Applying the method of Part
3
to the case where
\mathcal{S}
is the set of prime ideals allows one to sample in the family
\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
of near-prime ideals, of particular interest in that it constitutes a dense family of efficiently factorable ideals.
Therefore, our sampling method provides a rigorous way to transform any ideal
\mathfrak{a}
into an equivalent ideal
\mathfrak{b}
of known factorization.
Obtaining such factorable ideals (or elements) is a key step in algorithms to compute power residue symbols.
Specifically, it allows to perform the ‘principalization step’ in
[
16
, §5.2]
efficiently.
The first author of the present article has developed this idea in his PhD dissertation
[
12
]
, applying the main result of Part
3
to construct the first polynomial time algorithm to compute power residue symbols.

Related work

The aforementioned difficulties of ideal sampling and class group computation have already been overcome in the special case of

imaginary quadratic
number fields. Building on a result of Seysen
[
70
]
, Hafner and McCurley
[
38
]
gave a provable algorithm for computing class groups and unit groups of imaginary quadratic fields, assuming ERH.
This case distinguishes itself by the finiteness of the unit group and the existence of
reduced
representatives of ideal classes.
This algorithm exploits random walks in the class group to find
B
-smooth
principal
ideals.
The idea of performing a random walk in the class group was reused in the algorithms of Buchmann
[
17
]
and Biasse and Fieker
[
10
]
, in a heuristic way.
Rather than random walks in class groups, our method exploits the much richer Arakelov (ray) class groups. Random walks in Arakelov class groups were first studied in
[
14
]
to prove the random self-reducibility of computational problems in ideal lattices. Their technique to study the convergence of these walks plays a key role in the present paper.
We note that Schoof
[
69
]
rephrased Buchmann’s algorithm in terms of Arakelov theory, and we borrow from his formalism.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

We denote by
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
the natural numbers, the integers, the rationals, the real numbers, and the complex numbers respectively. The notation
\log
refers to logarithms in base
e
, whereas the notation
\log_{2}
denotes logarithms in base
2
.
For finite sets
X
we denote by
\lvert X\rvert
the number of elements in
X
. For infinite sets
X
with a well-defined volume, we use both notations
\vol(X)=\lvert X\rvert

for the volume of
X
. The transpose of a matrix
M
is denoted by
M^{\top}
.
For a ring
R
, we write
R^{\times}
the set of invertible elements of
R
, and
R^{*}:=R\setminus\{0\}
.

We use the classic big
O
and
\Omega
asymptotic notations, and all hidden constants are absolute (in particular, they never depend on the choice of a field
K
). We also use the notation
f=\poly(g)
as a synonym for
f=g^{O(1)}
.
We use the notation
O_{\varepsilon}
to signify that the hidden constants depend on
\varepsilon
.
As already mentioned, to denote the running time of subexponential algorithms, we use the asymptotic
L
-notation

L_{x}(\alpha,c)=\exp\left((c+o(1))(\log x)^{\alpha}(\log\log x)^{1-\alpha}\right),

where the
o(1)
is asymptotic in
x
(and does not depend on other parameters). We also write
L_{x}(\alpha)=L_{x}(\alpha,O(1))
.

For a real vector space
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we consider the Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
, and the infinity-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
.
We sometimes write
\|\cdot\|_{2}
for
\|\cdot\|
to emphasize the type of norm. We will occasionally use the notation
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}
for the unit ball with respect to the Euclidean and infinity norm respectively. In particular, the Euclidean ball of radius
r
in
V
is typically denoted

r\mathcal{B}=\{\mathbf{v}\in V\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|\mathbf{v}\|<r\}.

The vector space
V
is either clear from context or explicitly mentioned when introducing
\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}
.

2.2.
The Extended Riemann Hypothesis

All statements that mention (ERH), such as
Theorem

1.1
, assume the
Extended Riemann Hypothesis
, which refers to the Riemann Hypothesis for Hecke
L
-functions (see
[
44
, §5.7]
).

2.3.
Euclidean lattices

A lattice
\Lambda
is a discrete subgroup of a real vector space
V=\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
We write
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)
the real vector subspace of
V
spanned by the vectors of
\Lambda
.
The rank of the lattice is the dimension of
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)
, and
we say the lattice is full-rank if
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)=V
.
A lattice (of rank
n
) can be represented by a basis
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
such that
\Lambda=\{\sum_{i}x_{i}\mathbf{b}_{i}\,,\,x_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. For a given basis
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
we denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B})
the lattice spanned by the columns of this basis.

Geometric invariants.

The covolume of
\Lambda
, denoted by
\covol(\Lambda)
, is the volume of the quotient
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)/\Lambda
. If
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a basis of
\Lambda
(with column vectors), then the covolume of
\Lambda
is equal to
\covol(\Lambda)=\sqrt{\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})}
. This quantity is also the volume of any fundamental domain of the lattice. One such domain is of special interest: the Voronoi cell.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\Lambda\subseteq V
be a full-rank lattice. We denote by

\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda)=\{\mathbf{x}\in V\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\|\mathbf{x}-\ell\|\text{ for all }\ell\in\Lambda\}

the Voronoi cell of
\Lambda
around zero. It is a fundamental domain for the lattice
\Lambda
(up to a set of ‘faces’ of measure zero), thus has volume
\covol(\Lambda)
.

The
i
-th successive minimum of
\Lambda
is denoted by
\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)
. More precisely, each
\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)
is the smallest real number such that there exist at least
i
linearly independent vectors of euclidean norm at most
\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)
in
\Lambda
. We call
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)
the first minimum, and
\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
the last minimum (where
n
is the rank of the lattice).
The covering radius
{\operatorname{cov}}(\Lambda)
is the smallest
r>0
such that for any element
\mathbf{x}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)
there exists a lattice point at distance at most
r
from
\mathbf{x}
. The analogous notions with respect to the maximum norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
instead of the Euclidean norm are denoted by
\lambda_{i}^{(\infty)}(\Lambda)
and
{\operatorname{cov}_{\infty}}(\Lambda)
.

The following notion, the
generating radius

\rr(\Lambda)
, is closely related to
\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
and
\cov(\Lambda)
, but not as standard.

Definition 2.2

(Generating radius)
.

For a lattice
\Lambda
, the
generating radius

\rr(\Lambda)
is the smallest real number such that the set of all vectors of
\Lambda
of euclidean norm
\leq\rr(\Lambda)
generates the lattice
\Lambda
(as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module). In other words,

\rr(\Lambda)=\min\Big(r>0\,|\,\{\mathbf{x}\in\Lambda\,|\,\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq r\}\text{ generates }\Lambda\Big).

Lemma 2.3
.

For any lattice
\Lambda
, we have

\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\leq\rr(\Lambda)\leq 2\cdot\cov(\Lambda)\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda).

Proof.

The left-most inequality follows from the very definitions of
\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
and
\rr(\Lambda)
, and the right-most inequality can be found in
[
54
, Theorem 7.9]
.
We prove the middle inequality by
using the concept of
Voronoi-relevant vectors
of the lattice
\Lambda
.
The Voronoi cell
\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda)
of
L
contains all vectors of
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)
that are closer or equally close to
0
than to any other lattice point (see
Definition

2.1
).
We know (e.g.,
[
54
, Proposition 8.4]
) that
\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda)
is compact and convex and that
\vol_{\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)}(\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda))=\covol(\Lambda)
. From the definition of the Voronoi cell and of the covering radius of
\Lambda
, we also know that all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda)
satisfy
\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\cov(\Lambda)
(see also
[
54
, Proposition 8.4]
).

The Voronoi-relevant vectors of
\Lambda
are the vectors
S_{\mathcal{V}_{0}}=\{\mathbf{v}\in\Lambda\,|\,\exists\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda),\,\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}\|\}
; these are the vectors defining the facets of the Voronoi cell. From the definition of these vectors, it holds that
\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda)=\{\mathbf{x}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)\,|\,\forall\mathbf{v}\in S_{\mathcal{V}_{0}},\penalty\ \|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}\|\}
. Moreover, since
\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\cov(\Lambda)
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda)
, we have that
\|\mathbf{v}\|\leq 2\cov(\Lambda)
for all Voronoi-relevant vectors
\mathbf{v}
. We will show that
S_{\mathcal{V}_{0}}
generates the lattice
\Lambda
, which will conclude the proof.

Let
\Lambda^{\prime}
be the lattice generated by the vectors in
S_{\mathcal{V}_{0}}
. We know that
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
and that
\Lambda^{\prime}
has rank
n
(since
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda^{\prime})=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda))=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)
). Moreover, since
S_{\mathcal{V}_{0}}\subseteq\Lambda^{\prime}
, we know that
\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda^{\prime})\subseteq\{\mathbf{x}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)\,|\,\forall\mathbf{v}\in S_{\mathcal{V}_{0}},\,\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{v}\|\}=\mathcal{V}_{0}(\Lambda)
. From this we conclude that
\covol(\Lambda^{\prime})\leq\covol(\Lambda)
and so
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda
as desired.
∎

The next two results regarding counting lattice points in sets,
Lemma

2.4
and
Lemma

2.8
, are folklore. We include proofs for completeness, as they play an important role in the article.

Estimation on the
average
number of lattice points in a measurable volume

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
V
be a Euclidean vector space and let
\Lambda\subseteq V
be a full rank lattice. Let
S\subseteq V
be a measurable set, and let
\mathbf{c}\in V/\Lambda
be chosen uniformly. Then

\underset{\mathbf{c}\leftarrow V/\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}}[|(\Lambda+\mathbf{c})\cap S|]=\vol(S)/\covol(\Lambda).

Proof.

By integrating the measurable indicator set
1_{S}
, and choosing a fundamental domain
F
of
V/\Lambda
(which has volume
\covol(\Lambda)
), we obtain

\underset{\mathbf{c}\leftarrow V/\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}}[|(\Lambda+\mathbf{c})\cap S|]=\frac{1}{\vol(F)}\int_{\mathbf{c}\in F}\sum_{\ell\in\Lambda}1_{S}(\mathbf{c}+\ell)d\mathbf{c}=\frac{1}{\covol(\Lambda)}\int_{\mathbf{v}\in V}1_{S}(\mathbf{v})d\mathbf{v}=\frac{\vol(S)}{\covol(\Lambda)}.

∎

Estimation on the number of lattice points in a convex measurable volume

For the ideal sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

2
) we need to efficiently sample in a shifted box (see
Section

8
).

Lemma

2.8
, which shares some similarities with
[
64
, §4.2]
, provides
means to estimate the number of lattice elements in such a box. This estimate
is essential in the proof in
Section

8
. To prepare for
the proof of this lemma, we will need some facts on Minkowski sums of sets.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
V
be a Euclidean vector space. For two sets
X,Y\subseteq V
, we define
the Minkowski sum
X\boxplus Y
as follows.

X\boxplus Y=\{\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}\in X,\mathbf{y}\in Y\}.

For
c\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we denote by
cX
the set

cX=\{c\cdot\mathbf{x}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}\in X\}.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
V
be a Euclidean vector space and let
r,s>0
and let
X\subseteq V
be a convex volume.
Then

(rX)\boxplus(sX)=(r+s)X.

Proof.

We start with inclusion to the right. Suppose
\mathbf{y}\in(rX)\boxplus(sX)
, i.e.,
\mathbf{y}=r\mathbf{x}+s\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in X
.
Then
\tfrac{\mathbf{y}}{r+s}=\tfrac{r\mathbf{x}+s\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}{r+s}\in X
, since it is a weighted average of two
points in
X
and
X
is convex. So
\mathbf{y}\in(r+s)X
. Inclusion to the left holds because
\mathbf{y}\in(r+s)X
means that
\mathbf{y}=(r+s)\mathbf{x}=r\mathbf{x}+s\mathbf{x}\in(rX)\boxplus(sX)
.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
V
be a Euclidean vector space, let
r>0
, let
X,Y\subseteq V
be sets
and let
S\subseteq V
be a symmetric set, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}\in S\Leftrightarrow-\mathbf{x}\in S
. Then

(X\boxplus S)\cap Y\subseteq[X\cap(Y\boxplus S)]\boxplus S.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{y}\in(X\boxplus S)\cap Y
. Then
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{s}\in X\cap(Y\boxplus S)
, so
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{s}\in[X\cap(Y\boxplus S)]\boxplus S
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
V
be a
n
-dimensional Euclidean vector space,
let
\Lambda\subseteq V
be a full-rank lattice,
let
X\subseteq V
be a convex measurable volume for which
\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq cX
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{0}
is the
Voronoi cell of
\Lambda
(see
Definition

2.1
). Then, for all
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\in V
and all
r>2c
,

|(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\cap r(X+\mathbf{t}^{\prime})|\in[e^{-2nc/r},e^{2nc/r}]\cdot\frac{r^{n}\cdot\vol(X)}{\covol(\Lambda)},

where
r(X+\mathbf{t}^{\prime})=\{r\cdot(x+\mathbf{t}^{\prime})\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in X\}
is the scaling of the (translated) set
X+\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
by
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Proof.

As
|(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\cap(rX+r\mathbf{t}^{\prime})|=|(\Lambda+\mathbf{t}-r\mathbf{t}^{\prime})\cap rX|
, we just assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}=0
.
Note that
\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq cX
, and that
X
is convex. So, by
Lemma

2.6
, we have

(rX)\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq(rX)\boxplus(cX)=(r+c)X
. Similarly,
(r-c)X\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq rX
. Therefore

[(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\cap rX]\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq(r+c)X.

(1)

Note that
\mathcal{V}_{0}
is symmetric and
(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}=V
, the whole vector space. So, by
Lemma

2.7
and
(r-c)X\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq rX
,

\displaystyle(r-c)X

\displaystyle=[(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}]\cap(r-c)X

(2)

\displaystyle\subseteq[(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\cap((r-c)X\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0})]\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}\subseteq[(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\cap rX]\boxplus\mathcal{V}_{0}

(3)

By
Equations

1
and
3
and the fact that
\mathcal{V}_{0}
is a fundamental domain of
\Lambda
with
volume
\covol(\Lambda)
, we obtain

(r-c)^{n}\vol(X)\leq|(\Lambda+\mathbf{t})\cap rX|\cdot\covol(\Lambda)\leq(r+c)^{n}\vol(X).

Dividing by
\vol(\Lambda)
and using the estimate
e^{-2nc/r}\leq(1-c/r)^{n}\leq(1+c/r)^{n}\leq e^{2nc/r}
(note that
r>2c
) we
arrive at the final claim.
∎

2.4.
Number fields

Throughout this paper, we consider a number field
K
of rank
n
over
\mathbb{Q}
, having ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, discriminant
\Delta_{K}
, regulator
R_{K}
, class number
h_{K}
and group of roots of unity
\mu_{K}
. Additionally, we consider a
modulus
: a formal product
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
is an integral ideal and
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
is a formal product of infinite places (see more details below).
We know by Minkowski’s theorem
[
56
, pp. 261–264]

that
1
1

1

We have
|\Delta_{K}|\geq\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right)^{n}n^{2n}/(n!)^{2}\geq\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^{n}
for
n\geq 2
, since
n^{2n}/(n!)^{2}\geq 2^{n}
for
n\geq 2
.

\log|\Delta_{K}|\geq\log(\pi/2)n\geq 0.4n
for
n\geq 2
.

The number field
K
has
n
field embeddings into
\mathbb{C}
, which are divided in
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}
real embeddings and
{n_{\mathbb{C}}}
conjugate pairs of complex embeddings, with
n={n_{\mathbb{R}}}+2{n_{\mathbb{C}}}
. These embeddings combined yield the so-called Minkowski embedding

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\longrightarrow K_{\mathbb{R}}\subseteq\bigoplus_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma},

where

K_{\mathbb{R}}=\bigg\{x\in\bigoplus_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}\penalty\ \bigg|\penalty\ x_{\overline{\sigma}}=\overline{x_{\sigma}}\bigg\}.

Here,
\overline{\sigma}
equals the conjugate embedding of
\sigma
whenever
\sigma
is a complex embedding and it is just
\sigma
itself whenever it is a real embedding. We index the components of the vectors in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
by the embeddings of
K
, i.e., we write
x=(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Embeddings up to conjugation are called infinite places, denoted by
\nu
. With any embedding
\sigma
we denote by
\nu_{\sigma}
the associated place; and for any place we choose a fixed embedding
\sigma_{\nu}
. We will sometimes write
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
to mean that the associated place
\nu_{\sigma}
divides
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
. For any radius
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we write
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ |x_{\sigma}|\leq r\mbox{ for all }\sigma\}
, if it is clear from the context that
K_{\mathbb{R}}
is
the vector space at hand.

Ideals

The group of fractional ideals of
K
is denoted by
\ideals
. Fractional ideals are denoted by
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\ldots
, and the symbols
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
are generally reserved for integral prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Any
\mathfrak{a}\in\ideals
factors uniquely as a product of prime ideals (with possibly negative exponents), and we denote by
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a})
the exponent of
\mathfrak{p}
in the factorization of
\mathfrak{a}
; by extension, for any elements
\alpha\in K^{*}
we define
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\alpha)=\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a})
for
\mathfrak{a}=(\alpha)
the principal ideal generated by
\alpha
.
Two fractional ideals
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}
are said to be coprime if their unique decomposition into a product of prime ideals (with possibly negative coefficients) do not share any prime ideals.
The algebraic norm of a fractional ideal
\mathfrak{a}
or an element
\alpha\in K
is denoted
\norm(\mathfrak{a})
and
\norm(\alpha)
, respectively. When
\mathfrak{a}=(\alpha)
, we have
\norm(\mathfrak{a})=|\norm(\alpha)|
.

The class number formula.

Let
h_{K}
be the class number of
K
,
R_{K}
be its regulator and
\mu_{K}
be its group of roots of unity. Let also
\zeta_{K}
be the Dedekind zeta function of
K
, and
\rho_{K}
be its residue at
1
.
The class number formula
[
58
, VII.§5, Cor 5.11]
states that

\rho_{K}:=\lim_{s\rightarrow 1}(s-1)\zeta_{K}(s)=\frac{2^{n_{\mathbb{R}}}\cdot(2\pi)^{n_{\mathbb{C}}}\cdot R_{K}\cdot h_{K}}{|\mu_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}}.

(4)

The Dedekind residue
\rho_{K}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is bounded above
2
2

2

We use here that
\log(ex/2)\leq x
for all
x>0
and the fact that
\log(|\Delta_{K}|)/(n-1)>0
for
n\geq 2
.

[
53
]
: for
n=[K:\mathbb{Q}]>1
, we have

\log(\rho_{K})\leq(n-1)\cdot\log\left(\frac{e\log|\Delta_{K}|}{2(n-1)}\right)\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|.

(5)

It can be approximated in polynomial time up to some factor
[
5
]
, and so can the related quantity
R_{K}\cdot h_{K}
. More precisely, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.9

(ERH)
.

There exists a polynomial time algorithm (in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
) that takes as input any number field
K
,
and an LLL-reduced basis of its ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
and outputs
\rho_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that

\displaystyle\rho_{0}

\displaystyle\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]\cdot\rho_{K}

\displaystyle\eta_{0}

\displaystyle\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]\cdot R_{K}\cdot h_{K}.

Density of ideals

In this article, we consider families of ideals, like smooth ideals or prime ideals. Given a specifically randomly generated ideal, we want to estimate the probability that it belongs to a given family.
The notion of local density provides a certain approximation of this probability, for uniformly random ideals of bounded norm.

Definition 2.10
.

For any set of ideals
\mathcal{S}
, we define
\mathcal{S}(t)=\{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq t\}
.

Definition 2.11

(Local density of an ideal set)
.

Let
x>0
a positive real number, and let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of integral ideals of
K
. We define the
local density
of
\mathcal{S}
at
x
as

\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[x]=\min_{t\in[x/e^{n},x]}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}(t)\rvert}{\rho_{K}\cdot t}=\min_{t\in[x/e^{n},x]}\frac{|\{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq t\}|}{\rho_{K}\cdot t},

where
\rho_{K}=\lim_{s\rightarrow 1}(s-1)\zeta_{K}(s)
(see
Equation

4
).

Note that the local density tends to the (asymptotic) ‘natural density’ of
\mathcal{S}
as
x\rightarrow\infty
, since
|\{\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{a})<t\}|\sim\rho_{K}\cdot t

[
60
, §9.5]
. However, this notion of natural density is not fine enough as it is simply
0
for families of interest like smooth ideals or prime ideals.

Ray

The group of fractional ideals coprime with the finite part
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
of the modulus
\mathfrak{m}
is denoted by
\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
.
We denote by

K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=\langle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\equiv 1\mbox{ mod }\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mbox{ and }\sigma(\alpha)>0\mbox{ for all real }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\rangle

the
ray
modulo
\mathfrak{m}
, a multiplicative subgroup of
K^{*}
. Here, the notation
\langle\cdot\rangle
means that
K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
is multiplicatively generated by the elements
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
satisfying
\alpha\equiv 1\mbox{ mod }\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mbox{ and }\sigma(\alpha)>0\mbox{ for all real }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, which also includes fractions.

The
ideal ray class group

\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
modulo
\mathfrak{m}
is defined as the quotient of
\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
by the subgroup
\mbox{Princ}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}:=\{(\alpha)\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\}
.
One retrieves the (ordinary) ideal class group by taking an empty modulus
\mathfrak{m}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
, for which
K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=K^{*}
.
We also define
K^{\mathfrak{m}}=\langle\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\alpha)+\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}\rangle
, the multiplicative subgroup of
K^{*}
generated by elements coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. Note that
K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\subseteq K^{\mathfrak{m}}
and that
K^{\mathfrak{m}}=K^{*}
if
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
. The number of
real places

\nu\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
is denoted by
|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|
. We denote
\norm(\mathfrak{m})=\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}
. We will also use the generalized Euler totient
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=|K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}/K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}|
which equals
|(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}|
for
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subsetneq\mathcal{O}_{K}
and equals
1
for
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

We denote
K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x_{\sigma}>0\mbox{ for real }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\}

for the ‘positive part’ of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
with respect to the modulus
\mathfrak{m}
. For any
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
, we denote

\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x_{\sigma}/\sigma(\tau)>0\mbox{ for real }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\}
,
which is the part of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
that has the same sign as
\tau
at the real embeddings
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
.

2.5.
Ideal lattices

Ideals can be viewed as lattices in the real vector space
K_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
K_{\mathbb{R}}
has its (Euclidean or maximum)
norm inherited from the complex
vector space it lives in. Explicitly, the Euclidean and maximum norm of
\alpha\in K
are
respectively defined by the rules
\|\alpha\|^{2}=\sum_{\sigma}|\sigma(\alpha)|^{2}
and
\|\alpha\|_{\infty}=\max_{\sigma}|\sigma(\alpha)|
,
where
\sigma
ranges over all embeddings
K\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

For any ideal
\mathfrak{a}
of
K
, we define the associated lattice
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}
to be the image of
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq K

under the Minkowski embedding, which is clearly a discrete additive subgroup of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
. Abusing notation,
we denote both the ideal and the associated lattice with the same symbol
\mathfrak{a}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is a lattice.
Note that we have
\vol(\mathfrak{a})\!=\!\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\norm(\mathfrak{a})
for ideals
\mathfrak{a}\in\ideals
.
The notion of
ideal lattices
extends to a larger family of lattices in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
as follows.

Definition 2.12

(Ideal lattices)
.

Let
K
be a number field with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. An
ideal lattice
of
K
is a lattice in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
of the form
x\mathfrak{a}
where
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
is invertible and
\mathfrak{a}
is a
fractional ideal of
K
.
We denote the group of ideal lattices by
\idlat_{K}
.

The set of ideal lattices is a group with product
(x\mathfrak{a})(y\mathfrak{b})=(xy)(\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b})
,
inverse
x^{-1}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
and unit
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Bounds on invariants of ideal lattices

Denote
\Gamma(\Lambda)=\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)
, and define, for a fixed number field
K
:

\Gamma_{K}=\sup_{x\mathfrak{a}\in\idlat_{K}}\Gamma(x\mathfrak{a})

(6)

We have the following bounds.

Lemma 2.13
.

For any ideal lattice
x\mathfrak{a}\in\idlat_{K}
,

(i)

\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\geq\sqrt{n}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}
.

(ii)

\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\sqrt{n}\leq\Gamma_{K}\leq\lambda^{\infty}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\leq|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
.

(iii)

For cyclotomic number fields
K
,
\Gamma_{K}=1
.

(iv)

\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a})\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot\Gamma_{K}\cdot\vol(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}
.

(v)

{\operatorname{cov}_{\infty}}(x\mathfrak{a})\leq{\operatorname{cov}}(x\mathfrak{a})\leq n/2\cdot\Gamma_{K}\cdot\vol(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}
.

Proof.

The first item follows from the fact that for any non-zero element
z\in x\mathfrak{a}
, it holds that
\norm(x\mathfrak{a})\leq|\norm(z)|
and that
|\norm(z)|^{2/n}\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\|z\|^{2}
by the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means, applied to the
(|\sigma(z)|^{2})_{\sigma}
. Applying this to a
z
reaching
\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})
yields
\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\geq\sqrt{n}|\norm(z)|^{1/n}\geq\sqrt{n}\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}
.
For the second item, the inequality
\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\sqrt{n}\leq\Gamma_{K}
follows from the definition of
\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{K})
and the fact that
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{K})=\sqrt{n}
(using that
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\geq\sqrt{n}
by the first item and that this lower bound is reached by
1\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
).
To obtain the bound
\Gamma_{K}\leq\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
, pick an arbitrary ideal lattice
x\mathfrak{a}\in\idlat_{K}
and choose a shortest element
x\alpha\in x\mathfrak{a}
with
\alpha\in\mathfrak{a}\in\ideals
.
That means
\|x\alpha\|=\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})
. Then
x\mathfrak{a}\supset x\cdot(\alpha)
, and
therefore,

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{n}(x\cdot\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K})\leq\|x\alpha\|\cdot\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\leq\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\cdot\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K}).

(7)

The bound
\lambda^{\infty}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\leq|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
is obtained from
[
8
, Theorem 3.1]
and is tailored to our purposes in
Theorem

A.3.1
.
Part (iii) follows from part (ii) and the fact that
\|\zeta\|=\|1\|
for roots of unity
\zeta\in K
. Part (iv) is essentially Minkowski’s bound
\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\leq\sqrt{n}\vol(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}
combined with the definition of
\Gamma_{K}
. Finally, the last item follows from the fact that
{\operatorname{cov}}(\Lambda)\leq\sqrt{n}/2\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)

[
54
, Theorem 7.9]
.
∎

2.6.
Representation of elements and ideals

We assume throughout this paper that the number field
K
is represented by a monic irreducible polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
satisfying
\size(f):=\sum_{i}\log_{2}|f_{i}|\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
. This restriction is very mild, indeed we can prove that such a polynomial always exists (see
Section

A.4
) and there are
heuristic
polynomial time algorithms computing such polynomials, e.g.,
[
24
,
35
]
.

Additionally, we assume throughout this paper that we know an LLL-reduced basis
(\mathbf{b}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
of the
ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
of
K
.
Such a basis has vectors (represented as polynomials in
\mathbb{Q}[x]/(f(x))
) whose size
is polynomially bounded
3
3

3

Let
\theta
denote the class of
x
in
\mathbb{Q}[x]/(f(x))
, and write
b_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{i,j}\theta^{j-1}
for the elements of the LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
(with
q_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Q}
). Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be the matrix whose columns correspond to the Minkowski embedding of the
b_{i}
’s, and
\mathbf{T}
be the one corresponding to the
\theta^{j-1}
’s. Then
(q_{i,j})_{i,j}=\mathbf{T}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{B}
. By LLL reducedness of the
b_{i}
’s, it holds that the log of the coefficients of
\mathbf{B}
are polynomially bounded in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
. The coefficients of
\mathbf{T}^{-1}
correspond to the coefficients of the Lagrange polynomials associated to the roots of
f
, so their logarithm is also polynomially bounded in
\size(f)=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
(using e.g., Mignotte’s root separation lower bound and Cauchy’s upper bound on the roots). Hence, we conclude that
|\log(q_{i,j})|\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
.
It remains to prove that the denominators of the
q_{i}
are bounded.
As the integral basis
(1,\theta,\ldots,\theta^{n-1})
has discriminant
\Delta(f)
, any denominator of
q_{i}
must divide
\Delta(f)/\Delta_{K}
(since
\beta
is integral). Since the size of
\Delta(f)
is bounded by
\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
(per assumption), any denominator of
q_{i}
must so, too.

in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
. Note that such a basis can be computed in unconditional, probabilistic subexponential time in
\size(f)
(by factoring
\mathrm{disc}(f)
with
[
65
]
, computing a basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
with
[
20
, Theorem 1.4]
, and reducing it with the LLL algorithm
[
52
]
).

For the main result of Part
3
such a basis
is not required per se (see the discussion in
Section

9.5
), as any
sub-order of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
would suffice as well. This choice for a basis of the ring of integers
is done purely because it simplifies the description
and analysis of this main result.

All elements and ideals in
K
can then be
represented by vectors and bases with rational
coefficients, by using this basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
as
a coordinate system; which we do in this paper.

The
size

\size(k)
of a number
k\in\mathbb{Z}
is defined to be
\log_{2}|k|

and is extended to rationals
\frac{a}{b}
as
\size(a)+\size(b)
for reduced
fractions
\frac{a}{b}
. The size of a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector is just the sum of the
sizes of its entries, which allows to define the size of an element
\alpha\in K

as the size of the rational vector representing
\alpha
in the basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. The size
of a matrix
M_{\mathfrak{a}}
defining an ideal
\mathfrak{a}
is defined as the sum of the sizes
of the matrix entries.

Basic operations, such as addition, multiplication, inversion and approximate
computation of a complex embedding of elements in
K
, are all polynomial in
\log|\Delta_{K}|

and the (just defined) size of the elements involved.

Elements
x=(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
are represented with rational coefficients, that is,
x\in\bigoplus_{\sigma}(\mathbb{Q}+i\mathbb{Q})\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}
,
and the size of such an element is the size of the vector
(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
(which can be seen as a vector in
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
).

Representation of ideals.

We assume throughout the paper that
all integral ideals
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
are represented
by their Hermite Normal form (HNF) basis by default. This is possible since
their matrix, using the basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
as coordinate system, has integral coefficients.

This requirement allows us to abuse terminology and write sentences like “the algorithm takes as input an integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}
”, without having to specify which basis of the ideal is given to the algorithm.
As the HNF basis
of an ideal is a canonical representation of that ideal, it cannot lead to any confusion.

Addition, multiplication and inversion of ideals given in HNF basis can be performed in time polynomial in the input size and in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
.
Additionally, we have that the HNF basis of an integral
ideal
\mathfrak{a}
is bounded in size by
\poly(n,\log\norm(\mathfrak{a}))
. So, by using the HNF basis of an integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}
by default, one avoids the problem of the specific representation
of
\mathfrak{a}
bearing an influence on the running time of algorithms involving
\mathfrak{a}
.
We therefore define
\size(\mathfrak{a})
to be the size of its HNF basis.
For a finite set
{\mathbb{S}}
consisting of
ideals we define
\size({\mathbb{S}})=|{\mathbb{S}}|\cdot\max_{\mathfrak{a}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\size(\mathfrak{a})
.
For a modulus
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
is a formal product of infinite places, we define
\size(\mathfrak{m})=\size(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
. Namely, as higher powers of
infinite places do not have any influence as compared to a power of one, we will assume throughout this work that the infinite part
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
only consists of single powers of places of
K
.

Compact representation of elements.

In Part
11
of this article, an algorithm is
discussed that computes a fundamental system of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units.
In this algorithm, the output elements are given in a so-called
compact representation
,
which we will explain presently.

Given a set of elements
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k}\}
of
K
,
we can write the element
\eta=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\gamma_{j}^{n_{j}}

in compact representation by the pair of vectors

(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})\in K^{n}

(8)

In this way,
\eta
is not explicitly computed in terms of the
basis of
K
(or
\mathcal{O}_{K}
), but rather, the product is left implicit,
allowing for much larger elements
\eta\in K
to be described.
Indeed,
\eta=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\gamma_{j}^{n_{j}}
written in an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-basis might require
\sum_{j}n_{j}\size(\gamma_{j})

bits to write down, whereas the compact representation in
Equation

8

only requires at most
\sum_{j}\size(n_{j})+\sum_{j}\size(\gamma_{j})
bits to write down. The drawback of this representation is that it only allows polynomial time multiplication and inversion of elements, but generally not efficient addition.

2.7.
The logarithmic embedding

The logarithmic embedding of
K^{\times}
is the map

\Log:K^{\times}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}:\alpha\longmapsto(n_{\nu}\log|\sigma_{\nu}(\alpha)|)_{\nu},

where
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is a complex place and
1
otherwise.
This map naturally extends to all invertible elements of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
, and this extension is surjective, i.e.,
\Log K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}=\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
.
Denoting
K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \prod_{\sigma}x_{\sigma}=1\}
, we use this logarithmic map to define
the vector space
H=\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0})
(called the hyperplane) and the
logarithmic unit lattice

\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})\subseteq H,

a full-rank
lattice in
H
.
We write its dimension by
\mathbbm{r}=\dim(H)=n_{\mathbb{R}}+n_{\mathbb{C}}-1
. Explicitly, we have

H=\Log K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}=\{(x_{\nu})_{\nu}\in\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sum_{\nu}x_{\nu}=0\}.

The volume of the logarithmic unit lattice is given by the following formula (see, e.g.,
[
58
, I.§7 & I.§5, p. 33]
)

\vol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))=\sqrt{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}}\cdot R_{K}.

(9)

The first minimum of
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})

By a result of Kessler
[
46
]
, we have a lower bound on the first minimum of the lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
.

Lemma 2.14
.

We have
\lambda_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))\geq\frac{1}{1000\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\log(n)^{3}}
.

2.8.
Divisors

We define the divisor group
\Div_{K}
of
K
as

\Div_{K}:=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}\times\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R},

where
\nu
ranges over the set of all infinite places (embeddings into the complex numbers up to possible conjugation), and
\mathfrak{p}
ranges over all prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
(also referred to as the
finite places
of
K
).
We denote the canonical basis elements with the symbols
\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis
and
\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis
(the divisor with value
1
at
\mathfrak{p}
or
\nu
respectively, and
0
everywhere else).
Then, an arbitrary divisor can be written as

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis,

with only finitely many non-zero
a_{\mathfrak{p}}
and with
a_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
.
We will consistently use the symbols
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{e},\ldots
for such divisors.
Given such a divisor
\mathbf{a}
, we often write
{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis
for its “finite part” and
{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}=\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis
for its “infinite part”.

The degree map

The degree map is defined as

\displaystyle\deg:\Div_{K}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\log(\norm(\mathfrak{p}))+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}.

(10)

The kernel of this map is the subgroup
\Div^{0}_{K}=\ker(\deg)
of degree-zero divisors.

From field elements to divisors

Denoting
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}
for the valuation at the prime
\mathfrak{p}
, there is a canonical homomorphism

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis:K^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Div_{K}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\longmapsto\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrm{ord}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\alpha)\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis-\underbrace{\sum_{\nu}n_{\nu}\log|\sigma_{\nu}(\alpha)|\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis}_{\Log(\alpha)},

with
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise.
The
product formula
states that for any
\alpha\in K^{\times}
, we have
\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis\in\Div^{0}_{K}
, i.e.,
\llparenthesis K^{\times}\rrparenthesis\subset\Div_{K}^{0}
.

The exponential maps

The finite part
{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}\in\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}
of a divisor
\mathbf{a}
naturally corresponds to an ideal in the group
\ideals
of fractional ideals via the map

{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}:\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\ideals:\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis\longmapsto\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}.

This map is an isomorphism with inverse

d:\ideals\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}:\mathfrak{a}\longmapsto\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a})\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis.

(11)

We will often use a normalized section defined as

d^{0}:\ideals\longrightarrow\Div_{K}^{0}:\mathfrak{a}\longmapsto\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a})\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis-\frac{\log(\norm(\mathfrak{a}))}{n}\sum_{\nu}n_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis,

(12)

to map into
\Div_{K}^{0}
instead of
\Div_{K}
.

The infinite part
{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}\in\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
of
\mathbf{a}
can be mapped into
K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
via

{\Exp}_{\infty}:\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}:\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\longmapsto(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\cdot a_{\nu_{\sigma}}})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}.

The map
{\Exp}_{\infty}
is injective, but not surjective.
Note that the logarithmic embedding
\Log
introduced in
Section

2.7
is a retraction of
{\Exp}_{\infty}
(i.e.,
\Log\circ{\Exp}_{\infty}
is the identity).
Furthermore, for any
x=(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, we have
{\Exp}_{\infty}(\Log(x))=(|x_{\sigma}|)_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
.

The main reason for us to consider divisors is that they encode ideal lattices. This naturally follows by combining the above exponential maps into the following:

\Exp:\Div_{K}\longrightarrow\idlat_{K}:\mathbf{a}\longmapsto{\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}).

For a divisor
\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis
, the associated ideal lattice is

\Exp(\mathbf{a})=(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\cdot a_{\nu_{\sigma}}})_{\sigma}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}=\left\{(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\cdot a_{\nu_{\sigma}}}\cdot\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \alpha\in\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}\right\}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}.

It is a group homomorphism sending the additive operation in
\Div_{K}
to the multiplicative operation in
\idlat_{K}
.
For any divisor
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K}
, we have

\vol(\Exp(\mathbf{a}))\!=\!\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot\prod_{\nu}e^{a_{\nu}}\cdot\norm\left(\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{\mathfrak{p}}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}\right)=\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot e^{\deg(\mathbf{a})}.

Euclidean norm on
\Div_{K}

For
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K}
written as

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis,

we define the norm

\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert=\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

Note that, naturally,
H\subseteq\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times})\hookrightarrow\Div_{K}
, where the defined Euclidean norms are compatible.

Subgroups of
\Div_{K}

Given a set
{\mathbb{S}}
of prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we define the subgroup

\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathbb{Z}\times\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}\subseteq\Div_{K}.

In the next two parts of this article, we will work with two important cases.
In
Section

3
, we will consider a modulus
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, and let
{\mathbb{S}}
be the set of all prime ideals that do not divide
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. The resulting group
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}\times\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
is called the
Arakelov
\mathfrak{m}
-ray divisor group
.
In
Section

11
, we will consider cases where the set
{\mathbb{S}}
is finite. We then call
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
the
{\mathbb{S}}
-divisor group, and it relates to the classical notion of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units.
Further details on each specific case are discussed in the relevant sections.

2.9.
Probabilities and (Discrete) Gaussian distributions

Given a distribution
D
over a discrete set
X
and
x\in X
, we denote
D(x)
for the probability that
D
outputs
x
and
D(A)=\sum_{x\in A}D(x)
for any
A\subseteq X
.

Definition 2.15

(Statistical distance)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
be a measurable space with probability measures
P,Q
. The statistical distance (or total variation distance) between
P
and
Q
is defined by the rule

SD(P,Q)=\sup_{X\in\mathcal{S}}|P(X)-Q(X)|.

For a discrete space
\Omega
, we have

SD(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\Omega}|P(x)-Q(x)|=:\frac{1}{2}\|P-Q\|_{1}.

For a continuous space
\Omega
with probability densities
P,Q
, we have

SD(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{x\in\Omega}|P(x)-Q(x)|=:\frac{1}{2}\|P-Q\|_{1}.

For spaces that are partially discrete and partially continuous, some well-defined mix of an integral and a sum will define the statistical distance.
Due to the equivalence of these notions (up to a constant
\frac{1}{2}
) we will often describe closeness of probability distributions in terms of the distance metric
\|\cdot\|_{1}
, instead of
SD(\cdot,\cdot)
.

The data processing inequality captures the idea that an algorithm (by just processing a single query) cannot
increase the statistical distance between two probability distributions. A proof can be found for example in
[
28
, §2.8]
.

Theorem 2.16

(Data processing inequality)
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{S})
be a measurable space with probability measures
P,Q
. Let
f
be a (potentially probabilistic) function on
\Omega
. Then

\|f(P)-f(Q)\|_{1}\leq\|P-Q\|_{1}.

In the rest of this section, we recall known results about Gaussian distributions and prove some lemmas that will be useful for the rest of the article.

Gaussian distributions

We denote
g_{s}({x})=\exp(-\pi\|{x}\|^{2}/s^{2})
the
Gaussian function defined on any Euclidean vector space
V
.
Since
\int_{{x}\in V}g_{s}({x})d{x}=s^{\dim V}
,
we define the continuous Gaussian distribution on a Euclidean vector space
V
with parameter
s
and center
{c}
by
the density function

\mathcal{G}_{V,s,{c}}({v})=s^{-\dim(V)}\cdot g_{s}({v}-{c}).

For discrete sets
X\subseteq V
, specifically lattices, we use the same notation for
the
discrete Gaussian
, where the subscript
X
indicates that the
Gaussian is discrete.

\mathcal{G}_{X,s,{c}}({x})=g_{s}({x}-{c})/g_{s}(X-{c}),

where
g_{s}(X-{c})=\sum_{{x}\in X}g_{s}({x}-{c})
. This notation
f(X)=\sum_{{x}\in X}f({x})

for functions
f
and discrete sets
X
will be used frequently. Whenever the
Gaussian is centered at the origin, i.e.,
{c}=0
, we will suppress the subscript
c
from the notation, like this:
\mathcal{G}_{V,s}
.

Tail bounds of Gaussian distributions

The following lemma originates from Banaszczyk’s paper on transference theorems in lattices
[
6
, Lemma 1.5]
.

Lemma 2.17

(
[
6
, Lemma 1.5]
)
.

For any
c>1/\sqrt{2\pi}
and any
n
dimensional lattice
\Lambda
,
g_{1}(\Lambda\setminus c\sqrt{n}\mathcal{B})\leq C^{n}g_{1}(\Lambda)
, where
\mathcal{B}
is the euclidean ball of radius 1 and
C=c\sqrt{2\pi\mathrm{e}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\pi c^{2}}<1
.

Corollary 2.18
.

Let
\Lambda
be a lattice of rank
n
and
s>0
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, it holds that
\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s}}(\|x\|\geq s\cdot\sqrt{\log(1/\varepsilon)+2n})\leq\varepsilon
.

We also have a similar result in the case of a continuous Gaussian distribution.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
V
be a real vector space of dimension
n
and
s>0
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, it holds that
\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{V,s}}(\|x\|\geq s\cdot\sqrt{2n\cdot\log(2n/\varepsilon)})\leq\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Let
B
be an orthonormal basis of
V
and write
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
the coordinates of
x
in this basis. Then the random variables
x_{i}
are linearly independent Gaussian distributions over
\mathbb{R}
with standard deviation
s
. Moreover, for any
t>0
, if
\|x\|\geq t
, there should exist some
i
such that
|x_{i}|\geq t/\sqrt{n}
. Hence, we obtain

\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{V,s}}\big(\|x\|\geq t\big)\leq n\cdot\Pr_{x\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{R},s}}\big(|x|\geq t/\sqrt{n}\big)\leq 2n\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{2n\cdot s^{2}}\Big),

where the first inequality comes from the union bound and the last one comes from Chernoff’s bound. Taking
t=s\cdot\sqrt{2n\cdot\log(2n/\varepsilon)}
leads to the desired result.
∎

Smoothing on lattices

For any
\varepsilon>0
and a lattice
\Lambda
we define the smoothing parameter
[
55
]

\eta_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda)
to
be the smallest
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
g_{1/s}(\Lambda^{\vee}\backslash\{0\})\leq\varepsilon
, where

\Lambda^{\vee}=\{x\in\mbox{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \langle x,\ell\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ for all }\ell\in\Lambda\}
is the
dual lattice of
\Lambda
. This smoothing parameter satisfies the following bound
[
55
, Lemma 3.3]

\displaystyle\eta_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log(2n(1+1/\varepsilon))}{\pi}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda).

(13)

We will use the following classical results on the total Gaussian weight of a (shifted) lattice
\Lambda
.

Lemma 2.20

(Proof of
[
55
, Lemma 4.4]
)
.

Let
\Lambda
be an
n
-dimensional lattice and
s\geq\eta_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda)
for some
\varepsilon>0
. We have

g_{s}(\Lambda)\in\left[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\right]\cdot\frac{s^{n}}{\covol(\Lambda)}.

Lemma 2.21

(
[
55
, Lemma 2.9]
)
.

Let
\Lambda
be an
n
-dimensional lattice,
s>0
be a positive real number and
c\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)
be a vector, then
g_{s}(\Lambda+c)\leq g_{s}(\Lambda)
.

Approximating and computing discrete Gaussians

We will use the following lemma which says that one can sample from a distribution statistically close to a discrete Gaussian distribution.
It is an adaptation of
[
36
, Theorem 4.1]
for which the Gaussian can be approximated within any error and in which only bit operations are used (as opposed to real number operations as in the original article).

Lemma 2.22

(Adapted from
[
36
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

There exists a probabilistic algorithm that takes as input a basis
B=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n\times n}
of an
n
-dimensional lattice
\Lambda
, an error bound
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}\in(0,1]
, a parameter
s\geq\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})+2\log(n)+3}{\pi}}\cdot\max_{i}\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|
and a center
\mathbf{c}\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Lambda)\cap\mathbb{Q}^{n}
and outputs a sample from a distribution
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{B},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}},s,\mathbf{c}}
such that
\text{SD}(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{B},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}},s,\mathbf{c}},\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s,\mathbf{c}})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}
.
This algorithm runs in expected time (bit complexity) polynomial in the size of the input and in
\log(1/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})
. Additionally, the output of
\mathbf{v}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{B},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}},s,\mathbf{c}}
always satisfies
\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{c}\|\leq s\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n^{2}/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})}
.

Proof.

The proof can be adapted from the one of
[
36
, Theorem 4.1]
, which proves the result for
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}=n^{-\omega(1)}
. In this proof, the statistical distance between the sampled distribution
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{B},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}},s,c}
and the ideal distribution
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s,\mathbf{c}}
comes from two places.
The first one is that the algorithm makes
n
calls to a sub-routine algorithm
\texttt{Sample}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, sampling from a Gaussian distribution over
\mathbb{Z}
which is only statistically close to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Z},s^{\prime},c^{\prime}}
(for some parameters
c^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
depending on the input). Lemmas 4.2 and 4.3 from
[
36
]
show that the statistical distance between
\texttt{Sample}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Z},s^{\prime},c^{\prime}}
can be made as small as any arbitrary
\delta>0
, to the cost of increasing the running time of
\texttt{Sample}_{\mathbb{Z}}
in a way that is polynomial in

\log(1/\delta)
. We choose
\delta=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}/(2n)
, which provides a running time polynomial in
\log(1/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})
and
\log n
, and ensures that the
n
calls to
\texttt{Sample}_{\mathbb{Z}}
are responsible for a change of statistical distance that is at most
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}/2
.

The second reason why the algorithm only provides a distribution that is statistically close to
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s,c}
comes from the fact that
g_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}+c)
is only approximately close to
g_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z})
for an arbitrary
c\in\mathbb{R}
(where
s^{\prime}\geq s/(\max_{i}\|b_{i}\|)
). In the proof of Theorem 4.1, the authors of
[
36
]
show that, provided that
s\geq\eta_{\delta}(\mathbb{Z})\cdot\max_{i}\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|
, then this statistical distance is at most
\delta^{\prime}/2
, where
1+\delta^{\prime}=\left(\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\right)^{n}
.
We choose
\delta=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}/(8n)\leq 1/(8n)
.
Using the fact that
4
4

4

We have
\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\leq 1+4\delta
for
\delta\leq 1/2
. Also,
(1+x)^{n}\leq(e^{x})^{n}=e^{nx}\leq 1+2nx
for
x<1/(2n)
. Hence, for
\delta\leq 1/(8n)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
,
\left(\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\right)^{n}\leq(1+4\delta)^{n}\leq 1+8n\delta
.

\left(\frac{1+\delta}{1-\delta}\right)^{n}\leq 1+8n\delta
for all
\delta\leq 1/(8n)
and
n\geq 1
, we obtain that the statistical distance between the approximate distribution and the ideal one is at most
4n\delta=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}/2
.
Using
Equation

13
, we see that
\eta_{\delta}(\mathbb{Z})\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})+2\log(n)+3}{\pi}}
, hence
s\geq\eta_{\delta}(\mathbb{Z})\cdot\max_{i}\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|
as desired.

For the bound on
{v}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{B},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}},s,\mathbf{c}}
observe the output
\mathbf{v}_{0}
of the algorithm in
[
36
, Section 4.2]
. By
[
36
, Lemma 4.4]
, the output of this algorithm satisfies
\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{c}=\sum_{i\in[n]}(\hat{z}_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i})\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i}
, where the values
\hat{z}_{i}
and
c_{i}^{\prime}
are from the algorithm, and the
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i}
are the Gram-Schmidt vectors associated to the
\mathbf{b}_{i}
’s.
Hence, by the Pythagorean theorem,
\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{c}\|^{2}=\sum_{i\in[n]}|\hat{z}_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}\cdot\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i}\|^{2}
. In part 1(b) of the algorithm
[
36
, Section 4.2]
,
D_{\mathbb{Z},s^{\prime}_{i},c^{\prime}_{i}}
is implemented as in
[
36
, Section 4.1]
, i.e., the algorithm
Sample
\mathbb{Z}
is called (see the proof of
[
36
, Theorem 4.1]
). Therefore
\hat{z}_{i}
can be shown to lie in
\mathbb{Z}\cap[c^{\prime}_{i}-t(n)s^{\prime}_{i},c^{\prime}_{i}+t(n)s^{\prime}_{i}]
, where
t(n)=\sqrt{\log(n/\delta)}
(with
\delta=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}/(2n)
, as before). As a consequence,

\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{c}\|^{2}=\sum_{i\in[n]}|\hat{z}_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}\cdot\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i}\|^{2}\leq\sum_{i\in[n]}t(n)^{2}(s_{i}^{\prime})^{2}\cdot\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i}\|^{2}=\sum_{i\in[n]}t(n)^{2}s^{2}=nt(n)^{2}s^{2},

by the definitions of
s_{i}^{\prime}=s/\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i}\|
. Hence
\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{c}\|\leq\sqrt{n\log(n/\delta)}s
with
\delta=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}/(2n)
, as was required to prove.

We show now that the algorithm in
[
36
]
can be readily adapted into one without real number operations, but just bit operations. There are two places in the algorithm in
[
36
, Section 3.2]
where real arithmetic is used, namely in the subroutine
Sample
\mathbb{Z}
and in the computation of
s/\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{k}\|
where
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{k}
are the Gram-Schmidt vectors. Note that the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization itself (without normalizing) can be done with rational arithmetic in polynomial time and is thus not altered in this adapted version.

The subroutine
Sample
\mathbb{Z}
can be amended to avoid real operations by just approximating the sampling probabilities
\rho_{s}(x-c)
with
x\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[c-s\cdot t(n),c+s\cdot t(n)]
well enough. This can be done within polynomial bit complexity in the size of the input, and, by maybe slightly increasing
t(n)
, without loss in the approximation error
\delta
.

The value of
s/\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{k}\|
is only used in the subroutine
Sample
\mathbb{Z}
, where actually its
square
is used to compute
\rho_{s/\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{k}\|}(x-c)
and hence there is no need to compute
s/\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{k}\|
but rather its square
s^{2}/\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{k}\|^{2}
which consists of rational numbers.

Hence we can conclude that this slight adaptation of
[
36
, Section 3]
has polynomial bit-complexity in the size of its input.
∎

3.

\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
of Bhargava et al.

8

.

\@tocwriteb

PART I

Sampling ideals in a class:
\except
tocsmooth, near-prime or otherwise

4.
Introduction

In this first part of the article, we propose a general strategy to provably solve a recurring computational problem in number theory (assuming the extended Riemann hypothesis, ERH): given an ideal class
[\mathfrak{a}]
of a number field
K
, sample an ideal
\mathfrak{c}\in[\mathfrak{a}]
belonging to a particular family of ideals (e.g., the family of smooth ideals, or near-prime ideals).
While there is a simple heuristic algorithm for this task, it has proved notoriously difficult to resolve it rigorously. It has thereby been a central roadblock explaining the heuristic nature of many major algorithms in computational number theory.

The main result of this part is
Theorem

9.5
(page
9.5
). Its formulation in full generality is postponed, as it first requires the introduction of several notions. However, a simplified statement, which may already suit many needs, is available in
Theorem

1.1
(page
1.1
).

Roadmap

Fix an arbitrary family of ideals
\mathcal{S}
.
For convenience, we consider the input to be an ideal
\mathfrak{b}\in[\mathfrak{a}]^{-1}
, and are looking for an ideal
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}
in the inverse class of
\mathfrak{b}
.
The folklore strategy consists in considering
\mathfrak{b}
as an
ideal lattice
via the Minkowski embedding, and sampling random elements
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}
(within some bounds, say in a “box”
r\mathcal{B}
of radius
r
) until
\mathfrak{c}=\beta\mathfrak{b}^{-1}
falls in the desired family of ideals
\mathcal{S}
. Heuristically, one
expects
that for
\beta
“sufficiently random”, the ideal
\beta\mathfrak{b}^{-1}
falls in
\mathcal{S}
with probability proportional to the “density” of the family (think about the set of prime ideals that have norm around
x
having “density”
\approx 1/(\rho_{K}\log(x))
). This, of course, cannot be literally true for arbitrary families (e.g., principal ideals), since
\beta\mathfrak{b}^{-1}
is confined to one ideal class.
Instead, we will solve the problem for
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{B}
is the family of
B
-smooth ideals for some bound
B
. In all applications we are aware of,
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
(smooth ideals, near-prime ideals).

The Arakelov class group

The notion of
ideal lattice
plays a key role in this sampling strategy.
The space of ideal lattices up to isomorphism is naturally isomorphic to the so-called
Arakelov class groups

We open this part of the article in
Section

5
with an introduction to Arakelov ray class groups. They can be thought of as a “combination” of the ray class group and the ray unit group of a number field. In
Section

6
, we state a useful result on these Arakelov ray class groups: certain
random walks
in them rapidly converge to the uniform distribution. This is a generalization of
[
14
]
from Arakelov class groups to Arakelov
ray
class groups.

Average densities

In the folklore strategy, one is hoping that for (uniformly) random
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}\cap r\mathcal{B}
(the intersection of an ideal lattice and a “box”), the probability that
\beta\mathfrak{b}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}
is proportional to the density of
\mathcal{S}
. This is generally not true.
However, in
Section

7
, we prove that it
is
true
on average
when the ideal lattice
\mathfrak{b}
is also random, uniformly distributed in the Arakelov ray class group.
This might sound too weak for our goal: the input
\mathfrak{b}
of the algorithm is not random. It will actually be sufficient when properly combined with a randomization step.

Ideal sampling

We then turn these density results into an algorithm. First, we show in
Section

8
how to sample uniformly a random element in a set of the form
\mathfrak{b}\cap r\mathcal{B}
, the intersection of an ideal lattice and a “box”.

Then,
Section

9
culminates with the main algorithm,
Algorithm

2
.
It addresses the final obstacle: the density result of
Section

7
only holds on average, for uniformly random ideal lattices. The input
\mathfrak{b}
is not random, so we need to randomize it. This is where random walks in the Arakelov class group come in. Essentially,
Algorithm

2
starts by multiplying
\mathfrak{b}
with a few “small” prime ideals, resulting in a random ideal
\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{b}
where
\mathfrak{w}
is smooth (i.e.,
\mathfrak{w}\in\mathcal{S}_{B}
for some bound
B
).

Algorithm

2
then samples a uniformly random
\beta\in(\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{b})\cap r\mathcal{B}
. Then:

•

From
Section

6
and
[
14
]
(on the rapid equidistribution of random walks), the random ideal lattice
\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{b}
is close to uniformly distributed.

•

From
Section

7
, we obtain that
\beta(\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{b})^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}
with probability proportional to the density of
\mathcal{S}
.

Upon the event
\beta(\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{b})^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}
, we get

\beta\mathfrak{b}^{-1}=\mathfrak{w}\cdot(\beta(\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{b})^{-1})\in\mathcal{S}_{B}\cdot\mathcal{S},

as desired.

Further properties

Finally, in
Section

10
and
Section

11
, we develop tools to ease the applicability of this “ideal sampling” algorithm. These tools are indispensable for the application presented in Part
11
, and may be useful in other contexts.
More precisely,
in
Section

10
, three important properties of the ideal sampling algorithm are stated and proved: the
shifting property
,
boundedness
and

almost-Lipschitz-continuity
. In
Section

11
, we estimate quantities related to the modulus
\mathfrak{m}
, which affect the behavior of the ideal sampling algorithm.

5.
Background on the Arakelov class group

Recall that throughout this paper, we consider a number field
K
of rank
n
over
\mathbb{Q}
, having ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, discriminant
\Delta_{K}
, regulator
R_{K}
, class number
h_{K}
and group of roots of unity
\mu_{K}
. It has
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}
real embeddings and
{n_{\mathbb{C}}}
conjugate pairs of complex embeddings, with
n={n_{\mathbb{R}}}+2{n_{\mathbb{C}}}
.

5.1.
The Arakelov Ray Class Group

In this section, we rely heavily on the notation introduced in
Section

2.8
for the divisor group
\Div_{K}
.
The
Arakelov ray divisor group
with respect to a modulus
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
is a subgroup
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}\subseteq\Div_{K}
defined as

\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}\times\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}

where
\mathfrak{p}
ranges over the set of all prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
that do not divide the finite part
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
of the modulus, and
\nu
over the set of all infinite places (embeddings into the complex numbers up to conjugation).
The case
\mathfrak{m}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
yields the standard divisor group
\Div_{K}
.
Recall that we write an arbitrary element in
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}
as

\mathbf{a}=\underbrace{\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis}_{{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}}+\underbrace{\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis}_{{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}}.

(14)

with only finitely many non-zero
a_{\mathfrak{p}}
and with
a_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}
.
The map
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis:K^{\times}\to\Div_{K}
naturally restricts and co-restrict to

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis:K^{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\longmapsto\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\mathrm{ord}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\alpha)\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis-\sum_{\nu}n_{\nu}\log|\sigma_{\nu}(\alpha)|\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis,

where
n_{\nu}=2
whenever
\nu
is a complex place and
1
otherwise.
The divisors of the form
\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
for
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
are called

principal
\mathfrak{m}
-ray divisors
.

Just as the ideal ray class group is the group of ideals coprime with
\mathfrak{m}

quotiented by the ray
K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
, the
Picard ray group
is the group
of Arakelov ray divisors quotiented by the group of principal ray Arakelov divisors. In other
words, the Picard ray group
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}
is defined by the following exact sequence, where
\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}=\mu_{K}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
are the roots of unity in the ray:

0\rightarrow\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}\rightarrow K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\xrightarrow{\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis}\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}\rightarrow\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}\rightarrow 0.

For any Arakelov ray divisor
\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis
, we denote its class in the Picard ray group
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}
by
[\mathbf{a}]
, in the same fashion that

[\mathfrak{a}]
denotes the ideal class of the ideal
\mathfrak{a}
in
\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Since principal ray divisors
\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
for
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
are in the kernel of the degree map,
the degree factors through
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}
. We can therefore define the
degree-zero Arakelov ray divisor group

\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}=\{\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \deg(\mathbf{a})=0\}
and the
Arakelov ray class group

\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}=\{[\mathbf{a}]\in\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \deg([\mathbf{a}])=0\}
.

The maps
{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}
,
{\Exp}_{\infty}
,
d
and
d^{0}
defined in
Section

2.8
naturally restrict and co-restrict to

\displaystyle{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle:\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}\longmapsto\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}},

(15)

\displaystyle{\Exp}_{\infty}
