\displaystyle:\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}\longmapsto\left(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\cdot a_{\nu_{\sigma}}}\right)_{\sigma},

(16)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle:\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}\longmapsto\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a})\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis,

(17)

\displaystyle d^{0}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}\longmapsto d(\mathfrak{a})-\frac{n_{\nu}\log(\norm(\mathfrak{a}))}{n}\sum_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis.

(18)

The groups and their relations treated above fit nicely in the diagram of exact sequences given in
Figure

1
,
where the middle row sequence splits with the section
d^{0}
. In this diagram we use the notation
\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}=\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
and
\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}=\mu_{K}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
.
The group
T^{\mathfrak{m}}=H/\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})
is the
logarithmic ray unit torus
, with
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})
the
logarithmic ray unit lattice
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}/\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathfrak{m},1}/\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Princ}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\mathfrak{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
\Log
\mathbf{a}\mapsto{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
d^{0}

Figure 1
.
A commutative diagram of short exact sequences involving the Arakelov ray class group.

Relations between different ray groups

The (ray) unit groups
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times},\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}
, the (ray) class groups
\Cl_{K},\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
, and the
ray groups
K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
and
K^{\mathfrak{m}}
are tightly related by
an exact sequence (e.g.,
[
49
, Chapter VI, §1]
), relating
the (relative) cardinalities of these groups. Namely,

|\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}|\cdot|\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}|=\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot|\Cl_{K}|,

(19)

where
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=|K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}/K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}|
and
|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|
is the number of real places dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, (note that
|K^{\mathfrak{m}}/K^{\mathfrak{m},1}|=\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}
).
Also, by observing the kernel-cokernel sequence of the inclusions
\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\subseteq H=\log K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
, we obtain,

|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|\cdot|\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}|=|\mu_{K}|\cdot\vol(T^{\mathfrak{m}})/\vol(T).

(20)

Geometric properties of the divisor group and the Arakelov class group

Recall that the norm of a divisor is defined as

\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert=\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}^{2}+\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

In the following lemma, we show that the volume of the
Arakelov ray class group roughly follows the square root of the absolute value of the field discriminant times
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}
; with
a possible additional correction factor due to the roots of unity.

Lemma 5.1

(Volume of
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
)
.

For
n=[K:\mathbb{Q}]>1
, we have

\displaystyle\vol\left({\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=|\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot\vol(T^{\mathfrak{m}})=\frac{|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|}{|\mu_{K}|}\cdot\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot h_{K}\cdot\vol(T)

(21)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|}{|\mu_{K}|}\cdot\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot h_{K}\cdot R_{K}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}+1}\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|},

(22)

where
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=|K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}/K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}|
and
|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|
is the number of real places dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
. Additionally,

\log\left(\vol\left(\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}\right)\right)\leq\log\left(\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\right)+\log|\Delta_{K}|.

(23)

Proof.

The first identity involving the volume of the Arakelov ray class group follows from the exact sequence in
Figure

1
. The second one can be deduced from
Equations

19
and
20
. The third one follows from the volume computation of
T
in

Equation

9
.

The bound on the logarithm is obtained by using
\frac{|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|}{|\mu_{K}|}\leq 1
, applying the class number formula
[
58
, VII.§5, Cor 5.11]
and Louboutin’s bound
[
53
]
on the residue
\rho_{K}
of the Dedekind zeta function at
s=1
:

\displaystyle\lvert\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot h_{K}\cdot R_{K}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}+1}\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}=\frac{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}\cdot\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot|\mu_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}}{2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}-|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}}

\displaystyle\leq\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot\rho_{K}\leq\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot\left(\frac{e\log|\Delta_{K}|}{2(n-1)}\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle\leq\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|.

The first inequality follows from
\frac{|\mu_{K}|\sqrt{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}}{2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}}(2\pi)^{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}\leq\frac{n^{3/2}}{2^{n}}\leq 1
.
The last inequality follows from the fact that
\tfrac{e\log|x|}{|x|}\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. This inequality instantiated with
x=|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}}
then yields
\left(\frac{e\log|\Delta_{K}|}{2(n-1)}\right)^{n-1}\leq\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}
.
∎

5.2.
Divisors and ideal lattices

The
\Exp
map introduced in
Section

2.8
restricts to the group homomorphism

\Exp:\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}\longrightarrow\idlat_{K}:\mathbf{a}\longmapsto{\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}),

sending each Arakelov divisor to an ideal lattice. Recall that for any divisor
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}
, we have

\vol(\Exp(\mathbf{a}))\!=\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot e^{\deg(\mathbf{a})}.

5.3.
\tau
-equivalent elements and generators of an Arakelov ray divisor

Suppose that
\mathfrak{m}
is a modulus, and let
\mathfrak{a}
be any ideal coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.
An element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{a}
is said to be
\tau
-equivalent (with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
)
if
\alpha\equiv\tau\bmod\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and
\sigma_{\nu}(\alpha)/\sigma_{\nu}(\tau)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

for all real
\nu\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
.
If additionally,
\mathfrak{a}
is
principal
, any
\tau
-equivalent element
\alpha

such that
\mathfrak{a}=(\alpha)
is called a
\tau
-equivalent
generator
of
\mathfrak{a}
.

These notions generalize to Arakelov ray divisors.
As we can see Arakelov ray divisors as ideal lattices

x\mathfrak{a}
, an element of such a divisor is just an element of the shape
x\alpha

where
\alpha\in\mathfrak{a}
and
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
. Similarly, a generator of such divisor
is an element in
K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

of the shape
x\alpha
, where
\alpha
is a generator of
\mathfrak{a}
.
The precise definitions are as follows.

Definition 5.2

(
\tau
-equivalent elements of an Arakelov ray divisor)
.

Let
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
and let
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}
be an Arakelov ray divisor with
an infinite part
{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}
and a finite part
{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}
(see
Equation

14
). We define the set of
\tau
-equivalent elements

\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathbf{a}
by the following rule

\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}:={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot({\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})

Equivalently, we can write

\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}=\{\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot\alpha\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\}.

Definition 5.3

(
\tau
-equivalent generators of an Arakelov ray divisor)
.

Let
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
and let
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}
be an Arakelov ray divisor with
an infinite part
{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}
and a finite part
{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}
(see
Equation

14
). We define the set of
\tau
-equivalent generators

{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}
of
\mathbf{a}
by the following rule

{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}:=\begin{cases}{\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot(\kappa\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})\subseteq\Exp(\mathbf{a})&\mbox{ if }{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})=(\kappa)\\
&\mbox{ for some }\kappa\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Equivalently, we can write

{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}=\{\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot\alpha\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\text{ is a generator of }{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})\}.

5.4.
Uniform sampling of prime ideals in a certain Arakelov class

In the main result of this part, we will need to sample primes
\mathfrak{p}
that satisfy
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G
for a certain finite-index subgroup of
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
. The procedure explaining this and the respective running time can be found in this lemma; the proof is provided in
Section

A.2
.

Lemma 5.4

(Uniform sampling of prime ideals, ERH)
.

Let a basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
be known, and let
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
be a modulus.
Let
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
a finite-index subgroup and let
\mathbf{O}_{G}
be an oracle that on input an ideal
\mathfrak{c}
returns whether
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{c})]\in G
or not.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{B}=\{\mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime ideal of K }\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B,\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mbox{ and }[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G\}
.

There exists a bound

B_{0}=\widetilde{O}\Big([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]^{2}\cdot\big[n^{2}(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))^{2}+n^{2}(\log(1/\tilde{s}))^{2}+(\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m})))^{2}\big]\Big)

such that for all
B\geq B_{0}
, one can sample uniformly from
\mathcal{P}_{B}
in expected time
O([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot n^{3}\log^{2}B)
and using
O([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot n\log B)
queries to
\mathbf{O}_{G}
.

6.
Random walks in Arakelov ray class groups

In this section, we present a theorem on the rapid mixing of random walks on finite-index subgroups of Arakelov ray class groups.
It is a generalization of the main theorem of
[
14
]
, from Arakelov class groups to finite-index subgroups
G
of Arakelov ray class groups.
Starting with a point in the
hyperplane
H\hookrightarrow\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, sampled according to a Gaussian distribution, we prove that multiplying
this point sufficiently often by small random prime ideals whose Arakelov classes are in
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

yields a random ray divisor that is very close to uniformly distributed in
G
, (the concerning subgroup of the Arakelov ray class group).

Definition 6.1

(Random Walk Distribution in
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
)
.

For a number field
K
and a finite-index subgroup
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
,
we denote by
\mathcal{W}_{G}(B,N,s)\in L_{1}(\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0})
the distribution on
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
that is obtained by the following random walk procedure.

Sample
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

according to a centered Gaussian distribution with standard deviation
s>0
(see
Section

2.9
). Subsequently, sample
N
ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
uniformly from the set of all prime ideals coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, with norm bounded by
B
and whose Arakelov class
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})]
lies in
G
. Finally, output
a+\sum_{j=1}^{N}d^{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})
, where
a\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
is understood via the inclusion
H\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

Definition 6.2
.

For any distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, we define the distribution
[\mathcal{D}]

on
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
by the following rule:

[\mathcal{D}](\cdot)=\sum_{\kappa\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}/\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}}\mathcal{D}(\cdot+\llparenthesis\kappa\rrparenthesis).

This distribution
[\mathcal{D}]
on
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
arises whenever one samples
\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
and subsequently takes the Arakelov ray class
[\mathbf{a}]\in\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

Definition 6.3

(Random Walk Distribution in
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
)
.

We denote by
[\mathcal{W}_{G}(B,N,s)]
the distribution on the Arakelov class group obtained by sampling
\mathbf{a}
from
\mathcal{W}_{G}(B,N,s)
and taking the Arakelov ray class
[\mathbf{a}]\in\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
(as in
Definition

6.2
).

Theorem 6.4

(Random Walks in finite-index subgroups of the Arakelov Ray Class Group, ERH)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
s>0
be any positive real numbers and let
k\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a positive real number as well. Let
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

be a finite-index subgroup of the Arakelov ray class group.
Putting
5
5

5

Recall that for any lattice
\Lambda
, we write
\Lambda^{\vee}
for its dual, and
\eta_{1}(\Lambda)
for its smoothing parameter (see page
2.9
).

\tilde{s}=\min(\sqrt{2}\cdot s,1/\eta_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}))
,
there exists a bound

B=\widetilde{O}\Big([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]^{2}\cdot n^{2k}\big[n^{2}(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))^{2}+n^{2}(\log(1/\tilde{s}))^{2}+(\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m})))^{2}\big]\Big)

such that for any integer

N\geq\left\lceil\frac{1}{2k\log n}\cdot(\mathbbm{r}\cdot\log(1/\tilde{s})+2\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\lvert\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}\rvert-\log[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]+2)\right\rceil,

(24)

the random walk distribution
[\mathcal{W}_{G}(B,N,s)]
is
\varepsilon
-close to uniform in
L_{1}(G)
, i.e.,

\left\|[\mathcal{W}_{G}(B,N,s)]-\mathcal{U}(G)\right\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon,

where
\norm(\mathfrak{m})=\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}
,
\mathbbm{r}=\dim(H)=n_{\mathbb{R}}+n_{\mathbb{C}}-1
and
|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|
is the number of different real places dividing
\mathfrak{m}
(see
Section

2.4
).

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of
[
14
]
, adapted to account for the ray and the finite-index subgroup. Details can be found in
Section

A.1
.
∎

The following instantiation of the random walk theorem
gives the appropriate parameters for the main application
of this paper, the sampling algorithm (see
Algorithm

2
and
Theorem

9.5
)

Corollary 6.5

(ERH)
.

Let
n=[K:\mathbb{Q}]\geq 2
,
s=1/n^{2}
, let
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
be a finite-index subgroup and let
\varepsilon>0
be an error parameter.
There exists a bound
B=\widetilde{O}\Big([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]^{2}\cdot n^{2}\cdot\big[n^{2}(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))^{2}+(\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m})))^{2}\big]\Big)

such that for
N=\lceil 7n+2\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\lvert\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}\rvert-\log[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]+2\rceil
the random walk distribution
[\mathcal{W}(B,N,s)]
on
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
is
\varepsilon
-close to uniform in
L_{1}(G)
, i.e.,

\left\|[\mathcal{W}(B,N,s)]-\mathcal{U}(G)\right\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon.

Proof.

This formulation of the random walk theorem is obtained by instantiating
Theorem

6.4

with

s=1/n^{2}
and
k=1
. To obtain the bounds on
B
and
N
, we use the inequality
1/\tilde{s}=\max(n^{2}/\sqrt{2},\eta_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}))\leq 2800\cdot n^{2}
, which we will verify at the end of this proof.

One then gets the bound on
B
by applying
Theorem

6.4
and simply moving the
n^{2}(\log(1/\tilde{s}))^{2}
into the polylogarithmic factors. For the lower bound on
N
, note that
\tfrac{1}{2\log(2)}\leq 1
and
6
6

6

Using the bound
1/\tilde{s}\leq 2800\cdot n^{2}
, we have
\frac{\mathbbm{r}\cdot\log(1/\tilde{s})}{2\log n}\leq\frac{\mathbbm{r}\cdot\log(2800\cdot n^{2})}{2\log n}=\frac{\mathbbm{r}\cdot[\log(2800)+2\log n]}{2\log n}\leq\mathbbm{r}\cdot(\frac{\log(2800)}{2\log n}+1)\leq(\frac{\log(2800)}{2\log 2}+1)n\leq 7n
.

\mathbbm{r}\cdot\log(1/\tilde{s})/(2\log n)\leq 7n
. Hence, a sufficient lower bound on
N
is the one in
Theorem

6.4
with
\frac{1}{2k\log n}
removed and
\mathbbm{r}\cdot\log(1/\tilde{s})
replaced by
7n
.

As promised, we finish the proof by showing
1/\tilde{s}\leq 2800\cdot n^{2}
.
As
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})\subseteq\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
, we have
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}\supseteq\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})^{\vee}
. Therefore, the smoothing parameter of
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}
satisfies

\eta_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee})\leq\eta_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})^{\vee})\leq\frac{\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}}}{\lambda_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))}\leq 1000\cdot n\cdot\log(n)^{3}\leq 2800\cdot n^{2}.

Here, the first inequality follows from the fact that
\eta_{1}(\Lambda)\leq\eta_{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})
if
\Lambda\supseteq\Lambda^{\prime}
. The second inequality holds for general lattices
[
55
, Lemma 3.2]
, the third inequality holds by the fact that
1/\lambda_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))\leq 1000\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\log(n)^{3}
(see
Lemma

2.14
)
and
\mathbbm{r}\leq n
,
and the last inequality by

x\log(x)^{3}\leq 1.4\cdot x^{2}
for all
x>0
.
∎

7.
Average densities and sampling

7.1.
Result

Let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\ideals
be a set of integral ideals, and assume that
\mathbf{a}
is an Arakelov ray divisor whose
class is uniformly distributed. In this section, we show that one can sample elements
\beta
such that
\beta\mathbf{a}^{-1}
corresponds to an ideal of the family
\mathcal{S}
with the probability one would naturally expect, i.e., proportional to the
density
of
\mathcal{S}
.
This is made precise in
Theorem

7.1
via the notion of
local density
.
Recall that the local density of a set of ideals
\mathcal{S}
is defined (
Definition

2.11
) as

\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[x]=\min_{t\in[x/e^{n},x]}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}(t)\rvert}{\rho_{K}\cdot t}=\min_{t\in[x/e^{n},x]}\frac{|\{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq t\}|}{\rho_{K}\cdot t},

where,
\rho_{K}=\lim_{s\rightarrow 1}(s-1)\zeta_{K}(s)
(see
Equation

4
).

Recall that for
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the box of radius
r
in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
is

r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ |x_{\sigma}|\leq r\}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
be a finite-index subgroup of the Arakelov ray class group, and let
[\mathbf{b}]\in\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
arbitrary, and
let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution on
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
such that
[\mathcal{D}]
is uniform on the coset
G+[\mathbf{b}]
.
Let
r\geq 8\cdot n^{2}\cdot\Gamma_{K}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{1}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})^{1/n}
, where
\Gamma_{K}\leq\lambda^{\infty}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\leq|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
is defined in
Equation

6
, let
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
and
let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of integral ideals coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
for which holds
[d^{0}(\mathcal{S})]\subseteq G+[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]-[\mathbf{b}]
, with local density
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]
at
r^{n}
.
Then

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}}{\Pr}\left[(\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ \Big\lvert\penalty\ \alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\right]\right]

(25)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\cdot\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]

(26)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]

(27)

where
\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
is uniformly sampled from the finite set
\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
and
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=|K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}/K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}|\leq\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
.

Remark 7.2
.

The factor
1/3
in
Equations

26
and
27
can be made arbitrarily close to
1
by increasing the radius
r\in\mathbb{R}
and widening the density interval
[x/e^{n},x]
in
Definition

2.11
.

Remark 7.3
.

It is possible, with essentially the same proof, to rephrase this theorem in such a way that it concerns an
intersection
of events instead of a conditional probability.
The probability then also depends on the number

\norm(\mathfrak{m})=\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}
,
where
|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|
is the number of real places dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
.
Under the same conditions as in
Theorem

7.1
, one can prove that

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\\
\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\end{subarray}}{\Pr}

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c}(\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\mbox{, and }\\
\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\end{array}\right]\geq\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\cdot\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}].

7.2.
Proof of
Theorem

7.1

7.2.1.
Fixing the ideal

\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}

and the Arakelov divisor

\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

We concentrate on the ‘inner probability’ of
Equation

25
in
Theorem

7.1

in the case where
\mathcal{S}=\{\mathfrak{c}\}
consists of a
single
integral ideal.
We denote

p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}=\underset{\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}}{\Pr}\left[(\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{c}\penalty\ \Big\lvert\penalty\ \alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\right],

(30)

where we leave the dependency on
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the modulus
\mathfrak{m}
and
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}/K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
implicit.
By the law of conditional probability, we have that
p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}
in
Equation

30
equals

\underset{\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}}{\Pr}\left[\begin{matrix}(\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{c}\\
\mbox{ and }\\
\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\end{matrix}\right]\Bigg/\underset{\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}}{\Pr}\left[\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\right]

(31)

The following lemma addresses the probability values of the numerator and denominator in
Equation

31
separately.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
be a modulus, let
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}/K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
, let
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
be a fixed Arakelov ray divisor, and let
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
be an integral ideal. Then

\underset{\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}}{\Pr}\left[\begin{matrix}(\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{c}\\
\mbox{ and }\\
\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\end{matrix}\right]=\frac{|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|}{|\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|},

(32)

and, there exists some
\tilde{\tau}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\underset{\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}}{\Pr}\left[\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\right]=\frac{|(\Exp(\mathbf{a})\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}|}{|\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|},

(33)

where the sampling
\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
is uniform in both expressions, and where

\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x_{\sigma}/\sigma(\tau)>0\mbox{ for real }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\}
.

Proof.

By examining
Definition

5.3
closely, noting that
{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}_{\mathrm{f}})

={\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})\cdot\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
,
we see that for all
\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})
,

(\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a})=\mathfrak{c}\mbox{ and }\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \alpha\in{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}.

As the number of choices for
\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
equals
|\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
, the number of good choices equals
|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
and since the sampling procedure is uniform, we arrive at the first probability claim.
For the second probability claim, write
\mathfrak{a}={\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
, for conciseness. We note that for
\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})
,

\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
is equivalent to

\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=\mathfrak{a}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=(\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tau^{\prime})\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}},

where
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{a}
is such that
\tau^{\prime}\equiv\tau
modulo
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
(note that
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
are coprime).

So, for
\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
, the statement

\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
is equivalent to

\alpha\in{\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\big((\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tau^{\prime})\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}\big)\cap{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=(\Exp(\mathbf{a})\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}

where we use the fact that
\Exp(\mathbf{a})\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}=\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
and where we put
\tilde{\tau}={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\tau^{\prime}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
. This proves the claim.
∎

By combining
Equations

31
,
32
and
33
and
scratching redundant factors, one concludes that there exists
\tilde{\tau}\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})
such that

\displaystyle p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}=\frac{|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|}{|(\Exp(\mathbf{a})\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}|}.

(34)

7.2.2.
Estimating

|(\Exp(\mathbf{a})\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{m}}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}|

When the radius
r
is sufficiently large compared to the lattice
\Exp(\mathbf{a})\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}
,
one can deduce that for
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
the number of points in
(\Exp(\mathbf{a})\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}

is approximately
\vol(r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})\cdot 2^{-|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}/\det(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
, where the
2^{-|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}
accounts for the fact that
we only take the halves of the box for which
x_{\sigma}/\sigma(\tau)>0
at the real embeddings
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
. More precisely, we instantiate
Lemma

2.8
with

•

\Lambda=\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{m}_{0}))\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}
, for which holds
\covol(\Lambda)=\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}
,

•

t=\tilde{\tau}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
,

•

(t^{\prime})_{\sigma}=\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma(\tau))\cdot 1/2
for real
\nu\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, and
(t^{\prime})_{\sigma}=0
otherwise. Here
\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma(\tau))
is the sign of
\sigma(\tau)
.

•

X=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ |x_{\sigma}|\leq 1\mbox{ and }|x_{\sigma}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mbox{ if $\sigma$ is real and }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\}
, such that
X+t^{\prime}={{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
. Note that
\vol(X)=2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}-|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}
, due to the hybrid complex-real nature of
K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
and taking account for the required positivity (or negativity, depending on
\tau
) at the real places
\nu\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
.

•

c=n\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}\cdot\Gamma_{K}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{1}{2n}}\geq 2\cdot{\operatorname{cov}}(\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{m}_{0})))
(see
Lemma

2.13
(i) and (iv)), so that the Voronoi cell
\mathcal{V}_{0}
of
\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{m}_{0}))
lies in
cX
.

This yields, for

r>8\cdot n^{2}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}\cdot\Gamma_{K}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{1}{2n}}\geq 8nc
,

|(\Exp(\mathbf{a})\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}|\in[e^{-1/4},e^{1/4}]\cdot\frac{r^{n}\cdot 2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}-|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}}{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}}.

(35)

Applying this to the denominator of
Equation

34
, we directly deduce that

\displaystyle p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}\in[e^{-1/4},e^{1/4}]\cdot\frac{\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}}{r^{n}\cdot 2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}}\cdot|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|

(36)

7.2.3.
Estimating the probability of sampling a fixed ideal for a
random
Arakelov divisor

Still focusing on the simplified case where
\mathcal{S}=\{\mathfrak{c}\}
, the goal of this proof is to find a lower bound for
\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}[p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}]
. By linearity of expectation, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}\right]\in[e^{-1/4},e^{1/4}]\cdot\frac{\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}}{r^{n}\cdot 2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}}\cdot\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|\right].

(37)

So it remains to focus on the expected value of
|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
for
\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

7.2.4.
The number
|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|

only depends on the Arakelov ray
class
of

\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

A fact that plays a large role in the full proof, is that the number
|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|

of
\tau
-equivalent generators in a box depends on the Arakelov ray
class

[\mathbf{a}]
rather than the divisor
\mathbf{a}

itself. This has as a consequence that the involved probability distribution changes from
\mathcal{D}
to
[\mathcal{D}]=\mathcal{U}(G)
, uniform on
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
,
which is easier to analyze. This fact, among others, is proven in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

For all ray divisors
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, elements
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
, ideals
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
and real numbers
r>0
we have the following list
of facts.

(i)

|{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|=|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau^{\prime}\rrparenthesis)}^{\times}_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
, i.e., the number of
\tau
-equivalent ray generators of
\mathbf{a}
in a fixed box of radius
r
is equal to the number of
\tau\tau^{\prime}
-equivalent ray generators of
\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau^{\prime}\rrparenthesis
in the same box.

(ii)

|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|=|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d^{0}(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap\frac{r}{\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{1/n}}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
,
since the maps
d^{0}
and
d
only differ by some scaling
\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{1/n}
.

(iii)

Writing
{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}=\sum_{\nu}a_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, we have

|{\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})}^{\times}_{1}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|=|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|\cdot|(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})+(a_{\nu})_{\nu})\penalty\ \cap\penalty\ S_{\log(r)}|,

(38)

where
S_{\log r}=\{(b_{\nu})_{\nu}\in\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}\penalty\ |\penalty\ b_{\nu}\leq n_{\nu}\log(r)\,,\,\sum_{\nu}b_{\nu}=0\}\subseteq H
is a simplex (as in
Lemma

7.6
) and
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})=\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m},1})
. Here,
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise.

Proof.

For part (i), observe that multiplying by
\left(\frac{\sigma(\tau^{\prime})}{|\sigma(\tau^{\prime})|}\right)_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
yields a bijection from
\Exp(\mathbf{a})
to
\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau^{\prime}\rrparenthesis)
, preserving the maximum norm. It remains to show that this bijection sends
{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}
to
{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau^{\prime}\rrparenthesis)}^{\times}_{\tau^{\prime}\tau}
. Using
Definition

5.3
and assuming
{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})=\kappa\mathcal{O}_{K}
(and therefore
{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({[\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau^{\prime}\rrparenthesis]}_{\mathrm{f}})=\tau^{\prime}\kappa\mathcal{O}_{K}
), we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\sigma(\tau^{\prime})}{|\sigma(\tau^{\prime})|}\right)_{\sigma}\cdot{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}=\left(\frac{1}{|\sigma(\tau^{\prime})|}\right)_{\sigma}\cdot(\tau^{\prime})\cdot\underbrace{{\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot(\kappa\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})}_{{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{|\sigma(\tau^{\prime})|}\right)_{\sigma}\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})}_{{\Exp}_{\infty}({(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau^{\prime}\rrparenthesis)}_{\infty})}\cdot(\tau^{\prime}\kappa\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\cap\tau^{\prime}\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})={\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau^{\prime}\rrparenthesis)}^{\times}_{\tau^{\prime}\tau}

For part (ii), recall that multiplying the ideal lattice
\Exp(d(\mathfrak{c}))=\mathfrak{c}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}

by the scalar
\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{-1/n}
results in the ideal lattice
\Exp(d^{0}(\mathfrak{c}))
. Applying this scalar multiplication to the set
\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
yields a bijective correspondence with
\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d^{0}(\mathfrak{c}))\cap\frac{r}{\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{1/n}}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
.

In part (iii) it is enough to show that the logarithm
\Log:K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\rightarrow\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times})
takes
{\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})}^{\times}_{1}
to the shifted lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})+(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\subset H
and takes
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
to the simplex
S_{\log(r)}\subset H
. This logarithmic map is
|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|
-to-one
on
{\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})}^{\times}_{1}
, as it sends roots of unity to the all-zero vector in
\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times})
(which is the unit in that group), yielding the extra factor
|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|
in
Equation

38
. Here,
\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}=\mu_{K}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
, i.e., the roots of unity in
K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
.
∎

As a corollary of
Lemma

7.5
(
i
)
we deduce that
|{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|=|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\kappa\rrparenthesis)}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
for
\kappa\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
,
i.e., the number of elements
|{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
only depends on the Arakelov ray
class
of
\mathbf{a}
(next to
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathfrak{m}
and
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
). Choose a (measurable) fundamental domain
F\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
of the quotient group
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
. Put
F_{G}=\{\mathbf{a}\in F\penalty\ |\penalty\ [\mathbf{a}]\in G\}
, which is a subdomain of
F
for the subgroup
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
and likewise put
F_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\{\mathbf{a}\in F\penalty\ |\penalty\ [\mathbf{a}]\in T^{\mathfrak{m}}\}
, a fundamental domain of
T^{\mathfrak{m}}
in
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
. Note that
F_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\subseteq F_{G}
. By the assumption that
[\mathcal{D}]
is uniform on
G+[\mathbf{b}]
, and
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{c})]\in[d^{0}(\mathcal{S})]\subseteq G+[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]-[\mathbf{b}]
, we deduce, writing
\tilde{r}=r\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{-1/n}
,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|\right]=\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d^{0}(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap\tilde{r}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|\right]

(39)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(F_{G}+[\mathbf{b}])}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+d^{0}(\mathfrak{c}))}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap\tilde{r}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|\right]=\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(F_{G})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis)}^{\times}_{\tau}\cap\tilde{r}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|\right]

(40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(F_{G})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{1}\cap\tilde{r}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|\right]=\frac{1}{|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|}\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(F_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{1}\cap\tilde{r}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|\right]

(41)

where the first equality follows from scaling (
Lemma

7.5
(
ii
)) and the second one by the fact that the random variable is an Arakelov ray class invariant (
Lemma

7.5
(
i
)) and that
[\mathcal{D}]
is uniform on
G+[\mathbf{b}]
.
The third equality holds because the class
[\mathbf{a}+d^{0}(\mathfrak{c})]
for
\mathbf{a}\in\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
uniformly distributed over
F_{G}+[\mathbf{b}]
is distributed as
[\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]
for

\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(F_{G})
. This follows from the assumption
d^{0}(\mathfrak{c})\in G+[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]-[\mathbf{b}]
.
The fourth equality
follows directly from
Lemma

7.5
(
i
), and the last equality follows from

Definition

5.3
. Namely, an Arakelov divisor
\mathbf{a}
can only have generators if the ideal class
of
\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
is trivial, i.e., if
[\mathbf{a}]\in T^{\mathfrak{m}}
. So, instead,
\mathbf{a}
can be chosen
uniformly from a fundamental domain
F_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}
of
T^{\mathfrak{m}}
in
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, with a correction factor of
\tfrac{1}{|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|}

in the expected value.

7.2.5.
Volume of the simplex in
H

For the next step in the proof, we need to know
the volume of the simplex
S_{\log r}\subseteq H
,
hence the following lemma.

Lemma 7.6
.

The volume of the simplex
S_{\alpha}=\{(b_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H\penalty\ |\penalty\ b_{\nu}\leq n_{\nu}\alpha\}\subseteq H=\Log K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is given by

\vol(S_{\alpha})=\frac{\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}+1}\cdot(n\alpha)^{\mathbbm{r}}}{\mathbbm{r}!},

where
\mathbbm{r}=n_{\mathbb{R}}+n_{\mathbb{C}}-1
and where
n_{\nu}=2
whenever
\nu
is complex and
n_{\nu}=1
when
\nu
is real.

Proof.

By applying to
S_{\alpha}\subseteq H
the translation
c_{\nu}=n_{\nu}\alpha-b_{\nu}
, one can see that
S_{\alpha}
is a regular
\mathbbm{r}
-simplex
\{c\in\prod_{\nu}\mathbb{R}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sum_{\nu}c_{\nu}=n\cdot\alpha\mbox{ and }c_{\nu}\geq 0\}
with edge length
\sqrt{2}\cdot n\cdot\alpha
. Therefore, the volume of
S_{\alpha}
equals
\vol(S_{\alpha})=\frac{(n\alpha)^{\mathbbm{r}}\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}+1}}{\mathbbm{r}!}

[
66
]
.
∎

7.2.6.
Taking the logarithmic map into
H=\Log K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}

Applying the logarithmic map on the set
{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{1}\cap r\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{-1/n}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
,
sends
{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{1}
to a shift of the logarithmic ray unit lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})\subseteq H
and

r\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{-1/n}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
to a simplex
S_{n\log r-\log\norm(\mathfrak{c})}
, where
S_{x}=\Log(x{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})\subseteq H=\Log K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
as in
Lemma

7.6
(see also
Lemma

7.5
(
iii
)). Note that
\vol(T^{\mathfrak{m}})=\covol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}))
.

The expected value as in
Equation

41
then equals
the average number of points
of a randomly shifted logarithmic ray unit lattice into this simplex,
which equals
\vol(S_{n\log r-\log\norm(\mathfrak{c})})/\vol(T^{\mathfrak{m}})
(see
Lemma

2.4
).
Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|}\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(F_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}})}{\mathbb{E}}[|{\Exp(\mathbf{a})}^{\times}_{1}\cap r\norm(\mathfrak{c})^{-1/n}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|]=\frac{|\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}|\cdot\vol(S_{n\log r-\log\norm(\mathfrak{c})})}{|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot|T^{\mathfrak{m}}|}

(42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot|\mu_{K}|\cdot\vol(S_{n\log r-\log\norm(\mathfrak{c})})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot h_{K}\cdot\lvert T\rvert}=\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot|\mu_{K}|\cdot C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot h_{K}\cdot R_{K}}

(43)

where, for the second equation, we use that
|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot|T^{\mathfrak{m}}|=|G|=|\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}|/[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]
and
Equation

21
of
Lemma

5.1
. For the third equation we use the
fact that
\frac{\vol(S_{n\log r-\log\norm(\mathfrak{c})})}{\lvert T\rvert}=\frac{C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))}{R_{K}}
,
where we define
C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c})):=(n\log r-\log\norm(\mathfrak{c}))^{\mathbbm{r}}/\mathbbm{r}!
(see
Lemma

7.6
and
Equation

9
).

7.2.7.
Applying the Abel summation formula to get the probability for the ideal set
\mathcal{S}

By combining
Equations

37
,
41
and
43
, using the class number formula (see
Equation

4
) and the fact that
\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}=\frac{|\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0}|}{|(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}|}\geq 1
, one obtains,

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq e^{-1/4}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}}{r^{n}\cdot 2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}}\cdot\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot|\mu_{K}|\cdot C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot h_{K}\cdot R_{K}}

(44)

\displaystyle=e^{-1/4}\cdot\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))}{r^{n}\cdot\rho_{K}}\cdot\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}

(45)

where
|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|
is the number of real places dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
and where
C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))=(n\log r-\log\norm(\mathfrak{c}))^{\mathbbm{r}}/\mathbbm{r}!
.
By taking the sum over all
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}
(note that
[d^{0}(\mathcal{S})]\subseteq G
), using linearity of the expected value operator, one can achieve the following lower bound.

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}}p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}}\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[p_{\mathbf{a},\mathfrak{c}}\right]

(46)

\displaystyle\in[e^{-1/4},e^{1/4}]\cdot\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))}{\rho_{K}\cdot r^{n}}

(47)

By an application of the Abel summation formula, one can relate
the sum
\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}}C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))
to an integral involving the counting
function
\lvert\mathcal{S}(t)\rvert=|\{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{a})\leq t\}|
of the ideal set
\mathcal{S}

and the derivative of the volume function
C(r,N)=(n\log r-\log N)^{\mathbbm{r}}/\mathbbm{r}!
with respect to the variable
N
.
More precisely, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{C(r,\norm(\mathfrak{c}))}{\rho_{K}\cdot r^{n}}=-

\displaystyle\int_{t=1}^{r^{n}}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}(t)\rvert}{\rho_{K}\cdot r^{n}}\cdot\left[\tfrac{d}{dN}C(r,N)\Big|_{N=t}\right]dt

(48)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\mathbbm{r}-1)!}

\displaystyle\int_{t=1}^{r^{n}}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}(t)\rvert}{\rho_{K}\cdot t}\cdot\left[\tfrac{d}{dN}\Gamma\big(\mathbbm{r},n\log r-\log N\big)\Big|_{N=t}\right]dt,

(49)

Where the first equality is the Abel summation formula
[
2
, Theorem 4.2]
and the second equality follows from computing
the derivative of the upper incomplete Gamma function
\Gamma(\mathbbm{r},x)=\int_{x}^{\infty}u^{\mathbbm{r}-1}e^{-u}du
.

-\tfrac{d}{dN}C(r,N)\Big|_{t}=\frac{(n\log r-\log t)^{\mathbbm{r}-1}}{t\cdot(\mathbbm{r}-1)!}=\frac{r^{n}}{t\cdot(\mathbbm{r}-1)!}\cdot\left[\tfrac{d}{dN}\Gamma\big(\mathbbm{r},n\log r-\log N\big)\right]\!\!\Big|_{t}.

Using
Definition

2.11
about ideal density and the fact that the integrand is positive,
Equation

49
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\mathbbm{r}-1)!}\int_{t=(r/e)^{n}}^{r^{n}}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}(t)\rvert}{\rho_{K}\cdot t}\cdot\left[\tfrac{d}{dN}\Gamma\big(\mathbbm{r},n\log r-\log N\big)\Big|_{N=t}\right]dt

(50)

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}{(\mathbbm{r}-1)!}\int_{t=(r/e)^{n}}^{r^{n}}\left[\tfrac{d}{dN}\Gamma\big(\mathbbm{r},n\log r-\log N\big)\Big|_{N=t}\right]dt\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}],

(51)

where the last inequality (
Equation

51
) follows from the definition of the upper
incomplete Gamma function,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\mathbbm{r}-1)!}\int_{t=(r/e)^{n}}^{r^{n}}\!\!\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Gamma(\mathbbm{r},n\log r-\log N)\big\lvert_{N=t}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\mathbbm{r}-1)!}\cdot(\Gamma(\mathbbm{r},0)-\Gamma(\mathbbm{r},n))

\displaystyle=1-e^{-n}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathbbm{r}-1}\frac{n^{k}}{k!}\geq 1/2,

where we used the fact that
e^{-n}\sum_{k=0}^{\mathbbm{r}-1}\frac{n^{k}}{k!}
equals the probability
that a Poisson distribution with parameter
n
yields at most
\mathbbm{r}-1\leq n-1
occurrences, which is bounded by a half.

By combining
Equations

47
,
49
and
51
and using
e^{-1/4}/2>1/3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}}{\Pr}\left[(\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ \big\lvert\penalty\ \alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]/3.

which finishes the proof.∎

8.
Uniform sampling in a box intersected with an ideal lattice

8.1.
Introduction

In this section, we explain how one can efficiently sample in a (shifted) ideal lattice
x(\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)
intersected with a (partially positive) box, provided that the dimensions of the box are sufficiently large.
More precisely, let
r>0
and let
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
denote the box

r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}:=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\,|\,|x_{\sigma}|\leq r,\,\forall\sigma\}.

Now, the box of interest is
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
, whose coordinates at
\sigma
have the same sign as
(\sigma(\tau))_{\sigma}
for the real embeddings
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
. Our aim of this section is to perfectly uniform sample from the intersection
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}\cap x(\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)
.

The algorithm we use to sample uniformly in this box follows the framework described by Plançon and Prest in
[
64
]
, and is very similar to their instantiation of that framework
[
64
, Sec. 4.2]
. The main two differences are that
[
64
]
analyzes the running time of their algorithm only heuristically, whereas here we provide a provable running time; also,
[
64
]
assumes perfect uniform samples from real intervals, whereas here we only rely on perfectly uniform bits.

Technical idea of the algorithm

The core idea of the algorithm applies to general lattices and relies on two observations. The first observation: for lattices
\Lambda
satisfying
\Lambda\subseteq\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and sufficiently large, bounded convex sets
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the task of uniformly sampling from
S\cap\Lambda
reduces to uniformly sampling in
(1+c)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
for some constant
c>0
, where we understand
(1+c)\cdot S:=\{(1+c)\cdot s\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in S\}
. Indeed, we can use a ‘good’ fundamental domain
F
of
\Lambda
to round a uniform sample of
(1+c)\cdot S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
to a point in
\Lambda
. Since
(F+\ell)\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
contains the same number of elements for all
\ell\in\Lambda\cap S
(since
\Lambda\subseteq\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
), this yields a perfect uniform distribution over
S\cap\Lambda
, if
c>0
is chosen adequately. This observation is formalized in
Lemma

8.7
.

The second observation is that each lattice
\Lambda
given by a basis
{B}
can be
approximated
by a lattice
\tilde{\Lambda}
given by a basis
\tilde{{B}}
that
satisfies
\tilde{\Lambda}\subseteq\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Hence, by the first observation,
one can perfectly uniformly sample from
\tilde{\Lambda}\cap S
for suitable
S
. If the approximation
\tilde{{B}}\approx{B}

is good enough, one can transform this perfectly uniform sample from
\tilde{\Lambda}\cap S
to a perfectly uniform sample from
\Lambda\cap S
. This observation is formalized in
Proposition

8.8
.

Note that in the first observation, a ‘good’ fundamental domain of the lattice is required.
For such a fundamental domain,
we need to know a sufficiently good basis of the ideal we want to sample from.
We obtain such a basis by using one of the provable variants of the BKZ algorithm
[
68
,
39
,
33
,
1
]
.
Lemma

8.5
captures the end result of using a provable variant of the BKZ algorithm; for an in-depth proof of this lemma, we refer to
Sections

26
,
27
and
28
.

Remark 8.1
.

We note that this choice of basis reduction algorithm (BKZ) does not exploit the ideal structure of the lattice. There do exist algorithms that exploit the ideal structure
[
29
,
63
]
and might outperform BKZ in some context. However, these algorithms are heuristic, require a quantum computer and/or some exponential pre-processing on the number field and in the case of
[
29
]
are restricted to cyclotomic number fields. Hence, we chose to use the BKZ algorithm here in order to obtain a non-heuristic algorithm. If one allows heuristics and quantum algorithms, it may be possible to diminish the block-size dependent quantity
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
that appears in the running time of the basis reduction algorithm.

Ray

Actually, in this section, a slightly more general algorithm is described. In this more general algorithm, we sample elements from
an intersection of a (shifted) ideal lattice
x(\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)

and a box
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
, that also fall in the
\tau
-
ray
mod
\mathfrak{m}
, for some
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
.

That is, we will sample from
x((\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})
intersected with
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
; those are the elements of the shape
x(\beta+\gamma)
for which
\beta+\gamma\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
and
|x_{\sigma}\sigma(\beta+\gamma)|\leq r
for all embeddings
\sigma
. So, for the algorithm including the modulus
\mathfrak{m}
, the output additionally satisfies certain modular conditions depending on the modulus
\mathfrak{m}
.

One retrieves the simpler algorithm (without a modulus) that samples in
x(\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
by just putting
\mathfrak{m}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
(so that
\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=K^{\mathfrak{m}}=K^{*}
for all
\tau\in K^{*}
).

8.2.
On efficiently deciding whether an algebraic number is greater or equal to some rational number

To be able to perfectly
sample from an ideal lattice intersected with a box,
one needs to be able to efficiently decide, for an algebraic number
\alpha\in K

in symbolic representation, whether
|\sigma(\alpha)|>r
or
|\sigma(\alpha)|\leq r

(or, for real embeddings, whether
\sigma(\alpha)>r
or
\leq r
)
for some rational
r\in\mathbb{Q}
and embedding
\sigma
. Namely, the combined
information over all places
signifies whether the algebraic number
\alpha
is inside some box
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
or not.

The challenge in this question is that the algebraic number is given
in some symbolic representation, like
\alpha=408\sqrt{2}-577(\approx-0.0008665)
.
In order to decide, for example, whether
\alpha>0
or
\leq 0
,
we need to know
how well
to approximate this number (in this particular case
at least up to 3 decimals).

The result of this section, in a nutshell, is that for any algebraic number
\alpha\in K
we can efficiently decide
whether
|\sigma(\alpha)|
(or
\sigma(\alpha)
in the case of a real place)
is larger than some rational
r\in\mathbb{Q}
or not. This is proven in the following
sequence of lemmas. The next lemma is coined Liouville’s lemma, since the technique comes from the tendency
of algebraic numbers to avoid rationals, which was originally used to show the explicit
existence of transcendental numbers (Liouville numbers).

Lemma 8.2

(Louiville’s lemma)
.

Let
f(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
be a degree
n
polynomial. Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
be a real root of
f
. Then, if
\omega\notin\mathbb{Z}
, we have, for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}
,

|\omega-z|>\Big[(n+1)^{3}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot(2+\|f\|_{\infty})^{n}\Big]^{-1}=:\mathfrak{A}(f).

Proof.

Let
h\mid f
be an irreducible factor of
f
and let
\omega
be a real root of
h(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
. If
h
is linear,

\omega=\frac{c}{d}\in\mathbb{Q}
, and by the rational root theorem,
d
is an integer factor of the leading coefficient of
h
(and thus of that of
f
). Hence, if
\omega\notin\mathbb{Z}
, we have
|\omega-z|\geq 1/d\geq 1/\|f\|_{\infty}>\mathfrak{A}(f)
for every
z\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Assume now that
h
is non-linear.
By the mean value theorem, we have, for any
t\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]
,

|h(t)|=|h(t)-h(\omega)|\leq\max_{t_{0}\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]}|h^{\prime}(t_{0})|\cdot|t-\omega|

(52)

Additionally, for any
integer

z\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
|h(z)|\geq 1
, since
h(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
is irreducible and of degree
>1
(and hence cannot have integer roots). This immediately yields, for any
integer

z\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle|z-\omega|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|h(z)|}{\max_{x_{0}\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]}|h^{\prime}(x_{0})|}\geq\Big(\max_{x_{0}\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]}|h^{\prime}(x_{0})|\Big)^{-1}

(53)

\displaystyle\geq\Big(\max_{h\mid f}\max_{\omega\mbox{ \scriptsize{root of} }h}\max_{x_{0}\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]}|h^{\prime}(x_{0})|\Big)^{-1}

(54)

where, in the last line, the first maximum is over all divisors of
f
(also the possible linear ones) and the second maximum is over all real roots.
We now aim to find a easy-to-compute lower bound on
Equation

54
, which we call
\mathfrak{A}(f)
,
in terms of the
coefficients of
f
alone, using the Landau-Mignotte bound
[
76
, Theorem 6.32]
and Cauchy’s bound
[
27
, Section 3.6]
.

By the triangle inequality, writing
h(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{\deg(h)}h_{j}\cdot x^{j}
,
\|h\|_{\infty}=\max_{j}|h_{j}|
and
\|f\|_{\infty}=\max_{j}|f_{j}|
, we can deduce

\displaystyle\max_{x_{0}\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]}|h^{\prime}(x_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(h)}j\cdot|h_{j}|\cdot\max_{x_{0}\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]}|x_{0}|^{j-1}

\displaystyle\leq\deg(h)^{2}\cdot\|h\|_{\infty}\cdot\max_{x_{0}\in[\omega-1,\omega+1]}|x_{0}|^{\deg(h)-1}

\displaystyle\leq\deg(h)^{2}\cdot\|h\|_{\infty}\cdot(|\omega|+1)^{\deg(h)-1}

\displaystyle\leq n^{2}\cdot(n+1)\cdot 2^{n}\cdot\|f\|_{\infty}\cdot(|\omega|+1)^{n-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{(Landau-Mignotte bound)}

\displaystyle\leq n^{2}\cdot(n+1)\cdot 2^{n}\cdot\|f\|_{\infty}\cdot(2+\|f\|_{\infty})^{n-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{(Cauchy's bound)}

\displaystyle<(n+1)^{3}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot(2+\|f\|_{\infty})^{n}=\mathfrak{A}(f)^{-1}.

where we recall that
n=\deg(f)\geq\deg(h)
.
Thus, for any
z\in\mathbb{Z}
and any real root
\omega
of
f
satisfying
\omega\notin\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle|\omega-x|\geq\mathfrak{A}(f)=\left[(n+1)^{3}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot(2+\|f\|_{\infty})^{n}\right]^{-1}.

(55)

∎

Corollary 8.3
.

There exists an algorithm that, given
as input a degree
n
polynomial
f(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
and some rational
r\in\mathbb{Q}
,
decides whether the roots
\omega_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
of
f(x)
satisfy
|\omega_{i}|>r

or
|\omega_{i}|\leq r
for complex roots
\omega_{i}
, respectively,
\omega_{i}>r
or
\omega_{i}\leq r
for real roots
\omega_{i}
. The running time of the algorithm
is polynomial in the size of
f(x)
and
r
.

Proof.

Denote
r=\frac{a}{b}\in\mathbb{Q}
. We replace the roots
\omega_{i}
by
b\cdot\omega_{i}
, the polynomial
f(x)
by
b^{n}\cdot f(b^{-1}\cdot x)
and the rational
r
by
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence, we may without loss of generality assume that
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, with the expense of an increase in the size of the polynomial
f
by
n\cdot\size(r)
.

We start with the case of a real root
\omega
of
f
, and we then proceed
with the case of a complex root.

Real root case.

According to
Lemma

8.2
, we have for any real but non-integral root
\omega
of
f(x)
and any
z\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|\omega-z|>\mathfrak{A}(f)
. This lower bound gives us means to decide whether a real root of
f
is larger (or equal) or smaller than some integer. Namely,
approximate the real roots of
f
within precision
<\mathfrak{A}(f)/2=\left[(n+1)^{3}\cdot(2+\|f\|_{\infty})^{n}\right]^{-1}/2
, which can be done in time
\poly(n,\log\|f\|_{\infty})=\poly(n,\size(f),\size(r))
. Denote the approximations of these real roots by
\tilde{\omega}_{i}\approx\omega_{i}
. There are two cases:

•

|\tilde{\omega}_{i}-z|<\mathfrak{A}(f)/2
for some integer
z\in\mathbb{Z}
. In that case,
\omega_{i}=z
. Indeed, if
\omega_{i}
were not an integer, we must have

\mathfrak{A}(f)\leq|\omega_{i}-z|\leq|\omega_{i}-\tilde{\omega_{i}}|+|\tilde{\omega_{i}}-z|<\mathfrak{A}(f),

which is a contradiction. Hence,
\omega_{i}
is an integer and equal to
z\in\mathbb{Z}
. In such case, deciding whether
|\omega_{i}|>r
or
\leq r
is easily and efficiently done.

•

|\tilde{\omega_{i}}-z|\geq\mathfrak{A}(f)/2
for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, by the triangle inequality, we have
\omega_{i}\notin\mathbb{Z}
, since, for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}
,

|\omega_{i}-z|\geq\underbrace{|\tilde{\omega_{i}}-z|}_{\geq\mathfrak{A}(f)/2}-\underbrace{|\omega_{i}-\tilde{\omega}_{i}|}_{<\mathfrak{A}(f)/2}>0.

Hence, by
Lemma

8.2
,
|\omega_{i}-z|\geq\mathfrak{A}(f)
for any integer
z\in\mathbb{Z}
. We claim that
\tilde{\omega_{i}}>r
if and only if
\omega_{i}>r
. That is, by using the approximation
\tilde{\omega_{i}}
, we can decide whether
\omega_{i}>r
or
\leq r
.

Indeed, suppose (as to achieve a contradiction) that
\omega_{i}\leq r
but
\tilde{\omega_{i}}>r
. Then, since
|\omega_{i}-r|\geq\mathfrak{A}(f)
, and thus
r-\omega_{i}\geq\mathfrak{A}(f)
.

\underbrace{r-\omega_{i}}_{\geq\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}(f)}=\underbrace{(r-\tilde{\omega}_{i})}_{\leq 0}+\underbrace{(\omega_{i}-\tilde{\omega}_{i})}_{\in[-\mathfrak{A}(f)/2,\mathfrak{A}(f)/2]}\leq\mathfrak{A}(f)/2

which is a contradiction. The other case (
\omega_{i}>r
but
\tilde{\omega_{i}}\leq r
) can be excluded similarly.

Complex root case.

For the complex root case, we take the companion matrix
M_{f}
of
f=\sum_{j=0}^{n}f_{j}x^{j}
. This
n\times n
matrix
M_{f}
is defined by
(M_{f})_{j+1,j}=1
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
(M_{f})_{j,n}=-f_{j-1}/f_{n}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
(this is the companion matrix of the monic polynomial
f_{n}^{-1}\cdot f(x)
). It is known that
M_{f}
has the roots
\omega_{i}
of
f
as eigen values. Therefore, the matrix resulting from the Kronecker product
M_{f}\otimes M_{f}
has all products
\omega_{i}\omega_{j}
(
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
) as eigen values, in particular the square absolute values
|\omega_{i}|^{2}
for all original roots of
f
.

Computing the characteristic polynomial of
M_{f}\otimes M_{f}
, and clearing denominators of that polynomials yields the
n^{2}
-degree integer polynomial
g(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
. Identifying the roots of
g
that are the square absolute values of those of
f
(by approximating the roots of both), one can apply the same technique as in the real root case to decide whether
|\omega_{i}|^{2}>r^{2}
or not.

It remains to be shown that the polynomial
g
is polynomially bounded in size by the size of
f
, so that all operations can be done in polynomial time in
\size(f)
and
\size(r)
. The
j
-th coefficient
p_{j}
of the characteristic polynomial
p(x)
of
M_{f}\otimes M_{f}
can be computed (up to sign) by taking the sum of all
\binom{n^{2}}{j}
principal minors of
M_{f}\otimes M_{f}
of size
j

[
42
, §1.2, Equation (1.2.13)]
. By Hadamard’s bound, each of these principal minors has size at most
\|f\|_{\infty}^{2j}
and can only have a denominator that is a divisor of
f_{n}^{2j}
. Hence,
f_{n}^{2n}\cdot p(x)
must be a multiple of
g(x)
. This means that
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq\|p\|_{\infty}\leq(n^{2})!\|f\|_{\infty}^{2n}
. Hence,
\size(g)\leq\poly(n,\size(f))
, which was to be demonstrated.
∎

Corollary 8.4
.

There exist an algorithm
that on input a degree
n
number field
K=\mathbb{Q}(\theta)
, an ideal
\mathfrak{b}
, an element
x=(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
represented by rational numbers, an element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{b}
represented by the
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
K
(the power-basis formed by
\theta
), a rational
g\in\mathbb{Q}
and an integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, decides whether
x_{\sigma}|\sigma(\alpha)|>g^{1/k}
or
x_{\sigma}|\sigma(\alpha)|\leq g^{1/k}
, for every complex embedding
\sigma
; and decides whether
x_{\sigma}\sigma(\alpha)>g^{1/k}
or
x_{\sigma}\sigma(\alpha)\leq g^{1/k}
, for every real embedding
\sigma
.
Moreover, this algorithm runs in time
\poly(\size(\alpha),\size(x),\size(\mathfrak{b}),\size(g),n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,k)
.

Proof.

Throughout this paper we assume that the polynomial
f
defining the number field
K
satisfies
\size(f)\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
(see
Section

2.6
). As
x_{\sigma}|\sigma(\alpha)|>g^{1/k}
is equivalent to
|\sigma(\alpha^{k})|>g/x_{\sigma}^{k}
(with a possible sign change), we can reduce this result to that of
Corollary

8.3
.

The sole challenge is to find and bound the polynomial of which
\alpha^{k}
is a root. For this we start by bounding the polynomial for which
\alpha\in\mathfrak{b}
is a root. Write down the multiplication matrix of
\alpha
in the
\theta
-basis. That is, the matrix defined by the coefficients
q_{ij}
occurring in the identities
\alpha\theta^{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}q_{ij}\theta^{i}
for
j\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. Then the characteristic polynomial of this matrix is one of which
\alpha\in\mathfrak{b}
must be a root.

The size of the multiplication matrix of
\alpha
is bounded by
\poly(\size(\alpha),\size(f))=\poly(\size(\alpha),\log|\Delta_{K}|)
. Hence, the size of the characteristic polynomial (whose
j
-th coefficient can be shown to be the sum of all principal minors of size
j
of the multiplication matrix, which can be bounded by Hadamard’s bound) must be polynomially bounded in
\size(\alpha)
,
\log|\Delta_{K}|
and
n
as well. By a very similar reasoning, the size of the characteristic polynomial of
\alpha^{k}
must be
\poly(n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(\alpha),k)
. By applying
Corollary

8.3
, we obtain the final result.
∎

8.3.
Lattice reduction

Being able to successfully sample in an ideal lattice intersected with a box largely depends
on the maximum vector length (i.e., the quality) of the basis of the ideal lattice compared to the dimensions of the box. If these dimensions of the box are somewhat larger than the basis vectors, this sampling can be done efficiently. For the applications and algorithms of the present work, we generally require the dimensions of this box to be as small as possible, since this benefits their complexity. As a consequence, we would like to obtain a basis of the ideal lattice with as small as possible maximum vector length (i.e., best basis quality), but without paying too much time.

The standard algorithm for finding lattice bases of good quality is the Block Korkine Zolotarev (BKZ) algorithm, which allows for a controllable trade-off between the output basis quality and the heuristic run time. Unfortunately, no published versions of the BKZ-algorithm exist that have a
provable
run-time, which is what we require in the present work (as our end goal is an algorithm with a provable run-time). Hence,
Sections

26
,
27
and
28
of this paper is devoted to showing that there exists a variant of the BKZ-algorithm with the same trade-off between output basis quality and run time as the textbook version, but in which the run time is actually proven and non-heuristic.

The proof of this result requires both careful numerical analysis (for the ideal lattice case) and precise monitoring of the sizes (‘potential’) of the bases occurring intermediately in the algorithm. To stay relevant with the current topic, this proof is therefore postponed to
Section

21.5
. The overall result can be summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
K
be a number field of degree
n
. Let
x\mathfrak{a}
be an ideal lattice where
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
is represented by rational numbers, and where

\mathfrak{a}\in\ideals
is represented by a rational Hermite Normal Form matrix
M_{\mathfrak{a}}
with respect to a given LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Then there exists an algorithm that computes
a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(x\alpha_{1},\ldots,x\alpha_{n})
of
x\mathfrak{a}
with
\alpha_{i}\in K
such that

\|x\cdot\alpha_{i}\|\leq 2n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(x\cdot\mathfrak{a}),

using time at most
T=\poly(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}},\size(\mathfrak{a}),\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(x))
.

Proof.

The proof can be found in
Section

28.6
.
∎

8.4.
Perfectly uniform sampling in a bounded convex set intersected with a lattice

Notation 8.6
.

We denote
B_{r}=\{{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|x\|<r\}
. For any bounded convex set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
c\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we denote
cS:=\{c\cdot s\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in S\}
. For a basis
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B})=\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}:=\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{b}_{i}z_{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ z_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ for all $i$}\}
(which is a lattice).

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
with
\mathbf{b}_{i}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be a basis, let
D=\sum_{i}\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|
, let
U,c,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
c>\varepsilon>0
. Suppose
S
is a convex set satisfying
B_{D/\varepsilon}\subseteq S\subseteq B_{U}
for which we can perfectly uniformly sample in
(c+\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
within expected time
T
. Additionally, suppose we can efficiently decide membership in
cS
.

Then we can perfectly uniformly sample in
cS\cap\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
within time
\poly(T,\size(\mathbf{B}),\allowbreak\log U,\allowbreak\log N)
with success probability lower bounded by

\frac{|(c-\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}{|(c+\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}

(56)

Proof.

(
Algorithm description
) Sample a perfectly uniform
u\in(c+\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Compute
v=\mathbf{B}^{-1}u
(which can be done in time
\poly(T,\size(\mathbf{B}),\log U)
) and put
w_{i}=\lfloor v_{i}\rceil
, rounding to the nearest integer. Output
\mathbf{B}w
if
\mathbf{B}w\in cS
, otherwise output ‘failure’.

(
Analysis
)
We will prove that this procedure yields a perfectly uniform sample in
\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap cS
and that the
success probability of a single iteration is lower bounded by the quantity in
Equation

56
.

For a fixed
\mathbf{b}\in(\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n})\cap cS
, the probability that
\mathbf{b}
is outputted is proportional to the number
|(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{B}\cdot[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})^{n})\cap(c+\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|
. We have
\mathbf{b}\in cS
; and each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{B}\cdot[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})^{n}
satisfies
\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq D/2\leq D
, hence
\mathbf{x}\in B_{D}\subseteq\varepsilon\cdot S
(since
B_{D/\varepsilon}\subseteq S
). So, we obtain that
\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{B}\cdot[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})^{n}\subseteq(c+\varepsilon)S
by the convexity of
S
. Therefore

\displaystyle|(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{B}\cdot[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})^{n})\cap(c+\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|

\displaystyle=|(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{B}\cdot[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})^{n})\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|

\displaystyle=N^{n}\det(\mathbf{B}),

for all
\mathbf{b}\in(\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n})\cap S
. Note that this relies critically on
\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\subseteq\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. So the sampling probability is the same for all
\mathbf{b}\in(\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n})\cap cS
, which is the property of a perfect uniform distribution. It remains to show that the probability of success (a sample in
\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap cS
) is lower bounded.

If the initial uniform sample
u
satisfies
u\in(c-\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, then
\mathbf{B}w=\mathbf{B}\lfloor\mathbf{B}^{-1}u\rceil=u+y
for some
y\in\mathbf{B}[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})^{n}\subseteq\varepsilon S
(where we mean with
\lfloor\cdot\rceil
that every coordinate is rounded to the nearest integer). For such
u
holds that
u+y
must lie in
cS
. Therefore, the success probability is lower bounded by

\frac{|(c-\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}{|(c+\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}
.
∎

In our context, the basis
\mathbf{B}_{\mathfrak{b}}
of an ideal lattice
\mathfrak{b}
(as embedded in the Minkowski space) cannot be represented by rational numbers, but is rather symbolically represented. In the following proposition we show that this is not a real complication; namely, it is enough to be able to compute a sufficiently good rational approximation
\mathbf{C}\approx\mathbf{B}_{\mathfrak{b}}
in order to be able to perfectly uniformly sample in this lattice
\mathfrak{b}
intersected with a certain convex set
S
.

As a consequence, the algorithm of the following proposition takes as an input a sufficiently good approximation
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{B}
and uses only this rational matrix
\mathbf{C}
to compute with. To be able to check whether an element of the lattice
\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
lies in the convex set
S
or not, an auxiliary oracle is needed. In our use-case, this oracle can be implemented by using the results of
Section

8.2
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
with
\mathbf{b}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a basis of lattice
\Lambda
and
let
D=2\sum_{i}\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|
.

(1)

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
\|t\|<U
for some
U\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(2)

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
satisfying
\frac{n}{N}\leq D
and let
\mathbf{C}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be an approximation of
\mathbf{B}
satisfying

\|\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{B}\|\leq\|\mathbf{B}^{-1}\|^{-1}\cdot D/U
and
\sum_{i}\|\mathbf{c}_{i}\|\leq D
.

(3)

Let
\tilde{t}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
an approximation of
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
\|t-\tilde{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2N}
and
\|\tilde{t}\|<2U
.

(4)

Let
1/5>\varepsilon>0
and let
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a convex set satisfying
B_{D/\varepsilon}\subseteq S\subseteq B_{U}
.

(5)

Suppose we can perfectly uniformly sample in
(1+4\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
within expected time
T
.

(6)

Additionally, suppose we have an oracle
O_{\mathbf{B}}
that can decide, on input
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, whether
\mathbf{B}v\in S+t
or not.

Then we can sample
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{B}v
is perfectly uniformly distributed in
(S+t)\cap\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, within expected time
\poly(T,\allowbreak\size(\mathbf{C}),\allowbreak\log U,\allowbreak\log N)
, using a single call to
O_{\mathbf{B}}
, and with success probability lower bounded by

\frac{|(1-5\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}{|(1+5\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}

Proof.

(
Algorithm description)

(1)
We start with processing the shift
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We use the approximation
\tilde{t}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
that satisfies
\|t-\tilde{t}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2N}\leq D/(2n)
and hence
\|t-\tilde{t}\|_{2}\leq D/2
. We compute

w_{0}=\lfloor\mathbf{C}^{-1}\tilde{t}\penalty\ \rceil
(where
\lfloor\cdot\rceil
means that each component is rounded to the nearest integer).

(2)
Imitate the approach in
Lemma

8.7
, (taking
c=1+3\varepsilon
) by sampling
u\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap(1+4\varepsilon)S
and computing
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{C}\lfloor\mathbf{C}^{-1}u\rceil
until
\mathbf{c}\in(1+3\varepsilon)S
. By the exact same reasoning as in
Lemma

8.7
, one then obtains a perfectly uniform sample
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap(1+3\varepsilon)S
.

(3)
We compute
v=\mathbf{C}^{-1}\mathbf{c}=\lfloor\mathbf{C}^{-1}u\rceil
and we output
v+w_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
if
\mathbf{B}(v+w_{0})\in S+t
(using the oracle
O_{\mathbf{B}}
). Otherwise output ‘failure’.

(
Analysis
)
In this analysis we will denote
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{B}v,\mathbf{b}_{0}=\mathbf{B}w_{0},\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{C}v
and
\mathbf{c}_{0}=\mathbf{C}w_{0}
.
We will first show that the output
v+w_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
has the property that
\mathbf{B}(v+w_{0})
is perfectly uniformly distributed in
\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap(S+t)
. We will finish the proof with a lower bound on the success probability and a run time analysis.

By
Lemma

8.7
, we know that
v+w_{0}
has the property that
\mathbf{C}(v+w_{0})=\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{c}_{0}
is perfectly uniform in
\mathbf{C}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap[(1+3\varepsilon)S+\mathbf{c}_{0}]
. Hence
\mathbf{B}(v+w_{0})=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{c}_{0})
is perfectly uniform in
\mathbf{B}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap[\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(1+3\varepsilon)S+\mathbf{b}_{0}]
. So, since we reject those
v+w_{0}
for which
\mathbf{B}(v+w_{0})\notin S+t
, it is sufficient to show that
S+t\subset\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(1+3\varepsilon)S+\mathbf{b}_{0}
. This is equivalent to
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}(S+t)\subset(1+3\varepsilon)S+\mathbf{c}_{0}
which is in turn equivalent to
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}(S+t)-\mathbf{c}_{0}\subset(1+3\varepsilon)S
. To show this, take an arbitrary
s\in S
, and put

\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}(s+t)-\mathbf{c}_{0}=s+t-\mathbf{c}_{0}+(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}-I)s+(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}-I)t.

(57)

We will now show that
\|t-\mathbf{c}_{0}\|\leq D
,
\|(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}-I)s\|\leq D
and
\|(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}-I)t\|\leq D
, so that the right-hand side of
Equation

57
lies in
S\boxplus B_{3D}\subseteq S\boxplus(3\varepsilon)S=(1+3\varepsilon)S
(where
\boxplus
denotes the Minkowski sum).
We have
\|t-\mathbf{c}_{0}\|\leq\|t-\tilde{t}\|+\|\tilde{t}-\mathbf{c}_{0}\|\leq D/2+D/2
since
\|t-\tilde{t}\|\leq D/2
by construction and
\tilde{t}-\mathbf{c}_{0}=\tilde{t}-\mathbf{C}\lfloor\mathbf{C}^{-1}\tilde{t}\rceil=\tilde{t}-\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{C}^{-1}\tilde{t}+u)=\mathbf{C}u
with
u\in[-1/2,1/2)^{n}
. Hence
\|\tilde{t}-\mathbf{c}_{0}\|=\|\mathbf{C}u\|\leq D/2
, by assumption. Using the properties of matrix norms, we obtain
\|(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{-1}-I)\|=\|(\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{B})\mathbf{B}^{-1}\|\leq\|\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{B}\|\|\mathbf{B}^{-1}\|\leq D/U
. Combining this with the assumptions
\|s\|\leq U
and
\|t\|\leq U
, we obtain that all the summands’ norms are bounded by
D
.

(
Success probability
)
For the success probability, note that, if
u\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
from step (2) of the algorithm description were to be in
(1-5\varepsilon)S
, a similar reasoning as above shows that, for
v=\lfloor\mathbf{C}^{-1}u\rceil
, we surely have
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{C}v\in(1-4\varepsilon)S
.

We have, by the Neumann series of
(I-(I-\mathbf{C}^{-1}\mathbf{B}))^{-1}
, noting that
\|I-\mathbf{C}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\|\leq D/U\leq\varepsilon<1/5
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}-I\|

\displaystyle=\|(I-(I-\mathbf{C}^{-1}\mathbf{B}))^{-1}-I\|=\big\|\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(I-\mathbf{C}^{-1}\mathbf{B})^{j}\big|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|I-\mathbf{C}^{-1}\mathbf{B}\|^{j}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{5}{4}D/U.

Hence, by similar computations as above, we can bound the norms of the following summands:

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}(v+w_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{c}_{0})-t

\displaystyle=\mathbf{c}+\underbrace{(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}-I)\mathbf{c}}_{\leq\tfrac{5}{4}D}+\underbrace{(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}-I)t}_{\leq\tfrac{5}{4}D}+\underbrace{(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}^{-1}-I)(\mathbf{c}_{0}-t)}_{\leq\tfrac{5}{4}D^{2}/U\leq D/4}+\underbrace{\mathbf{c}_{0}-t}_{\leq D}.

So, since
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{C}v\in(1-4\varepsilon)S
and all the other summands together have norm at most
4D
and their sum thus lies in
B_{4D}\subseteq 4\varepsilon S
, we see that
\mathbf{B}(v+w_{0})\in(1-4\varepsilon)S\boxplus 4\varepsilon S\subseteq S
.

Therefore, the success probability of the entire procedure is lower bounded by

\frac{|(1-5\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}{|(1+4\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}\geq\frac{|(1-5\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}{|(1+5\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}
.

(
Run time analysis
) Note that the additional running time of this algorithm compared to
Lemma

8.7
is caused by the (possible) extra computations on
\tilde{t}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(which satisfies
\|\tilde{t}\|\leq 2U
, which can be at most
\poly(\size(\mathbf{C}),\allowbreak\log U,\allowbreak\log N)
. The dependency on
n
is hidden in
\size(\mathbf{C})
.
∎

Notation 8.9
.

We define
