\phi:\prod_{\sigma}\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow K_{\mathbb{R}}
,
(n_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\mapsto(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}

by putting

\begin{cases}x_{\sigma_{\nu}}=n_{\sigma_{\nu}}+in_{\bar{\sigma}_{\nu}}&\mbox{ if $\nu$ is complex}\\
x_{\bar{\sigma}_{\nu}}=n_{\sigma_{\nu}}-in_{\bar{\sigma}_{\nu}}&\mbox{ if $\nu$ is complex}\\
x_{\sigma_{\nu}}=n_{\sigma_{\nu}}&\mbox{ if $\nu$ is real}\end{cases}

(58)

Abusing notation, we will just denote
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
or
\prod_{\sigma}\mathbb{Z}
for
\phi(\prod_{\sigma}\mathbb{Z})
.

Corollary 8.10
.

Let
K
be a degree
n
number field, let
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
be represented by rational numbers, let
\mathfrak{b}\in\ideals
be an ideal and let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
be a modulus. Let
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
and let
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
. Let
\mathbf{B}_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
with
\mathbf{b}_{j}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
(i.e., in the Minkowski space) be a basis of
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
satisfying

\|x\cdot\mathbf{b}_{i}\|\leq 2n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(x\cdot\mathfrak{b}\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{0})

Let
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
satisfying
\|x\tau\|\leq r
. Then we can perfectly uniformly sample from

x(\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tau)\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}.

within expected time
\poly(n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(\mathfrak{b}),\size(\mathfrak{m}),\size(x),\size(\omega))

Proof.

We apply
Proposition

8.8
. For this, we need to satisfy all requirements (1) - (6) in
Proposition

8.8
. We start with (1) and (4), then proceed with (2), (3), (5) and (6). Note that
Proposition

8.8
only outputs some
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, but because the elements in
x(\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tau)
can be symbolically represented, we will see that such an output
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
can be converted into
x\sum_{j=1}^{n}\beta_{j}v_{j}+x\tau
where
\beta_{j}\in K
is the element in
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
associated with the basis element
\mathbf{b}_{j}
.

Requirements (1) and (4)
.
Since a uniform sample from
x(\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tau)\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}
can be simply obtained by taking a uniform sample of
x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}\cap(r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}-x\tau)
and adding
x\tau
afterwards, we concentrate on taking a uniform sample from this latter set.
Recall that

r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ |x_{\sigma}|\leq r\mbox{ and }x_{\sigma}/\sigma(\tau)>0\mbox{ for real }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}\}.

Hence we can write
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}=(r^{\prime}_{\sigma})_{\sigma}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}+t^{\prime}=S+t^{\prime}
with
r^{\prime}_{\sigma}=r/2
for real
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
and
r^{\prime}_{\sigma}=r
otherwise, and
t^{\prime}_{\sigma}=r/2
for real
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
and
t^{\prime}_{\sigma}=0
otherwise; and
S=(r^{\prime}_{\sigma}){{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
(which is a convex set). Then, putting
t:=t^{\prime}-x\tau
, we have

r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}-x\tau=S+t^{\prime}-x\tau=S+t

Note that
\|x\cdot\mathbf{b}_{i}\|\leq r/(24n^{2})
(by the definition of
r
and
Lemma

2.13
(
ii
) and (
iv
); see also
Equation

62
).
Hence, writing
D=2\sum_{i}\|x\cdot\mathbf{b}_{i}\|\leq r/(12n)
, we see that

B_{6nD}\subseteq B_{r/2}\subseteq S\subseteq B_{r}
and
\|t\|_{\infty}\leq\|t^{\prime}\|+\|x\tau\|\leq 2nr
. So, taking
\varepsilon:=1/(6n)
and
U:=2nr
satisfies the requirements (1) and (4) of
Proposition

8.8
.

Requirement (2) and (3)
. Let us first compute an upper bound on
U/D
. We have (see
Lemma

2.13
)
\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{b})\geq\sqrt{n}\norm(x\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{b})^{1/n}
and hence
D\geq 2n^{3/2}\norm(x\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{b})^{1/n}
. Hence, since
U=2nr
, and
\mathsf{b}^{1/\mathsf{b}}\leq e^{1/e}
,

U/D\leq\frac{(2\cdot n)\cdot 48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}}{2n^{3/2}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{b})^{1/n}}=48\cdot\omega\cdot e^{2n/e}\cdot n^{3}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|

(59)

We need to (efficiently) approximate
\mathbf{C}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\|\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{B}\|\leq\|\mathbf{B}^{-1}\|^{-1}\cdot D/U
, with
\mathbf{B}=x\mathbf{B}_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
. For that it is sufficient
7
7

7

We have
\|A\|\leq\|A\|_{F}=(\sum_{ij}|A_{ij}|^{2})^{1/2}
(Frobenius norm) and hence approximating
\mathbf{B}
by
\mathbf{C}\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
yields a
\mathbf{C}
for which
|\mathbf{C}_{ij}-\mathbf{B}_{ij}|\leq N^{-1}
and hence
\|\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{C}\|\leq\|\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{C}\|_{F}\leq nN^{-1}
.
to choose
N\geq n\cdot\|\mathbf{B}^{-1}\|\cdot U/D
.
Using
Lemma

24.2
, noting that
\lambda_{n}(x\cdot\mathfrak{b}\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{0})/\lambda_{1}(x\cdot\mathfrak{b}\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{0})\leq|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
(see
Lemma

2.13
) and
\mathsf{b}^{1/\mathsf{b}}\leq e^{1/e}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{B}^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq n^{n/2+1}\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{2n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(x\cdot\mathfrak{b}\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq n^{n/2+1}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot(2n)^{n}\cdot e^{2n^{2}/e}.

(60)

Combining
Equation

59
and
Equation

60
, instantiating

\displaystyle N:=\lceil n\cdot n^{n/2+1}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot(2n)^{n}\cdot e^{2n^{2}/e}\cdot 48\cdot\omega\cdot e^{2n/e}\cdot n^{3}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\rceil\geq n\cdot\|\mathbf{B}^{-1}\|^{-1}\cdot U/D

(61)

we can certainly compute such approximation
\mathbf{C}
within time
\poly(n,\log(N),\log(r))
for such
N
(since
\|x\mathbf{b}_{i}\|<r
). Similarly, for requirement (3), we can efficiently compute
\tilde{t}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
satisfying
\|t-\tilde{t}\|\leq 1/(2N)
. Since
1/(2N)\leq D/2\leq U
we certainly have
\|\tilde{t}\|\leq 2U
.

Requirement (5)
.
We can efficiently and perfectly uniformly sample in
\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap(1+c)S
for every
c>0
(where we understand
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\hookrightarrow K_{\mathbb{R}}
as in
8.9
) by simple rejection sampling (where the ‘circles’ associated to the complex places are done one-by-one).
Indeed, for this
N
, the uniform random sampling in

(1+4\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
(with
\varepsilon=1/(6n)
) has bit complexity
O(n\log(rN))
.

Requirement (6)
.
For the implementation of the oracle
O_{x\mathbf{B}_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}}}
, it is enough to show that we can efficiently decide for any rational number
g\in\mathbb{Q}
, integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and any embedding
\sigma
of
K
, whether an algebraic number
x\beta
with
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
satisfies
|\sigma(x\beta)|>g^{1/k}
or
|\sigma(x\beta)|\leq g^{1/k}
. Indeed, this holds since the set
S
is entirely defined in terms of these (absolute values of) embeddings. By putting
k=2\cdot\mathsf{b}\cdot n
we can write
r=g^{1/k}
for some rational
r
. The existence and the effectiveness of such an oracle is precisely the object of
Corollary

8.4
; it is clear that it runs in expected time
\poly(n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(\mathfrak{b}),\size(\mathfrak{m}),\size(x),\size(\omega))
.

Bit complexity

Since
\log(r),\log(N)
are both
\poly(n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(\mathfrak{b}),\allowbreak\size(\mathfrak{m}),\allowbreak\size(x),\allowbreak\size(\omega))
, a single run of the algorithm takes bit complexity
\poly(n,\allowbreak\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size(\mathfrak{b}),\allowbreak\size(\mathfrak{m}),\allowbreak\size(x),\allowbreak\size(\omega))
. If the success probability of a single run is bounded from below by a constant, the entire algorithm (until success) has the same expected bit complexity. It thus remains to show that, for the box
S=(r^{\prime}_{\sigma}){{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
,
\varepsilon=1/(6n)
, and
N
as in
Equation

61
, the success probability is lower bounded by a constant. We have that the success probability is lower bounded by (using
Lemma

2.8
with
c=(\min_{\sigma}|r^{\prime}_{\sigma}|)^{-1}
and (the
r
of that lemma)
r=(1\pm 5\varepsilon)\in[\tfrac{1}{6},2]
)

\frac{|(1-5\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}{|(1+5\varepsilon)S\cap\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n}|}\geq\frac{(1-5\varepsilon)^{n}|S|}{(1+5\varepsilon)^{n}|S|}\frac{N^{n}e^{-12nc}}{N^{n}e^{4nc}}\geq\frac{(1-1/n)^{n}e^{-16nc}}{(1+1/n)^{n}}\geq e^{-2}\cdot e^{-1}\geq e^{-3},

since
c=(\min_{\sigma}|r^{\prime}_{\sigma}|)^{-1}\leq(r/2)^{-1}\leq 1/(24n)
.
∎

8.5.
Algorithm for sampling in a box intersected with an ideal lattice

Algorithm 1

Uniform sampling in
x\cdot((\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}

0:

•

An LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,

•

A modulus
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
of the degree
n
number field
K
.

•

An ideal
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
,

•

An element
\gamma\in K
,

•

An element
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
,

•

A block size parameter
\mathsf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
2\leq\mathsf{b}\leq n
.

•

A element

x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
,

•

A real number
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
.

0:

A uniformly distributed element
\beta\in x\cdot((\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
with
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

1:

Defining the radius of the ball.
Define
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

2:

Obtaining a short basis of
x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.
Apply
Lemma

8.5
to obtain a
\mathsf{b}
-BKZ reduced basis
B_{\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
of
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
that satisfies

\displaystyle\|x\cdot\mathbf{b}_{i}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(x\cdot\mathfrak{b}\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq 2n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{3/(2n)}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{1/n}\leq r/(24n^{2}),

(62)

where the second inequality follows from
Lemma

2.13
(
ii
) and (
iv
) and the third from the definition of
r
.

3:

Computing a new shift
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}
to take account for the modulus.
Compute
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathfrak{b}+\gamma
such that
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\equiv\tau
modulo
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, which is possible by the fact that
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
are coprime.
If
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we put
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}=\gamma
.

4:

Reducing the shift
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}
modulo the short basis of
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.
Reduce

\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in K

modulo this
\mathsf{b}
-BKZ-reduced basis
(\mathbf{b}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
of
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, yielding
\gamma_{\mathrm{red}}\in\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. That is, write
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}=\sum_{i}t_{i}\mathbf{b}_{i}
and put
\gamma_{\mathrm{red}}=\sum_{i}(t_{i}-\lfloor t_{i}\rceil)\mathbf{b}_{i}
.

5:

Sampling an element in
x\cdot((\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma_{\mathrm{red}})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}
.
Using
Corollary

8.10
, sample
\beta\in x\cdot((\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma_{\mathrm{red}})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}
.

6:

return

\beta
.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
K
be a number field,
let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
be a modulus of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
let
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
be an ideal of
K
coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and
let
\gamma\in K
be a shift. Let
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathfrak{b}+\gamma
such that
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\equiv\tau
modulo
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.

Then

(\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=(\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}

Proof.

By scaling up
\mathfrak{b},\gamma,\tau
by an integer
M\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}

coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, we can assume that they lie in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. It is then enough
to show that
(M\mathfrak{b}+M\gamma)\cap M\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=(M\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+M\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})\cap M\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
.

We start with inclusion to the right. Since
M\mathfrak{b}+M\gamma\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we have that
\alpha\in(M\mathfrak{b}+M\gamma)\cap M\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
satisfies
\alpha=M\beta+M\gamma=M\tau+\mu
with
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}
and
\mu\in\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Additionally
\sigma(\alpha)/\sigma(\tau)>0
for real
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
(since multiplying with
M
does not change the sign of
\sigma(\tau)
).

By the definition of
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in K
, we have
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathfrak{b}+\gamma
and
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tau
. Hence
\alpha-M\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\equiv 0
mod both
M\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. Hence
\alpha\equiv M\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}
modulo
M\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, i.e.,
\alpha\in M\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+M\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Also,
\sigma(\alpha)/\sigma(\tau)>0
for real
\sigma\mod\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, so
\alpha\in\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
.

The inclusion to the left can be done similarly. Any
\alpha\in(M\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+M\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
satisfies
\alpha\in M\mathfrak{b}+M\gamma
. Also, since
\alpha\equiv M\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\equiv M\tau
mod
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and
\alpha\in\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
, we have
\alpha\in M\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 8.12

(Correctness and efficiency of
Algorithm

1
)
.

Let
K
be a number field of degree
n
,
let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
be a modulus of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
let
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}
be an
ideal,
let
\gamma\in K
be a shift,
let
\tau\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
be coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
,
let
\mathsf{b}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
be a block size parameter,
let
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
be represented by rational numbers,
let
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
and put
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

Then, the randomized algorithm

Algorithm

1
samples from a
uniform distribution over

x\cdot((\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
.
Moreover, this algorithm runs in expected time
\poly(n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(\mathfrak{m}),\size(\mathfrak{b}),\size(\gamma),\size(\tau),\size(x),\size(\omega))
.

Proof.

The correctness of the algorithm follows from
Corollary

8.10
and
Lemma

8.11
. The only thing that needs to be checked is that
\gamma_{\mathrm{red}}
satisfies
\|x\gamma_{\mathrm{red}}\|\leq r
. We have
\gamma_{\mathrm{red}}=\sum_{i}c_{i}\mathbf{b}_{i}
with
c_{i}\in[-1/2,1/2)
, hence
\|x\gamma_{\mathrm{red}}\|\leq\sum_{i}|c_{i}|\|x\mathbf{b}_{i}\|\leq r
, since the basis was BKZ-reduced (see line
2
).

We conclude the proof with the estimate on the running time. In line
1
, a mere instantiation of the radius
r
is given.

In line
2
, a short basis of
x\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
is computed using a BKZ-like algorithm. This takes, by
Lemma

8.5
,
time
\poly(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}},\size(\mathfrak{b}),\size(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(x))
.

In line
3
, a
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in K
is computed satisfying
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}\in(\mathfrak{b}+\gamma)\cap(\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tau)
. For this, it suffices to compute elements
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}
and
\mu\in\mathfrak{m}_{0}
such that
\beta+\mu=1
(by putting
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}=\tau\beta+\gamma\mu
). Such a pair
(\beta,\mu)\in\mathfrak{b}\times\mathfrak{m}
can be found by applying the Hermite normal form to the concatenated basis matrices of
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{m}

[
23
, Proposition 1.3.1]
. This requires
\tilde{O}(n^{5}\log(M)^{2})
time
[
72
]
, where
M
is the maximum entry occurring in the basis matrices. Clearly this overall process takes time polynomial in
n,\size(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\size(\gamma)
and
\size(\mathfrak{b})
.

In line
4
, this element
\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}}
is reduced modulo
\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, which takes time at most
\poly(\size(\mathfrak{b}),\size(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\size(\gamma))
, since
\size(\gamma_{\mathfrak{m}})=\poly(\size(\mathfrak{b}),\size(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\size(\gamma))
.

Lastly, in line
5
, a uniform sample is taken, following
Corollary

8.10
. The expected bit complexity then follows.
∎

9.
Ideal sampling

9.1.
Introduction

In this section, we prove the main result of this part,
Theorem

9.5
.
Recall the task at hand.
Fix a family of ideals
\mathcal{S}
. Given an ideal
\mathfrak{a}
, find
\beta\in\mathfrak{a}
such that
\beta\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
with probability proportional to the density of
\mathcal{S}
.

In
Section

6
is proven that the input ideal lattice
\mathfrak{a}
can be randomized so that its Arakelov class is uniformly distributed. For such random ideal lattices, by
Section

7
, the event
\beta\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
happens with the anticipated probability (the ideal density of
\mathcal{S}
) when
\beta
is sampled in a large enough box. Subsequently, we proved in
Section

8
that one can efficiently sample from such a box. Combining these results together leads to
Algorithm

2
and
Theorem

9.5
, of which the latter can be informally rephrased as follows: There is an efficient way to sample
\beta\in\mathfrak{a}
satisfying
\beta\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}
with a provable lower bound on the sampling probability.

In the proof of
Theorem

9.5
we require three technical results,
which are in the later separate
Section

9.3
and
Section

9.4
.
The first of these three results is a lemma that
states that part of
Algorithm

2
is exactly a random walk as in
Theorem

6.4
,
applied to the input ideal lattice
y\cdot\mathfrak{b}
. This allows to apply the density result as in
Theorem

7.1
.
The second result consists of a lemma that states that a distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

for which holds that the ‘folded’ distribution
[\mathcal{D}]
is close to uniform in
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
,
the original distribution
\mathcal{D}
is close to some
\mathcal{D}_{U}
on
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
satisfying
[\mathcal{D}_{U}]=U(\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0})
.
This allows for the statistical argument that we may assume the ‘randomized’ ideal lattice being drawn
from
\mathcal{D}_{U}
rather than the original distribution
\mathcal{D}
, for the cost of a small error coming
from the statistical distance.

The last of these three results is
Proposition

9.12
, which shows that
the output distribution of

Algorithm

2
and that of the ‘continuous variant of
Algorithm

2
’ (which
is to be specified precisely later) are close. This result is very useful because some algorithmic properties
are much easier to prove for this ‘continuous variant’; this closeness of the output distributions then
show that these properties must also hold for the original variant of
Algorithm

2
, though
with a small error due to the slight difference between the output distributions of the two variants.

9.2.
Ideal sampling

Definition 9.1
.

We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{B}^{G}
the set of
B
-smooth integral ideals coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and whose prime divisors lie in the subgroup
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{S}_{B}^{G}=\big\{\mathfrak{a}\mbox{ ideal of }\mathcal{O}_{K}\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{a}\mbox{ implies }\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0},\norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B\mbox{ and }[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G\}.

For
G=\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
we just get the set of smooth ideals
\mathcal{S}_{B}
coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.

Notation 9.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
be a modulus of
K
and let
\tau\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
be an element coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. Then we denote

\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}:=\{(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sign(x_{\sigma})=\sign(\sigma(\tau))\mbox{ for all real }\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\}.

Alternatively, this set consists of all
(x_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
for which holds
x_{\sigma}/\sigma(\tau)>0
for all real
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
.

Notation 9.3
.

Let
H=\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0})
be the hyper space of the number field
K
, i.e.,
H=\{(x_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sum_{\nu}x_{\nu}=0\}
. Let
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{\mathbbm{r}+1})
be an ordering of the places (with
\mathbbm{r}+1={n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}
). We denote
\mathbb{Z}_{H}\subseteq H
for the integral lattice with basis
\mathbf{B}_{H}=(e_{\nu_{1}}-e_{\nu_{2}},\ldots,e_{\nu_{i}}-e_{\nu_{i+1}},\ldots,e_{\nu_{\mathbbm{r}}}-e_{\nu_{\mathbbm{r}+1}})
, where
e_{\nu_{i}}
is the standard basis. It
satisfies
\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{H})=\lambda_{\mathbbm{r}}(\mathbb{Z}_{H})=\sqrt{2}
and hence
\cov(\mathbb{Z}_{H})\leq n
.

We will denote
\ddot{H}=\frac{\delta}{n}\mathbb{Z}_{H}
for the ‘discretized’ hyper space, where
\delta>0
is some grid parameter. We then have
\cov(\ddot{H})\leq\delta
.

Lemma 9.4
.

The output distribution of
Algorithm

2
is independent of the absolute value of the norm
|\norm(y)|
of
y
, and independent of the signs (and complex phase) of the entries
y_{\sigma}
of
y=(y_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
.

Proof.

The variable
y
only occurs in lines
6
and
7
. The element
\tilde{\beta}
is perfectly uniformly random over
\big((A_{\sigma}\cdot y_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\cdot\big[\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\big]\big)\cap r\cdot\norm(y\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
. Hence,
\beta:=(A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot y_{\sigma}^{-1})_{\sigma}\cdot\tilde{\beta}
is uniformly distributed over

\big(\big[\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\big]\big)\cap r\cdot(A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot y_{\sigma}^{-1})_{\sigma}\cdot|\norm(y)|^{1/n}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}

=\big(\big[\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\big]\big)\cap r\cdot(A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot(y^{0})_{\sigma}^{-1})_{\sigma}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}},

where
y^{0}:=y/|\norm(y)|^{1/n}
. Hence, the algorithm depends on
y^{0}
and in particular not on
|\norm(y)|
. Since the set
{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
is symmetric around zero, the signs (and complex phases) of
y^{0}_{\sigma}
(and hence of
y
) do not have any influence on the set
(A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot(y^{0})_{\sigma}^{-1})_{\sigma}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
.
∎

Theorem 9.5

(ERH, Ideal sampling theorem)
.

Let
K
be a number field of degree
n
, let
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
be a modulus, let
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
a finite-index subgroup of
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}

and let
\mathbf{O}_{G}
be an oracle that on input an ideal
\mathfrak{c}

outputs whether
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{c})]\in G
or not.
Let
\mathfrak{b}\in\ideals
be an ideal coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
,
let
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
(see
Section

2.4
) satisfying
[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]\in G
,
and let
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
be represented by rational coordinates.
Let
\mathsf{b}\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
be an integer, let
0<\varepsilon<\min(1,\tfrac{20}{n})
be an error parameter and let
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of integral ideals coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, satisfying
[d^{0}(\mathcal{S})]\subseteq G-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})]
.

(A) Correctness.

Algorithm

2
outputs
an element
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}
such that

•

(\beta)\cdot\mathfrak{b}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}^{G}
,

•

\beta\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
(i.e.,

\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\beta-\tau)\geq\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
for all
\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}

and
\sigma(\beta/\tau)>0
for all real
\sigma\mid\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
),

•

|\norm(\beta)|\leq\norm(\mathfrak{b})\cdot B^{N}\cdot r^{n}

with probability at least

\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\cdot\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]-\varepsilon\geq\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]-\varepsilon.

(63)

Here,
B=\widetilde{O}\Big([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]^{2}\cdot n^{2}\cdot\big[n^{2}\cdot(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))^{2}+(\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m})))^{2}\big]\Big)
,

N=\lceil 7n+\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m}))+\log|\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}|-\log[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]+2\log(1/\varepsilon)+1\rceil
as in
Corollary

6.5
,

r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
,
and
\norm(\mathfrak{m})=2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
with
|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|
being the number of different real embeddings dividing
\mathfrak{m}
(see
Section

2.4
).

(B) Running time.

Furthermore,
Algorithm

2
has a bit complexity of
\poly(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}},\allowbreak\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size(\mathfrak{b}),\allowbreak\log(1/\varepsilon),\allowbreak\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m})),\allowbreak[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G],\allowbreak\size(y),\allowbreak\size(\tau),\allowbreak\size(\omega))
and uses at most

\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}))\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]

queries to
\mathbf{O}_{G}
.

Proof of
Theorem

9.5
(A) Correctness.

This proof is structured as follows. We will assume, purely for the sake of argument, that lines
4
and
5
in
Algorithm

2
are replaced by the following,

“
Sample
a=(a_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{H,s}
and put
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}=(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}a_{\sigma}})_{\sigma}
”.

(64)

and that
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
is replaced
8
8

8

Note that this particular change of
y
by
y^{0}
does not impact the output distribution, by
Lemma

9.4
.
by
y^{0}=y/|\norm(y)|^{1/n}
(so that
b=\Log(y)\in H
). This is indeed purely
for the sake of argument, since these changes renders this algorithm unprocessable by a computer, due to the real arithmetic. We will show
two things: (1) After these changes, which we will call the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’, both the correctness and the success probability claim
as stated in
Equation

63
of (A) do hold (but with
\varepsilon/2
instead of
\varepsilon
); and (2) the output distribution of this ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’ is
\varepsilon/2
-statistically close
to the output distribution of the ordinary (or discrete) version of
Algorithm

2
(that is, without the changes on
y
and line
4
and
5
).
Together, we may then conclude that the correctness and success probability claim holds for the ordinary version of
Algorithm

2
, which is
what we aimed to show.

Part (1): Showing the correctness and success probability for the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’
.
We assume that lines
4
and
5
from
Algorithm

2
are replaced by
Equation

64

and that
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
. We will denote
b=\Log(y)\in H
. Then, by
Lemma

9.8
, which we will treat later, the ideal-element pair
\big((\beta)\bar{\mathfrak{b}}^{-1},\beta\big)\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}\times\mathfrak{b}
from
Algorithm

2
is distributed as

\big((\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a}),\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\big),

with
\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{G}(N,B,s)+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
and subsequently
\alpha\leftarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
uniformly. Here
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{G}(N,B,s)+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
is the random walk distribution starting on the point
d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
(see
Definition

6.1
). Here,
\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}
is defined in
Definition

5.2
and
b\in H\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

For the random walk distribution
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{G}(N,B,s)+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
on
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
with these parameters
(N,B,s)
holds that
[\mathcal{W}]
on
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
is

\varepsilon/2
-close
to the uniform distribution
\mathcal{U}(G+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b}))
on the coset
G+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})
in the total variation distance. So, allowing an error of

\varepsilon/2

we may as well assume that
\mathbf{a}
instead comes from a distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
that satisfies
[\mathcal{D}]=\mathcal{U}(G+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b}))
(see
Lemma

9.9
).

By applying
Theorem

7.1
, using that
[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]\in G
, one then obtains that the probability that
(\beta)\cdot\bar{\mathfrak{b}}^{-1}=(\alpha)\Exp(-\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{S}
given that
\beta=\alpha{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
(which is precisely the way how
\alpha
is sampled) is at least
\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]-\varepsilon/2
. From the fact that
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathfrak{b}\cdot\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j}
with
\mathfrak{p}_{j}\nmid\mathfrak{m}
,
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})]\in G
, and
\norm(\mathfrak{p}_{j})\leq B
, we have that
(\beta)\cdot\mathfrak{b}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B}^{G}
in that case, and the probability claim of
Equation

63
(with
\varepsilon/2
instead of
\varepsilon
) follows.

We finish part (1) of this proof by showing that the output
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}
of
Algorithm

2
satisfies all bullet points of the theorem.
By lines
6
and
7
it follows that

\beta\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
. By line
6
it follows that
\tilde{\beta}\in r\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
, hence
|\norm(\tilde{\beta})|\leq r^{n}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})\leq r^{n}\cdot B^{N}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{b})
. Since the Gaussian distortion
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
(in the ‘continuous version’) does not change the norm (as
a\in H
), we have
|\norm(\beta)|=|\norm(\tilde{\beta})|
.

Part (2): Showing that the output distribution of the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’ and the discrete version are
\varepsilon/2
-close
. By the proposition in
Section

9.4
, together with the fact that the discrete Gaussian in line
4
is approximated within statistical distance
\varepsilon/4
, we conclude that with the choice of the ‘grid parameter’
\delta
of
\ddot{H}
in line
4
of
Algorithm

2
, the statistical difference between the ‘continuous variant’ and the ordinary variant of
Algorithm

2
is at most
\varepsilon/4+\varepsilon/4=\varepsilon/2
.

Hence, the success probability of the ordinary
Algorithm

2
as in the theorem statement is lower bounded by

\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]-\varepsilon/2-\varepsilon/2=\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]-\varepsilon,

as was required to prove.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

9.5
(B) Running time.

In the following complexity analysis, any complexity that is within
\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size(\mathfrak{b}),\allowbreak\log(1/\varepsilon),\allowbreak\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m})),\allowbreak[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G],\allowbreak\size(y),\allowbreak\size(\tau))
we will call ‘polynomial in the size of the input’.
Note that
\log B
and
N
are
\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|
,
\size(\mathfrak{b}),

\log(1/\varepsilon),

\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m})),

\log([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]))
,
so any complexity polynomially bounded by
\log B
and
N
must be polynomial in the size of the input as well. We can omit the dependency here on
|G|
since
\log|G|\leq\log|\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}|\leq\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m}))
(see
Lemma

5.1
).

We go through lines
1
to
7
of
Algorithm

2
.
Line
1
just initializes
B
and
N
and
s
. Line
2
uses
Lemma

5.4

N
times to obtain
the random primes
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{N}
. This takes complexity
O([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot n^{3}\cdot\log^{2}(B)\cdot N)
and
O(N\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot n\log B)=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log\norm(\mathfrak{m})))\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]
queries to
\mathbf{O}_{G}
.

In line
3
,
\mathfrak{b}
is multiplied by the
N
sampled prime ideals.
Multiplication of two ideals can be done by LLL-reducing the
n^{2}\times n
matrix involving all products of the
\mathbb{Z}
-generators of the respective ideals, taking bit complexity
\tilde{O}(n^{11}\log(M)^{3})

[
59
]
, where
M
is the maximum entry of the matrix involved. This multiplication is done with
N
ideals for which
\log(M)
is bounded by
\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\size(\mathfrak{b}),\log(B))
, which means
that the total bit complexity of this ideal multiplication is in polynomially bounded in the size of the input. An alternative way
to see this is by using the two-element representation of ideals (e.g.,
[
26
, §4.7]
).

Line
4
requires to sample
\ddot{a}
from an approximate discrete Gaussian
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\frac{\delta}{n}\mathbf{B}_{H},\varepsilon/4,s,0}
in
\ddot{H}=\frac{\delta}{n}\mathbb{Z}_{H}
as in
Lemma

2.22
(with
\mathbf{B}_{H}
as in
9.3
), which is within statistical distance
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}:=\varepsilon/4
of an exact
discrete Gaussian. Indeed, observing that
the maximum length of the basis of
\frac{\delta}{n}\mathbb{Z}_{H}
(see
9.3
) is at most
\sqrt{2}\delta/n
we see that certainly (see line
4
)

s\geq\delta\sqrt{n\log(2n/(\varepsilon/40))}\geq\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\varepsilon)+2\log(n)+3}{\pi}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}\delta}{n}.

This, according to
Lemma

2.22
, takes expected time polynomial in the input size and
\log(1/\varepsilon)
. Since the input size is
\poly(1/\delta)=\poly(n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log(1/\varepsilon),\log(\omega))

this Gaussian sampling algorithm has a expected bit complexity of
\poly(n,\allowbreak\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\log(1/\varepsilon),\allowbreak\log(\omega))
. Note that, by
Lemma

2.22
, the length of
\ddot{a}
cannot exceed
s\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n^{2}/\varepsilon)}
.

In line
5
we need to approximate
(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\ddot{a}_{\sigma}})_{\sigma}
by rational
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
such that the relative multiplicative error is small enough. Since the length of
\ddot{a}
does not exceed
s\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n^{2}/\varepsilon)}
(note that
s=1/n^{2}
) we clearly see that
\min_{\sigma}\ddot{a}_{\sigma}
cannot be smaller than
\varepsilon/(2n^{2})<e^{-\sqrt{\log(2n^{2}/\varepsilon)}}
. Hence, approximating
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
as in line
5
can be done in time
\poly(n,\log(1/\delta),\log(1/\varepsilon))
which is
\poly(n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log(1/\varepsilon),\log(\omega))
. Note that the size of
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
itself is also bounded polynomially in these latter parameters.

Line
6
requires

Algorithm

1
,
which uses expected bit complexity at most

\poly(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}},\allowbreak\size(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}),\allowbreak\size(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\allowbreak\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size(y),\allowbreak\size((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}),\allowbreak\size(\tau))
. Note that
\size(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})=\poly(\size(\mathfrak{b}),\allowbreak N,\allowbreak\log(B))
.
Proposition

8.12
can be applied here because we have

r\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot|\norm(y)|=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{\frac{1}{n}}\cdot|\norm(y)|
.

The last line, line
7
, only multiplies
\tilde{\beta}
with
(A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot y_{\sigma}^{-1})_{\sigma}
, which has bit complexity polynomial in
\size((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma},\allowbreak(y_{\sigma})_{\sigma},\allowbreak\size(\tilde{\beta}))
which is polynomial in the size of the input, as
\size(\tilde{\beta})
must be
\poly(r,\size(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}),\size(y),\size((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma})
.

Therefore, all steps require a bit complexity at most polynomial in the size of the input and
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
. The total number of queries to
\mathbf{O}_{G}
is at most
\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log\norm(\mathfrak{m})))\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]
.
∎

Remark 9.6
.

If
\mathfrak{m}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we have
\norm(\mathfrak{m})=\phi(\mathfrak{m})=1
, and
K^{\mathfrak{m}}=K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=K^{*}
. In this case,
\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=K^{*}

Remark 9.7
.

The requirement
[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]\in G
is not necessary. The same proof and algorithm applies for arbitrary
[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]
; but, as
\beta\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
, we have
[\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis]=[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]
. Hence, if we do not assume
[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]\in G
, the property
[\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis]\in G
is generally not valid anymore.

Algorithm 2

Sampling of
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}
such that
\beta\in\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}

0:

•

An LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,

•

A modulus
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
of the degree
n
number field
K
.

•

A finite-index subgroup
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
,

•

An ideal
\mathfrak{b}\in\ideals
coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
,

•

An element

y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
,

•

An element
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
, satisfying
[\llparenthesis\tau\rrparenthesis]\in G
,

•

An error parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

•

A block size parameter
\mathsf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
2\leq\mathsf{b}\leq n
.

•

An oracle
\mathbf{O}_{G}
that on input an ideal
\mathfrak{c}
outputs whether
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{c})]\in G
.

•

An element
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
.

0:

An element
\beta\in\mathfrak{b}

1:

Let
B,N
be as in
Corollary

6.5
, put
s=1/n^{2}
and put
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

2:

Sample
N
random prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{N}
uniformly from the set
\{\mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime ideal of }K\penalty\ |\penalty\ N(\mathfrak{p})\leq B,\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0},[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G\}
, using the oracle
\mathbf{O}_{G}
and
Lemma

5.4

3:

Multiply
\mathfrak{b}
by these
N
random prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
, obtaining
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathfrak{b}\cdot\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j}
.

4:

Sample, using
Lemma

2.22
, an approximation of the discrete Gaussian

\ddot{a}=(\ddot{a}_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\frac{\delta}{n}\mathbf{B}_{H},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}},s,0}

with error parameter
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}:=\varepsilon/4
, where
\frac{\delta}{n}\mathbf{B}_{H}
is a basis of
\ddot{H}=\frac{\delta}{n}\mathbb{Z}_{H}
(see
9.3
) and

\delta:=\frac{(\varepsilon/40)^{4n^{2}s+1}\cdot s}{\omega^{n}\cdot e^{10n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n/(\varepsilon/40))}}.

5:

Compute a rationally represented
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
such that

\max_{\sigma}|A_{\sigma}/e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\ddot{a}_{\nu_{\sigma}}}-1|\leq\delta/(2n),

where
n_{\nu_{\sigma}}=2
if
\sigma
is complex and
1
otherwise.

6:

Sample, following
Algorithm

1
with input
\gamma=0
, a uniformly random element

\tilde{\beta}\in\big((A_{\sigma}\cdot y_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\cdot\big[\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\big]\big)\cap r\cdot|\norm(y)|^{1/n}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}

7:

return

\beta=(A_{\sigma}^{-1}\cdot y_{\sigma}^{-1})_{\sigma}\cdot\tilde{\beta}\in\mathfrak{b}
.

9.3.
Two help lemmas

The following two lemmas are used in the proof of

Theorem

9.5
.
The first of these lemmas shows that part of
Algorithm

2
consists of a random walk, whereas the second one shows that
one can replace the random walk distribution over
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
with a close distribution
\mathcal{D}_{U}
whose ‘folded analogue’
[\mathcal{D}_{U}]

in
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
is uniform.

Lemma 9.8

(The ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’ mimicks a random walk)
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
a modulus, let
N,B,s
and
r
as in
Algorithm

2
and let
\mathcal{W}_{G}=\mathcal{W}_{G}(N,B,s)
be the random walk distribution on
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
(see
Definition

6.1
). Let
\mathcal{W}_{r}
be the distribution on
(\alpha,\mathbf{a})\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\times\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
obtained by sampling
\mathbf{a}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}_{G}(N,B,s)+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
with
b=\Log(y)
where
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
followed by sampling

\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}

uniformly.
Then the pair
\big((\beta)\cdot\bar{\mathfrak{b}}^{-1},\beta\big)\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}\times\mathfrak{b}
obtained by running
Algorithm

2
where lines
4
and
5
are replaced by
Equation

64
(coined the ‘continuous version’), follows the same distribution as
\big((\alpha)\cdot\Exp(-\mathbf{a}),\alpha\cdot{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\big)
with
(\alpha,\mathbf{a})\leftarrow\mathcal{W}_{r}
.

Proof.

Both the distribution
\mathcal{W}_{G}
and the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’ (where lines
4
and
5
are replaced by
Equation

64
, as in the proof of
Theorem

9.5
) involve the following two random processes: picking
N
uniformly random primes from
\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}\mbox{ prime }\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B\mbox{ and }[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G\}
and sampling a Gaussian
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H
; both with the exact same parameters. Without loss of generality, we can therefore focus on one fixed sample
\{\mathfrak{p}_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leq j\leq N\}
of primes and one fixed vector
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H
.
This means that we consider the fixed
\mathbf{a}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}d^{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})+a+d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
for the procedure involving
\mathcal{W}_{r}
and the fixed ideal
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathfrak{b}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\mathfrak{p}_{j}
and distortion

\Exp(a+b)=(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}(a_{\nu_{\sigma}}+b_{\nu_{\sigma}})})_{\sigma}

(recall that
(y_{\sigma})_{\sigma}=(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}b_{\nu_{\sigma}}})_{\sigma}
; note that, by
Lemma

9.4
, we may assume that the entries of
y_{\sigma}
are all positive) for the procedure involving the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’. Then, writing
\bar{b}=\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}=\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
, we have

\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}=\Exp(a+b)(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})/\bar{b}\penalty\ \mbox{ and }\penalty\ {\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})=\Exp(a+b)/\bar{b}

Thus,
\alpha\Exp(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})
for uniformly random
\alpha\in\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
is distributed as

\displaystyle{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\cdot\mathcal{U}\big(\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\big)

(65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Exp(-a-b)\cdot\bar{b}}_{{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})}\cdot\mathcal{U}\Big(\underbrace{\Exp(a+b)(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})/\bar{b}}_{\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\Big)

(66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\Big((\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})\cap\Exp(-a-b)\cdot\bar{b}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\Big)

(67)

which is exactly the distribution of
\beta\in\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
in
Algorithm

2
for fixed
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
and
(a_{\nu})_{\nu}
. It follows that

(\alpha)\Exp(-\mathbf{a})=(\alpha){\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}){\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})=(\alpha){\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}

is distributed as
(\beta)\bar{\mathfrak{b}}^{-1}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

We will need the following ‘lifting’ lemma, which states that the random walk distribution
\mathcal{W}_{G}(N,B,s)
over
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
is close to a distribution
\mathcal{D}
such that the class
[\mathcal{D}]
is uniform over a coset
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

Lemma 9.9

(Lifting property of distributions)
.

Let
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
be a finite-index subgroup and suppose that a distribution
\mathcal{D}:\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfies
\|[\mathcal{D}]-\mathcal{U}(G+[\mathbf{b}])\|_{1}<\varepsilon
for some coset
G+[\mathbf{b}]
, where
\mathbf{b}\in\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.
Then there exists a ‘lifted’ distribution
\mathcal{D}_{U}:\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
[\mathcal{D}_{U}]=\mathcal{U}(G+[\mathbf{b}])
and
\|\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{D}_{U}\|_{1}<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Put

\mathcal{D}_{U}(\mathbf{a})=\left\{\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{\vol(G)}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{a})}{[\mathcal{D}]([\mathbf{a}])}&\mbox{ if }[\mathcal{D}]([\mathbf{a}])\neq 0\\
u&\mbox{ otherwise }\end{matrix}\right.,

for some
u:\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that satisfies
[u]=\frac{1}{\vol(G)}\cdot 1_{G}\in L_{1}(\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0})
. Then, one can check that
[\mathcal{D}_{U}]=\frac{1}{\vol(G)}\cdot 1_{G}
is uniform on
G+[\mathbf{b}]
. Furthermore, writing
F
for a fundamental domain in
\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
for
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{D}_{U}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\sum_{\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}/\mu_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}}|\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)-\mathcal{D}_{U}(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)|d\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{[\mathbf{a}]\in\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}\left|[\mathcal{D}]([\mathbf{a}])-\frac{1_{G+[\mathbf{b}]}([\mathbf{a}])}{\vol(G)}\right|d([\mathbf{a}])=\|[\mathcal{D}]-\mathcal{U}(G+[\mathbf{b}])\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon.

The first equation holds by definition, the second equation by the fact that the sign of
(\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)-\mathcal{D}_{U}(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis))
depends (by construction) solely on the coset
[\mathbf{a}]
.∎

9.4.
Statistical distance between the ‘continuous variant’ and the ordinary variant of
Algorithm

2

In this subsection we show that there is only a small statistical distance between the output distributions
of the ‘continuous variant of
Algorithm

2
’ (where lines
4
and
5
are
replaced by
Equation

64
) and the ordinary variant of
Algorithm

2
(with no changes).
This result is used in the proof of part (A) of
Theorem

9.5
and in the proof that
Algorithm

2
is almost-Lipschitz in the later
Lemma

10.6
.

To prove the closeness of these two distributions, we fix all the input parameters and compare the two output distributions;
where we note that we may assume that
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
and that the entries of
y
are positive, by
Lemma

9.4
.
By the law of total probability, it is enough to show the closeness of these two distributions for a fixed sample of the
primes
\{\mathfrak{p}_{j}\}
(as in line
3
), and hence a fixed ideal
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
. Indeed, the difference between
the two distributions can be attributed solely to the way the Gaussian
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H
(discrete or continuous) is sampled and the influence
it bears on the uniform distribution of
\beta
.

Though the input of
Algorithm

2
is given in terms of an
ideal
\mathfrak{b}
and an element
y=\Exp((b_{\nu})_{\nu})\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
with
b\in H

we will often consider the
divisor
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
as input instead.
As this is a one-to-one correspondence, and is used only theoretically (for cleanness of notation),
no harm is done.

By a similar computation as in the proof of
Lemma

9.8
, one can deduce that the output
\beta
is distributed as (denoting
b=\Log(y)\in H
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\Big((\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1})\cap\Exp(-\Log((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma})-b)\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\Big)

(68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{U}\Big((y\cdot(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}))\cap\Exp(-\Log((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}))\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\Big)

(69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{U}\Big((y\cdot(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma))\cap\Exp(-\Log((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}))\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}\Big)

(70)

both in the ‘continuous version’ as in the ordinary version (since the definition of
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
changes accordingly). Equation
Equation

70
follows
from
Lemma

8.11
; where
{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}={{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
(see
9.2
). Note that from
Equation

70
follows that
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
has only an influence on the shape (‘skewness’) of the box
\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}

where the uniform sampling takes place.
All other things are equal; hence we introduce the following notation.

Notation 9.10
.

For
a\in H
, we denote
\mathcal{U}_{a}
for the uniform distribution over the set

(y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma))\cap\Exp(a)\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}
, where
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
with
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
; as in
Algorithm

2
, i.e.,

\mathcal{U}_{a}:=\mathcal{U}\Big((y\cdot(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma))\cap\Exp(a)\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}\Big)=\mathcal{U}\Big((y\cdot(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma))\cap\Exp(a)\cdot r_{0}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}\Big).

where we write
r_{0}:=\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.
Note that, for
y\beta\in y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma)\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
, we have that

\mathcal{U}_{a}[y\beta]>0\Longleftrightarrow\log|\sigma_{\nu}(y\beta)|\leq\log r_{0}+n_{\nu}^{-1}a_{\nu}\mbox{ for all }\nu

where
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise.
We denote

S_{y\beta}=\{a\in H\penalty\ |\penalty\ n_{\nu}^{-1}a_{\nu}\geq\log|\sigma_{\nu}(y\beta)|-\log r_{0}\mbox{ for all }\nu\}.

I.e., we have, for
y\beta\in y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma)\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
and
a\in H
,

a\in S_{y\beta}\Leftrightarrow y\beta\in\Exp(a)\cdot r_{0}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}[\beta]>0.

We denote
\partial S_{y\beta}
for the boundary of
S_{y\beta}
; note that this boundary is a subset of
\mathbbm{r}=\dim(H)
hyperplanes in
H
of dimension
\mathbbm{r}-1
.

In the ordinary variant of
Algorithm

2
, an approximation
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
of

(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\ddot{a}_{\nu_{\sigma}}})_{\sigma}

with
\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}
is computed, hence leading to a slight error. For ease of notation we denote
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}=\Exp(\tilde{\ddot{a}})=(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\tilde{\ddot{a}}_{\sigma}})_{\sigma}
. By construction (see line
4
) we have (for
\delta<1/2
)

\displaystyle\|\ddot{a}-\tilde{\ddot{a}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq n\max_{\sigma}|\log(A_{\sigma})-\log(e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\ddot{a}_{\nu_{\sigma}}})|=n\max_{\nu}|\log(A_{\sigma_{\nu}}/e^{n_{\nu_{\sigma}}^{-1}\cdot\ddot{a}_{\nu}})|

(71)

\displaystyle\leq n\max_{\sigma}\log|1-\delta|\leq 2n\cdot\delta/(2n)=\delta.

(72)

So, the ordinary variant of
Algorithm

2
, with fixed choices of primes
\{\mathfrak{p}_{j}\}
leading to
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
, has output distribution

\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}\mathcal{G}_{\ddot{H},s}(\ddot{a})=\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}})\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\ddot{H},s}(\ddot{a}),

whereas the ‘continuous variant of
Algorithm

2
’ (with the same choices of
\{\mathfrak{p}_{j}\}
), has output distribution

\int_{a\in H}\mathcal{U}_{a}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)=\int_{a\in H}\mathcal{U}_{a}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}})\cdot\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a).

Lemma 9.11
.

Using
9.10
, let
\delta>0
be a distance parameter and denote
B_{\delta}(a)\subseteq H
for the
2
-ball of radius
\delta
around
a\in H
(where we denote
B_{\delta}:=B_{\delta}(0)
).
Then, for
a\notin\bigcup_{y\beta\in y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma)}(\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{\delta})
and
a^{\prime}\in B_{\delta}(a)
,

\mathcal{U}_{a}=\mathcal{U}_{a^{\prime}}.

Proof.

For
a\notin\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{\delta}
holds that
B_{\delta}(a)\cap\partial S_{y\beta}=\emptyset
. Hence for all
a^{\prime}\in B_{\delta}
, the status of
y\beta\in e^{a+a^{\prime}}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r_{0}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}^{\tau}}
(where
r_{0}=\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r
) does not change. Hence, if
a\notin\bigcup_{y\beta\in y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma)}(\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{\delta})
, the entire distribution
\mathcal{U}_{a-a^{\prime}}=\mathcal{U}_{a}
is constant for
a^{\prime}\in B_{\delta}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 9.12
.

Using the notation of
9.10
, let
1/(2n)>\varepsilon>0
be an error parameter. Let
s>0
be a Gaussian parameter and let
\ddot{H}\subseteq H
be a full-rank lattice whose Voronoi domain
F
satisfies

\delta:=\max_{a\in F}\|a\|\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{4n^{2}s+1}\cdot s}{\omega^{n}\cdot e^{10n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}}

(73)

Then

\left\|\int_{a\in H}\mathcal{U}_{x}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da-\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}\mathcal{G}_{\ddot{H},s}(\ddot{a})\right\|_{1}\leq 9\varepsilon

Proof.

Writing
F
for the Voronoi fundamental domain of the lattice
\ddot{H}
, using the identity
AB-A^{\prime}B^{\prime}=(A-A^{\prime})B+A^{\prime}(B-B^{\prime})
, the triangle inequality and the fact that
\|\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}\|_{1}=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{a\in H}\mathcal{U}_{x}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da-\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}\mathcal{G}_{\ddot{H},s}(\ddot{a})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{a\in F}\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\mathcal{U}_{\ddot{a}+a}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(\ddot{a}+a)-\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\ddot{H},s}(\ddot{a})}{\vol(F)}da\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{a\in F}\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\|\mathcal{U}_{\ddot{a}+a}-\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}\|_{1}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(\ddot{a}+a)da}_{(I)}+\underbrace{\int_{a\in F}\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\left|\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(\ddot{a}+a)-\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\ddot{H},s}(\ddot{a})}{\vol(F)}\right|da}_{(II)}.

(74)

We will bound the parts (I) and (II) separately, starting with (I).

Bound on (I)
.

In the following sequence of inequalities we use respectively
Lemma

9.11
(in combination with
Equation

71
),
\cov(\ddot{H})=\max_{x\in F}\|x\|=\delta
and the fact that total variation distances are always bounded by
2
. Also, we use a Gaussian tail bound (see
Lemma

2.19
) with radius
R:=s\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}>2\delta>0

(since we can deduce that
\delta<s/2
from the assumption in
Equation

73
).
Combining this, writing
T_{\delta}=\bigcup_{y\beta\in y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma)}(\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{\delta})
, we obtain

\displaystyle(I)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{a\in F}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}\\
\ddot{a}\in T_{\delta}\end{subarray}}\|\mathcal{U}_{\ddot{a}+a}-\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{\ddot{a}}}\|_{1}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(\ddot{a}+a)da\leq 2\int_{a\in F}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}\\
\ddot{a}\in T_{\delta}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(\ddot{a}+a)da

(75)

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{a\in T_{2\delta}}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da\leq\varepsilon+2\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in T_{2\delta}\\
\|a\|<R\end{subarray}}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da

(76)

The third inequality follows from the observation that
\ddot{a}+a\in\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{2\delta}
if
\ddot{a}\in\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{\delta}
(since
a\in F\subseteq B_{\delta}
).

If
a\in\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{2\delta}
and
\|a\|<R
, then (for all
\nu
)
n_{\nu}^{-1}a_{\nu}\geq\log|\sigma_{\nu}(y\beta)|-\log r_{0}-R
(since
R>2\delta
), and hence
\log|\sigma_{\nu}(y\beta)|\leq n_{\nu}^{-1}a_{\nu}+\log r_{0}+R
, i.e.,

\max_{\sigma}|\sigma(y\beta)|\leq r_{0}\cdot\exp(\max_{\nu}a_{\nu})e^{R}\leq r_{0}e^{2R}
(since we assumed
\|a\|<R
). Recall, by definition,
r_{0}=\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot r
. Hence, by the condition
\|a\|<R
, we may take the union
T_{2\delta}=\bigcup_{y\beta\in y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma)}(\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{2\delta})
only over those
y\beta
satisfying
y\beta\in r_{0}e^{2R}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
.

We concentrate for the moment on the Gaussian weight on a single
S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{2\delta}
in the union of
T_{2\delta}
.
Note that
\partial S_{y\beta}
lies in a union of
\mathbbm{r}=\dim(H)\leq n
hyperplanes in
H
. Denoting
P
for a hyperplane in
H
of dimension
\dim(H)-1
that goes through the origin, we have, for all
y\beta\in y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma)
,

\int_{a\in\partial S_{y\beta}\boxplus B_{2\delta}}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da\leq n\int_{a\in P\boxplus B_{2\delta}}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da\leq n\cdot\mbox{erf}(2\delta/s)\leq 4n\cdot\delta/s,

(77)

where
erf
is the standard error function of the standard normal distribution that satisfies
\mbox{erf}(x)\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}x
. The inequality in
Equation

77
holds because the hyperplane
P\subseteq H
going through the origin is the one where
P\boxplus B_{2\delta}
has the largest Gaussian weight.

Writing
e^{2nR}\cdot N_{0}
for an upper bound for
|(y(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\gamma))\cap r_{0}e^{2R}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}|
for which we will instantiate
N_{0}
later, we combine
Equation

76
and
Equation

77
, and the assumption in
Equation

73
on
\delta
, to obtain

(I)\leq\varepsilon+2\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\bigcup_{\beta\in\mathfrak{b}}(\partial S_{\beta}\boxplus B_{2\delta})\\
\|a\|<R\end{subarray}}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da\leq\varepsilon+\frac{8n\cdot e^{2nR}\cdot N_{0}\cdot\delta}{s}\leq 2\varepsilon.

(78)

It remains to show that
\delta
as in the assumptions of this proposition indeed satisfies
\delta\leq\varepsilon\cdot s\cdot(8n)^{-1}e^{-2nR}\cdot N_{0}^{-1}
. We have
\exp(-2nR)=\exp(-2n^{3/2}s\sqrt{\log(2n/\varepsilon)})\geq\exp(-4n^{2}s\log(1/\varepsilon))=\varepsilon^{4n^{2}s}
(where we use that
\varepsilon<1/(2n)
). Also, by a similar reasoning as in
Section

7.2.2
, using
r_{0}=\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}r
, and the definition
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
, we have

\displaystyle N_{0}

\displaystyle\in[e^{-1/4},e^{1/4}]\cdot\frac{\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})\cdot 2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}\cdot r^{n}}{\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\sqrt{|\Delta_{K}|}}

\displaystyle\in[e^{-1/4},e^{1/4}]\cdot 48^{n}\cdot\omega^{n}\cdot 2^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}}\cdot(2\pi)^{{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n^{2}/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7n/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|

And hence
N_{0}\leq\omega^{n}\cdot e^{6n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|
since
48^{n}\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n^{2}/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7n/2}\leq e^{n\log(48)+2n^{2}/e+7n\log(n)/2}\leq e^{6n^{2}}
(because
\log(48)+(2/e)\cdot n+7\log(n)/2\leq 6n
for all
n>0
).
Therefore, indeed,

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{4n^{2}s+1}\cdot s}{\omega^{n}\cdot e^{10n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\cdot s\cdot e^{-4n^{2}}\cdot\varepsilon^{4n^{2}s}\cdot\omega^{-n}\cdot e^{-6n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{-1}\cdot(n\log(2n/\varepsilon))^{-1/2}

(79)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\cdot s\cdot(8n)^{-1}e^{-2nR}\cdot N_{0}^{-1}

Bound on (II)

We still write
F
for the Voronoi domain of
\ddot{H}
. We apply
Lemma

9.13
to obtain a bound on (II). Indeed, by putting
\Lambda=\ddot{H}
and noting that

\lambda_{n}(\ddot{H})\leq 2\delta=2\cov_{2}(\ddot{H})
(see
Lemma

2.3
) we surely have

\lambda_{n}(\ddot{H})\leq 2\delta\leq 2\cdot\frac{\varepsilon^{4n^{2}s+1}\cdot s}{e^{10n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon\cdot s}{5\pi n\cdot 2\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}}

where we use that
e^{10n^{2}}>20\pi n
for
n\geq 1
. Now, since
2\cdot\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}>\sqrt{n\log(4n/\varepsilon)}
the parameter
s
satisfies the assumptions of
Lemma

9.13
, hence we can bound (II) by
7\varepsilon
.

Combining
Equation

74
and the bound on (II), we obtain

\left\|\int_{a\in H}\mathcal{U}_{x}\mathcal{G}_{H,s}(a)da-\sum_{\ddot{a}\in\ddot{H}}\mathcal{U}_{\ddot{a}}\mathcal{G}_{\ddot{H},s}(\ddot{a})\right\|_{1}\leq 9\varepsilon,

as required.

∎

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
\Lambda\subseteq V
be a full-rank lattice, let
F
be the Voronoi domain of
\Lambda
, let
\varepsilon<1/2
be some error parameter and let

s>5\pi\cdot\sqrt{n\log(4n/\varepsilon)}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot n\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
. Then

\int_{x\in F}\sum_{\ell\in\Lambda}\big|\mathcal{G}_{V,s}(\ell+x)-|F|^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s}(\ell)\big|dx\leq 7\varepsilon

Proof.

Note that
\cov_{2}(\Lambda)=\max_{x\in F}\|x\|\leq n\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)/2
(see
Lemma

2.3
).
Using smoothing arguments, (using that
s>\sqrt{\log(2n(1+1/\varepsilon))/\pi}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
) writing
d=\dim(V)
,
we see that (see
Lemma

2.20
)

|F|^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s}(\ell)=g_{s}(\ell)\cdot\frac{1}{|F|\cdot g_{s}(\Lambda)}\in[1-2\varepsilon,1+2\varepsilon]\cdot s^{-d}\cdot g_{s}(\ell)=[1-2\varepsilon,1+2\varepsilon]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{V,s}(\ell),

where we used that
1-2\varepsilon\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{-1}\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\leq 1+2\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon<1/2
. Using this, in combination with the tail bound (both using
Corollary

2.18
and
Lemma

2.19
) with tail cut parameter
R:=s\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}>\cov_{2}(\Lambda)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{x\in F}\sum_{\ell\in\Lambda}\big|\mathcal{G}_{V,s}(\ell+x)-|F|^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s}(\ell)\big|dx

(80)

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon+

\displaystyle\int_{x\in F}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\Lambda\\
\|\ell\|\leq 2R\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{G}_{V,s}(\ell+x)-|F|^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda,s}(\ell)\right|dx

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon+4\varepsilon+

\displaystyle\int_{a\in F}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\Lambda\\
\|\ell\|\leq 2R\end{subarray}}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{V,s}(\ell+x)-\mathcal{G}_{V,s}(\ell)\Big|dx

\displaystyle\leq 6\varepsilon+

\displaystyle\int_{x\in F}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in\Lambda\\
\|\ell\|\leq 2R\end{subarray}}\mathcal{G}_{V,s}(\ell+x)|1-e^{5\pi\cdot\cov_{2}(\Lambda)\cdot R/s^{2}}|dx

(81)

\displaystyle\leq 6\varepsilon+\frac{10\pi\cov_{2}(\Lambda)R}{s^{2}}\leq 6\varepsilon+\frac{5\pi n\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)R}{s^{2}}<7\varepsilon.

(82)

The inequality in
Equation

81
follows from the fact that

\displaystyle-\frac{s^{2}}{\pi}\log g_{s}(\ell+x)

\displaystyle=\|\ell+x\|^{2}=\|\ell\|^{2}+2\langle\ell,x\rangle+\|x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\in[\|\ell\|^{2}-4R\cov_{2}(\Lambda),\|\ell\|^{2}+5R\cov_{2}(\Lambda)]

which follows by Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that
\max_{x\in F}\|x\|\leq\cov_{2}(\Lambda)\leq R
. The last inequalities in
Equation

82
follow from
|1-e^{x}|\leq 2x
for
x<1/2
, and

\frac{5\pi n\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)R}{s^{2}}=\frac{5\pi n\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\sqrt{n\log(2n/\varepsilon)}}{s}<\varepsilon.

∎

9.5.
The sampling theorem in arbitrary orders of a number field

The sampling theorem (
Theorem

9.5
) is not restricted to the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}

of a number field
K
. Below we give a quick sketch how to amend the reasoning as to
obtain a similar result for arbitrary orders within
K
.

Write
R\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
for an order,
\mathfrak{f}_{R}=\{x\in K\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\mathcal{O}_{K}\subseteq R\}
for its conductor
and
\Delta_{R}
for its discriminant. Then we can in fact apply the sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

2
) for
R
in place of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
if
\mathfrak{f}_{R}\mid\mathfrak{m}
.
Indeed, for all ideals and elements involved are assumed to be coprime with
\mathfrak{f}_{R}
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between ideal arithmetic
in
R
and
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, by the maps
\mathfrak{A}\mapsto\mathfrak{A}\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
\mathfrak{a}\mapsto\mathfrak{a}\cap R
for ideals
\mathfrak{A}
of
R
and
ideals
\mathfrak{a}
of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. These maps are compatible with ideal multiplication, as long as all ideals involved are coprime to
\mathfrak{f}_{R}
(e.g., combine
[
58
, Chapter I, Proposition 12.10]
and
[
3
, Chapter 1, Extension and Contraction]
).

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
R\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
an order of a number field
K
with conductor
\mathfrak{f}_{R}
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{f}_{R}\mid\mathfrak{m}
and that
\tau\in R
. Then replacing every occurring ideal
\mathfrak{a}
(that is,
\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{p}_{j},\mathfrak{m}_{0}
) by the ideal
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{a}\cap R
in
Algorithm

2
and using only arithmetic in
R
, does not change the output distribution.

Proof.

We go through every step of
Algorithm

2
, where we will use that
\mathfrak{b}
and the primes
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
are coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. In step
3
we multiply
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{b}\cap R

by random prime ideals
\mathfrak{P}_{j}=\mathfrak{p}_{j}\cap R
satisfying
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})]\in G
. Hence we obtain
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}=\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap R
with
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}=\mathfrak{b}\cdot\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j}
. The Gaussian distortion in step
4
, is not affected by the specific order
R
.

In step
6
, we have

\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap\tau K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}}
for
\tilde{\tau}\in\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
satisfying
\tilde{\tau}\equiv\tau
mod
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
(see
Lemma

8.11
).
Since
\mathfrak{f}_{R}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, we have that
\mathfrak{M}_{0}:=\mathfrak{m}_{0}\cap R=\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, by the very definition of the conductor; it is the largest ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
that is also an ideal in
R
. Therefore,
\tilde{\tau}\in\tau+\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq R
, as
\tau
is assumed to be in
R
.

So,
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\cap\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}\cap R=(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau})\cap R
. Since
\mathfrak{f}_{R}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, we have that
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}\mathfrak{M}_{0}=(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cap R=\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. Hence,
\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{m}_{0}+\tilde{\tau}=\bar{\mathfrak{B}}\mathfrak{M}_{0}+\tilde{\tau}\subseteq R
.

Then it follows that the sampling an output distribution of steps
6
and
7
are the same as in the original algorithm.
∎

Remark 9.15
.

Note that, if only a sub-order
R\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
is known, the call to
Algorithm

1
in line
6
of
Algorithm

2
cannot be done with
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
. Instead, the larger
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta(R)|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
must then be used, where
|\Delta(R)|=[\mathcal{O}_{K}:R]^{2}|\Delta_{K}|
is the discriminant of
R
.

10.
Properties of the ideal sampling algorithm

10.1.
Introduction

In this section we will treat several important properties
of the ideal sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

2
). These
properties are phrased in terms of the
output distribution
of
Algorithm

2
,
that is, the distribution of
\beta\in K
on line 5.
This distribution has three important properties: having the
shifting property
, being
bounded

and being
almost Lipschitz continuous
.

The boundedness and the shifting property are useful to show certain

randomness properties
of the output distribution of
Algorithm

2
on a random input.
In
Section

11
of this paper, we will use

Algorithm

2

on a ‘Gaussian’ input
to rigorously
compute
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit groups. For this, these two properties will be of fundamental
importance to show that sampling sufficiently many ‘relations’ will eventually
lead to the full
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit group.

The (almost-) Lipschitz continuous property is there
to show that rounding or slightly disturbing the input of the ideal sampling algorithm
does not impact the output of this algorithm significantly
(this concerns the variable
\Log(y)=b=(b_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H
).
From this one can conclude that rounding or finite precision issues do not
play a role at all for the input of
Algorithm

2
.

By
Lemma

9.4
, the output distribution of
Algorithm

2

is independent on the norm of
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
and the signs of the entries. Hence, in theoretical arguments, we are always
allowed to replace
y
by
y^{0}=y/|\norm(y)|^{1/n}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
and to assume that the entries of
y^{0}
are positive. In the actual running algorithm
we will not do this, because in general
y^{0}
does not consist of rational numbers.

The shifting property

The shifting property of
Algorithm

2
relates a certain change
in the input to a similar change in the output. For this property to be phrased succinctly
it is useful to consider the input of
the algorithm
to be the zero-degree Arakelov divisor
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
(one retrieves

\mathfrak{b}=\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
and
b=\mathbf{a}-d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})
, where
b=\Log(y)
; here we use
H\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
).

Shifting the input
\mathbf{a}
by a principal divisor, yielding
\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
(with
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
),
has as a result that the output distribution of
Algorithm

2
is ‘multiplied’ by
\alpha
.
More precisely, the probability of sampling
\eta\in K
in the ideal sampling algorithm on input divisor
\mathbf{a}
is
precisely the same as the probability of sampling
\alpha\cdot\eta
on input
\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
.
Writing
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
for the output distribution of
Algorithm

2
(see
10.1
) this can be more succinctly written as:

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}(\eta)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}(\alpha\cdot\eta).

This is called the ‘shifting’ property because
a shift of the input divisor by a principal divisor
\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
causes
the output distribution to ‘shift’ (multiplicatively) by the same element
\alpha
.

This property of
Algorithm

2
is proven in
Section

10.3
.

Boundedness

The output distribution of
Algorithm

2
being bounded means here that the output
cannot exceed a certain size.
This property is almost immediate by the
fact that the output element is sampled in a (slightly) distorted box, where the distortion is dictated
by a truncated discrete Gaussian (see
Lemma

2.22
). Exactly the dimensions of this box
give the upper bound on the size of the elements; this is proven in
Section

10.4
.

Lipschitz continuous

The output distribution being almost-
Lipschitz
is useful when one considers the input
to be discretized or rounded. Again, using the notation
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
for
the output distribution of
Algorithm

2
on input divisor
\mathbf{a}
(see
10.1
),
being almost Lipschitz means that the total variation distance between
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+a}
with
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H

is linearly upper bounded by
\|a\|
, allowing some slack by adding a small error. More precisely, there exist
L\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and a small
\eta\in[0,1)
such that for all
\mathbf{a},a\in H
,

\|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+a}-\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}\|_{1}\leq L\cdot\|a\|+\eta.

This fact is proven in
Section

10.5
.

10.2.
Preliminaries

The input of
Algorithm

2
is given in terms of an
ideal
\mathfrak{b}
and an element
y=\Exp((b_{\nu})_{\nu})\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
with
b\in H
. In this section
about properties of this algorithm, it is cleaner to use an Arakelov
divisor
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
as input instead
9
9

9

This is interchangeable, as the ideal
\mathfrak{b}=\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
and element
b=\mathbf{a}-d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})

are readily retrieved (where
b=\Log(y)
).
It is important to note, however, that
\Exp(\mathbf{a})\neq y\cdot\mathfrak{b}
. Instead,
\Exp(\mathbf{a})\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{b})^{1/n}=y\cdot\mathfrak{b}
. We chose for this convention to make
\mathbf{a}
a degree-zero Arakelov divisor.
.

Notation 10.1

(Output distribution)
.

For a fixed number field
K
, with modulus
\mathfrak{m}
, finite-index subgroup
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, element
\tau\in\mathcal{O}_{K}

coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and fixed error parameter
\varepsilon>0
, block size
\mathsf{b}
,
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
and
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
(where
b
is understood via the inclusion
H\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
), we denote

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}\in L_{1}(K^{*})

for the output distribution of
\beta
from
Algorithm

2
on input
\mathfrak{b}

(coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
) and

y=\Exp(b)\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
.
We will use the notation
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}(\alpha)
for the probability of sampling
\alpha\in K
from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
.

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}(\alpha)=\underset{x\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}}{\mathbb{P}}[x=\alpha].

10.3.
Shifting Property

In order to prove the shifting property of
Algorithm

2
,
we first start by the following lemma, that shows what impact
multiplication by an element
\alpha\in K^{*}
has on a (
\tau
-equivalent)
ideal lattice
\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}
. This will then be used to show that
the output distribution of
Algorithm

2

‘shifts’ (i.e., multiplies) by
\alpha
if the input
divisor is shifted by the principal divisor
\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
in
Lemma

10.3
.

Lemma 10.2
.

For all
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, multiplication by
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
gives an additive group isomorphism

\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\rightarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a}+{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\mathrm{f}})_{\tau},\penalty\ \gamma\mapsto(\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}\cdot\gamma

and additionally, restricted to
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
, a bijection

\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\rightarrow\Exp(\mathbf{a}+{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\mathrm{f}})_{\tau}\cap\Exp(-{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\infty})\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}.

Proof.

Very similar as in
Lemma

7.5
part (
i
), multiplication by
(\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
yields a bijection from
\Exp(\mathbf{a})_{\tau}
to
\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)_{\tau}\Exp(-{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\infty})=\Exp(\mathbf{a}+{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\mathrm{f}})_{\tau}
. Multiplication by an element is straightforwardly a group morphism, so the first claim follows by the fact that bijective group morphisms are isomorphisms.

For the second part, observe that multiplication by
(\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}
transforms the box
r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
into
(\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}=(|\sigma(\alpha)|)_{\sigma}\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}={\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\infty})\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
.
∎

Lemma 10.3
.

For all degree-zero Arakelov divisors
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
,
the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}

satisfies the following
shifting property
for all
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
,

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}\cdot\alpha)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}})

Proof.

Take an arbitrary
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
and write
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
for an ideal
\mathfrak{b}
and an
b=(b_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H
(where we see
b\in H\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
). To prove the statement it is enough to show for every
\beta\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}(\beta\cdot\alpha)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}(\beta)

As can be proved in the same fashion as in
Lemma

9.8
, the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
is the same as the distribution resulting from

\alpha\leftarrow{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{(\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b})}_{\infty})\cdot\mathcal{U}(\Exp(\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})\mbox{ with }\mathbf{b}=d^{0}(\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j})+a,

where
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
are sampled according to step
2
and
a=\Log((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma})
is sampled from a discrete Gaussian, as in step
4
.
Note that this
a
is slightly distorted, due to the computation of
(A_{\sigma})_{\sigma}
, but that is not problematic; the property used here is that this
a
is distributed independently of the value of
\mathbf{a}
. We write
\mathbf{b}=d^{0}(\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j})+a
. This divisor corresponds to the ‘random walk part’ of the algorithm.

We seek to compare the distributions
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}
. By the law
of total probability,
since the sampling of
\mathbf{b}=d^{0}(\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j})+a
is independent of

\mathbf{a}
and
\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
,
it is sufficient to consider a fixed sample

\mathbf{b}=d^{0}(\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j})+a

and compare the resulting distributions.
These resulting distributions, for a fixed
\mathbf{b}
, denoted
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis|\mathbf{b}}
, are

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}={\Exp}_{\infty}(-{(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a})}_{\infty})\cdot\mathcal{U}(\Exp(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})

(83)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis|\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle={\Exp}_{\infty}(-{(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)}_{\infty})\cdot\mathcal{U}(\Exp(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})

(84)

\displaystyle={\Exp}_{\infty}(-{(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a})}_{\infty})\cdot\mathcal{U}(\Exp(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a}+{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\mathrm{f}})_{\tau}\cap{\Exp}_{\infty}({-\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\infty})\cdot r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})

(85)

\displaystyle={\Exp}_{\infty}(-{(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a})}_{\infty})\cdot\mathcal{U}\Big((\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}\cdot(\Exp(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a})_{\tau}\cap r{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})\Big)

(86)

\displaystyle=(\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}

(87)

where the first equality holds by definition, the second equality follows from pulling
{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\infty})
into the uniform distribution and using the relation
\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis={\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\mathrm{f}}+{\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}_{\infty}
, the third equality follows from
Lemma

10.2
and the last equality by
Lemma

9.8
.

The result quickly follows from the law of total probability and the fact that multiplying by
(\sigma(\alpha))_{\sigma}
and
\alpha
is the same in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
.
∎

Lemma 10.4

(Post-selection shifting lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of ideals coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and let
\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{a}}
be the distribution obtained by running
Algorithm

2
on input
\mathbf{a}
and only outputting
\beta
if
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\in\mathcal{S}
and otherwise outputting
\perp
.

Then, this distribution satisfies

\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}\cdot\alpha)=\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{a}}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}),

for all
