\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
.

Proof.

This uses the same proof structure as
Lemma

10.3
, again using
Lemma

9.8
.
Reusing the notation
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}
for

\mathbf{b}=d^{0}(\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j})+a

as in
Lemma

10.3
,
we proved that, for a fixed sample
\mathbf{b}
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}})=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis|\mathbf{b}}(\alpha\cdot\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}).

(88)

The ideal
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
(where
\beta,\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
are from
Algorithm

2
on input
\mathbf{a}
) is distributed as
10
10

10

The distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}
concerns the right part
\alpha{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})
of
Lemma

9.8
, whereas
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
concerns
\alpha\Exp(-\mathbf{a})=\alpha{\Exp}_{\infty}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}){\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
.

{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})\cdot\gamma
with
\gamma\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}
(on input
\mathbf{a}
with fixed
\mathbf{b}
).

But replacing
\mathbf{a}
with
\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
here yields that the distribution of
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
of
Algorithm

2
on input
\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
is equal to (for a fixed sample
\mathbf{b}
)

{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}(-{(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)}_{\mathrm{f}})\cdot\gamma\mbox{ with }\gamma\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis|\mathbf{b}}

which is, by
Equation

88
, equal to

{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}(-{(\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis)}_{\mathrm{f}})\cdot\alpha\cdot\gamma\mbox{ with }\gamma\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}

which is, by manipulation, equal to

{\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}(-{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})\cdot\gamma\mbox{ with }\gamma\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}.

So, for a fixed sample of

\mathbf{b}=d^{0}(\prod_{j}\mathfrak{p}_{j})+a

the probabilities of obtaining
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
are the same, no matter
whether the input was
\mathbf{a}
or
\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
.

As a result,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}^{\mathcal{S}}=\alpha\cdot\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis|\mathbf{b}}
, where
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}|\mathbf{b}}^{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the output distribution of
Algorithm

2
on input
\mathbf{a}
with fixed sample
\mathbf{b}\leftarrow\mathcal{W}(N,B,s)
and the additional post-selection (i.e., outputting
\beta
whenever
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\in\mathcal{S}
and
\perp
otherwise).

By the law of total probability the distributions of
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}

for
\mathbf{a}
or
\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis
are the same; and, thus the rejection of
\beta/\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
for not being in
\mathcal{S}
happens for the same occurrences. Therefore,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{a}+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}\cdot\alpha)=\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathbf{a}}(\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}})
for all
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m},1}
.
∎

10.4.
Boundedness Property

In this section we show that
the output distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
is bounded, i.e.,
no arbitrarily large inputs occur. This can be seen
by the fact that the output is sampled from a distorted box,
where the distortion is distributed as a truncated discrete Gaussian (a bounded distribution by itself).
Thus, the output must be confined to a certain box, slightly larger than the dimensions of the original
undistorted box. This is made more formal in the following text.

Recall the Euclidean distance notion on
\Div_{K}
in
Section

2.8
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b\in\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
with

\mathfrak{b}\in\ideals
and
b=\Log(y)\in H

and let
B,N,s,r,\varepsilon
be as defined in

Algorithm

2
.
Then, we have, for
\beta\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
,

\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\rVert\leq 5\log(B^{N}\cdot r^{n})+\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert+s\cdot\sqrt{n\log(8n^{2}/\varepsilon)}.

Proof.

Bound on the norm of
\beta
.

We start with a bound on
|\norm(\beta)|
, which is of use for the rest of the proof.
The output of
\beta\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}
with
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
lies in the box

\Exp(-a-b)\cdot r\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}
(see lines
6
and
7
in
Algorithm

2
), where
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}=\Log((A_{\sigma})_{\sigma})\in H
is an approximation a sample of a (truncated) Gaussian distribution (see line
4
and
5
of
Algorithm

2
); and
b=(b_{\nu})_{\nu}=\Log(y)
. Hence, since
a,b\in H
do not change the norm (we may assume this by
Lemma

9.4
), we obtain
|\norm(\beta)|\leq\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})\cdot r^{n}\leq B^{N}\cdot r^{n}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{b})
; a bound on the norm of
\beta
.

Split the bound on
\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\rVert
into three parts.

We have, by the triangle inequality,

\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\rVert\leq\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis-\mathbf{a}\rVert+\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert.

(89)

We seek to bound
\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\rVert
. To do so, we concentrate on
\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis-\mathbf{a}\rVert
, which we can split in a ‘finite part’, a ‘constant part’ and an ‘infinite part’.

\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis-\mathbf{a}=\underbrace{\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}m_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis}_{\mbox{\scriptsize{finite part }}\mathbf{f}}-\underbrace{\sum_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}\log\norm(\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}})}{n}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis}_{\mbox{\scriptsize{constant part }}\mathbf{c}}+\underbrace{\sum_{\nu}\iota_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis}_{\mbox{\scriptsize{infinite part }}\mathbf{i}}.

(90)

where
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise.

Bounding the finite part
\mathbf{f}
and constant part
\mathbf{c}
.

Since
\beta\in\bar{\mathfrak{b}}\subseteq\mathfrak{b}
, we see that
(\beta)={\mathfrak{a}}\cdot\mathfrak{b}
for an
integral ideal

{\mathfrak{a}}
(recall that
\mathfrak{b}=\Exp({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
). Hence,
\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}m_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})=d^{0}(\mathfrak{a})
, has only positive coefficients
m_{\mathfrak{p}}\geq 0
. Since
\norm(\mathfrak{a})=|\norm(\beta)|/\norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq B^{N}\cdot r^{n}
by the norm bound on
\beta
in the very beginning of this proof, we quickly deduce

B^{N}\cdot r^{n}\geq\norm(\mathfrak{a})\geq\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\norm(\mathfrak{p})^{m_{\mathfrak{p}}}\geq 2^{\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}|m_{\mathfrak{p}}|}\geq 2^{(\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}|m_{\mathfrak{p}}|^{2})^{1/2}},

which implies

\lVert\mathbf{f}\rVert\leq(\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}|m_{\mathfrak{p}}|^{2})^{1/2}\leq\log(B^{N}\cdot r^{n})/\log(2)\leq 2\log(B^{N}\cdot r^{n})

(91)

Similarly, for the constant part
\mathbf{c}
, using
\mathfrak{a}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}
, we have,

\lVert\mathbf{c}\rVert\leq\sqrt{\sum_{\nu}n_{\nu}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\log(\norm(\mathfrak{a}))}{n}\leq\log(\norm(\mathfrak{a}))\leq\log(B^{N}\cdot r^{n})

(92)

Bounding the infinite part
\mathbf{i}
.

By definition (see
Equation

90
), and by the inclusion of
H\subseteq\Div_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
we have

\displaystyle\iota_{\nu}

\displaystyle=-n_{\nu}\log|\sigma_{\nu}(\beta)|+n_{\nu}\frac{\log(|\norm(\beta)|)}{n}-b_{\sigma}\in H

(93)

since
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
. By the fact that
\beta
lies in the box

\Exp(-a-b)\cdot r\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n}\cdot{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}
, we have
\log|\sigma_{\nu}(\beta)|\leq-n_{\nu}^{-1}a_{\sigma}-n_{\nu}^{-1}b_{\sigma}+\log r+\log(\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle-\log|\sigma(\beta)|

\displaystyle\geq n_{\nu}^{-1}a_{\sigma}+n_{\nu}^{-1}b_{\sigma}-\log r-\log(\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{1/n})

(94)

Hence, substituting
Equation

94
into
Equation

93
, and using that
\beta\in\bar{\mathfrak{b}}
(and hence
|\norm(\beta)|\geq\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})
), we obtain

\iota_{\nu}\geq a_{\nu}+n_{\nu}\log(|\norm(\beta)|^{1/n}\cdot\norm(\bar{\mathfrak{b}})^{-1/n}\cdot r^{-1})\geq a_{\nu}-n_{\nu}\log(r).

So, writing
\iota-a\in H
, it satisfies
\sum_{\nu}(\iota-a)_{\nu}=0
and
\iota_{\nu}-a_{\nu}\geq-n_{\nu}\log(r)
for all
\nu
.
Then
\|\iota-a\|=\sqrt{\sum_{\nu}|\iota_{\nu}-a_{\nu}|^{2}}
is bounded by
11
11

11

Suppose a sequence
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
satisfies
\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}=0
and
x_{j}\geq-t
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Then
\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{2}\leq n(n-1)t^{2}
. This maximum is attained by, for example,
x_{1}=(n-1)t
and
x_{j}=-t
for
1<j\leq n
.
We prove this by induction, where the case
n=1
is trivial (since then
x_{1}^{2}=x_{1}=0\leq n(n-1)t^{2}
). For
n>1
, we can assume that a non-zero sequence
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
has a positive co-ordinate (by the zero-sum requirement). Moreover, we can assume it has only a single positive co-ordinate. Indeed, if there are multiple positive coordinates, say
x_{1},x_{2}
, then the sequence
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-1})=(x_{1}+x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n})
satisfies
\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}y_{j}=0
and
y_{j}\geq-t
. Hence,
\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{2}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}y_{j}^{2}\leq(n-1)(n-2)t^{2}\leq n(n-1)t^{2}
, by induction. For a single positive co-ordinate it is clear that
x_{1}=(n-1)t
and
x_{j}=-t
for
1<j\leq n
is the optimal solution.
Applying this to the sequence
q_{\sigma}=n_{\nu}^{-1}(\iota_{\nu_{\sigma}}-a_{\nu_{\sigma}})
, we see that

\|\iota-a\|\leq 2\|(q_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\|\leq 2\sqrt{n(n-1)}\log(r)
.

2\sqrt{n(n-1)}\log(r)\leq 2\log(r^{n})
. Hence, writing
\mathbf{i}=(\iota_{\nu})_{\nu}
, we have

\lVert\mathbf{i}\rVert=\lVert\iota\rVert\leq\lVert a\rVert+2\log(r^{n})\leq\lVert a\rVert+2\log(B^{N}\cdot r^{n}).

(95)

Combining the bounds on
\lVert\mathbf{i}\rVert
and
\lVert\mathbf{f}\rVert
.

Concluding, by combining
Equations

89
,
90
,
91
,
92
and
95
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis-\mathbf{a}\rVert+\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert\leq\lVert\mathbf{f}\rVert+\lVert\mathbf{c}\rVert+\lVert\mathbf{i}\rVert+\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert

(96)

\displaystyle\leq 5\log(B^{N}\cdot r^{n})+\|a\|+\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert.

(97)

The final bound then comes from the fact that
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H
is (an approximation of a) discrete Gaussian distributed with deviation
s
computed according to the algorithm in
Lemma

2.22
with
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}:=\varepsilon/4
(see
4
of
Algorithm

2
). Hence,
\|a\|\leq s\cdot\sqrt{n\log(8n^{2}/\varepsilon)}
.
∎

10.5.
(Almost)-Lipschitz Property

The almost-Lipschitz-continuous property of the output distribution of

Algorithm

2
implies that it does not matter
too much if the input parameter
y=\Exp(b)\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
is slightly
inaccurate or rounded to a vector of rational numbers due
to machine precision.

This is relevant for
Section

11
of this paper,
where we will feed
Algorithm

2

y=\Exp((b_{\nu})_{\nu})
with
a continuous Gaussian distributed

b\in H
. The Lipschitz-continuous property in this subsection essentially
implies that
using a discrete Gaussian distribution instead for
b\in H
will
only cause a very small error overhead.

Lemma 10.6
.

The output distribution of the sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

2
) is
\varepsilon
-almost Lipschitz continuous
in the input parameter
b\in H
with
y=\Exp(b)
. That is, for
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
and
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}+(b^{\prime}-b)
we have

\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}-\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{2}\cdot\|b-b^{\prime}\|+\varepsilon,

where
\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}}-\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\|_{1}
is the total variation distance of the two distributions.

Proof.

In this proof, like in the proof of
Theorem

9.5
, we will again assume that line
4
in
Algorithm

2
is replaced by the line in
Equation

64
, and assume that
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
. Again we will refer to this as the ‘continuous
version of
Algorithm

2
’. This proof will be structured as follows. First we will show that this ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’ is actually Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
n^{2}/2
. After that we will use
Proposition

9.12
to show that the Lipschitz continuity also ‘almost’ holds for the ordinary version of
Algorithm

2
, with an additional error of
\varepsilon
.

In the following, we will reason about the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’.
The input
b\in H
is only used in
Algorithm

2
(as
b=\Log(y)
) in combination with
the variable
a=(a_{\nu})_{\nu}\in H
, which sampled according to a continuous Gaussian distribution with deviation
s=1/n^{2}
.
The sum
a+b
is then used combined in the rest of the algorithm.

So, for two different inputs
b,b^{\prime}\in H
, the only thing that differs in the algorithm are the distributions of

a+b
and
a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}
, for which
a,a^{\prime}
are (independently) sampled according to the same Gaussian distribution.

We denote
the distributions respectively
\mathcal{G}_{b}
and
\mathcal{G}_{b^{\prime}}
; these are Gaussian distributions with deviation
s=1/n^{2}

centered at
b
and
b^{\prime}
respectively (see
Section

2.9
).

Therefore, by the
data processing inequality (
Theorem

2.16
), one immediately deduces that
the statistical distance between the output distribution of the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

2
’ on input
\mathfrak{b},b
and
\mathfrak{b},b^{\prime}
is bounded above by

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{G}_{H,b}-\mathcal{G}_{H,b^{\prime}}\|_{1}.

The total variation distance of two continuous (multivariate) Gaussian distributions with different centers can be bounded by the Kullback-Leibler divergence
[
61
, Ch. 1, Ex. 11]
,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{G}_{H,b}-\mathcal{G}_{H,b^{\prime}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}D_{KL}(\mathcal{G}_{H,b}\penalty\ \|\penalty\ \mathcal{G}_{H,b^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{4}\|b-b^{\prime}\|^{2}/s^{2}}=\frac{\|b-b^{\prime}\|}{4\cdot s}=\frac{n^{2}\cdot\|b-b^{\prime}\|}{4}

The last equality is obtained by instantiating
s=1/n^{2}
.

As sketched in the introduction of this proof, we use that the ordinary version and the continuous version of
Algorithm

2
only differ by
\varepsilon/2
, by the very same argument as in the proof of
Theorem

9.5
. Hence, the statistical distance between the ordinary version of
Algorithm

2
input
\mathfrak{b},b
and
\mathfrak{b},b^{\prime}
is bounded by
\frac{n^{2}}{2}\cdot\|b-b^{\prime}\|+\varepsilon
, as required.
∎

In most use-cases,
Algorithm

2
is
repeated until success
, for which
the following lemma is more useful: it shows that the output distribution of the algorithm

conditioned on a successful outcome
is also almost-Lipschitz. This distribution conditioned
on a successful outcome is exactly the same as the output distribution resulting from repeating the algorithm
until success.

Lemma 10.7
.

The output distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{suc}}}_{\mathbf{a}}
, resulting from repeating the sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

2
) until success, is
\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}
-almost Lipschitz continuous
in the input parameter
b\in H
with
y=\Exp(b)
. That is, for
\mathbf{a}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b
and
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+b^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}+(b^{\prime}-b)
we have

\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{D}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{suc}}}_{\mathbf{a}}-\mathcal{D}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{suc}}}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{2\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}\cdot\|b-b^{\prime}\|+\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]},

where
\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{D}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{suc}}}_{\mathbf{a}}-\mathcal{D}^{\mbox{\scriptsize{suc}}}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\|_{1}
is the total variation distance of the two distributions.

Proof.

For this we apply the next
Lemma

10.8
with the lower bound
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]
on the success probability.
∎

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a randomized algorithm that can have input
y
and
y^{\prime}
.
Denote
\mathcal{A}_{y}
respectively
\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}
for the algorithm using
y
respectively
y^{\prime}
as input. Assume that both
\mathcal{A}_{y}
and
\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}
have success probability at least
p\in(0,1]
.

Now denote
\mathcal{B}_{y}
for the algorithm running
\mathcal{A}_{y}
until it outputs a successful output, and similarly denote
\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}}
for the algorithm running
\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}
until success. By abuse of notation, denote
\mathcal{B}_{y}
,
\mathcal{B}_{y}
,
\mathcal{A}_{y}
,
\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}
for the output distributions of these algorithms. Then the total variation distances are related by

\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{B}_{y}-\mathcal{B}_{y^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq\frac{\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{A}_{y}-\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}\|_{1}}{p}.

Proof.

Denote
X\cup\{\perp\}
for the output space of
\mathcal{A}
, where
\perp
denotes failure (unsuccessful output) and where
x\in X
are all successful outputs.

The output distribution
\mathcal{B}_{x}
is the output distribution of
\mathcal{A}_{x}

conditioned
on a successful output. Hence, denoting
\mathcal{A}_{x}^{\mathrm{success}}
(and similarly for
x^{\prime}
) for this conditional distribution, we have

\|\mathcal{B}_{x}-\mathcal{B}_{x^{\prime}}\|_{1}=\|\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{success}}_{x}-\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{success}}_{x^{\prime}}\|_{1}=\sum_{x\in X}|\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{success}}_{y}[x]-\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{success}}_{y^{\prime}}[x]|,

where
X
is the probability space of the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
. The conditional probability of
\mathcal{A}
outputting
x

conditioned
on a successful output (which are all of
x\in X
) then must equal
\frac{\mathcal{A}_{y}[x]}{p_{0}}

with
p_{0}=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}_{y}\mbox{ gives successful output}]
; and similarly for input
y^{\prime}
. Note that
|p_{0}-p_{1}|\leq\|\mathcal{A}_{y}-\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}\|_{1}
. Hence, writing
p=\min(p_{0},p_{1})
and noting that
p_{1}=\sum_{x\in X}\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}|\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{success}}_{y}[x]-\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{success}}_{y^{\prime}}[x]|=\sum_{x\in X}\left|\frac{\mathcal{A}_{y}[x]}{p_{0}}-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]}{p_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\left|\frac{\mathcal{A}_{y}[x]}{p_{0}}-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]}{p_{0}}\right|+\sum_{x\in X}\left|\frac{\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]}{p_{0}}-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]}{p_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p_{0}}\sum_{x\in X}\left|\mathcal{A}_{y}[x]-\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]\right|+\frac{1}{p_{0}}|p_{1}-p_{0}|=\frac{1}{p_{0}}\sum_{x\in X\cup\{\perp\}}\left|\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]-\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}[x]\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\|\mathcal{A}_{y}-\mathcal{A}_{y^{\prime}}\|_{1}}{p}.

∎

11.
Estimation of the factor
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})/\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
K
be a number field and let
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
be an integral ideal. Then we have

\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{1}{1-\norm(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}.

where
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=|K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}/K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}|
and where
\mathfrak{p}
ranges over the prime ideals of
K
dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we have
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=1
. Otherwise
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=|(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}|
.
Writing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\mathfrak{p}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}
, we have, by multiplicativity of the norm
\norm
and the (generalized) Euler totient function
\phi
(which follows from the Chinese remainder theorem),

\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\norm(\mathfrak{p})^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\penalty\ \mbox{ and }\penalty\ \phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\norm(\mathfrak{p})^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0})-1}(\norm(\mathfrak{p})-1)

The quotient
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})/\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
then equals

\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{p})}{\norm(\mathfrak{p})-1}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{1}{1-\norm(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}
.
∎

Proposition 11.2

(ERH)
.

Let
K
be a number field and let
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\prod_{\norm(\mathfrak{p})<x}\mathfrak{p}
be the product of all prime ideals with norm below
x\geq 10
.
Then there exists
c_{0}\in[-8,8]
and
c_{1}\in[0,2]
such that

\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}=\log(x)\cdot\exp\left(c_{1}+c_{0}\cdot\frac{\log|\Delta_{K}|+n\log x}{\sqrt{x}}\right).

Proof.

Write, as in Bach’s paper
[
5
, §6]
,

A(x):=\prod_{p<x}\frac{1-p^{-1}}{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}|p\\
\norm(\mathfrak{p})<x\end{subarray}}(1-\norm(\mathfrak{p})^{-1})},

(98)

for which we have the bound
12
12

12

In his theorem, Bach writes
\frac{\zeta_{K}}{\zeta}(1)
for the residue
\rho_{K}
at
s=1
of the Dedekind zeta function
\zeta_{K}

[
5
, Thm. 6.2]
.

[
5
, Thm. 6.2 & Table 2]

|\log(\rho_{K})-\log A(x)|\leq 8\cdot\left(\frac{\log|\Delta|+n\log x}{\sqrt{x}}\right),

(99)

assuming the Riemann Hypothesis for the Dedekind zeta function of
K
.

By
Lemma

11.1
and
Equation

98
, we have

\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{1}{1-\norm(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}=\prod_{p<x}\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}}\cdot A(x)

(100)

By taking logarithms of
Equation

100
and subsequently applying Bach’s bound (
Equation

99
), we obtain

\displaystyle\log\big(\tfrac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\big)-\log(\rho_{K})=

\displaystyle\log(\prod_{p<x}\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}})+\log A(x)-\log(\rho_{K})

(101)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log(\prod_{p<x}\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}})+c_{0}\cdot\left(\frac{\log|\Delta|+n\log x}{\sqrt{x}}\right),

(102)

for some
c_{0}\in[-8,8]
, by
Equation

99
. Apply
Lemma

11.3
for the left-hand side of
Equation

102
, to obtain
\prod_{p<x}\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}}\in[e^{0},e^{2}]\cdot\log x
. Exponentiating the expression yields the final result. ∎

Lemma 11.3
.

For
x\geq 2
, we have

\log x\leq\prod_{p<x}\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}}\leq 6\cdot\log x,

where the product ranges over all prime numbers up to
x
.

Proof.

By the Euler product formula, we certainly have

\prod_{p<x}\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}}\geq\sum_{n<x}\frac{1}{n}\geq\log x.

For the upper bound, we invoke an explicit version of Mertens’ third theorem of Rosser and Schoenfeld

[
67
, Corollary 1, Equation 3.30]
,

\prod_{p<x}\frac{1}{1-p^{-1}}<\log(x)\cdot e^{\gamma}\cdot(1+\frac{1}{\log(x)^{2}}).

Here,
\gamma\leq 0.578
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant. Instantiating with
x=2
and explicitly computing
e^{\gamma}

yields a bound of
6\cdot\log(x)
.
∎

The following result is a simplified version of a result by Grenié–Molteni
[
37
, Cor. 1.4]
.

Lemma 11.4

(ERH, Grenié–Molteni)
.

Let
K
be a number field, with degree
n
, discriminant
\Delta_{K}
, and ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\prod_{\norm(\mathfrak{p})<x}\mathfrak{p}
be the product of all prime ideals with norm below
x>100
.
We have

\displaystyle\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq x+\sqrt{x}\left(\left(\frac{\log x}{2\pi}+2\right)\log|\Delta_{K}|+\left(\frac{(\log x)^{2}}{8\pi}+2\right)n\right)

\displaystyle=x+O\left(\sqrt{x}\log(x)\left(\log|\Delta_{K}|+n\log x\right)\right).

\@tocwriteb

PART II

Rigorous computation of
\except
tocclass groups and unit groups

12.
Introduction

In this second part of the article, we present the first algorithm for computing class groups and unit groups of arbitrary number fields that provably runs in probabilistic subexponential
time, assuming the Extended Riemann Hypothesis.
Let
K
be a number field of degree
n
and discriminant
\Delta_{K}
. The determination of the structure of its class group
\Cl(K)
, together with a system of fundamental units, is one of the main problems of computational number theory
[
22
, p. 217]
.
Previous subexponential algorithms were either restricted to imaginary quadratic fields, or
relied on several heuristic assumptions that have long resisted rigorous analysis.

12.1.
The Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice

While we stated our main result as an algorithm for computing units and class groups in
Theorem

1.3
, our algorithm actually does slightly more than that: it computes the so-called Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice for any finite set
{\mathbb{S}}
of prime ideals. For such a set
{\mathbb{S}}
of prime ideals of
K
, we consider the group
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathbb{Z}\times\prod_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
, where
\nu
ranges over all infinite places of
K
. The Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
is a lattice living in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
generated by all vectors of the form
(v,-\Log(\alpha))
with
\Log:K^{\times}\rightarrow\prod_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
the logarithmic embedding of
K
, where
\alpha\in K
and
v=(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
are such that
\alpha\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}
. Such elements
\alpha
are called
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, hence the name of the lattice. See
Section

15
for more details.
The main result of this part is the following theorem.

Theorem 12.1

(ERH)
.

There is a probabilistic algorithm which, on input a number field
K
of degree
n
and discriminant
\Delta_{K}
,
an LLL-reduced basis of its ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}

and a finite set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
, computes the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice and runs in expected time polynomial in the length of the input, in
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2)
, in
L_{n^{n}}(2/3)
, and in
\min(\rho_{K},L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(2/3+o(1)))
, where
\rho_{K}
is the residue at
1
of the Dedekind zeta function
\zeta_{K}
.

The Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice computed by the algorithm is represented exactly by elements
\alpha_{i}\in K
and vectors
v_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
such that the vectors
((v_{i},-\Log\alpha_{i}))_{i}
form a basis of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. These elements
\alpha_{i}\in K
are represented by a so-called compact representation, i.e., as a product of smaller elements
\beta_{j}\in K
(see
Section

2.6
). Even though this is called a compact representation (and it is indeed more compact than representing the
\alpha_{i}
in a basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
), the bit-size of this representation of the
\alpha_{i}
might be about as large as the running time of the algorithm.

12.2.
Previous work

Shanks proposed in 1968 the first algorithm to compute the structure of the class group. His method, described for quadratic number fields, had an exponential running time
O_{\varepsilon}(|\Delta_{K}|^{1/4+\varepsilon})
(or
O_{\varepsilon}(|\Delta_{K}|^{1/5+\varepsilon})
assuming ERH). In 1989, Hafner and McCurley
[
38
]
proposed the first subexponential time probabilistic algorithm to compute the class group of imaginary quadratic number fields. Assuming ERH, they prove that the expected running time is
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2,\sqrt{2})
. The case of imaginary quadratic number fields distinguishes itself by the finiteness of the unit group, and the existence of a
reduced
ideal in each class, two properties that proved helpful for a rigorous analysis of the algorithm.

In 1990, Buchmann
[
17
]
generalized this algorithm to fields of arbitrary degree. The analysis proved much more delicate, requiring several heuristic assumptions, notably on the distribution of smooth ideals. From these assumptions, Buchmann argued that the expected running time is
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2,1.7)
when the degree of the field is constant. Practical improvements ensued, notably by Cohen, Diaz Y Diaz and Olivier
[
25
]
. In 2014, Biasse and Fieker
[
10
]
proposed an algorithm capable of dealing with fields of varying degree: under heuristic assumptions, they argue that the algorithm computes class groups in subexponential time
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(2/3+\varepsilon)
for arbitrary families of number fields, even with growing degree (and
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2)
whenever
n\leq(\log|\Delta_{K}|)^{3/4-\varepsilon}
, matching Buchmann’s claimed complexity for fixed-degree fields). In the meantime, no progress was made towards a rigorous analysis.

On the quantum computing side, a non-heuristic (assuming ERH) quantum polynomial time algorithm for computing
{\mathbb{S}}
-units and class groups was developed in 2016 by Biasse and Song
[
11
]
, for any number field. The structure of this algorithm is different from the classical algorithms mentioned above: it relies on a quantum polynomial time algorithm solving the Continuous Hidden Subgroup Problem over
\mathbb{R}^{m}

[
30
,
13
]
.
In the present article, we only consider classical algorithms.

12.3.
The blueprint of a ‘classical’ Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice algorithm

For the sake of simplicity we limit ourselves in this
overview to the computation of the class group of a number field.
The situation for the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice is, at this high level,
very similar.

Classical algorithms computing the class group proceed by
generating many class group relations of the shape
[\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}]=1
,
where the prime factors
\mathfrak{p}
in the ideal
\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}
have norm bounded by
B
, i.e., the product is a
B
-smooth ideal. Since there are only
finitely many generators
\mathfrak{p}
, enough ‘sufficiently independent’ relations
of this shape then yield a description of the (finite) class group.

Such a relation
[\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}]=1
is generally found by
taking a random smooth ideal
{\mathfrak{s}}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{b_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, and repeatedly
sampling random elements
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{s}}
until
(\alpha){\mathfrak{s}}^{-1}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{c_{\mathfrak{p}}}
is

also
a smooth ideal. This gives the relation
1=[\alpha]=[\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}]
with
a_{\mathfrak{p}}=b_{\mathfrak{p}}+c_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Heuristics in previous work.

The usual heuristics for class group algorithms (and Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice algorithms) are twofold.
The first kind of heuristic assumption concerns a lower bound on the probability of the random ideal
(\alpha){\mathfrak{s}}^{-1}
to be smooth, see for instance
[
9
, Heuristic 1, 2]
.
This allows one to bound the expected running time of sampling a single relation of the shape
[\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}]=1
.
This smoothness probability is typically assumed to match the
density
of smooth ideals in the set of all ideals, a somewhat well-understood quantity related to the Dickman function.

The second kind of heuristic assumption concerns the number of such relations required in order to get the class group (rather than a larger group, of which the class group would be a quotient), see for instance
[
9
, Heuristic 3]
. The heuristic running time is then obtained as the product of the running time per relation and the required number of relations.

12.4.
Why our algorithm is rigorous

We would like to stress that the following overview of our proof makes substantial simplifications for expository purposes (in particular, we ignore complex embeddings, which in the full proof play a similar role to prime ideals).

To circumvent the first heuristic about the cost of finding one relation, we use a special
sampling algorithm

(
Algorithm

2
) from
Section

3
of this paper.
This algorithm samples an element
\alpha\in\mathfrak{a}
in such a way that the success probability of
(\alpha)\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
being smooth is
actually provably lower bounded
by the density of smooth ideals in the set of all ideals (up to a multiplicative constant). This yields an upper bound on the expected cost of finding one class group relation. The sampling algorithm is the object of

Section

3
; this algorithm, together with its properties is used here as a black-box.

The remaining challenge addressed in the current part is circumventing the
second heuristic
, concerning
the number of sampled group relations
[\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}]=1
required to
compute the class group (rather than an extension). To achieve that goal, we investigate the lattice of class group relations, which consists of those vectors
(a_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}}
for which
[\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}]=1
.

The class group lattice.

Denoting
{\mathbb{S}}
for the set of prime ideals with norm bounded by
B
,
we can construct the class group lattice

L_{\Cl}=\{(a_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{S}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ [\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}]=1\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
. In this paper we will construct (here treated quite informally) a randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}

which has the following two properties.

(i)

For
{u}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}({z})
, we have
{u}+{z}\in L_{\Cl}
and
\|{v}\|<E
for some bound
E
.

(ii)

The distribution of
\mathcal{A}({z})
only depends on the coset

{z}+L_{\Cl}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}({z^{\prime}})
and
\mathcal{A}({z})
have the same distribution for any
{z}^{\prime}\in{z}+L_{\Cl}
.

This algorithm
\mathcal{A}
uses the sampling algorithm from
Section

3
mentioned above. We will use algorithm
\mathcal{A}
to sample vectors of
L_{\Cl}
in such a way that we can prove that they will generate the full lattice with sufficiently good probability.

Gaussians over the class group lattice.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{X,s}
denote the discrete Gaussian distribution with parameter
s
over the discrete set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
.
We will make use of the following observation: when the deviation
s
is large enough, sampling from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}},s}
is indistinguishable from sampling first a uniform
{c}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}/L_{\Cl}
, then sampling from
{c}+\mathcal{G}_{L_{\Cl},s}
.

For such
{z}\leftarrow{c}+\mathcal{G}_{L_{\Cl},s}
for
{c}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}/L_{\Cl}
uniformly distributed, we see by property (ii) of
\mathcal{A}
that

\mathcal{A}({z})+{z}\sim\mathcal{A}({c})+({c}+\mathcal{G}_{L_{\Cl},s})=(\mathcal{A}({c})+{c})+\mathcal{G}_{L_{\Cl},s}

By property (i) of
\mathcal{A}
, the distribution
\mathcal{A}({c})+{c}
is
bounded
, i.e., for all
{z}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}({c})+{c}
we have
\|{z}\|<E^{\prime}
with overwhelming probability for some bounded
E^{\prime}
(which depends on
E
and on the covering radius of the lattice
L_{\Cl}
). Then the variable
\mathcal{A}({z})+{z}
is distributed as a Gaussian over
L_{\Cl}
with some
independent ‘noise’
of size
E^{\prime}
caused by
\mathcal{A}({c})+{c}
. By taking the standard deviation
s
(of all discrete Gaussians involved) much larger than this ‘noise’ of size
E^{\prime}
,
one can deduce that, for

{z}\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}},s}
,

\mathcal{A}({z})+{z}\approx\mathcal{G}_{L_{\Cl},s}.

Summarizing, sampling
{z}\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}},s}
with large enough
s

and computing
\mathcal{A}({z})+{z}
allows to compute
close to Gaussian samples

from the class group lattice
L_{\Cl}
. There are well-known bounds for the number of samples required to generate the entire lattice
L_{\Cl}
for such (close to) Gaussian samples.

The algorithm
\mathcal{A}
.

The algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}

satisfying the properties (i) and (ii), is defined as follows.
For
{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
, compute
\mathfrak{a}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
Sample
\alpha\in\mathfrak{a}
according to a not-too-wide discrete Gaussian and
put
\mathfrak{b}=(\alpha)\cdot\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
. Then, use the sampling algorithm
from
Section

3

to find a representative
\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\in[\mathfrak{b}]=[\mathfrak{a}]^{-1}=[\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{-{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}}]
, and output
{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
. We have that
{u}+{z}\in L_{\Cl}
and that
{u}
is
bounded (by properties of the sampling algorithm), so property (i) of algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is true.

Property (ii) follows from the fact that the sample
\mathfrak{b}=(\alpha)\cdot\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
only depends on the
class
[\mathfrak{a}]
of
\mathfrak{a}
, and not of the representation thereof
13
13

13

This is actually false when considering the class group alone. To apply this ‘independence of representation’ technique, the infinite places (i.e., complex embeddings) must be included in the logarithmic embedding. The Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice accounts for the infinite places, so this technique applies.
.
Therefore, the output
{u}
only depends on the class, i.e., the coset
{z}+L_{\Cl}
of the input
{z}
.

Technicalities involving the infinite places.

In the general case of the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice, there is also a
continuous

part involving the logarithmic unit lattice. Essentially the same proof structure applies, with additional technicalities introduced by the numerical approximation of the real numbers involved.

12.5.
More algorithmic problems.

Once we have an algorithm computing the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice for any set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
, it is well known that one can use it to obtain other quantities, such as the units of the number field, or the structure of the class group. Combined with
Theorem

17.7
, which allows to decompose any integral ideal as an equivalent product of prime ideals in a sufficiently large set
{\mathbb{S}}
, this can also be used to solve other algorithmic problems, such as the principal ideal problem, or the class group discrete logarithm problem.

Units.

To obtain the units of a number field
K
, one computes the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice with an empty set
{\mathbb{S}}
. The output elements
\alpha_{i}
of the algorithm is then a fundamental system of units of the number field. Note that these elements
\alpha_{i}
, even when they are represented in compact representation (see
Section

2.6
), might have a large bit-size (as large as the running time of the algorithm). Note also that computing a fundamental system of units of
K
only describes the units modulo the roots of unity of
K
. Computing the roots of unity of
K
can be done efficiently using, e.g.,
[
22
, Section 4.9.4]
, and is not related to the algorithm we describe here.

Class group.

In order to obtain the structure of the class group, one chooses a set
{\mathbb{S}}
generating the class group. It is known that under the extended Riemann hypothesis, there are such sets
{\mathbb{S}}
of size polynomial in
\log|\Delta_{K}|

[
4
]
. Given a basis
(\alpha_{i},v_{i})
of the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice, one then only keeps the rank
|{\mathbb{S}}|
lattice
L
generated by the vectors
v_{i}
. The class group of
K
is then isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}/L
. Note that
L
is an integral lattice whose determinant is the class-number
h_{k}
of
K
. Hence, the HNF basis of
L
has bit-size polynomial in
|{\mathbb{S}}|
and
\log h_{k}
, which is polynomial in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
. This means that the output size of the algorithm computing the class group is polynomially bounded in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
.

Class group discrete logarithm.

The class group discrete logarithm problem asks, given as input any integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}
and a set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
, to compute
\alpha\in K
and
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
such that
\mathfrak{a}=\alpha\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{-v_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
Solving the class group discrete logarithm problem is exactly what is done by
Algorithm

3
, provided that the set
{\mathbb{S}}
contains sufficiently many ideals of small norm.
In order to solve the class group discrete logarithm problem for smaller sets
{\mathbb{S}}
, one can set
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
to be the minimal set containing
{\mathbb{S}}
and sufficiently many small prime ideals so that
Theorem

17.7
applies. One can then solve the class group discrete logarithm problem in
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
and compute the log-
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
-unit lattice. Solving the class group discrete logarithm in the smaller set
{\mathbb{S}}
can then be performed efficiently by linear algebra, using the knowledge of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
-unit lattice to replace primes of
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathbb{S}}
by a product of primes of
{\mathbb{S}}
.

Principal ideal problem.

The principal ideal problem is the problem of computing a generator
\alpha
of a principal integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}
(represented by an HNF basis). Again, this can be solved by choosing a set
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
containing sufficiently many ideals of small norm and solving the class group discrete logarithm problem with respect to
\mathfrak{a}
and this set
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
using
Algorithm

3
. Then, computing the log-
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
-unit lattice allows one to solve the principal ideal problem by linear algebra. The size of the computed generator
\alpha
, even in compact representation (see
Section

2.6
), might be as large as the running time of the algorithm.

12.6.
Road map

We start in
Section

13
by specializing the ideal sampling theorem of Part
3
to our needs. Then, a lower bound is given on the density of smooth ideals, by means of a combinatorial technique, in
Section

14
.
After that, some preliminaries on
{\mathbb{S}}
-units are treated in
Section

15
, and useful properties of
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattices are analyzed in
Section

16
.

Applying both the specialized sampling theorem and the density of the smooth ideals, we obtain an algorithm that samples a
single

{\mathbb{S}}
-unit in
Section

17
.

In order to have an algorithm that outputs sufficiently ‘random’
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, as to generate the entire
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit group, we combine two results. One is a bound on the ‘generating radius’
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice that is polynomial in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
, which is proven in
Section

16
. This is combined with the properties of the sampling algorithm (see
Section

10
in Part
3
), to build an algorithm that indeed outputs ‘sufficiently random’
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, as proved in
Section

18
.

After gathering many such
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, we would like to compute a set of
fundamental
{\mathbb{S}}
-units
, which can be seen as the multiplicative analogue of a basis of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice. This is done in
Section

19
by ‘post-processing’ the many
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, which essentially consists in applying the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm.

Part
11
is then concluded in
Section

21
with the final theorem on the complexity of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit algorithm.

13.
Specialized ideal sampling algorithm

In this section, we
specialize the main result
from
Section

3

to
G=\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

Definition 13.1
.

We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{B}
the set of
B
-smooth ideals, i.e.,

\mathcal{S}_{B}=\{\mathfrak{a}\mbox{ integral ideal of }\mathcal{O}_{K}\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ \mbox{ for any prime factor }\mathfrak{p}\text{ of }\mathfrak{a}\mbox{ we have }\norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B\}

We also extend the notion of smoothness to sets
{\mathbb{S}}
of prime ideals: we say that
{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
is
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth if all its prime factors belong to
{\mathbb{S}}
:
\mathcal{S}_{{\mathbb{S}}}
is the set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth ideals.
Recall that the local density of a set of ideals
\mathcal{S}
is defined (
Definition

2.11
) as

\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[x]=\min_{t\in[x/e^{n},x]}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{S}(t)\rvert}{\rho_{K}\cdot t}=\min_{t\in[x/e^{n},x]}\frac{|\{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq t\}|}{\rho_{K}\cdot t},

where,
\rho_{K}=\lim_{s\rightarrow 1}(s-1)\zeta_{K}(s)
(see
Equation

4
).

Theorem

13.2
below is a specialization of
Theorem

9.5
from
Section

3
, together with the properties proved in
Section

10
. We will only use this specialization in this part.

Theorem 13.2

(ERH)
.

There is a randomized algorithm
Sample
such that the following holds.
Let
K
be a number field, with degree
n
, discriminant
\Delta_{K}
, and ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
of which an LLL-reduced basis is given.
Let
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
be an ideal modulus of which the prime ideal factorization is known,
let
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\ideals

be an ideal coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, and let
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
be represented by rational numbers.
Let
\mathsf{b}\geq 2
be an integer, let
\omega\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 1}
, and let
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

Given the above data, the algorithm
\textup{{Sample}}({\mathfrak{a}},y,\mathfrak{m}_{0},\mathsf{b},\omega)
outputs
\beta\in\mathfrak{a}
coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
such that for any set
\mathcal{S}
of ideals in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, we have

(\beta)\cdot{\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B_{\mathrm{rw}}}

with probability at least

\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}{6},

for some smoothness bound

B_{\mathrm{rw}}=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0}))
,
and where
\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=|(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}|
.
Furthermore, the algorithm
Sample
runs in time

\poly(\allowbreak\size({\mathfrak{a}}),\allowbreak\size(y),\allowbreak\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})),\allowbreak\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}},\allowbreak\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size(\omega)).

Additionally, the output distribution
\mathcal{D}_{y,\mathfrak{a}}
of
Sample
on input
(y,\mathfrak{a})\in(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times},\ideals)
(with
\mathfrak{m}_{0},\mathsf{b}
and
\omega
implicit) satisfies the following properties.

(1)

(Almost Lipschitz-continuous). For all
y,y^{\prime}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
,

\|\mathcal{D}_{y,\mathfrak{a}}-\mathcal{D}_{y^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}}\|_{1}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{2}\|\Log(y)-\Log(y^{\prime})\|+\frac{1}{1200\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}}.

(2)

(Shifting property). For all
\beta\in K
and
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}
, we have the following identity of distributions:

\mathcal{D}_{y,\mathfrak{a}}[\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]=\mathcal{D}_{y\cdot(|\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}|)_{\sigma},\mathfrak{a}\cdot(\alpha)}[\alpha\cdot\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]

(3)

(Bounded). Writing
\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\in\Div_{K}^{0}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert
for the norm on
\Div_{K}
, we have

\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\rVert\leq\lVert d(\mathfrak{a})+\Log(y)\rVert+O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})+n\log(\omega))

(4)

(Independent of
|\norm(y)|
).
\mathcal{D}_{y,\mathfrak{a}}=\mathcal{D}_{y^{0},\mathfrak{a}}
,
where
y^{0}=y/|\norm(y)|^{1/n}
.

Remark 13.3
.

In the case where
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we get
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=1
, yielding a lower bound
\delta_{{\mathbb{S}}}[r^{n}]/6
on the success probability.

Proof.

The algorithm
Sample
is
Algorithm

2
from Part
3
instantiated with modulus
\mathfrak{m}:=\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, subgroup
G:=\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
(yielding
[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]=1
), ideal
\mathfrak{b}:=\mathfrak{a}
,
y:=y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, block size
\mathsf{b}:=\mathsf{b}
and element
\tau\in(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}
uniformly random
14
14

14

Uniform sampling of
\tau\in(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}
can be done in time
\poly(\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\log|\Delta_{K}|)
by sampling uniformly in
(\mathcal{O}_{K}/{\mathfrak{q}})^{\times}
for all prime power divisors
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and using the Chinese Remainder theorem. This uses that the factorization of
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
is assumed to be known.
if
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\neq\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
\tau=1
if
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
(note that
\tau K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=K^{\mathfrak{m},1}=K^{*}
if
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
).
We put the error parameter
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{1200\cdot|\Delta_{K}|r^{n}}\leq\frac{1}{6\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}}\leq\frac{1}{6\cdot\rho_{K}\cdot r^{n}}
(see
Equation

5
). We will use
Theorem

9.5
.

Note that we may assume that
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]=\min_{t\in[r^{n}/e^{n},r^{n}]}\frac{|\{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq t\}|}{\rho_{K}\cdot t}>0
, since otherwise the statement of the theorem is inherently true, as any probability is at least zero. Hence, the set
\{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq r^{n}/e^{n}\}
is nonempty, and we can immediately deduce

\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]=\min_{t\in[r^{n}/e^{n},r^{n}]}\frac{|\{\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{b})\leq t\}|}{\rho_{K}\cdot t}\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{K}\cdot r^{n}}\geq 6\cdot\varepsilon.

(103)

By
Theorem

9.5
, the success probability of
Algorithm

2
with these parameters is at least

\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}{3}-\varepsilon\geq\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}{3}-\frac{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}{6}\geq\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]}{6}.

where the first inequality holds by
Equation

103
and the second one by the fact that
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\geq\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
. This proves the lower bound on the success probability of algorithm
\mathcal{A}
in the theorem.

It remains to show the bound on
B_{\mathrm{rw}}
and the running time. Note that
[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]=1
,

\size(\tau)=1
, and that

\log(1/\varepsilon)=O(\log(|\Delta_{K}|r^{n}))=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})+\log\omega)

by direct computation.
Hence

B_{\mathrm{rw}}=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\allowbreak\log\omega)

and we have a running time of

T=\poly(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}},\allowbreak\size({\mathfrak{a}}),\allowbreak\size(y),\allowbreak\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})),\allowbreak\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size(\omega)),

with no queries to any oracle, since
G=\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

The properties (
1
)-(
3
), directly follow from the almost-Lipschitz continuity (
Lemma

10.6
), the shifting property (
Lemma

10.3
) and the boundedness property (
Lemma

10.5
) of
Algorithm

2
, with the following extra remarks. For the almost-Lipschitz continuity,
we use that
\|\Log(y^{0})-\Log(z^{0})\|\leq\|\Log(y)-\Log(z)\|
for
y^{0}=\frac{y}{|\norm(y)|^{1/n}}
and
z^{0}=\frac{z}{|\norm(z)|^{1/n}}\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
. For the Lipschitz error,
we just substitute
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{1200\cdot|\Delta_{K}|r^{n}}
. Here,
\Log(y),\Log(z)\in\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times})\hookrightarrow\Div_{K}
inherit their ‘natural’ norm from the norm on
\Div_{K}
(see
Section

2.8
).

For the boundedness property we use that

\log(B^{N}r^{n})=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})+n\log(\omega))

and
s=1/n^{2}
for the parameters used in
Algorithm

2
. Additionally, with the instantiation
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{1200|\Delta_{K}|r^{n}}
we have

s\sqrt{n\log(8n^{2}/\varepsilon)}\leq O(\log|\Delta_{K}|+\log(r))\leq O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})+\log\omega).

With these simplifications,
Lemma

10.5
tells us that
\lVert\llparenthesis\beta\rrparenthesis\rVert\leq\lVert d^{0}(\mathfrak{a})+\Log(y)\rVert+O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})+n\log(\omega))
. In order to obtain the desired bound (i.e., with
d(\mathfrak{a})
instead of
d^{0}(\mathfrak{a})
), we use property (
4
) and we replace the input
(\mathfrak{a},y)
by
(\mathfrak{b},z):=(\mathfrak{a},y\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{a})^{1/n})
, so that
d^{0}(\mathfrak{b})+\Log(z)=d(\mathfrak{a})+\Log(y)
.

For the shifting property, we use the fact that
d(\mathbf{a})+\Log(y)+\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis=d({\mathfrak{a}}\cdot(\alpha))+\Log(y\cdot(|\sigma(\alpha)|^{-1})_{\sigma})\in\Div_{K}
.
For the last property, item (
4
), note that
Sample
does not depend on
|\norm(y)|
, as follows from
Lemma

9.4
.
∎

Remark 13.4
.

In the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithm in this paper, we assume that we are given an LLL-reduced basis of the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. In other words, we take such a basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
as part of the input. We refer the reader to the discussion in
Section

2.6
.

Remark 13.5
.

Looking into the details of
Section

3
,
the part
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
in the running time of
Theorem

9.5
is caused by a
\mathsf{b}
-BKZ reduction on an ideal lattice
\mathfrak{a}
(see
Section

9
).
One may wonder whether, in the context of this section, using an ideal-SVP algorithm specifically designed for ideals (e.g.,
[
29
,
63
]
), instead of the BKZ algorithm, might lead to better results.
This is unfortunately not the case at the moment, since all current algorithms
exploiting the special structure of ideal lattices are heuristic. Additionally, they all use an algorithm computing the class group and the unit group of
K
as a building block, which would be a circular requirement here.

Remark 13.6
.

Theorem

13.2
can also be made to work for arbitrary
\tau\in K^{\mathfrak{m}}
(with an additional
\size(\tau)
in the running time). Even a random
\tau
is possible (which might be useful in certain use-cases), though then the factorization of
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
must be known. Indeed, using this factorization, uniform sampling of
\tau\in(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}
can be done in time
\poly(\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0}),\log|\Delta_{K}|)
by sampling uniformly in
(\mathcal{O}_{K}/{\mathfrak{q}})^{\times}
for all prime power divisors
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and using the Chinese Remainder theorem.

14.
Density of smooth ideals

From the early work of Hafner and McCurley
[
38
]
for imaginary quadratic fields, to the generalizations for larger degree fields
[
17
,
10
]
, the core of the strategy to compute class groups as been to find random relations between classes involving smooth ideals.
The probability of a somewhat random ideal to be smooth thus plays a key role in the analysis of such algorithms.

For any
0<B\leq x
, let
\Psi_{K}(x,B)
be the number of
B
-smooth integral ideals of
K
of norm at most
x
.
It is well known
[
75
]
that whenever
(\log\log x)^{5/3+\varepsilon}\leq\log B\leq\log x
, we have

\Psi_{K}(x,B)=x\cdot\rho_{K}\cdot\rho(u)\left(1+O_{\varepsilon,K}\left(\frac{\log(u+1)}{\log B}\right)\right),

(104)

where
\rho
is the Dickman function, and
u=\frac{\log x}{\log B}
.
One then needs to estimate the dependence in
K
hidden in the big O error.
Unfortunately, scrutinizing the proof of
[
75
]
, and using the best known uniform estimates of the ideal counting function
[
73
]
, one finds that the hidden constant is exponential in the degree
n
of
K
. This means that for the estimate of
Equation

104
to be non-vacuous, one needs the smoothness bound
B
to be doubly exponential in
n
.

It is not clear at the time whether this issue is unavoidable (i.e., the estimate
\Psi_{K}(x,B)\sim x\cdot\rho_{K}\cdot\rho(u)
is truly only valid when
B
is large compared to the degree), or is an artifact of the techniques not being precise enough. Therefore, instead of this estimate, we rely on the following weaker but simpler combinatorial bound.

Lemma 14.1

(ERH)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C
such that the following holds.
Let
B\geq B_{\mathrm{sm}}:=C(n+\log|\Delta_{K}|)^{2+\varepsilon}
, and let
A\leq B/(4\log B)
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
be the set of integral ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
whose prime divisors have norm in
(A,B]
. Let
x\geq B\cdot e^{n}
, and
u=\frac{\log x}{\log B}
. Then the density at
x
of
\mathcal{S}_{A,B}
satisfies

\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{A,B}}[x]\geq\frac{(4\log B)^{1-u}}{\rho_{K}B}u^{-u}.

Proof.

Let
y\in[x/e^{n},x]
. Note that, by the assumption
x\geq B\cdot e^{n}
, we have
y\geq B
.
Let
(\mathfrak{p}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
be the list of all prime ideals in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
of norm in
(A,B]
. We have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}_{A,B}(y)|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{e_{i}}\middle|\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}\log(\norm(\mathfrak{p}_{i}))\leq\log y\right\}\right|\geq\left|\left\{(e_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}\middle|\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}\leq\frac{\log y}{\log B}\right\}\right|.

Writing
u_{y}=\frac{\log y}{\log B}
and
v=\lfloor u_{y}\rfloor\geq 1
(since
y\geq B
), we deduce

|\mathcal{S}_{A,B}(y)|\geq\binom{v+N-1}{v}\geq\left(\frac{v+N-1}{v}\right)^{v}\geq N^{v}v^{-v}\geq N^{v}u_{y}^{-u_{y}}.

There exists
D>0
depending only on
\varepsilon
such that for any
X>D
, we have
X^{1/(2+\varepsilon/2)}<X^{1/2}/(\log X)^{2}
.
Then, by a result of Lagarias and Odlyzko
[
47
]
, there is a constant
15
15

15

The original statement
[
47
]
(putting
L=K
and
G=\{1\}
) reads
|\pi_{K}(X)-\mbox{Li}(X)|=O\left(X^{1/2}\log(|\Delta_{K}|X^{n})+\log|\Delta_{K}|\right)
, where the constants in the big-
O
are absolute, and
Li
is the logarithmic integral.
E
(absolute) such that for any
X\geq\max(D,E(n+\log|\Delta_{K}|)^{2+\varepsilon/2})=:F
, we have
\frac{X}{2\log X}\leq\pi_{K}(X)\leq X
. Let
A^{\prime}=\max(A,F)
.
Recall that
A\leq B/(4\log B)
. If furthermore
F\leq B/(4\log B)
,
then,
A^{\prime}\leq B/(4\log B)
, and we get

N=\pi_{K}(B)-\pi_{K}(A)\geq\frac{B}{2\log B}-\pi_{K}(A^{\prime})\geq\frac{B}{2\log B}-A^{\prime}\geq\frac{B}{4\log B}.

We now show that we can choose the constant
C
such that indeed
F\leq B/(4\log B)
(hence the conditions of the lemma do imply
N\geq\frac{B}{4\log B}
). There exists
G>0
depending only on
\varepsilon
such that for any
X>G
, we have
X^{\frac{2+\varepsilon/2}{2+\varepsilon}}<X/(4\log X)
.
Therefore, for any
\delta>0
and
B\geq\max(G,F^{\delta})
, we have

B/(4\log B)\geq B^{\frac{2+\varepsilon/2}{2+\varepsilon}}\geq F^{\delta\frac{2+\varepsilon/2}{2+\varepsilon}}.

Choosing
\delta=\frac{2+\varepsilon}{2+\varepsilon/2}
, we get that
B/(4\log B)\geq F
(as desired).
Now, we have
F^{\delta}=\max(D^{\delta},E^{\delta}(n+\log|\Delta_{K}|)^{2+\varepsilon})
. One can therefore choose the constant
C=\max(G,D^{\delta},E^{\delta})
,
to enforce
B/(4\log B)\leq F
.

We have just proved that
N\geq\frac{B}{4\log B}
. Then, we have

N^{v}\geq N^{u_{y}-1}\geq(y/B)(4\log B)^{1-u_{y}}.

We obtain

|\mathcal{S}_{A,B}(y)|\geq(y/B)(4\log B)^{1-u_{y}}u_{y}^{-u_{y}},

hence, writing
u=u_{x}
, we have

\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{A,B}(y)|}{\rho_{K}\cdot y}\geq\frac{(4\log B)^{1-u_{y}}}{\rho_{K}B}u_{y}^{-u_{y}}\geq\frac{(4\log B)^{1-u}}{\rho_{K}B}u^{-u}.

∎

15.
Background on
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattices

15.1.
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

In this section, we formally define
{\mathbb{S}}
-units and the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice, and recall some of its properties.

Definition 15.1

(
{\mathbb{S}}
-units)
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
be a set of prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
An element
\alpha\in K
is an
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit if and only if
\alpha\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}
for some
(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
. We write
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}\subset K
for the set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units.

We will use the language of divisors introduced in
Section

2.8
.

Definition 15.2

(
{\mathbb{S}}
-Divisor group)
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
be a (finite) set of prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. We define the
{\mathbb{S}}
-divisor group
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}\subseteq\Div_{K}

\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}:=\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathbb{Z}\times\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R},

where
\nu
ranges over the set of all infinite places (embeddings into the complex numbers up to possible conjugation).
Additionally, we define the degree-zero
{\mathbb{S}}
-divisor group
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}=\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}\cap\Div_{K}^{0}
.

In the present part of the article,
we often write elements of
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
as a vector
((a_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}},(a_{\nu})_{\nu})
to emphasize that it has finite dimension and that these elements are computationally represented as lists. We have the inclusion
H\subseteq\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times})\subseteq\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
.
This degree-zero
{\mathbb{S}}
-divisor group serves as an ambient space for the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice,
which is defined as follows.

Definition 15.3

(Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice)
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
be a set of prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. The map
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}
is defined over
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
by

\displaystyle\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}:\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\longmapsto((-v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}},\Log(\alpha))=-\llparenthesis\alpha\rrparenthesis.

where the
v_{\mathfrak{p}}
are such that
\alpha\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, and
where
\Log(\alpha)=(n_{\nu}\log|\sigma_{\nu}(\alpha)|)_{\nu}\in\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
, with
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise, see
Section

2.7
.

The Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice is defined as

\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})=\{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)\,|\,\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}\}\subset\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}.

Remark 15.4
.

Note that the image of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}
on
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
is indeed in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
, since this map
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}

is just the
negative
of the ‘principal divisor’ map
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis:K\rightarrow\Div_{K}
restricted to
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
, and
principal divisors have degree zero (see
Section

5.1
).

The Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice is a lattice in the real vector space

\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R}):=\Big\{((x_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}},(x_{\nu})_{\nu})\in\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathbb{R}\times\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ \sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}x_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\log\norm(\mathfrak{p})+\sum_{\nu}x_{\nu}=0\Big\}.

(105)

Here the sum formula in above definition is a generalization of the degree function
to real
x_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathbb{R}
. This lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
has
rank
\mathbbm{r}+|{\mathbb{S}}|
where
\mathbbm{r}={n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1
, i.e., it is full rank in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
.

Note that
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\subseteq\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}\subseteq\Div_{K}
inherits a Euclidean length notion via that of
\Div_{K}
(see
Section

2.8
).
When
{\mathbb{S}}=\emptyset
, the lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
coincides with the Log-unit lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
.

Lemma 15.5
.

If
{\mathbb{S}}
is a set of prime ideals generating the class group, then it holds that

16
16

16

We understand
\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
as the volume of the quotient
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))=h_{K}\cdot\covol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))=h_{K}\cdot R_{K}\cdot\sqrt{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}
, where
h_{K}
is the class number of
K
,
R_{K}
is the regulator,
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}
is the number of real embedding and
{n_{\mathbb{C}}}
is the number of complex pairs of embeddings. In particular,
\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
does not depend on the choice of
{\mathbb{S}}
(as long as it generates the class group). Moreover, we have

\log(\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})))\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|.

Proof.

The lemma follows from
Lemma

5.1
and the observation that the group
\Pic_{K}^{0}
in
[
14
]
is isomorphic to
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, whenever
{\mathbb{S}}
generates the class group.
∎

A lower bound on the first minimum of the lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
can be derived from Kessler’s lower bound on the first minimum of the Log-unit lattice (
Lemma

2.14
).

Lemma 15.6
.

For any set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
, it holds that
\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))\geq\frac{1}{1000\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\log(n)^{3}}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
be such that
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)
reaches the first minimum of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, i.e.,
\|\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)\|=\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
. Let
v_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\alpha\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}
(we know that they exist since
\alpha
is an
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit).
Assume first that one of the
v_{\mathfrak{p}}
is non-zero (say
v_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}
), then
\|\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)\|\geq|v_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}|\geq 1
since
v_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}
is an integer. This gives us
\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))\geq 1\geq\frac{1}{1000\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\log(n)^{3}}
as desired.

Suppose now that all
v_{\mathfrak{p}}
’s are zero. Then
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
is a unit and
\|\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)\|=\|\Log(\alpha)\|
, and the bound follows from
Lemma

2.14
.
∎

Representation of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice.

In the present part of this article,
we will compute a generating set of the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice by a collection of pairs
((v^{(j)}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}},\alpha^{(j)})\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}}\times K
, which generate
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. How this is done is explained more precisely in
Section

18
. Up to and including that section, the elements
\alpha^{(j)}
in these pairs are represented by their coordinates with respect to the basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
(as explained in
Section

2.6
).

In the sections after that,
Section

19
and
Section

21
, instead of a generating set, a
basis
of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
is computed. This will again lead to a collection of pairs
((w_{\mathfrak{p}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}},\beta)\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
but this time the
\beta\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
are written in a so-called
compact representation
, see
Section

2.6
. Indeed, the elements
\beta
will be of the form
\beta=\prod_{j}(\alpha^{(j)})^{N_{j}}
, where
\alpha^{(j)}\in K
come from the generating set, and
N_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
. The product is
not
evaluated but rather stored as
(\alpha^{(j)},N_{j})_{j}
. Even in this compact representation, the bit size of the final
N_{j}
can be as large as the running time of the entire algorithm. For more details, see
Section

2.6
and
Section

21
.

16.
The generating and covering radius of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice

In this section, we give an upper bound on the generating radius
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, which is related to the covering radius of the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice (see
Definition

2.2
and
Lemma

2.3
). Recall that
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
is the smallest real number
r>0
such that
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
can be generated by vectors of Euclidean norm
\leq r
. Having an upper bound on this quantity
r
will be useful in
Section

18
. Indeed, we will first see in
Section

17
how to compute a single vector in
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, and we will then want, in
Section

18
, to compute a generating set of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. In order to do so, we will need to know how short we can expect the vectors of this generating set to be, which is exactly the generating radius
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
.

The first part of this section (until and including
Lemma

16.8
) is devoted to computing an upper bound on the covering radius
17
17

17

This covering radius makes sense when seeing
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
as a lattice in the real vector space
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathbb{R})
, see
Equation

105
.

\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
of the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
-unit lattice for a specific set
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
(of relatively small cardinality). This upper bound is obtained due to the random walk result from
[
14
, Theorem 3.3]
. When rephrased with our formalism, and with a bit of work, the random walk theorem from
[
14
]
states that there exists a distribution
\mathcal{W}^{T}
outputting somewhat short vectors of
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{0}
(the ambient space of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
) and such that
\mathcal{W}^{T}\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
is close to uniform in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{0}\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
. The shortness bound on the output vectors of
\mathcal{W}^{T}
is proven in
Lemma

16.5
, and the close to uniformity modulo
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
is obtained by combining
Theorem

16.2
and
Lemma

16.4
.
Intuitively, this means that any vector from
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{0}\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
is somewhat close to a vector in the support of the distribution
\mathcal{W}^{T}
, which are all somewhat short. Hence, the covering radius of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
is somewhat small (see
Lemma

16.8
). In order to formalize this intuition, and because our distribution
\mathcal{W}^{T}
is not perfectly uniform but only statistically close to uniform, we need to show that the volume of a small ball in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{0}\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
is not too small (it could happen that the ball folded modulo
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
becomes much smaller than the original unfolded ball). This is what is done in
Lemma

16.6
.

Once we have an upper bound on
\cov(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))
, we also have an upper bound on
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))
by
Lemma

2.3
. We then need to extend this to larger sets
{\mathbb{S}}\supseteq{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
, which is done in
Lemma

16.9
. We could also extend directly the bound on
\cov(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
to larger sets
{\mathbb{S}}
, but this would grow as
\sqrt{|{\mathbb{S}}|}
, whereas our sharper bound on the generating radius
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
only grows as
\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})
. Using the larger bound on
\cov(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
would have no impact on the asymptotic complexity of our algorithm, but we prefer to keep the intermediate results as tight as possible, to facilitate reusability in other works.
Overall, our upper bound on
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, which is the main result of this section, is stated in
Proposition

16.10
.

We start by rephrasing the random walk theorem from
[
14
, Theorem 3.3]
(see also
Definition

6.1
) in
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit terminology.
As already mentioned in the preliminaries, the quotient group
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
is isomorphic to the Arakelov class group
\Pic_{K}^{0}
,
provided that
{\mathbb{S}}
generates the class group.

Definition 16.1

(Random walk distribution, rephrased from
[
14
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
be a set of prime ideals of
K
,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
s>0
. The distribution
\mathcal{W}({\mathbb{S}},N,s)
is a probability distribution over
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}

obtained by the following procedure.

(1)

Set
y=0\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
. The first
|{\mathbb{S}}|
coordinates of
y
are denoted by
y_{\mathfrak{p}}
(with
\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}})
and the last
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}
coordinates of
y
are denoted by
y_{\nu}
(with
\nu
the places associated with the (pairs of) embeddings
\sigma:K\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
).

(2)

Sample
x\in H
from a continuous centered Gaussian distribution with standard deviation
s
, where
H=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))

(i.e.,
H=\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0})=\{(x_{\nu})_{\nu}\in\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \sum_{\nu}x_{\nu}=0\}
).
Set
y_{\nu}=x_{\nu}
for all places
\nu
.

(3)

For j from
1
to
N
, sample
{\mathfrak{p}}
uniformly at random in
{\mathbb{S}}
and update
y_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=y_{{\mathfrak{p}}}+1
and
y_{\nu}=y_{\nu}-\frac{n_{\nu}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})}{n}
for all places
\nu
. Here
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise.

(4)

Return
y

Observe that the distribution
\mathcal{W}({\mathbb{S}},N,s)
produces vectors in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
,
(i.e., vectors
y\in\bigoplus_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathbb{Z}\times\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
such that
\sum_{\nu}y_{\nu}+\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}y_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cdot\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})=0
.

Theorem 16.2

(
[
14
, Theorem 3.3]
, ERH)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
s>0
be positive real numbers and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
Let
s^{\prime}=\min(\sqrt{2}\cdot s,1/\eta_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})^{\vee}))
, where
\eta_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})^{\vee})
is the smoothing parameter of the dual lattice of the log-unit lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
.

Then there exists a bound

B=\widetilde{O}(n^{2k}[n^{2}(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))^{2}+n^{2}(\log(1/s^{\prime}))^{2}+(\log|\Delta_{K}|)^{2}])

such that for any

N\geq\frac{\frac{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1}{2}\cdot\log(1/s^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\log(\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})))+\log(1/\varepsilon)+1}{k\log n},

the random walk distribution
\mathcal{W}({\mathbb{S}},N,s)\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
is within statistical distance at most
\varepsilon/2
from uniform in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
,
where
{\mathbb{S}}=\{{\mathfrak{p}}\text{ prime ideal }\,|\allowbreak\,\norm({\mathfrak{p}})\leq B\}
.

Proof.

This theorem is a direct translation of
[
14
, Theorem 3.3]
(or
Theorem

6.4
) with this section’s
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit terminology: recall that if
{\mathbb{S}}
generates the class group, then
\Pic_{K}^{0}
is isomorphic to
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. By a result of Bach
[
4
]
,
{\mathbb{S}}
indeed generates the class group for
B\geq 12\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|
, hence indeed the bound in the theorem statement suffices.
∎

In order to prove
Proposition

16.10
, we will use a tail-cut random walk distribution.

Definition 16.3

(Tail-cut random walk distribution)
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
be a set of prime ideals of
K
,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
s>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. The distribution
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}},N,s,\varepsilon)
is a probability distribution over
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
obtained as in
Definition

16.1
, except that the Gaussian distribution used to sample
x
is tail-cut at
t:=s\cdot\sqrt{2\cdot n\cdot\log\big(\frac{4\cdot n}{\varepsilon}\big)}
. In other words,
x
is sampled from
\mathcal{G}_{H,s}
conditioned on
\|x\|\leq t
.

Lemma 16.4
.

For any set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
s>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, the statistical distance between
\mathcal{W}({\mathbb{S}},N,s)
and
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}},N,s,\varepsilon)
is upper bounded by
\varepsilon/2
.

Proof.

By the data processing inequality (
Theorem

2.16
), it suffices to prove that the statistical distance between
\mathcal{G}_{H,s}
and its tail-cut variant is bounded by
\varepsilon/2
. This directly follows from
Lemma

2.19
.
∎

Lemma 16.5
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
be a set of prime ideals,
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
s>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. Then the support of the distribution
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}},N,s,\varepsilon)
is included in
\{x\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}\,|\,\|x\|\leq R\}
, where

R=s\cdot\sqrt{2\cdot n\cdot\log\left(\frac{4\cdot n}{\varepsilon}\right)}+N\cdot\left(1+\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})\right).

Proof.

Let
y
be output by
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}},N,s,\varepsilon)
. By the definition of this distribution, we have
y\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
.
We want to show that
\|y\|\leq R
.

Let us consider the components of
y=(({y}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}},({y}_{\nu})_{\nu})
in
\bigoplus_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathbb{Z}
and
\bigoplus_{\nu}\mathbb{R}
separately. The vector
({y}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}
satisfies
\|({y}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\|\leq\|({y}_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\|_{1}\leq N
, by definition.
From the tail-cut definition of
\mathcal{W}^{T}
, we also know that
({y}_{\nu})_{\nu}
is the sum of a vector of norm
\leq t
(where
t
is defined in
Definition

16.3
) and of
N
vectors of norm at most
\frac{\sqrt{2n}\cdot\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})}{n}\leq\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})
. Summing all these terms provides the desired upper bound on
\|y\|
.
∎

We will also need the following auxiliary lemma, which lower bounds the probability that a uniform element modulo
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
falls in a small ball.
We denote

t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}:=\{((x_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}},(x_{\nu})_{\nu})\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|((x_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}},(x_{\nu})_{\nu})\|_{\infty}\leq t\},

where
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
was defined in
Equation

105
(note that this is the
\mathbb{R}
-span, even for the coordinates in
{\mathbb{S}}
).
Recall that

\mathcal{U}(\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
is the uniform distribution over the compact group
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

Lemma 16.6
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
be any set of prime ideals generating the class group and let
{x}\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
be fixed.
Then, for
t=1+\frac{1}{n}\cdot\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})
,

\displaystyle\Pr_{y\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))}\Big(\exists z\in t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\mbox{ so that }y={x}+z\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\Big)
