\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(1000\cdot n\cdot\log(n)^{3})^{n}\cdot h_{K}\cdot\covol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))}.

Remark 16.7
.

Note that in this lemma, the element
y
belongs to
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
, hence has its first coordinates in
\bigoplus_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathbb{Z}
, but
{x}
and
z
are only required to live in the larger space
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))=\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
, which means that their coordinates
{x}_{\mathfrak{p}},{z}_{\mathfrak{p}}
might be real.

Proof.

We will start with writing the probability in terms of volumes in the quotient
X_{\mathbb{S}}=\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. For convenience, we will use the notation

\mathrm{Fold}_{\mathbb{S}}:\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}\longrightarrow X_{\mathbb{S}}.

We have

\displaystyle\Pr_{y\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(X)}\Big(\exists z\in t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\mbox{ so that }y={x}+z\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\mathbb{S}}((t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}+x)\cap\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}))}{\vol(X_{\mathbb{S}})}

To address the semi-continuous nature of
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
(as opposed to
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
),
we define
\bar{x}\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
by the rule
\bar{x}_{\mathfrak{p}}:=\lceil x_{\mathfrak{p}}\rfloor
and
\bar{x}_{\nu}=x_{\nu}-\frac{n_{\nu}}{n}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}}(\bar{x}_{\mathfrak{p}}-x_{\mathfrak{p}})\log\norm(\mathfrak{p})
(for all
\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}
and places
\nu
; here
n_{\nu}=2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise). Then
\bar{x}\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
and

\|\bar{x}-x\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm(\mathfrak{p}).

(106)

In particular,
\tfrac{1}{2}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}+\bar{x}\subset t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}+x
.
Since volumes in
X_{\mathbb{S}}
are invariant by translation (by elements of
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
), we get

\displaystyle\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\mathbb{S}}((t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}+x)\cap\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}))

\displaystyle\geq\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\mathbb{S}}((\tfrac{1}{2}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}+\bar{x})\cap\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}))

\displaystyle=\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\mathbb{S}}(\tfrac{1}{2}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}))

We have
\tfrac{1}{2}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\cap\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}=\{0\}^{\mathbb{S}}\times(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\emptyset}),

where
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\emptyset}
is the unit
\infty
-ball in the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\Div_{K,\emptyset}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
. We deduce

\displaystyle\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\mathbb{S}}(((\tfrac{1}{2}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}})\cap\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}))=\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\emptyset}(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\emptyset}))\geq\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\emptyset}(r\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\emptyset}))

for any
0<r\leq 1/2
. If furthermore
n^{1/2}r\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))
, the restriction of
\mathrm{Fold}_{\emptyset}
to
r\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\emptyset}\subset n^{1/2}r\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\emptyset}
is injective, and we get

\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\emptyset}(r\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\emptyset}))=\vol(r\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{\emptyset})=(2r)^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1}\geq(2r)^{n}.

Composing all our volume (in)equalities, for
r\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\min(1,\lambda_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))/\sqrt{n})
we have

\vol(\mathrm{Fold}_{\mathbb{S}}((t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}+x)\cap\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}))\geq(2r)^{n}.

From
Lemma

2.14
, we know that
\lambda_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))\geq(1000\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\log(n)^{3})^{-1}
. Hence, taking
r=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\tfrac{1}{1000n(\log n)^{3}}
satisfies the above condition.
Together with the identity
\vol(X_{\mathbb{S}})=h_{K}\cdot\covol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))
(see
Lemma

15.5
), we obtain the desired bound.
∎

From this, we can prove the following upper bound on the covering radius of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
when
|{\mathbb{S}}|
is sufficiently large.

Lemma 16.8

(ERH)
.

There exist
B_{1}=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
such that if
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
is the set of all prime ideals of norm
\leq B_{1}
, then
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
generates the class group of
K
and we have

\cov(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|).

Proof.

Let us take
k=1
,
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2\cdot(1000\cdot n\cdot\log(n)^{3})^{n}\cdot h_{K}\cdot\covol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))}
and
s=1/n
. Let
B
and
N
be as in
Theorem

16.2
, for these choices of
k
,
\varepsilon
and
s
, with
N
minimal.

We will prove that the lemma holds for
B_{1}=B
. We have already seen that the
B
from
Theorem

16.2
is such that all prime ideals of norm
\leq B
generates the class group, which proves the first part of the lemma.

Let us now prove the second part of the lemma. Let
x\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))=\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
. We would like to show that there exists a vector
y
in the support of the tail-cut random walk distribution
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}}^{\prime},N,s,\varepsilon)
that is not too far away from
x\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
.

From
Lemma

16.6
and by definition of
\varepsilon
, we know that

\Pr_{y\leftarrow\mathcal{U}(\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))}\Big(\exists z\in t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\mbox{ such that }y=x+z\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})\Big)\geq 2\varepsilon,

where

{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}:=\{((x_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}},(x_{\nu})_{\nu})\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|((x_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}},(x_{\nu})_{\nu})\|_{\infty}\leq t\}

and
t=1+\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})
.

Moreover, combining
Lemma

16.4
and
Theorem

16.2
, we get that the statistical distance between
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}}^{\prime},N,s,\varepsilon)
and the uniform distribution over
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
is at most
\varepsilon
. Hence, we obtain that

\Pr_{y\leftarrow\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}}^{\prime},N,s,\varepsilon)}\Big(\exists z\in t{{\mathcal{B}}_{\infty}}\mbox{ such that }y=x+z\bmod\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})\Big)\geq 2\varepsilon-\varepsilon>0.

This proves the existence of some
y
in the support of
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}}^{\prime},N,s,\varepsilon)
and some
z\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}(\mathbb{R})=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
with
\|z\|_{\infty}\leq 1+\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})
such that
x+z-y\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
. This means in particular that

\min_{v\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})}\|x-v\|\leq\|z\|+\|y\|.

We know that the number of prime ideals of norm bounded by
B
is at most
B\cdot n
(since there are at most
n
prime ideals above any prime integer in
\mathbb{Z}
). Hence,
\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})\leq B\cdot n\cdot\log B
and we have
\|z\|\leq\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}+1+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|}\cdot(1+nB\log B)\leq\poly(B,\log|\Delta_{K}|)
.

Since
y
belongs to the support of
\mathcal{W}^{T}({\mathbb{S}}^{\prime},N,s,\varepsilon)
, we know from
Lemma

16.5
that
\|y\|\leq s\cdot\sqrt{2\cdot n\cdot\log\big(\frac{4\cdot n}{\varepsilon}\big)}+N\cdot\big(1+\log B\big)
.

Let us upper bound the terms
\log(1/\varepsilon)
,
B
and
N
appearing in the two upper bounds.
Recall from
Lemma

15.5
that
\log(h_{K}\cdot\covol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})))\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|
. From this, we see that
\log(1/\varepsilon)=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
.
It was shown in
[
14
]
(in the proof of Corollary 3.4) that
\eta_{1}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})^{\vee}))\leq\poly(n)
. Hence, by choice of
s
, we have
1/s^{\prime}=\poly(n)
. From this,
Lemma

15.5
, and the choice of
k=1
, we obtain that
B=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
and
N=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
.

We conclude that for any
x\in\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))
, we have

\min_{v\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})}\|x-v\|\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|),

hence
\cov(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|).

∎

Only one last lemma remains to be proven before being able to prove
Proposition

16.10
. This lemma relates the generating radii
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
and
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))
when
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
is a subset of
{\mathbb{S}}
.

Lemma 16.9
.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}
and
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
be finite sets of prime ideals in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
satisfying
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{S}}
. Let
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
generate the class group. Then we have

\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))\leq(n+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|)\cdot\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))+\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})+1.

Proof.

In order to show an upper bound on the generating radius
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, we will construct short vectors that generate the lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
,
using
the short vectors that generate the lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
. In this way, we relate the generating radii of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
and
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
. We will first show that these vectors that we construct span a lattice of rank
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}|-1
(the same as the rank of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
) included in
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. We then conclude that both lattices are equal by a volumetric argument.

Let us order the elements of
{\mathbb{S}}=\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{p}}_{|{\mathbb{S}}|}\}
such that
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}=\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{p}}_{|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|}\}
. By definition of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
and
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
, we know that every vector
v\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
padded with
|{\mathbb{S}}|-|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|
zeros (at the ‘prime places’) belongs to
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

Let us fix some
b_{1},\cdots,b_{r}
(for some
r>0
) generating
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
with
\|b_{i}\|\leq\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))
for all
i
’s, and let us consider the associated vectors
{\bar{b}}_{1},\cdots,{\bar{b}}_{r}\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
obtained by padding them with zeros. Since padding with zeros does not change the euclidean norm, we still have
\|{\bar{b}}_{i}\|\leq\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))
for all
i
’s.
Moreover, the vectors
{\bar{b}}_{i}
span a lattice of rank
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|-1
, included in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
.

Let us now construct
|{\mathbb{S}}|-|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|
other vectors
c_{|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}+1|},\cdots,c_{|{\mathbb{S}}|}
in
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times})
.
Let us fix some
i
with
|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|<i\leq|{\mathbb{S}}|
and consider the prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{S}}\setminus{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
. Since
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
generates the class group, we know that there exists
\alpha_{i}\in K
and
v^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}
such that
\alpha_{i}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}={\mathfrak{p}}_{i}\cdot\prod_{j\leq|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|}{\mathfrak{p}}_{j}^{v^{(i)}_{j}}
. For
j>|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|
, let us define
v^{(i)}_{j}=1
if
j=i
and
0
otherwise.
Then, the vector
c_{i}:=\big((v^{(i)}_{j})_{j\leq|{\mathbb{S}}|},-\Log(\alpha_{i})\big)
belongs to
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

By definition, we know that we can decompose
c_{i}=d_{i}+e_{i}
where
d_{i}
belongs to
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}({\bar{b}}_{1},\cdots,{\bar{b}}_{r})
and
e_{i}=((0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0),(-\frac{n_{\nu}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}}_{i})}{n})_{\nu})\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
(with the last coefficients (associated with the infinite places) equal to
\frac{n_{\nu}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}}_{i})}{n}
and the
1
in position of the prime
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
). We can reduce the vector
d_{i}
by using the vectors
{\bar{b}}_{1},\cdots,{\bar{b}}_{r}
to ensure that
\|d_{i}\|\leq(n+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|)\cdot\max_{i}\|{\bar{b}}_{i}\|
(for example, by taking
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|-1
linearly independent vectors among the
{\bar{b}}_{i}
’s and performing Babai’s round-off algorithm).

Hence, without loss of generality, we can assume that

\|c_{i}\|\leq(n+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|)\cdot\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))+\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})+1.

So if we can show that the
{\bar{b}}_{i}
’s and
c_{i}
’s generate
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, we have
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))\leq(n+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|)\cdot\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))+\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})+1
.

So, it remains to show that the vectors
{\bar{b}}_{i}
’s and
c_{i}
’s generate
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.
Let
L
be the lattice generated by the
{\bar{b}}_{i}
’s and the
c_{i}
’s. By construction,
L\subseteq\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. Moreover, from the structure of the
c_{i}
’s, we know that
L
has rank
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|-1+(|{\mathbb{S}}|-|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|)
, i.e., the same rank as
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.
Finally, by choice of the
c_{i}
’s, we know that
\covol(L)=\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))=\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, where the last equality comes from
Lemma

15.5
. From this, we conclude that
L=\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, as desired.
∎

Combining everything, we can now prove
Proposition

16.10
.

Proposition 16.10

(ERH)
.

There exists some
B_{1}=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
such that for any set
{\mathbb{S}}
containing all prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
of norm
\leq B_{1}
, it holds that

\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})).

Moreover,
B_{1}
is such that the prime ideals of norm
\leq B_{1}
generate the class group of
K
.

Proof of
Proposition

16.10
.

We prove that the lemma holds for the same bound
B_{1}
as in
Lemma

16.8
. We already know from
Lemma

16.8
that this bound
B_{1}
is such that all prime ideals of norm
\leq B_{1}
generates the class group.

Let us call
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
the set of prime ideals of norm
\leq B_{1}
. From
Lemma

16.8
and
Lemma

2.3
, we know that

\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))\leq 2\cdot\cov(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
.

From
Lemma

16.9
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n+|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|}\cdot\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}^{\times}))+\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})+1

\displaystyle\leq\poly\Big(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}})\Big),

where we used the fact that
|{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}|\leq nB_{1}=\poly(B_{1},\log|\Delta_{K}|)
(see the proof of
Lemma

16.8
).
∎

17.
Generating a single
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit

In this section, we describe an algorithm that computes in subexponential time a vector in the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice when the set
{\mathbb{S}}
is sufficiently large.
Our algorithm even does slightly more than that, namely given as input an integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}
, the algorithm computes a relation between the ideal
\mathfrak{a}
and the ideals of
{\mathbb{S}}
, that is, it outputs
\alpha\in K
and
(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}
such that
\mathfrak{a}=\alpha\cdot\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{-v_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

Running the algorithm with
\mathfrak{a}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
provides a vector in the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice. However, in
Section

18
we will use the fact that the algorithm can take as input any integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}
and not just
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Indeed, in
Section

18
, we want to generate many independent vectors in the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice (so that they generate the full lattice with good probability once we have enough of them). In order to ensure independence of the vectors, we will crucially rely on the fact that we can choose as input any ideal
\mathfrak{a}
.

Another place where we need to take as input an ideal
\mathfrak{a}
is when we want to recover a generator of a principal ideal or compute a class group discrete logarithm (as explained in the introduction). In these cases, which correspond to the descent phase of the sieving algorithm, we need to find a relation between any input ideal
\mathfrak{a}
and the prime ideals of
{\mathbb{S}}
for which we computed the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice.

Given as input an integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}
,
Algorithm

3
uses
Theorem

1.1
to sample elements
\alpha\in\mathfrak{a}
until the relative ideal
\alpha\cdot\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
is
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth. Any such smooth relative ideal provides a relation between
\mathfrak{a}
and the ideals of
{\mathbb{S}}
. By
Theorem

1.1
, the success probability of this procedure is related to the local density of smooth ideals, which we computed in
Section

14
. As will be discussed later (see
Section

21.1
) there are certain regimes, depending intricately on
\rho_{K},n
and
\log|\Delta_{K}|
where the sampling algorithm’s success probability increases when the first small primes are omitted from the computation, thus introducing a modulus
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
consisting of the product of small primes. This is the main reason why
Algorithm

3
starts with computing an approximation of
\rho_{K}
and chooses different avenues depending on the value of
\rho_{K}
, leading to either a modulus
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
consisting of small primes (if
\rho_{K}
is large) or an empty modulus
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
(if
\rho_{K}
is small).

17.1.
The algorithm that generates a single
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit relation

The following definition of
\omega
will be used in
Algorithm

3
.

Definition 17.1
.

For a given number field
K
of degree
n
, a modulus
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, and real numbers
x,\mathsf{b}\geq 1
, we define
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
as the smallest positive integer such that
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
satisfies
r^{n}\geq e^{n}\max(B_{\mathrm{sm}},B_{\mathrm{rw}},10x^{2})
, where
B_{\mathrm{sm}}
is defined in
Lemma

14.1
(with
\varepsilon:=1
loc. cit.),
and
B_{\mathrm{rw}}
is defined in
Theorem

13.2
.

Note that using the fact that
B_{\mathrm{sm}}=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
and
B_{\mathrm{rw}}:=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0}))
, we can see that
\omega=O(1)
is a constant (and so in particular
\size(\omega)=O(1)
since it is an integer).

We can now describe
Algorithm

3
, which computes one relation.

Algorithm 3

Computing one relation

0:

(i)

An LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,

(ii)

an ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\ideals
,

(iii)

a set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
of
K
,

(iv)

y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
.

0:

\alpha\in{\mathfrak{a}}
and
(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
such that
{\mathfrak{a}}=\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{-v_{\mathfrak{p}}}

1:

define
\mathsf{b}=n^{\frac{2}{3}}

2:

compute an approximation
\tilde{\rho}_{K}
of
\rho_{K}
such that
\tilde{\rho}_{K}\leq\rho_{K}<2\tilde{\rho}_{K}
(see
Proposition

2.9
)

3:

define
x=\max\left(\frac{\log^{2/3}|\Delta_{K}|}{\log^{4/3}\log|\Delta_{K}|},\frac{n^{2/3}}{\log^{2/3}(n)}\right)

4:

if

\tilde{\rho}_{K}\leq\exp(x\log^{2}(x))

then

5:

define
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=(1)

6:

else

7:

define
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\prod_{\norm(\mathfrak{p})<x}\mathfrak{p}

8:

end

if

9:

Define
\omega
as in
Definition

17.1

10:

repeat

11:

\alpha\leftarrow\textup{{Sample}}({\mathfrak{a}},y,\mathfrak{m}_{0},\mathsf{b},\omega)

(see
Theorem

13.2
)

12:

until

\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot{\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}
is
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth

13:

compute
(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
such that
\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot{\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}

14:

return

(\alpha,(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}})
.

Notation 17.2
.

Throughout the following lemmas, we will use the following notation:

•

We put
B_{\mathrm{max}}=\max(\exp(\sqrt{\log(r^{n})\log\log(r^{n})}),B_{\mathrm{sm}},B_{\mathrm{rw}},10x^{2})
. Note that this instantiation implies
x<\frac{10x^{2}}{4\log(10x^{2})}<B_{\mathrm{max}}/(4\log B_{\mathrm{max}})
(in the first inequality we used
x\geq 1
and for the second one we used the fact that
y\mapsto y/(4\log(y))
is increasing for
y>3
).

•

u=\frac{\log(r^{n})}{\log(B_{\mathrm{max}})}
;

Note that, since
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}\geq 48
, we certainly have
r^{n}\geq e^{n}\cdot\exp(\sqrt{\log(r^{n})\log\log(r^{n})})
and hence
r^{n}\geq e^{n}B_{\mathrm{max}}
.

In order to analyze the running time of
Algorithm

3
, we need to get a lower bound on the success probability
p_{\mathrm{success}}
that the
repeat...until
loop from lines
10
-
12
terminate. This is what we compute in the next four lemmas. In order to improve readability, we decomposed the computation into small lemmas, the most interesting one being the last one (
Lemma

17.6
).

Lemma 17.3

(ERH)
.

Put
r,B_{\mathrm{max}},u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as in
17.2
. Assume that
{\mathbb{S}}
contains all prime ideals not dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
of norm
\leq B_{\mathrm{max}}
, and that
{\mathfrak{a}}
is coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. Then the success probability
p_{\mathrm{success}}
of
\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}{\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}
being
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth in the repeat-loop (lines
10
-
12
) of
Algorithm

3
satisfies

p_{\mathrm{success}}\geq\frac{1}{6}\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}\frac{(4\log(B_{\mathrm{max}}))^{1-u}\cdot u^{-u}}{B_{\mathrm{max}}}.

(107)

Proof.

We denote
\mathcal{S}_{{\mathbb{S}}}
for the
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth integral ideals. Since
{\mathbb{S}}
contains all prime ideals (coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
) of norm
\leq B_{\mathrm{max}}
per assumption, and
B_{\mathrm{max}}\geq B_{\mathrm{rw}}
by definition, we have
\mathcal{S}_{{\mathbb{S}}}\mathcal{S}_{B_{\mathrm{rw}}}=\mathcal{S}_{{\mathbb{S}}}
(where we considered only prime ideals coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
in
\mathcal{S}_{B_{\mathrm{rw}}}
).

In line
11
of
Algorithm

3
, we
use
Sample
from
Theorem

13.2
. According to that theorem,
18
18

18

We can apply the theorem because
{\mathfrak{a}}
is assumed to be coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.

the probability
p_{\mathrm{success}}
of
\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
lying in
\mathcal{S}_{{\mathbb{S}}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{B_{\mathrm{rw}}}=\mathcal{S}_{{\mathbb{S}}}
is at least

p_{\mathrm{success}}\geq\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}\frac{\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{S}}}[r^{n}]}{6}\geq\frac{1}{6}\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}\frac{(4\log(B_{\mathrm{max}}))^{1-u}\cdot u^{-u}}{B_{\mathrm{max}}}.

Here, the last inequality comes from computing the density of smooth numbers as in
Lemma

14.1
. We instantiate that lemma with
A:=x
if
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\neq\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
A:=1
otherwise (this is to take into account that the prime dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
are omitted, see lines
7
and
5
),
\varepsilon:=1
, and
B:=B_{\mathrm{max}}
. These parameters indeed satisfy the constraints of
Lemma

14.1
since
B_{\mathrm{max}}\geq B_{\mathrm{sm}}
,
r^{n}\geq e^{n}B_{\mathrm{max}}
, and
A\leq B_{\mathrm{max}}/(4\log B_{\mathrm{max}})
(see
17.2
).
∎

Lemma 17.4
.

Keeping the notations from
Lemma

17.3
, it holds that

\frac{(4\log(B_{\mathrm{max}}))^{1-u}\cdot u^{-u}}{B_{\mathrm{max}}}\geq\frac{1}{(B_{\mathrm{max}})^{3}}.

(108)

Proof.

Taking negative logarithms, and writing
R=\log(r^{n})
,
\beta=\log(B_{\mathrm{max}})
and
u=R/\beta
, we obtain

-\log\left(\frac{(4\log(B_{\mathrm{max}}))^{1-u}\cdot u^{-u}}{B_{\mathrm{max}}}\right)=\beta+\left(\frac{R}{\beta}-1\right)\log(4\beta)+\frac{R}{\beta}\log(R/\beta).

(109)

It suffices to show that the two right-most summands of
Equation

109
are upper bounded by
\beta
. Note that, by
17.2
,
we have that
\beta\geq\sqrt{R\log R}
. Hence, we immediately see
R/\beta\cdot\log(R/\beta)\leq\sqrt{R\log R}\leq\beta
.
For the middle summand, note that
(\tfrac{R}{\beta}-1)\log(4\beta)\leq\beta
is equivalent to
R\leq\beta^{2}/\log(4\beta)+\beta
. The
latter inequality is true because
\beta\mapsto\beta^{2}/\log(4\beta)+\beta
is an increasing function for
\beta\geq 1/2
and, for
R\geq 3
(which is clearly satisfied since
r\geq 16
),

R\leq\frac{(\sqrt{R\log R})^{2}}{\log(4\sqrt{R\log R})}+\sqrt{R\log R}\leq\beta^{2}/\log(4\beta)+\beta,

where the first inequality follows by graphical inspection and the second by the monotonicity of the function
\beta\mapsto\beta^{2}/\log(4\beta)+\beta
.
∎

Lemma 17.5
.

Keeping the notations from
Lemma

17.3
, it holds that

B_{\mathrm{max}}\in\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3})).

Proof.

From the definition of
B_{\mathrm{max}}
in
17.2
, we have that

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{max}}

\displaystyle\in\poly(\exp(\sqrt{\log(r^{n})\log\log(r^{n})}),\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0}))

(110)

\displaystyle=\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3})).

(111)

Indeed, by the definition of
r

and the definition of
\mathsf{b}
, and using that
\omega=O(1)

\log(r^{n})\leq O\Big(\frac{n^{2}\log\mathsf{b}}{\mathsf{b}}+\log|\Delta_{K}|+\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\Big)=O(n^{4/3}\log n,\log|\Delta_{K}|,\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})).

And hence

\exp(\sqrt{\log(r^{n})\log\log(r^{n})})\leq\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3})).

(112)

∎

Lemma 17.6
.

Keep the notations from
Lemma

17.3
and define

\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}=\min\Big(\rho_{K},\max(e^{\log^{\frac{2}{3}}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log^{\frac{2}{3}}(\log|\Delta_{K}|)},e^{n^{\frac{2}{3}}\log^{\frac{4}{3}}(n)})\Big).

Then it holds that
B_{\mathrm{max}}
and
p_{\mathrm{success}}^{-1}
are both in

\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3}),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}})
.

Proof.

Combining
Lemma

17.3
,
Lemma

17.4
we obtain that

p_{\mathrm{success}}^{-1}\in\poly(\tfrac{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})},B_{\mathrm{max}}).

Moreover,
Lemma

17.5
gives us
B_{\mathrm{max}}\in\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3}),L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2}))
.
To prove the result, it then suffices to show that

\poly(L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2}),\tfrac{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})})=\poly(\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}).

We write
x=\max\left(\frac{\log^{2/3}|\Delta_{K}|}{\log^{4/3}\log|\Delta_{K}|},\frac{n^{2/3}}{\log^{2/3}(n)}\right)
as in line
3
of
Algorithm

3
.
Note that
\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}=\min\left(\rho_{K},\exp(\Theta(x\log^{2}(x)))\right).

We distinguish two cases.

Case 1:
\tilde{\rho}_{K}\leq\exp(x\log^{2}(x))
.

By line
4
and
5
, we then have
\rho_{K}<2\tilde{\rho}_{K}\leq 2\cdot\exp(x\log^{2}(x))
(in particular,
\rho_{K}=\poly(\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}})
) and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=(1)
. Hence
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=1
and thus

\poly(L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2}),\tfrac{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})})=\poly(\rho_{K})=\poly(\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}).

Case 2:
\tilde{\rho}_{K}>\exp(x\log^{2}(x))
.

In this case, it holds that
x\log(x)^{2}=O(\log\rho_{K})
and have
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\prod_{\norm(\mathfrak{p})<x}\mathfrak{p}
(by lines
4
and
7
).
On one hand, by
Proposition

11.2
we have

\displaystyle-\log\left(\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8\log|\Delta_{K}|}{\sqrt{x}}+\frac{8n\log x}{\sqrt{x}}

\displaystyle\leq O(x\log^{2}(x))=O(\log\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}).

(113)

This follows from the fact that
\log|\Delta_{K}|\leq O(x^{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\log^{2}(x))
and
n\leq O(x^{\frac{3}{2}}\log x)
, since
x=\max\left(\left(\frac{\log|\Delta_{K}|}{\log^{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}},\left(\frac{n}{\log n}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)
.
On the other hand, using the same bounds on
\log|\Delta_{K}|
and
n
, we have, by
Lemma

11.4

\displaystyle\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq O(x+\sqrt{x}\log(x)\log|\Delta_{K}|+\sqrt{x}\log^{2}(x)\cdot n)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(x^{2}\log^{3}(x)\right).

(114)

We deduce that

\log L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2})\leq O(\sqrt{\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\log\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})})\leq O(x\log^{2}(x))=O(\log\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}).

(115)

Combining
Equations

113
and
115
, we thus obtain

\poly(L_{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}(\tfrac{1}{2}),\tfrac{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})})=\poly(\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}).

∎

Theorem 17.7

(ERH)
.

There exists some
B_{\mathrm{max}}=\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}})
,
where

\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}=\min\Big(\rho_{K},\max(e^{\log^{\frac{2}{3}}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log^{\frac{2}{3}}(\log|\Delta_{K}|)},e^{n^{\frac{2}{3}}\log^{\frac{4}{3}}(n)})\Big),

such that the following holds.
Assume that
{\mathbb{S}}
contains all prime ideals coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
of norm
\leq B_{\mathrm{max}}
.
Then, on input an integral ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}
coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, the set
{\mathbb{S}}
and
y\in H=\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0})
,

Algorithm

3
outputs
(\alpha,(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}})\in{\mathfrak{a}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{|{\mathbb{S}}|}
such that

{\mathfrak{a}}=\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{-v_{\mathfrak{p}}}.

Furthermore,
Algorithm

3
runs in expected time

\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3}),\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}),\allowbreak\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\allowbreak\size({\mathfrak{a}}),\allowbreak\size(y),\log|\Delta_{K}|).

Proof.

Thanks to the
repeat...until
loop from lines
10
to
12
, the output of
Algorithm

3
is correct whenever it terminates.
The fact that
B_{\mathrm{max}}=\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),\allowbreak L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}})
follows from
Lemma

17.6
.
Hence, the main focus of this proof is the expected running time of the algorithm.

Since lines
1
up to
10
do not significantly contribute to the running time, it suffices to concentrate on the expected running time of lines
11
up to
14
.

For this time analysis we will use that
\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
, by

11.4
and the definition of
x
on line
3
. Also, we will use that
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}\leq L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3})
, from the definition of
\mathsf{b}=n^{2/3}
.

•

(Line

) The sampling algorithm
Sample
takes (by

13.2
) time
\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size({\mathfrak{a}}),\allowbreak\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})),\allowbreak\size(y),\allowbreak\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}})=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size({\mathfrak{a}}),\allowbreak\size(y),\allowbreak L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3}))
. Here we use that
\size(\omega)=O(1)
(see

17.1
).

•

(Line

and

) Checking for
\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot{\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}
being
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth (line

), as well as writing down the decomposition (line

division of ideals.

•

(Line

) This is the return statement.

Hence, denoting
p_{\mathrm{success}}
for the probability of

being
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth in line

, we obtain an expected running time of

p_{\mathrm{success}}^{-1}\cdot\poly(L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3}),\size({\mathbb{S}}),\size({\mathfrak{a}}),\size(y),\log|\Delta_{K}|).

(116)

Using

provides an upper bound on

∎

17.2.
Properties of the output distribution of
Algorithm

3
.

From now on, we assume that the input ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}
is
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth, since this will be the case when we will call
Algorithm

3
in the next section (this ensures in particular that
{\mathfrak{a}}
is coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, which is needed to apply
Theorem

17.7
).
We describe the input of
Algorithm

3
as a divisor
\mathbf{a}=d(\mathfrak{a})+\Log(y)\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
(we recover
\mathfrak{a}={\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
and
y={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
).
The following properties of
Algorithm

3

are required in the later proof
that

4
(which is essentially
Algorithm

with a

) has an evenly distributed and concentrated output distribution (see

and

). These two properties of this last output distribution are indispensable to show that sufficiently many samples from this algorithm eventually generate the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

We denote the output distribution of
\alpha\in K
in
Algorithm

3
with input

\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}

by
19
19

19

Note the difference in notation compared to
\mathcal{D}
in
Section

10
; the bar signifies that the distribution is from
Algorithm

3
.

\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}
.
By abuse of notation, we denote by the same symbol
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}
the distribution over

\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})

defined by sampling
\alpha\leftarrow\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}
and taking
the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit map
20
20

20

This
sends the roots of unity to the zero-vector, but that is not troublesome, as elements
\alpha,\zeta\alpha\in K^{*}

for a root of unity
\zeta
have equal probability to be sampled.

\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)
.
Note that this map is well-defined, as

only outputs

whose prime divisors lie in
{\mathbb{S}}
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{S}}
-units (by line

and because
{\mathfrak{a}}
is
{\mathbb{S}}

This yields a distribution
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}\in L^{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
for each

.
Note that
Algorithm

and hence the distribution

is independent on the degree of

(as in

), as

from

is independent on the norm.

Lemma 17.8
.

For all
\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
, the output distribution
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}
of
Algorithm

3
(where we consider the divisor
\mathbf{a}=d(\mathfrak{a})+\Log(y)
as input, which is equivalent to the input
(\mathfrak{a},y)
)
satisfies the
following three properties.

(i)

We have

\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})]=1
.

(ii)

For all
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}
and all
x\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})

\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[x]=\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}-\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)}[x+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)].

(iii)

There exists some
R=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)+\|\mathbf{a}\|
such that

\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}\left[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\backslash(R\cdot\mathcal{B}_{2})\right]=0

where

R\cdot\mathcal{B}_{2}=\{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \|\mathbf{a}\|\leq R\}
.

(iv)

For
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
with
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}+\sum_{\nu}b_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis
, we have

\|\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}-\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq 3\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}\cdot n^{2}\cdot\|b\|+1/200.

with
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.

Proof.

Statement (

) follows from the fact that

3
outputs
\alpha
only if
\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot{\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}
is
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth. Together with the fact that
{\mathfrak{a}}
itself is assumed to be
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth (it is made out of
\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
), we deduce that
\alpha
is
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth, thus an
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit. Therefore,
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}
only has support on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\subseteq\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
.

Statement (

) follows from property (
2
) of
Theorem

13.2
. Let
\mathbf{a}=d(\mathfrak{a})+\Log(y)
for
\mathfrak{a}={\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
and
y={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})
. By definition of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)
, we have that
\mathbf{a}-\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)=d(\mathfrak{a}\cdot(\alpha))+\Log(y\cdot\alpha^{-1})
. Property (
2
) of

13.2
tells us that
\mathcal{D}_{y,\mathfrak{a}}[\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]=\mathcal{D}_{y\cdot\alpha^{-1},\mathfrak{a}\cdot(\alpha)}[\alpha\cdot\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]
(note the different distribution, this is the distribution from
Theorem

13.2
). As the exit criterion of the repeat loop (line

of
Algorithm

3
) happens at the same occurrences, the output distribution function
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}
satisfies
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[x]=\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}-\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)}[x+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)]
for all
x\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

The statement (
iii
) follows from property (
3
) of
Theorem

13.2
,
\omega=O(1)
(see
Definition

17.1
) and the fact that
\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})=O(x\cdot\log^{2}(x)\cdot\log|\Delta_{K}|)=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)
(see

in the proof of

, and see the definition of

in line

of

). Note that here the correspondence
between

and

is used.

Statement (

) follows from

and

(property (1), Lipschitz-property), with the fact that the success probability of a single loop of

is lower bounded by
\delta_{\mathcal{S}}[r^{n}]/6\geq\frac{1}{6\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}}

in the proof of

). Hence we have (applying

with

)

\|\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}-\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq 3\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}\cdot n^{2}\cdot\|\Log(y)\|+1/200.

∎

18.
Obtaining a generating set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

18.1.
Introduction

In
Section

17
a probabilistic algorithm is described that computes a

single

{\mathbb{S}}
-unit (or a logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice point).
But to actually obtain the
entire
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit group
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
, one needs a
generating set

of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units. Or, equivalently, a
\mathbb{Z}
-generating set of
lattice points for the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

A ‘good’ output distribution

One could hope that such a generating set will eventually
be formed by
repeating
the probabilistic algorithm (
Algorithm

3
) many
times and joining the outputs. The chance for this approach to succeed strongly
depends on the
output distribution
of the mentioned
probabilistic algorithm. Indeed, one could imagine cases
where the output distribution is, for example,
only being supported on a strict
sublattice
of
the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice. In such instances
one never obtains a generating set of the full logarithmic

{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice.

It turns out that, for the repeated sampling approach to succeed, it is sufficient
for the output distribution to have the following two properties:
‘evenly distributed’, meaning not having too much
weight on a strict sublattice, and ‘concentrated’, meaning that
most of the weight is reasonably centered around the origin. These
two properties are made more precise in
Section

18.2
,
as well as the consequences of these properties to the number of samples
needed to generate the whole lattice.

The output distribution of the sampling algorithm

It remains to be shown that the output distribution of the
sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

3
) is indeed actually ‘good’, i.e., evenly distributed and concentrated.

It turns out that the output distribution of
Algorithm

3
for a
fixed
input
(\mathfrak{a},y)\in\ideals\times K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}

is hard to analyze; and it seems very difficult to show that it satisfies these properties.
Fortunately, one can feed the algorithm
different inputs, causing to gain more
control of the output distribution.

More specifically, in our approach we randomly sample a
Gaussian distributed point from the space
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
, which
can be considered as an ‘ambient space’ of the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-units.
This randomly sampled point is then transformed into an element
(\mathfrak{a},y)\in\ideals\times K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
(
\mathfrak{a}
from the finite places and
y
from the infinite places), which is then given as an input to the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit
sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

3
).

We show that this ‘compound’ distribution, taking into account
both the randomness of the Gaussian
over
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
and the randomness of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit
sampling algorithm, indeed satisfies the ‘evenly distributed’ property (see
Lemma

18.11
) and ‘concentrated’ property (see
Lemma

18.10
). Thus, repeating the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit sampling algorithm with Gaussian
distributed inputs lets us indeed obtain a generating set of the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice with high probability; this
final statement is the object of
Theorem

18.14
.

Intuition

The intuition why this approach (of letting the input
be Gaussian distributed) works is already sketched in
Section

12.4
.
Another way of looking at it is by seeing
Algorithm

3

as a specific CVP-algorithm for the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, given
an input vector
\mathbf{a}
in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
.

As a CVP-algorithm,
Algorithm

3
outputs
a
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)
‘close’ to
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
, so one can imagine
that the output only differs slightly from the input.
If then the input element
\mathbf{a}\in\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
is distributed according to a sufficiently wide Gaussian,
and this specific
CVP output of
Algorithm

3
is sufficiently close to
\mathbf{a}
,
this CVP output distribution must be close to
a Gaussian as well, but instead its support is on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. For such Gaussian distributions over lattices
one can show that sufficiently many samples thereof generate the entire lattice
(see
Proposition

18.2
).

18.2.
Obtaining a generating set of a lattice

Given a certain distribution
\psi
on a lattice
\Lambda
, this section is about
how many samples one has to draw from
\psi
to obtain a generating set of the
lattice
\Lambda
. Of course, this depends on the distribution at hand: for example, the
distribution that always outputs
0\in\Lambda
would never yield a generating set
of the lattice
\Lambda
, no matter how many samples one takes.

For a distribution on a lattice
\Lambda
it it is sufficient to consider two particular properties
to analyze the number of samples required to draw in order to obtain a generating set of
\Lambda
. Those two
properties are
evenly distributedness
, which measures the maximum weight of the distribution
\psi

on strict sublattices of
\Lambda
, and
concentratedness
, which measures how much weight
of the distribution
\psi
is (relatively) close to the origin. This is formalized in the following definition and proposition.

Definition 18.1
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a full-rank lattice, and
\psi
be a distribution on
\Lambda
. The distribution
\psi
is called
p
-evenly distributed
if
\Pr_{x\leftarrow\psi}[x\in\Lambda^{\prime}]\leq p
for any proper sublattice
\Lambda^{\prime}\subsetneq\Lambda
. It is called
(R,q)
-concentrated
if
\Pr_{x\leftarrow\psi}[\|v\|\geq R]\leq q
.

Proposition 18.2

(
[
13
, Lemma 5]
)
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a full-rank lattice, and
\psi
be a distribution on
\Lambda
. Suppose that
\psi
is
p
-evenly distributed and
(R,q)
-concentrated for
R\geq\covol(\Lambda)^{1/n}
. Denote by
S
the random variable defined as the number of samples drawn from
\psi
until these samples generate
\Lambda
. Then, for any
\alpha>0
,

\Pr\left[S>(2+\alpha)\frac{t+n}{1-p-q}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha(t+n)/2},

where
t=n\log_{2}(R)-\log_{2}(\covol\Lambda)\geq 0
.

18.3.
The discrete Gaussian over a lattice is evenly distributed

In the following two lemmas, we show that a Gaussian distribution over a lattice with an arbitrary center point is
p
-evenly distributed. This fact is used to show that the output of the sampling algorithm with a (continuous) Gaussian input is
p
-evenly distributed as well (see
Lemma

18.11
).

Recall that
g_{\varsigma}(x):=e^{-\pi\lVert x\rVert^{2}/\varsigma^{2}}
is the Gaussian function, whereas
\mathcal{G}_{X,\varsigma}
is the Gaussian distribution over
X
(where the definition depends on whether
X
is discrete or continuous, see
Section

2.9
).

Lemma 18.3
.

For any lattice
\Lambda\subseteq V
(where
V
is a Euclidean space) any
\varsigma>0
and any
t,w\in V
,
we have

g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t+w)+g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t-w)\geq 2g_{\varsigma}(w)g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t).

Proof.

This lemma is a simple generalization of
[
41
, Claim 2.10]
, and we follow the same strategy:

\displaystyle g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t+w)+g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t-w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\Lambda+t}\left(e^{-\pi\|x+w\|^{2}/\varsigma^{2}}+e^{-\pi\|x-w\|^{2}/\varsigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=2e^{-\pi\|w\|^{2}/\varsigma^{2}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda+t}\left(e^{-\pi\|x\|^{2}/\varsigma^{2}}\cosh(2\pi\langle x,w\rangle/\varsigma^{2})\right)

\displaystyle\geq 2g_{\varsigma}(w)g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t),

where the last inequality follows from
\cosh(\alpha)\geq 1
for any real
\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 18.4
.

Let
\Lambda\subseteq V
be a lattice and
V
an Euclidean space,
t\in V
and
\varsigma\geq c\cdot\rr(\Lambda)
for some
c>0
(see
Definition

2.2
). Then the discrete Gaussian distribution on
\Lambda
with parameter
\varsigma
and centered at
t
is
p
-evenly distributed with
p=\frac{1}{1+e^{-\pi c^{-2}}}
. Concretely, for any strict sublattice
\Lambda^{\prime}\subsetneq\Lambda

\frac{g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda^{\prime}+t)}{g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t)}\leq\frac{1}{1+e^{-\pi c^{-2}}}.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda^{\prime}\subsetneq\Lambda
be a sub-lattice of
\Lambda
and
let
w\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}
. Then, by
Lemma

18.3
,

\displaystyle g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t)

\displaystyle\geq g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda^{\prime}+t)+\frac{g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda^{\prime}+t+w)+g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda^{\prime}+t-w)}{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1+g_{\varsigma}(w))g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda^{\prime}+t).

Writing
\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda+t,\varsigma}
for the Gaussian distribution on
\Lambda+t
with parameter
\varsigma
, we have,

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda+t,\varsigma}\end{subarray}}[x\in\Lambda^{\prime}+t]=\frac{g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda^{\prime}+t)}{g_{\varsigma}(\Lambda+t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1+g_{\varsigma}(w)}.

Since
\Lambda^{\prime}\subsetneq\Lambda
, we know from the definition of
\rr(\Lambda)
that there exists
w\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}
such that
\|w\|\leq\rr(\Lambda)\leq\varsigma/c
, hence

g_{\varsigma}(w)\geq\exp\left(-\pi c^{-2}\right)
, proving the lemma.
∎

18.4.
The (semi-)discrete Gaussian over
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}

We introduce the following notation for the discretization of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-divisor group as in
Definition

15.2
.
It is needed in
Algorithm

4
, as this algorithm samples a Gaussian over
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
and feeds it to
Algorithm

3
.
As
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}
is partially continuous (for which a finite computer cannot sample), we thus need this discretized
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
version of
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
.

Notation 18.5

(Discretized
{\mathbb{S}}
-divisor group)
.

For a set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
, we denote by
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}\subseteq{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
the
discrete
subgroup of the restricted divisor group, for which

x_{\nu}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}
for all places
\nu
. In other words, any element of
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
can be written as

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}x_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ (with $x_{\nu}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}$)}

Remark 18.6
.

In
Algorithm

4
, we need to compute an approximation of the discrete Gaussian over the group
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
(see
Lemma

2.22
). For this we need a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
, which is naturally given by
\{\frac{\mathbf{e}_{\nu}}{N}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \nu\mbox{ places of }K\}\cup\{\mathbf{e}_{\mathfrak{p}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}\}
, where
\mathbf{e}_{x}
is one on the coordinate
x
and zero elsewhere (where
x
can be a place or a prime). We call this basis
\mathbf{B}({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}})
.

In order to define a Gaussian distribution over

{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
, we recall the distance notion on this space (see
Section

2.8
).
Since the group
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
is partially discrete, namely, at the finite places,
the Gaussian over this group is semi-discrete as well.

Definition 18.7

(The semi-discrete Gaussian over
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
)
.

The semi-discrete Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}},\varsigma}\in L_{1}({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}})

is the distribution defined by

\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}},\varsigma}[\mathbf{a}]:=g_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbf{b}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}g_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{b})d\mathbf{b}\right)^{-1}

where
g_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})=\exp(-\pi\|\mathbf{a}\|^{2}/\varsigma^{2})
(with the distance notion from
Section

2.8
) and where we use the shorthand notation

\int_{\mathbf{b}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}g_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{b})d\mathbf{b}:=\sum_{(a_{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{S}}}\int_{(b_{\nu})_{\nu}\in\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times})}g_{\varsigma}\Big(\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}b_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\Big)db

Definition 18.8

(The discrete Gaussian over
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
)
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
we define the discrete Gaussian over
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
by the rule

\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}},\varsigma}:=g_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})\cdot\left(\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}}g_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{b})\right)^{-1}

where
g_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})=\exp(-\pi\|\mathbf{a}\|^{2}/\varsigma^{2})
(with the distance notion from
Section

2.8
).

18.5.
Sampling a set of generators for the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice

18.5.1.
Algorithm

4
:
Algorithm

3
with Gaussian input

Algorithm 4

Computing a random relation of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

0:

A number field
K

, an LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
and a set
{\mathbb{S}}
of prime ideals of
K
.

0:

An random element of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

1:

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
be as in
Definition

17.1
(with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
,
\mathsf{b}
, and
x
as in
Algorithm

3
).

2:

Let
\tilde{\rr}=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\max_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\log\norm({\mathfrak{p}}))
the bound from
Proposition

16.10
, such that
\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))\leq\tilde{\rr}
.

3:

Put
\varsigma=3\cdot\max(\sqrt{\log({n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}|)},\tilde{\rr})
,
and put

N:=\omega^{n}\cdot\exp(11+16\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+9n^{2})
.

4:

Sample, using
Lemma

2.22
with
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}:=1/100
, and the basis
\mathbf{B}({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}})
described in
Remark

18.6
,

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}b_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{B}({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}),\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}},\varsigma,0},

(117)

put
z={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})

and compute a rational
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
such that
\|y-z\|_{\infty}\leq\min_{\nu}z_{\nu}/N
.

5:

Put
\mathfrak{a}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{a_{\mathfrak{p}}}={\Exp}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}})
.

6:

Apply
Algorithm

3
with
\mathfrak{a}\in\ideals
,
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, and
{\mathbb{S}}
, yielding
(\alpha,(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}})
.

7:

return

(-(v_{\mathfrak{p}}+a_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}},\Log(\alpha))
where the
a_{\mathfrak{p}}
are from line
117
.

In this section, we prove that the output distribution of
Algorithm

4

(which is essentially

Algorithm

3
with input
\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
following a (discrete) Gaussian distribution),
is evenly distributed and concentrated on the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice (for certain adequate parameters).
Hence, by repeating
Algorithm

4
sufficiently many times, one obtains a generating set of the
Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice, which is made precise in the later
Theorem

18.14
.

Lemma 18.9
.

Assume that
{\mathbb{S}}
contains all prime ideals coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
of norm
\leq B_{\mathrm{max}}
(where
B_{\mathrm{max}}
is defined as in
Theorem

17.7
).
Then, on input
{\mathbb{S}}
,
Algorithm

4
runs in expected time

\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\varsigma,\size({\mathbb{S}}))+T,

where
T
is an upper bound on the expected time of
Algorithm

3
on input
\mathfrak{a}
and
y
of size
\size(\mathfrak{a})\leq\poly(\varsigma,\size({\mathbb{S}}),\log|\Delta_{K}|)
and
\size(y)\leq O(\varsigma n\cdot(\size({\mathbb{S}})+\log|\Delta_{K}|))
.

Proof.

Apart from the line
6
, in which
Algorithm

3
is called,
Algorithm

4
only requires computation power during the sampling of the approximate discrete Gaussian and the computation of
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
. By
Lemma

2.22
, and denoting
n_{0}=|{\mathbb{S}}|+\mathbbm{r}+1=|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}=\dim({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}})
,
the sampling of the approximate discrete Gaussian takes time (since
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}:=1/100
) polynomial in the size of the input, which can be given by the size of the lattice basis of
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
, which is
\poly(n_{0})
. Note that
\max_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathbf{B}({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}})}\|\mathbf{b}\|=1
, so, by choice of
\varsigma
, we have
\varsigma\geq\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})+2\log(n_{0})+3}{\pi}}\cdot\max_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathbf{B}({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}})}\|\mathbf{b}\|
as required to apply
Lemma

2.22
.
The run time of the computation of
y\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
depends on the minimum of
z_{\nu}/N
and the maximum of
z_{\nu}
. We have that
|b_{\nu}|\leq\varsigma\sqrt{n_{0}\log(2n_{0}^{2}/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})}
by
Lemma

2.22
(with
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}:=1/100
).
Hence, in the worst case, we must approximate
y\approx z={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty})=(e^{b_{\nu}})_{\nu}
up to precision
\exp(-\varsigma\cdot\sqrt{n_{0}\cdot\log(2n_{0}^{2}/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})})/N
.
For the maximum of
z_{\nu}
, a similar reasoning tells us that
\max_{\nu}z_{\nu}\leq\exp(\varsigma\cdot\sqrt{n_{0}\cdot\log(2n_{0}^{2}/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})})
.

Since we have
\log N=O(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
and
\sqrt{n_{0}\log(2n_{0}^{2}/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})}=O(\size({\mathbb{S}})+\log|\Delta_{K}|)
, the approximation
y\approx z
required in line
117
can be computed within time
\poly(\varsigma,\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}),\allowbreak\log|\Delta_{K}|)
. This reasoning also shows that
\size(y)\leq O(\varsigma n\cdot(\size({\mathbb{S}})+\log|\Delta_{K}|))
. Additionally, using the same bound of
Lemma

2.22
, the size of
\mathfrak{a}
must also be bounded by
\poly(\varsigma,\size({\mathbb{S}}),\log|\Delta_{K}|)
.
∎

18.5.2.
The output distribution of
Algorithm

4
is concentrated.

In the next lemma, we prove that the output distribution of
Algorithm

4
is concentrated.

Lemma 18.10
.

The output distribution
\bar{\mathcal{D}}
of
Algorithm

4
is
(R_{0},0)
-concentrated for some

R_{0}=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)+3\cdot\varsigma\cdot(|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}).

Proof.

The output distribution of
Algorithm

4
depends on an approximate discrete Gaussian from line
117
, which is computed using
Lemma

2.22
. From that lemma, denoting
n_{0}=|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}=\dim({\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}})
follows that

\|\mathbf{a}\|\leq\varsigma\cdot\sqrt{n_{0}\log(2n_{0}^{2}/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}))}\leq 3\varsigma n_{0}

with
\varsigma
from line
3
and
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}=1/100
(see line
117
of
Algorithm

4
). Here we use that
\sqrt{x\log(2x^{2}/\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}})}\leq 3x
for
x\geq 1
and
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}=1/100
.

But since
Algorithm

3
satisfies property (
iii
) from
Lemma

17.8
, we know that

\|\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)\|\leq O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)+\|\mathbf{a}\|\leq O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)+3\cdot\varsigma\cdot n_{0}.

Hence, there exists a
R_{0}=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)+3\cdot\varsigma\cdot(|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}})
for which no
\alpha
with
\|\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)\|>R_{0}
can be the output of
Algorithm

4
.
∎

18.5.3.
The output distribution of
Algorithm

4
is evenly distributed.

We now prove that the output distribution of
Algorithm

4

is evenly distributed on the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice.

The strategy to show evenly distributedness is more convoluted than was concentratedness. We start with defining the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’, in which in line
117
is used a
semi-discrete Gaussian
(as in
Definition

18.7
) instead of a fully discrete Gaussian (as in
Definition

18.8
).
More precisely, the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’ is
Algorithm

4
in which line
117
is replaced with

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}b_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}},\varsigma},\mbox{ and put }y={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}).

We will show in
Lemma

18.13
that the output distribution of the ‘continuous version’ and the ordinary version of
Algorithm

4
are close in statistical distance.

In the present section, we focus on the following step: proving that the output distribution of ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’ is evenly distributed for certain parameters. By the closeness of the ‘continuous version’ and the ordinary version of
Algorithm

4
, the ordinary must then also be evenly distributed.

Lemma 18.11
.

Assume that
{\mathbb{S}}
generates the ray class group
\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
of
K
and does not contain any prime dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. Then the output distribution of
Algorithm

4
is
2/3
-evenly distributed on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

Proof.

We will later show, in
Lemma

18.13
, that
Algorithm

4
is
1/50
-close in statistical distance to the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’. We show in the current proof that this ‘continuous version’ is
6/10
-evenly distributed on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, and hence the ordinary version is then
2/3
-evenly distributed on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, since
6/10+1/50<2/3
.

The remainder of this proof is then devoted to showing that the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’ is
6/10
-evenly distributed on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

The output distribution
\bar{\mathcal{D}}
of the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’ is supported on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
and given by the rule, for
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
,

\bar{\mathcal{D}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)]=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\alpha)]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}.

To prove that this distribution is
p
-evenly distributed (with
p=6/10
), we need to show that
\bar{\mathcal{D}}[M]=\sum_{m\in M}\bar{\mathcal{D}}[m]\leq p

for every strict sublattice
M\subsetneq\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

We first prove this for the simpler case where the modulus is trivial, i.e.,
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
; in which case we have
K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}=K^{*}
, and the shifting property (Property (
ii
) of
Lemma

17.8
) holds for any
\alpha\in K^{*}
, so in particular for
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
.

So, pick any strict sublattice
M\subsetneq\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. Then, by Property (
ii
) of
Lemma

17.8
, and, subsequently, by change of variables, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M}\bar{\mathcal{D}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)}[0]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[0]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}-\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu))d\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[0]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+M)d\mathbf{a}

(118)

Using the assumption that
{\mathbb{S}}
generates the full class group, we take a fundamental domain
F
of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
in
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
, in order to decompose
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}=\mathbb{R}\cdot\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})+F
, where
\mathbf{1}=\sum_{\nu}n_{\nu}\cdot\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\in\Log(K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times})
is the vector consisting
n_{\nu}
, which equals
2
if
\nu
is complex and
1
otherwise. This vector corresponds to the freedom of the norm (or rather, degree) in the space
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
, compared to
\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{0}
.

We can then rewrite any integral over
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
with integrand
h(\mathbf{a})
as

\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}h(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}=\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}h(\mathbf{a}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+t\cdot\mathbf{1})d\mathbf{a}dt

We apply this to
Equation

118
, we use again
Lemma

17.8
(
ii
) to write
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)}[0]=\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)]
and we use the fact that
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}}=\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}
, since
Algorithm

3
does not depend on the norm of the input (since
Sample
does not, see
Theorem

13.2
). Then,
Equation

118
equals

\displaystyle=\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\!\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+M)d\mathbf{a}dt

(119)

By Hölder’s inequality, the positivity of all arguments and the fact that (by
Lemma

17.8
(
i
))

\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)]=1,

we can bound
Equation

119
by

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\max_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+M)d\mathbf{a}dt

(120)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))}{1+e^{-\pi/c^{2}}}d\mathbf{a}dt\leq\frac{1}{1+e^{-\pi/c^{2}}}.

(121)

whenever
\varsigma\geq c\cdot\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
. Here we apply
Lemma

18.4
, which gives us

\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+M)\leq\tfrac{\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))}{1+e^{-\pi/c^{2}}}=\tfrac{\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))}{1+e^{-\pi/c^{2}}}.

By picking
c=3
, it holds that
\varsigma\geq c\cdot\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
(see line
3
of
Algorithm

4
) and that
(1+e^{-\pi/c^{2}})^{-1}\leq 6/10
.

We now explain how to amend the proof for the case where
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\neq\mathcal{O}_{K}
, i.e., a non-trivial modulus. In this case, the shifting property (Property (
ii
) of
Lemma

17.8
) holds for
\alpha\in K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}
only.

Write
M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}=\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})\cap M
and
put
\{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{1}),\ldots,\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{w})\}\subseteq M
for a set of representatives of
M/M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
(which is finite by the fact that
K/K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}
is finite). By similar computations as in
Equation

118
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M}\bar{\mathcal{D}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{w}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{i})+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{w}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{i})]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu)\in M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{w}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{i})]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}-\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu))d\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{w}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{i})]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}})d\mathbf{a}

(122)

Now we use that
{\mathbb{S}}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
do not share primes, and that
{\mathbb{S}}
generates the ray class group
\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
, so that we can split up the space

{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}=\mathbb{R}\cdot\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\cap\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})+F_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}},

(123)

with a compact
F_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
. The set
{\mathbb{S}}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
not sharing primes is essential for the fundamental domain
F_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
to be compact (which is required for the current proof); indeed, if
{\mathbb{S}}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}

would
share a prime,
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\cap\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})
would be of rank stictly less than that of
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}}
, resulting in a
F_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
in
Equation

123
of infinite volume.

For
{\mathbb{S}}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
not sharing a prime divisor, one could construct
F_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
as follows. Pick a set of ideals
A=\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{a}_{w}\}
(with all prime factors in
{\mathbb{S}}
) that are representatives of the ray class group
\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
. Next, define
V
to be the Voronoi fundamental domain of
\Log(K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1}\cap\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})\subseteq H
. Then
F_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}=\{\llparenthesis\mathfrak{a}\rrparenthesis+x\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{a}\in A\mbox{ and }x\in V\}
is an example of a fundamental domain satisfying
Equation

123
.

Similar computations as in
Equation

119
then show that
Equation

122
equals (where
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)
sums over
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})
)

\displaystyle=\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}}\!\sum_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)}\!\sum_{i=1}^{w}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathbf{a}}[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{i})]\cdot\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}})d\mathbf{a}dt

(124)

Since
\{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{1}),\ldots,\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{w})\}
are different representatives in
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})/\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})
(indeed, if
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{1})-\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{2})\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})
, we have
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{1})-\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mu_{2})\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})\cap M
, contradiction), we can apply Hölders inequality again, yielding an upper bound of

\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\!\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\max_{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)\in\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})}\mathcal{G}_{\varsigma}(\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{1}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta)+M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}})d\mathbf{a}dt\leq\frac{1}{1+e^{\pi/c^{2}}}

whenever
\varsigma\geq c\cdot\rr(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, since
M_{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}+\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m}_{0},1})
is a lattice in
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.
∎

18.5.4.
About the closeness of the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’

In this part, we show that the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’ and the ordinary one
are close in statistical distance. Before we do that, we first need a
lemma that shows that
N
, the discretization parameter in
{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}
in
Algorithm

4

satisfies some bound.

Lemma 18.12
.

Let
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
as used in
Algorithm

3
. Then we have

40000\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}\cdot n^{5/2}\leq\omega^{n}\cdot\exp(11+16\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+9n^{2})=N,

where
N
is as in line
3
in
Algorithm

4
.

Proof.

Let
r=48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and
\mathsf{b}
are as chosen in
Algorithm

3
. The largest value
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
can have is whenever
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
is the product of the prime ideals of norm smaller than
x\leq\tfrac{3}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|
(by line
3
of
Algorithm

3
and Minkowski’s theorem, which tells that
\log|\Delta_{K}|\geq\log(\pi/2)n\geq 0.4n
). Hence, using
Lemma

11.4
with
x\leq\tfrac{3}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|
, using that
\sqrt{x}(\log(x)/(2\pi)+2)\leq 3x
and
\sqrt{x}(\log(x)^{2}/(8\pi)+2\leq 3x
(for
x>0.5
),

\displaystyle\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq x+\sqrt{x}\left(\left(\frac{\log x}{2\pi}+2\right)\log|\Delta_{K}|+\left(\frac{(\log x)^{2}}{8\pi}+2\right)n\right)

(125)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{x}_{\leq\tfrac{3}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|}+\underbrace{3x\log|\Delta_{K}|}_{\leq\tfrac{9}{2}\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|}+\underbrace{3xn}_{\leq\tfrac{9}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\tfrac{3}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|}\leq 13\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|.

(126)

By taking logarithms and using that
\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\leq 13\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|
, that
\log(\mathsf{b}^{2n^{2}/\mathsf{b}})\leq n^{2}
, that
n^{7n/2}\leq e^{3n^{2}}
, and that
48^{n}\leq e^{4n^{2}}
, we have that

\displaystyle r^{n}

\displaystyle=(48\cdot\omega\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot n^{7/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\frac{1}{n}})^{n}

\displaystyle\leq\omega^{n}\cdot e^{4n^{2}+n^{2}+3n^{2}}\cdot e^{\frac{3}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|+13\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|}\leq\omega^{n}\cdot e^{8n^{2}}e^{15\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|}.

Hence, using
40000\leq e^{11}
and
n^{5/2}\leq e^{n^{2}}
, we obtain

40000\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}\cdot n^{5/2}\leq\omega^{n}\cdot\exp(11+16\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+9n^{2})=N.

∎

Recall that the ‘continuous version of
Algorithm

4
’ means:
Algorithm

4

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}a_{\mathfrak{p}}\llparenthesis\mathfrak{p}\rrparenthesis+\sum_{\nu}b_{\nu}\llparenthesis\nu\rrparenthesis\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}},\varsigma},\mbox{ and put }y={\Exp}_{\infty}({\mathbf{a}}_{\infty}).

Lemma 18.13
.

The statistical distance of the output distributions of

and the ‘continuous version of

.

Proof.

The output distribution of the ‘continuous version of

whereas the output distribution of the ordinary one can be written as

\sum_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}}[\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}},\varsigma}(\ddot{\mathbf{a}}),

which is
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}
-close (see

) to

\sum_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}}[\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}},\varsigma}(\ddot{\mathbf{a}}),

(128)

where the approximation

is caused by the computation of

in line

.
Writing

for a fundamental domain of

\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left\|\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\sum_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a}}[\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}},\varsigma}(\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a})-|F|^{-1}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}}[\makebox[8.61108pt]{{${-}$}}]\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}},\varsigma}(\ddot{\mathbf{a}})d\mathbf{a}\right\|_{1}

By the trick
ab-a^{\prime}b^{\prime}=b(a-a^{\prime})-a^{\prime}(b^{\prime}-b)
, the triangle inequality, and the fact that the

, the quantity above can be bounded by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\underbrace{\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\sum_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}}\|\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a}}-\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}}\|_{1}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}},\varsigma}(\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a})d\mathbf{a}}_{(A)}

(129)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\underbrace{\int_{\mathbf{a}\in F}\sum_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}\in{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}}}|\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}},\varsigma}(\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a})-|F|^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{{\Div_{K,{\mathbb{S}},N}},\varsigma}(\ddot{\mathbf{a}})|d\mathbf{a}}_{(B)}

(130)

By property

17.8
(

), we have that

is almost Lipschitz-continuous in
\mathbf{a}
,

\|\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a}}-\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}}\|_{1}\leq 3\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot r^{n}\cdot n^{2}\cdot\|\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a}-\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}\|+\tfrac{1}{200},

and
\|\ddot{\mathbf{a}}+\mathbf{a}-\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}\|\leq\|\tilde{\ddot{\mathbf{a}}}-\ddot{\mathbf{a}}\|+\|\mathbf{a}\|\leq 2\sqrt{n}/N
by construction
21
21

21

We have
\|\Log(y\cdot z^{-1})\|\leq\sqrt{n}\max_{\nu}\log(y_{\nu}z^{-1}_{\nu})\leq\sqrt{n}\max_{\nu}|y_{\nu}z_{\nu}^{-1}-1|\leq\sqrt{n}(\min_{\nu}z_{\nu})^{-1}\cdot\max_{\nu}|y_{\nu}-z_{\nu}|\leq\sqrt{n}/N
, by line

of

4
.

of

on line

for

.

by (using

For the bound on

, note that the sole continuity is on the

where we understand the inclusion

.
