18.6.
Conclusion: the number of samples that generate the full
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice

The concluding theorem of this section quantifies how many samples
of the output distribution
\bar{\mathcal{D}}
of
Algorithm

4

one needs to draw in order to get a generating set of the logarithmic unit lattice with high probability.
The proof of the theorem uses the fact that this output distribution is evenly distributed and concentrated
and applies
Proposition

18.2
on distributions over lattices.

Theorem 18.14
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
and some
B_{\mathrm{max}}=\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}})
,
where

\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}=\min\Big(\rho_{K},\max(e^{\log^{\frac{2}{3}}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log^{\frac{2}{3}}(\log|\Delta_{K}|)},e^{n^{\frac{2}{3}}\log^{\frac{4}{3}}(n)})\Big),

such that the following holds.
For every number field
K
, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and for every set of primes
{\mathbb{S}}
that generates the ray class group
\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
of
K
, does not contain primes dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and contains all primes (coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
) of norm
\leq B_{\mathrm{max}}
,
the probability that

6\cdot k+6\cdot(|{\mathbb{S}}|+\mathbbm{r})\cdot\Big[\log((|{\mathbb{S}}|+\mathbbm{r})\cdot\varsigma)+C\log\log|\Delta_{K}|\Big]

samples from
Algorithm

4
generate the entire logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})

is at least
1-e^{-k}
. Here,
|{\mathbb{S}}|+\mathbbm{r}=\dim(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
(with
\mathbbm{r}=\dim(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}))
) and
\varsigma
is from line
3
of
Algorithm

4
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

18.11
and
Lemma

18.10
the output distribution of
Algorithm

4
on
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
is
2/3
-evenly distributed and
(R_{0},0)
-concentrated for

R_{0}=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)+3\varsigma\cdot n_{0}
,
where
\varsigma
is the deviation of the Gaussian distribution as in
Algorithm

4
, and where
n_{0}=|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}=\dim(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))+1
.

So, one can apply
Proposition

18.2
with
p=\tfrac{2}{3}
and
q=0
(using
(1-p-q)^{-1}=3
) to deduce that the number of samples
S
required from the output distribution of
Algorithm

4
to generate the entire log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice satisfies, for any
\alpha>0
,

\Pr\left[S>3\cdot(2+\alpha)(t+n_{1})\right]\leq e^{-\alpha(t+n_{1})/2},

(132)

where
n_{1}=|{\mathbb{S}}|+\mathbbm{r}=\dim(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))=n_{0}-1
and
22
22

22

We use here that
\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))=h_{K}\cdot R_{K}\cdot\sqrt{n_{\mathbb{R}}+n_{\mathbb{C}}}\geq R_{K}\geq 0.206
, since
R_{K}\geq 0.206
uniformly for all number fields
[
32
, Theorem B]
.

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=n_{1}\cdot\log_{2}(R_{0})-\log_{2}(\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})))

\displaystyle\leq n_{1}\cdot\log_{2}(R_{0})+2

\displaystyle\leq n_{1}[\log(n_{0}\varsigma)+O(\log\log|\Delta_{K}|)].

Taking
\alpha=2k/(t+n_{1})
, we have
\alpha(t+n_{1})/2=k
, and

3\cdot(2+\alpha)(t+n_{1})=6(t+n_{1})+3\alpha(t+n_{1})=6(t+n_{1})+6k.

Hence, replacing the above formula into
Equation

132
, we obtain

\Pr\left[S>6(t+n_{1})+6k\right]\leq e^{-k}.

Therefore, the probability that
6(t+n_{1})+6k
samples from the output distribution of
Algorithm

4
generate the entire logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})

is at least
1-e^{-k}
. Replacing
t+n_{1}
by the larger
n_{1}[\log(n_{1}\varsigma)+C(\log\log|\Delta_{K}|)]
(for some absolute constant, and hiding
n_{1}
under this constant by increasing the constant slightly if needed),
yields the final claim.
∎

This theorem roughly states that the number of samples drawn from
\bar{\mathcal{D}}
(the output distribution of
Algorithm

4
)
for the sampled vectors to generate the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
(with high probability) only needs to exceed a small multiple of the dimension of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. In other words, if one assembles sufficiently many of such samples (quasi-linearly in the dimension), they generate the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice except for an exponentially small probability.

In almost all applications involving
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, a generating set with high probability is not sufficient. Instead, often a
basis
of the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-units is demanded (or, equivalently, a
fundamental set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units
, see
Section

19.1
); and, additionally, no probabilistic error is allowed (i.e., one wants to be sure that the basis at hand truly is a basis of the full log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice).

To resolve these issues, one needs to post-process the set of generators by lattice reduction techniques. This is the subject of
Section

19
.

19.
Post-processing phase

19.1.
Introduction

Up to now, the algorithm of this paper allows to provably sample
{\mathbb{S}}
-units
in
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
with a certain probability. Furthermore, a sufficient number of these

{\mathbb{S}}
-units samples will generate the
entire
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
with overwhelming probability. To complete
the entire algorithm, two tasks remain to be done.

(I)

An algorithm to
verify
that the sampled elements truly generate all of
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
and not a subgroup thereof
23
23

23

One could say that this algorithm changes the probabilistic algorithm of this paper from a Monte Carlo algorithm (a randomized algorithm whose output may be incorrect with a small probability, but with bounded running time) to a Las Vegas algorithm (a randomized algorithm that always gives correct results, but whose running time is a random variable)
.

(II)

An algorithm to compute a
fundamental system of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units
of
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
out of these generators, i.e., a sequence of elements
(\eta_{j})_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
with
j\in\{1,\ldots,\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\}
such that every element
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
is a power product of these elements (and a possible root of unity):
\eta=\tau_{K}\prod_{j=1}^{\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}\eta_{j}^{m_{j}}
for some
m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
; where
\tau_{K}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
is some root of unity
24
24

24

As roots of unity vanish under the Logarithmic embedding into
H
and are easily computed, we omit them often in discussions.
.

19.2.
Solving both (I) and (II) by computing bases

Let
G\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
be a finite set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units. Then its associated lattice

\mathcal{L}_{G}:=\sum_{\eta\in G}\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta)\cdot\mathbb{Z}=\{\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta^{\prime})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \eta^{\prime}\in\langle G\rangle\}

satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{G}\subseteq\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, i.e.,
it is a
sublattice
of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

An immediate observation is now that both the tasks (I) and (II) can be solved
25
25

25

Note that we assume that the primes in
{\mathbb{S}}
generate the ideal class group.

if one is able to compute a
basis
of the lattice
\mathcal{L}_{G}
. Indeed, such a
basis allows to retrieve the rank and the determinant of
\mathcal{L}_{G}
. If
\rank(\mathcal{L}_{G})=\rank(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))={n_{\mathbb{C}}}+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}|-1

and
\covol(\mathcal{L}_{G})=\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))=R_{K}\cdot h_{K}\cdot\sqrt{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}}
(see
Equation

9
), we deduce that

\mathcal{L}_{G}=\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
(solving (I)) and that the basis of
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is a basis for
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
(solving (II)).

So, the naive approach would be to create the matrix
\mathbf{G}
whose rows
consist of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta)
with
\eta\in G
; the rows of this matrix
then generate
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{L}_{G})
. Apply LLL-reduction to
\mathbf{G}

to obtain a basis of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{L}_{G})
and compute its rank and determinant.
Unfortunately, this approach is not directly applicable.

19.3.
The challenge of approximate matrices

The challenge lies in the fact that the infinite places
of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta)
for
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
can only be
approximated
. Indeed,
these infinite places consists of logarithms of algebraic numbers,
whose can
only be computed with a certain precision.

This has as a consequence that the row-oriented matrix
\mathbf{G}
consisting
of the (exact) elements
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta)
for
\eta\in G
is computationally
out of reach. Instead one is forced to work with
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
, an
approximation

of
\mathbf{G}
. This rational matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
consists of elements
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta)+\epsilon_{\eta}
for
\eta\in G
and
\|\epsilon_{\eta}\|_{1}<\epsilon
.
Given the set
G
, we can compute
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
in time
|G|\cdot\poly(\log(\epsilon))
.
Of course, we have

\|\mathbf{G}-\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon.

where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is the induced
\infty
-norm on matrices
26
26

26

We have
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{\infty}=\max_{j}\|\mathbf{a}_{j}\|_{1}
where
\mathbf{a}_{j}
are the rows of
\mathbf{A}
.
.

19.4.
The Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm

For sufficiently (but still feasibly) small
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, an algorithm by Buchmann, Pohst and Kessler
[
18
,
19
]
allows to compute an integer matrix
\mathbf{M}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{M}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
is an approximate basis that satisfies

\|\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\|_{\infty}\leq C\cdot\epsilon\penalty\ \mbox{ and }\penalty\ \|\mathbf{M}\|_{\infty}\leq C

for some very large, but sufficiently bounded
C
(depending on
\|\mathbf{G}\|_{\infty}
and invariants of the lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
) and where
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{G}
is a well-conditioned basis for the lattice
\mathcal{L}_{G}
generated by
\mathbf{G}
.

Though this algorithm is analyzed well
[
18
]
, its analysis can only be applied whenever the rank of the lattice
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is known.
A slight variation of this analysis, in which the rank of
\mathcal{L}_{G}
is not required to be known beforehand, is described in
Section

23
.
Note that this analysis proves that the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm actually
computes
the rank of
\mathcal{L}_{G}
.

19.5.
Computing the determinant

So, the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm allows to compute a
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{B}+\epsilon^{\prime}X
close to a well-conditioned basis
\mathbf{B}
; say,

\|X\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\epsilon^{\prime}<1
is small.

Then, by a bound of Ipsen and Rehman
[
43
, Corollary 2.14]
, for sufficiently (but feasibly) small
\epsilon^{\prime}<1
, one
has

\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})\in[\tfrac{7}{8},\tfrac{9}{8}]\cdot\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}).

The determinant
\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
of the
full
log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice can be (multiplicatively) approximated by means of Euler products (e.g.,
[
5
]
or
Proposition

2.9
). In other words,
we can efficiently compute a
D\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that

D\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]\cdot\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})).

The index
[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}):\mathcal{L}_{G}]
can then be approximated by computing
\sqrt{\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})}/D
, and satisfies
27
27

27

We have
\sqrt{\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})}\cdot c_{1}=\sqrt{\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})}
for
c_{1}\in[\sqrt{\tfrac{7}{8}},\sqrt{\tfrac{9}{8}}]
and
D=c_{2}\cdot\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
with
c_{2}\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]
. Therefore,
\sqrt{\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})}/D=(c_{1}/c_{2})\cdot\sqrt{\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})}/\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, with
(c_{1}/c_{2})\in[\sqrt{\tfrac{7}{8}}\cdot\tfrac{4}{5},\sqrt{\tfrac{9}{8}}\cdot\tfrac{4}{3}]\subseteq(0.74,1.42)
.

\sqrt{\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})}/D\in(0.74,1.42)\cdot[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}):\mathcal{L}_{G}].

One deduces that if
[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}):\mathcal{L}_{G}]\geq 2
, then
\sqrt{\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})}/D>1.48
. Contrarily, if
[\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}):\mathcal{L}_{G}]=1
, then
\sqrt{\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})}/D<1.42
, which allows us to distinguish the two cases.
Additionally, in the second case
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
is an approximate
basis of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

19.6.
Assembling a fundamental set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

Recall that
G=\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
is a set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units
and
\mathbf{G}=(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{j}))_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}}
its associated matrix of logarithmic
images. Assume that (by means of the algorithm in the text above) one has deduced that
G
indeed
generates
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
, i.e.,
\langle G\rangle=\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
.

By the algorithm of Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst in
Section

19.4
, we can find an
\mathbf{M}=(m_{ij})_{ij}

for which
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{G}
is a basis of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
. In other words, for
j=1,\ldots,\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
,

\mathbf{b}_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{ji}\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{i})=\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\beta_{j})\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ form a basis of $\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})$}.

As a consequence, by taking exponentials, for
j=1,\ldots,\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
,

\beta_{j}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\eta_{i}^{m_{ji}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ form a system of fundamental units of $\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}$}.

(133)

Note that the expression in
Equation

133
is in exact arithmetic (i.e., no precision issues). Namely, the elements
\eta_{i}\in G
are
written in the basis of the number ring and the exponents
m_{ji}
are integers from the integer matrix
\mathbf{M}
.

19.7.
A fundamental set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units by means of compact representation

Note that actually representing
\beta_{j}
in terms of basis elements of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
or
K

is generally computationally out of reach. Indeed, since the coefficients
m_{ij}
are rather large,
the computation of
\beta_{j}
(i.e., expanding the product in
Equation

133
) suffers from coefficient explosion.

This is the reason why the computation of a system of fundamental (
{\mathbb{S}}
)-units is
generally given in ‘compact representation’ (e.g.,
[
10
]
,
[
26
, §5.8.3]
). That just means that,
instead of giving the
\beta_{j}
in
Equation

133
by expanding its product and writing

\beta_{j}
in the basis of the number ring, one represents
\beta_{j}
by the formal product consisting of
m_{ji}
-th powers of
\eta_{i}
.

In other words, a compact representation of
(\beta_{j})_{j=1,\ldots,\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}

is just given by the pair
(\mathbf{M},G)
with
\mathbf{M}=(m_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times(\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))}
and
G=\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{k}\}
,
by which we represent, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})\}
,

\beta_{j}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\eta_{i}^{m_{ij}}.

The final output of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit algorithm of this paper is thus the pair
(\mathbf{M},G)
.

19.8.
Final post-processing theorem

A rigorous treatment of the post-processing phase can be found in
Sections

23
,
24
and
25
,
as well as the proof of the following theorem.
The techniques used in that treatment generally involve
well-known lattice reductions and computations, though for their rigorousness there is a heavy emphasis on numerical stability. For now, to stay close to the current subject manner, those numerical stability computations are thus deferred to Part
21.5
.

Theorem 19.1
.

Let
K
be a number field with degree
n
, and let
G=\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
a finite set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units.
There exists an algorithm that takes as input the set
G
28
28

28

The elements
\eta_{i}\in K
are represented as vectors of rational coordinates in a fixed
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-basis, as explained in
Section

2.6
.
and computes a system of fundamental
{\mathbb{S}}
-units for the subgroup

\langle G\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}

generated by
G
in compact representation, i.e., a pair
(\mathbf{N},G)
with
\mathbf{N}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times k}
such
that
\upsilon_{j}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\eta_{i}^{\mathbf{N}_{ji}}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
form
a system of fundamental
{\mathbb{S}}
-units for
\langle G\rangle
.

Additionally, this algorithm decides whether
\langle G\rangle=\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
or not,
and runs in time polynomial in
k
,
\log|\Delta_{K}|
,
|{\mathbb{S}}|
,
\log\big(\max_{j}\lVert\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{j})\rVert\big)
and
\max_{j}(\size(\eta_{j}))
.

20.
Generating the exceptional
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

20.1.
Introduction

In
Theorem

18.14
, the main result
of
Section

18
, the set of primes
{\mathbb{S}}

is assumed to contain no primes dividing the modulus
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.
This modulus
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
is defined in lines
2
-

8
of
Algorithm

3
, and
only depends on the Dedekind residue
\rho_{K}
, the degree
n=[K:\mathbb{Q}]

and the absolute discriminant
|\Delta_{K}|
.

In our end result, we would like to be able to compute
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
-units
for
any
set of primes
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
, without any restrictions. Indeed,
we do want to allow
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
to contain primes dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.
Hence, in this section we aim, for any
\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
,
for computing an
({\mathbb{S}}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}\})
-unit

\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}\}}^{\times}

that additionally satisfies
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\eta_{\mathfrak{p}})=1
.
Such an
({\mathbb{S}}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}\})
-unit we will then call an

exceptional
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit
.

By adding these exceptional
{\mathbb{S}}
-units
\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}
(for
\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
)
to a fundamental system of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, one gets a fundamental system
of
({\mathbb{S}}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{p}\text{ divides }\mathfrak{m}_{0}\})
-units. Hence computing
these
\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}
allows for computing a
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
unit group without
any restrictions on the prime ideals.
Note that, if
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=(1)
is trivial, no exceptional
{\mathbb{S}}
-units
exist, and the algorithm of this section can be omitted. Hence, throughout
this section we will assume that
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\neq(1)
.

20.2.
Algorithm for generating exceptional
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

In
Algorithm

5
, we describe how to compute an exceptional

{\mathbb{S}}
-unit in the case of
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\neq(1)
. The following lemma
shows that the running time of this algorithm is the same as
the running time of sampling a single
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit as in
Theorem

17.7
.

Algorithm 5

Computing an exceptional
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit

0:

(i)

An LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,

(ii)

a prime ideal
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, (we assume
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\neq(1)
)

(iii)

a set of prime ideals
{\mathbb{S}}
of
K
, that does not contain the primes dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
.

0:

\alpha\in{\mathfrak{q}}
and
(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{|{\mathbb{S}}|}_{\geq 0}
such that
\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}={\mathfrak{q}}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}

1:

define
\mathsf{b},\omega
as in
Algorithm

3
.

2:

repeat

3:

\alpha\leftarrow\textup{{Sample}}({\mathfrak{q}},y=1,\mathfrak{m}_{0}\cdot{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1},\mathsf{b},\omega)

(see
Theorem

13.2
)

4:

until

\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}
is
{\mathbb{S}}
-smooth

5:

compute
(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{{\mathbb{S}}}
such that
\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}

6:

return

(\alpha,(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}})
.

Proposition 20.1
.

There exists some
B_{\mathrm{max}}=\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}})
,
where

\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}=\min\Big(\rho_{K},\max(e^{\log^{\frac{2}{3}}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log^{\frac{2}{3}}(\log|\Delta_{K}|)},e^{n^{\frac{2}{3}}\log^{\frac{4}{3}}(n)})\Big),

such that the following holds.
Assume that
{\mathbb{S}}
contains all prime ideals coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
of norm
\leq B_{\mathrm{max}}
(where
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
is defined as in lines
2
-

8
of
Algorithm

3
).

Then, on input
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and the set
{\mathbb{S}}
,

Algorithm

5
outputs
(\alpha,(v_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}})\in{\mathfrak{q}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{|{\mathbb{S}}|}
such that

\alpha\mathcal{O}_{K}={\mathfrak{q}}\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\mathfrak{p}^{v_{\mathfrak{p}}}.

Furthermore,
Algorithm

3
runs in expected time

\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3}),\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}),\allowbreak\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\log|\Delta_{K}|).

Proof.

The proof can be copied from that of
Theorem

17.7
. The sole differences are the instantiation
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\cdot{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}
instead of
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, and the instantiations
{\mathfrak{a}}={\mathfrak{q}}
and
y=1
. The latter instantiations do not impact the proof, and since
\size({\mathfrak{q}})\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)\cdot x\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
by the definition of
x
in line
3
of
Algorithm

3
, the size of
{\mathfrak{q}}
can be omitted in the running time.

Note that in
Theorem

17.7
, the input ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}
is required to be coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. In the context of the current theorem,
{\mathfrak{q}}
is coprime to
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\cdot{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}
by construction, and hence the same reasoning as in
Theorem

17.7
applies.

The instantiation
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\cdot{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}
instead of
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
does impact the value of
N(\mathfrak{m}_{0})/\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
, but only by a factor
\frac{N({\mathfrak{q}})}{N({\mathfrak{q}})-1}\in[1,2]
, which only impacts the success probability by a factor in
[1,2]
and hence does not impact the final running time. Therefore, the running time of
Proposition

20.1
equals that of
Theorem

17.7
, with
\size({\mathfrak{a}})
and
\size(y)
deleted.
∎

21.
Full algorithm, final theorem and discussion

Algorithm 6

Computing a fundamental system of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units

0:

A number field
K

, an LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
and a set
{\mathbb{S}}
of prime ideals of
K
.

0:

A pair
(\mathbf{M},G)
with
\mathbf{M}=(m_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}
and
G=\{\eta_{1},\ldots\eta_{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
such that
\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}\}
is a fundamental set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units,
where

\beta_{j}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\eta_{i}^{m_{ji}}.

1:

Put
G=\emptyset

2:

Define
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
as in lines
2
-

8
of
Algorithm

3
.

3:

Define
\mathbb{T}={\mathbb{S}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{p}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}\}
.

4:

repeat

5:

Apply
Algorithm

4
with input
\mathbb{T}
, yielding
(\alpha,(a_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{T}})
.

6:

Add the output
\alpha
(and its factorization) to
G^{\prime}
.

7:

until
the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm as in
Section

19
finds that
G^{\prime}
generates
\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathbb{T}}^{\times}
.

8:

Let
(\mathbf{M}^{\prime},G^{\prime})
be the output of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm, as in
Theorem

19.1
. Write
G^{\prime}=\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{k^{\prime}}\}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}=(m^{\prime}_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k^{\prime}\times\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}
.

9:

Compute for any
{\mathfrak{q}}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, an exceptional
\mathbb{T}
-unit
\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathbb{T}\cup\{{\mathfrak{q}}\}}^{\times}
, using
Algorithm

5
.

10:

Order
\{\eta_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ {\mathfrak{q}}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}\}=\{\eta_{k^{\prime}+1},\ldots,\eta_{k}\}
and extend the set
G^{\prime}
to
G=\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{k^{\prime}},\eta_{k^{\prime}+1},\ldots,\eta_{k}\}
. Put
m_{ji}=m^{\prime}_{ji}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathbb{T}}^{\times})\}
, put
m_{ji}=\delta_{ji}
(Kronecker delta) for
i\in\{k^{\prime}+1,\ldots,k\}
. Set
\mathbf{M}=(m_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times\rank(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})}

11:

return

(\mathbf{M},G)
.

Proposition 21.1

(ERH)
.

There is a randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}
(
Algorithm

6
) for which the following holds.
Let
K
be a number field and let

\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}=\min(\rho_{K},\max(e^{\log^{\frac{2}{3}}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log^{\frac{2}{3}}(\log|\Delta_{K}|)},e^{n^{\frac{2}{3}}\cdot\log^{\frac{4}{3}}(n)})).

Note that
\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}\leq L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(2/3+o(1))
.

Then there exists a bound

B=\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}).

such that if the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
is given a set of primes
{\mathbb{S}}
of
K
containing all primes with norm bounded by
B
and generating the ray class group
\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
of
K
,
the algorithm
\mathcal{A}

computes a fundamental system of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units of
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
in compact representation, in expected time

\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\size({\mathbb{S}})).

Proof.

The correctness of the algorithm follows from the following two arguments. By the ‘until’ criterion in step
7
, that requires
G^{\prime}
to generate the
\mathbb{T}
-unit group, where
\mathbb{T}={\mathbb{S}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{p}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}\}
.
Since the exceptional
\mathbb{T}
-units
\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}
satisfy
\ord_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\eta_{\mathfrak{q}})=1
and
\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
, adding these indeed result in a fundamental set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units.

The running time of the algorithm is dominated by the repeat loop and the computation of the exceptional units. We will use that
\size(\mathbb{T})\leq\size({\mathbb{S}})
.

In the repeat loop,
Algorithm

4
and the ‘post-processing’ Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm take expected time
\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),\allowbreak L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\allowbreak\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}))
(see
Lemma

18.9
,
Theorem

17.7
,
Theorem

19.1
, and the bound

\varsigma\leq 3\max(\sqrt{\log({n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}|)},\allowbreak\tilde{\rr})\allowbreak\leq\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}))

from
Proposition

16.10
). The number of repetitions is expected to be within
\widetilde{O}(|{\mathbb{S}}|\log\log|\Delta_{K}|)=\poly(\size({\mathbb{S}}))
(see
Theorem

18.14
).

The run time of computing all exceptional units is, by
Proposition

20.1
, at most
\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\tfrac{1}{2}),L_{n^{n}}(\tfrac{2}{3}),\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}),\allowbreak\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\log|\Delta_{K}|)
, where we use that the number of prime ideals dividing
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
can be at most
O(\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})))=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
(see
Equation

114
).

Therefore, the expected running time of the overall computation is
\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),\allowbreak L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\allowbreak\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}))
as well.
∎

The following theorem is the same as
Proposition

21.1
except that the set of primes
{\mathbb{S}}
can now be arbitrary.

Theorem 21.2

(ERH)
.

There is a probabilistic algorithm which, on input
a number field
K
and a set of primes
{\mathbb{S}}
of
K
,
computes a fundamental system of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units of
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
in compact representation, in expected time

\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\size({\mathbb{S}})).

where
\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}=\min(\rho_{K},\max(e^{\log^{\frac{2}{3}}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log^{\frac{2}{3}}(\log|\Delta_{K}|)},e^{n^{\frac{2}{3}}\cdot\log^{\frac{4}{3}}(n)}))
. Note that
\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}\leq L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(2/3+o(1))
.

Proof.

Extend
{\mathbb{S}}
to a set
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}\supset{\mathbb{S}}
of primes, containing all primes with norm below
B=\poly(\max(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2,1),L_{n^{n}}(2/3,1),\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}))
and generating the ray class group
\Cl_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}_{0}}
of
K
, as in
Proposition

21.1
. Note that, since
\log(\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0}))=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)
(see
Equation

114
) and by Bach’s bound
O(\log(|\Delta_{K}|^{2}N(\mathfrak{m}_{0})))
for ray class groups
[
4
]
this is asymptotically clearly satisfied.

Then, apply
Algorithm

6
to get the pair
(\mathbf{M},G)
representing a fundamental system of
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
-units. This costs expected time
\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),\allowbreak L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\allowbreak\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}))
, since
\size({\mathbb{S}}^{\prime})=\poly(B,\size({\mathbb{S}}))
.

As a preparation for the Hermite normal form algorithm, we put the infinite primes and the primes of
{\mathbb{S}}
to the very left in the representation of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\gamma)
, i.e.,

\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\gamma):=\Big(\underbrace{\Log(\gamma)}_{\mbox{ \footnotesize{infinite places} }},\underbrace{(-\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\gamma))_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}}_{\mbox{ \footnotesize{places in ${\mathbb{S}}$} }},(-\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\gamma))_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}\backslash{\mathbb{S}}}\Big)

Put
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
for the row-matrix consisting of the approximations of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}}(\gamma_{j})
for
j=1,\ldots,|G|
, where
G=\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{|G|}\}
. We then can write
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{M}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
for an approximate basis for the log-
{\mathbb{S}}^{\prime}
-unit lattice (with the infinite places and the places of
{\mathbb{S}}
at the very left).

We now apply a Hermite normal form algorithm to
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
, leading to
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}=\mathbf{U}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
that is in a lower-triangular shape. Hence, the first
|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1
rows must then represent the Log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-units, as it is the dimension of
{\mathbb{S}}
plus the dimension of the logarithmic unit lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
.
Hence, the
{\mathbb{S}}
-units can be given in compact representation
(\mathbf{N},G)
with
\mathbf{N}
being the first
|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1
rows of
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{M}
. I.e., a fundamental set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units is

\eta_{k}=\prod_{j=1}^{|G|}\gamma_{j}^{\mathbf{N}_{kj}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ for }k\in\{1,\ldots,|{\mathbb{S}}|+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1\}.

Since the running time of the Hermite normal form is polynomial in the dimensions of the input matrix and the maximum
\size(c_{ij})
of the coefficients
29
29

29

We might have to scale up the coefficients of the infinite places to integers (column-wise), but that does not impact significantly the size of the coefficients.

[
72
]
, we simply deduce that computing this Hermite normal form also takes time
\poly(L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2),\allowbreak L_{n^{n}}(2/3),\allowbreak\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}},\allowbreak\size({\mathbb{S}}))
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Figure 2
.
A depiction of the (dominant part of the) provable running time compared to the (dominant part of the) heuristic running time for number fields
K
, depending on
\log(\rho_{K})
and the value of
\log(|\Delta_{K}|)
compared to the degree
n
.

The blue line corresponds to the heuristic running time claimed in
[
10
]
.

The provable running time of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit algorithm of the present work varies between the blue and red lines, depending on
\log(\rho_{K})
. The running time can be found in this graph by first searching the point
\log|\Delta_{K}|
(in terms of powers of the degree
n
) on the
x
-axis, and then read off, depending on the size of
\log(\rho_{K})
, where between the blue and red line the (logarithm of the) run time must be.
For number fields with
\log(\rho_{K})\leq n^{2/3}
, the running time is dictated by the blue line, and when
\log(\rho_{K})\geq n
, the complexity is dictated by the red line.

21.1.
On the complexity of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithm

Now that we have proved the main result of this part of the article, we turn to its meta-analysis, comparing it with former heuristic claims.
Both the provable and the heuristic running time of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithm
depend heavily and quite intricately on both the parameters
\log(|\Delta_{K}|)
and the degree
n
of
the number field
K
at hand (see
Figure

2
). We will explain here the origin of the various changes in the running times, starting with that of the heuristic algorithm in
Section

21.3
and ending with the running time of the provable algorithm of this paper in
Section

21.4
.

In this explanation of the running times, we will only discuss the the running time in terms of
their ‘dominant part’, which are of the shape
\exp(\widetilde{O}(\log^{\delta}|\Delta_{K}|))
or
\exp(\widetilde{O}(n^{\delta}))

for some
\delta\in[0,1]
. Also, in
Figure

2
only this ‘dominant part’ is shown. It is useful
to think of, for example,
\exp(\widetilde{O}(\log^{\delta}|\Delta_{K}|))
of being of ‘approximately the same magnitude’ as
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\delta,1)
, while keeping in mind that
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(\delta,1)
is a much more precise way of estimating complexities.
For the the more intricate analysis, we refer to the proof of
Theorem

17.7
.

21.2.
A general formula for the complexity of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithm

At the very core, most
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithms consist of the following two steps (in which
{\mathbb{S}}=\{\mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime ideal}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B\}
):

(I)

Assemble
R
‘relations’ in the shape of
B
-smooth elements of norm
\leq M
. Those are just elements of
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
.

(II)

Post-process these
R
‘relations’ to get a system of fundamental
{\mathbb{S}}
-units using lattice reduction techniques. Such a system of fundamental
{\mathbb{S}}
-units can be seen as a (multiplicative) ‘basis’ of
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
.

21.2.1.
Part (I)

The
B
-smooth elements are sampled
in a probabilistic way, by ‘picking an element of norm
\leq M
at random and hoping it will be smooth’. One such attempt costs
1
sampling of a norm
\leq M
element (denoted
S_{M}
) and
1
check for being
B
-smooth (denoted
C_{B}
). Let us denote
p_{M,B}
for the probability of success, namely
that such sampling indeed yields a
B
-smooth element. Then, the running time for sampling a
single
B
-smooth element (a ‘relation’) with constant success probability takes time

(S_{M}+C_{B})\cdot p_{M,B}^{-1}
. To obtain
R
such relations, one obtains for part (I) an expected complexity of

\poly\big(R(S_{M}+C_{B})\cdot p_{M,B}^{-1}\big).

21.2.2.
Part (II)

The post-processing step consists of lattice reduction on a sort-of ‘valuation matrix’
of the gathered relations (elements in
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
). Namely, each of these
R
elements
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}

are decomposed as

\big[\Log(\eta),(v_{\mathfrak{p}}(\eta))_{\mathfrak{p}\in{\mathbb{S}}}\big]\in\mathbb{R}^{{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1}\times\mathbb{Z}^{{\mathbb{S}}},

where each vector entry is bounded by
30
30

30

This is not the case per se, a fixed norm element can have arbitrarily large entries of the logarithmic embedding. Though, here, in this quick explanation, we assume that this is the case.

\poly(\log(M))
, where
M
is the maximum norm of the elements.
Reducing an
R\times({\mathbb{S}}+{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}-1)
-matrix like this costs, roughly said, time at most
\poly(R\cdot B\cdot\log(M))
(where we use
B\approx|{\mathbb{S}}|
). Here we assume that the decomposition of the elements into prime ideals has already be done in the ‘check for being smooth’ step in Part (I).

21.2.3.
The general formula for the complexity

Adding the complexities, using that
R\geq B
and assuming that
\log(M)
is negligible
compared to
B
, we obtain a general formula for the complexity, by effectively ignoring the costs of Part (II):

\poly\big(R\cdot(S_{M}+C_{B})\cdot p_{M,B}^{-1}\big),

(134)

where
S_{M}
is the cost of sampling a random element in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
of norm
\leq M
, where
C_{B}
is the
costs of checking whether an element is
B
-smooth, where
p_{M,B}
is the probability that a randomly sampled norm
\leq M
element in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is
B
-smooth, and where
R
is the (average)
number of relations (elements in
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
) needed to generate
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
.

21.3.
Heuristic running time of Biasse & Fieker
[
10
]

For the heuristic running time
[
10
]
, there are essentially two ‘regimes’ of number fields. Namely, ‘regime A’, the fields for which
n\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|\leq n^{4/3}
and ‘regime B’ the fields for which
n^{4/3}\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|<\infty
(see the blue ‘Heuristic’ line in
Figure

2
). We will explain, with the general formula of
Equation

134
and the heuristic assumptions of
[
10
]
, why
these regimes pop up.

Heuristic 1 (and 2) of
[
10
]
roughly state that they assume that
the probability
p_{M,B}
of a random element of norm
\leq M
being
B
-smooth equals

p_{M,B}=\exp\Big(-\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{\log(M)}{\log(B)}\Big)\Big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ (heuristic)}

Additionally, Heuristic 3 of
[
10
]
, roughly states that the number
of elements (‘relations’)
R
required to generate the full
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit group
\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}
,
is only slightly larger than
B
, i.e.,

R=\widetilde{O}(B)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ (heuristic)}

Using trivial trial division for
B
-smooth checking yields
C_{B}=O(B)
. One of the main
improvements of
[
10
]
is the use of stronger lattice reduction techniques (BKZ-
\mathsf{b}
)
to sample small norm elements. More precisely, in their paper, they roughly take
M=e^{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|
and
S_{M}=\widetilde{O}(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}})
; where
\mathsf{b}
is the block size
parameter of BKZ.

Filling in these parameters into the formula of
Equation

134
and simplifying adequately, we obtain

\poly\big(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}\cdot B\cdot p_{M,B}\big)=\exp\Big(\widetilde{O}\Big(\mathsf{b}+\log(B)+\frac{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}+\log|\Delta_{K}|}{\log(B)}\Big)\Big)

Optimizing the parameters
B
and
\mathsf{b}
, we quickly arrive at a complexity
31
31

31

A lower bound of this complexity is
\exp(\widetilde{O}(\log^{1/2}|\Delta_{K}|))

, which is
achieved when
\log B=\mathsf{b}=\log^{1/2}|\Delta_{K}|
and
n^{2}/\mathsf{b}\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|
, i.e.,
n^{2}\leq\log^{3/2}|\Delta_{K}|
. This explains the running time for ‘regime B’, in which
n^{4/3}\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|
.
In ‘regime A’, we have
n^{4/3}\geq\log|\Delta_{K}|
, for which a lower bound for the complexity is
\exp(\widetilde{O}(n^{2/3}))
which is achieved by taking
\log(B)=\mathsf{b}=n^{2/3}
.

of
\exp(\widetilde{O}(\log^{1/2}|\Delta_{K}|))

whenever
n^{4/3}\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|
and
\exp(\widetilde{O}(n^{2/3}))

whenever
n^{4/3}\geq\log|\Delta_{K}|
. This explains the differences in complexity in the two regimes.

Remark 21.3
.

Intuitively, these two regimes can be explained by the hardness
of sampling small-norm elements in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Recall that for a sample of an element of maximum norm
M=e^{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}}|\Delta_{K}|
one has to pay
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
.

For
|\Delta_{K}|
large compared to the degree (regime B), the extra factor
e^{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}}
in
M
does not impact the asymptotic size of
M
. Contrarily, if the discriminant is small (regime A), the factor
e^{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}}
in
M
becomes dominant. Therefore, the block size
\mathsf{b}
needs to be increased, resulting in a larger complexity.

To summarize, for large discriminants, it is the probability of sampling smooth elements that is the bottleneck of the algorithm, whereas for small discriminants it is the run time of BKZ.

21.4.
Provable running time of this paper

We revisit the running time of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit group computing algorithm,
but now without heuristics and only assuming the Generalized Riemann hypothesis.
The provable running time depends again on the parameters
n
and
\log|\Delta_{K}|
and fall into three regimes. Regime A are the number fields for which
n\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|\leq n^{3/2}
, regime B the number fields satisfying
n^{3/2}\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|\leq n^{2}
and regime C the number fields for which
n^{2}\leq\log|\Delta_{K}|<\infty
(see the red ‘Provable’ line in
Figure

2
). In the following we show how these different regimes arise.

In the algorithm of this paper the sampling probability for smooth elements has a provable lower bound from
Section

3
(see
Theorem

9.5
)

p_{M,B}\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{K}}\cdot e^{-\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\log M}{\log B}\right)}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ (provable)}

where
\rho_{K}=\lim_{s\rightarrow 1}(s-1)\zeta_{K}(s)
is the residue of the
Dedekind zeta function at
s=1
.
Additionally, in
Section

11
, we prove that taking

R=\widetilde{O}(B)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ (provable) }

is indeed sufficient to generate the full
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit group. Instantiating the
rest of the parameters like in the heuristic version (
Section

21.3
), we obtain the same running time,
with an extra factor
\rho_{K}
.

\exp\Big(\widetilde{O}\Big(\mathsf{b}+\log(B)+\log(\rho_{K})+\frac{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}+\log|\Delta_{K}|}{\log(B)}\Big)\Big)

Regime C

As we have
\log(\rho_{K})\leq n\log(\log(|\Delta_{K}|)/n)

[
53
]
, we can deduce,
with the same reasoning as in
Section

21.3
that the run time
is bounded by
\exp(\log^{1/2}|\Delta_{K}|)
whenever
\log|\Delta_{K}|\geq n^{2}
. Then, namely,
\log\rho_{K}\leq\widetilde{O}(\log^{1/2}|\Delta_{K}|)
and therefore
\rho_{K}
has essentially no significant influence on the running time. This
explains the complexity of
\exp(\widetilde{O}(\log^{1/2}|\Delta_{K}|))
for regime C in
Figure

2
.

Regime B

When
\log|\Delta_{K}|\leq n^{2}
, the running time of the provable algorithm possibly depends on
\rho_{K}
. As
\rho_{K}
can be exponentially large in
n
, the worst-case running time for this regime is
\exp(O(n))
. Note, however, that for this regime, depending on the magnitude of
\rho_{K}
, the running time can lie anywhere in the gray area of
Figure

2
.

Regime A

Whenever the discriminant is sufficiently small, namely,
\log|\Delta_{K}|\leq n^{3/2}
, one can apply a trick to significantly diminish the influence of the residue
\rho_{K}
on the running time.

Instead of sampling random elements in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, one samples random elements

coprime with some modulus ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
that is the product of all primes below some bound
X
. This sampling is done in a specific way, namely, by first sampling
\tau\in(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathfrak{m}_{0})^{\times}
and then sampling a random element equivalent to
\tau
modulo
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. This causes
the sampling probability of smooth elements (coprime with
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
) to

increase
by a factor
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})/\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
,
but it also causes the maximum norm
M
of the sampled elements to increase
32
32

32

Because, instead of sampling in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we now have to sample in the shifted lattice
\tau+\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, which has covolume
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
times the covolume of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

by a factor
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
, i.e.,

p_{M,B}\geq\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}\cdot e^{-\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{\log M+\log\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\log B}\Big)}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ (provable)}

(135)

with
M=2^{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}}|\Delta_{K}|
. Using the fact that
\frac{\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})}{\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot\rho_{K}}\geq\exp(-\widetilde{O}(\log|\Delta_{K}|/\sqrt{X}))
(see
Proposition

11.2
), and
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\approx\exp(\widetilde{O}(X+\log|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{X}))
(see
Lemma

11.4
),
we can rewrite
Equation

135
:

p_{M,B}\geq e^{-\widetilde{O}\Big(\frac{\log|\Delta_{K}|}{\sqrt{X}}+\frac{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}+\log|\Delta_{K}|+X+\log|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{X}}{\log B}\Big)}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ (provable)}.

(136)

Plugging in this probability lower bound into the general formula, the logarithm
of the running time becomes, asymptotically,

\widetilde{O}\left(\mathsf{b}+\log(B)+\frac{n^{2}/\mathsf{b}+\log|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\sqrt{X}}{\log(B)}+\frac{\log|\Delta_{K}|}{\sqrt{X}}\right).

(137)

This has as asymptotic lower bound
\widetilde{O}(\log^{2/3}|\Delta_{K}|)
, attained
when
X=\log^{2/3}|\Delta_{K}|
and
\log(B)=\log^{2/3}|\Delta_{K}|
.
Note that this trick is only useful whenever
\log^{2/3}|\Delta_{K}|\leq\log\rho_{K}\leq n\log(\log|\Delta_{K}|/n)
,
otherwise the approach as in regime B (whose running time depends on
\rho_{K}
) would be faster. So,
surely whenever
\log^{2/3}|\Delta_{K}|\leq n
, i.e.,
\log|\Delta_{K}|\leq n^{3/2}
, this trick could outperform
regime B (if
\rho_{K}
is large), which explains regime A of the provable running time (see
Figure

2
).

21.5.
Discussion

Is the dependency of the running time on
\rho_{K}
innate?

The (dominant parts of the) provable expected running time of our
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation
algorithm is depicted in
Figure

2
depending
on the parameters
\log(\rho_{K})
and
\log|\Delta_{K}|
compared to the degree
n
.
For a fixed number field with
\log|\Delta_{K}|=n^{\delta}
for
\delta\in[1,2]

the provable expected running time can be anywhere where the line
x=n^{\delta}
intersects the shaded area, depending on the size of
\rho_{K}
. For small
\rho_{K}
, this running time can be equal to the heuristic one (the bottom blue line), whereas for large
\rho_{K}
, it might be equal to the worst-case provable one (the top red line).
As this quantity
\rho_{K}
is computable by Euler products.

The main question in this discussion is:
does
\rho_{K}
really have this influence on the running time
or is it merely an artifact of the lack of
tight bounds on smooth ideal densities?

Namely, there are asymptotic formulae
[
75
]
(see also
Equation

104
) indicating that, for
u=\log(x)/\log(B)
,

\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{B}}[x]\geq u^{-u}

but, generally, nothing is said about what the smallest
x\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is
for which this holds. For our purposes, we would like this smallest
x
to be at most
O(|\Delta_{K}|)
(and
B=\exp(O(\log^{1/2}(|\Delta_{K}|)))
). This is not something we could deduce from
asymptotic results. Therefore, we chose to use a combinatorial result (
Lemma

14.1
), which shows that, for every
x>\log(B)\geq 2\log\log|\Delta_{K}|
,

\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{B}}[x]\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{K}\cdot B\cdot(4\log(B))^{u}}u^{-u}.

This lower bound has as an advantage that it is explicit and holds for small values of
x
(in particular the range around
|\Delta_{K}|
we need). The disadvantage is the extra factor
\frac{1}{\rho_{K}}
, which increases, for large
\rho_{K}
, the provable running time of our algorithm drastically.

We could ask the main question of this discussion differently:

Are there bounds
B=\exp(O(\log^{1/2}(|\Delta_{K}|)))
,
X=O(|\Delta_{K}|)
, and
C^{-1}=\poly(B,\exp(\log^{1/2}|\Delta_{K}|))
such that
for all
x\geq X
, and for
u=\log(x)/\log(B)
, the inequality

\delta_{\mathcal{S}_{B}}[x]=\frac{|\{{\mathfrak{a}}\in\mathcal{S}_{B}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm({\mathfrak{a}})\leq x\}|}{\rho_{K}\cdot x}\geq C\cdot u^{-u}

holds?

If the answer is
yes
, our algorithm would reach the heuristic running time proposed by
[
10
]
.
If the answer is
no
, it is likely that the heuristic algorithm of
[
10
]
does not achieve its claimed running time, and instead has the same dependence on
\rho_{K}
as our algorithm.

The use of variants of the Riemann Hypothesis

An interesting follow-up question would be how the algorithms in this paper
would perform without assuming variants of the Riemann Hypothesis. To aid such research we now identify which type of Riemann Hypothesis is used for which task and discuss the possible impact of letting go of these hypotheses.

(1)

Random Walk Theorem.
In
Theorem

6.4
, the Riemann Hypothesis for all Hecke L-functions associated with a Hecke-character over a number field
K
(with fixed modulus
\mathfrak{m}
) is assumed. The Riemann Hypothesis in this form here seems indispensable for this proof, due to its influence on the bound on the Hecke eigenvalues (see
Equation

172
).

Without the Riemann Hypothesis, one might have to resort to an alternative, weaker bound, optimistically maybe of the following form, inspired by bounds
[
74
,
31
]
for the prime counting function:

\lambda_{\chi}=O\left(c_{K,\mathfrak{m}}\cdot e^{-\sqrt{\log(B)}}\right).

(138)

Here,
c_{K,\mathfrak{m}}
is some constant depending on
K
and
\mathfrak{m}
, and the big-O is, as it is throughout the entire present work, absolute. A bound as
Equation

138
for all eigenvalues of Hecke characters under Hecke operators, of which we do not know a proof
and what the constant
c_{K,\mathfrak{m}}
then might be, implies then that we are required to choose

B=O(e^{\log^{2}(c_{K,\mathfrak{m}})}),

which might or might not be useful, depending on the value of
c_{K,\mathfrak{m}}
.

(2)

Estimation of the factor
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})/\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
.
In
Section

11
, the Riemann hypothesis for the Dedekind zeta function of
K
is assumed to estimate the factor
\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})/\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{0})
. This estimation is used to diminish the impact of
\rho_{K}
on the running time of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithm. To be more precise, this estimate on this factor allows for making the running time depend on
\rho_{K}^{\mathrm{cut}}
instead of
\rho_{K}
(see
Theorem

21.2
, also the left-hand down-sloping part of the red graph in
Figure

2
).

Not assuming the Riemann hypothesis for the Dedekind zeta function of
K
might impact the bound in
Proposition

11.2
as well as the bound in
Lemma

11.4
. Hence, the precise implications of letting go of the Riemann hypothesis is not totally clear, but might heavily impact the dependency of the provable running time on
\rho_{K}
.

(3)

Density of smooth ideals.
In
Section

14
, a combinatorial bound is given for the density of smooth ideals, in which bounds on the number of prime ideals in a number field are used. Letting go of the Riemann Hypothesis here might have as a consequence that heavier restrictions on the numbers
B_{\mathrm{sm}}
and
A
are required.

(4)

Computation of an approximation of
\rho_{K}
.
In multiple parts of the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithm an approximation of the Dedekind zeta function residue
\rho_{K}
is required. In
Algorithm

3
, one uses this approximation whenever
\rho_{K}
is large, to apply the ‘modulus trick’ in order to diminish the influence of
\rho_{K}
on the running time. In the full algorithm,
Algorithm

6
, an approximation of
\rho_{K}
(or equivalently, of
R_{K}\cdot h_{K}
) is needed to decide whether the algorithm indeed found the full logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice (see
Section

19.5
). The knowledge of an approximation of
\rho_{K}
thus allows to change the full algorithm from a Las Vegas algorithm (i.e., the run time is a random variable but the answer of the algorithm is always correct) into a Monte Carlo algorithm (i.e., the run time is bounded but the answer might be wrong or inconclusive with a certain fixed small probability).

If the Riemann hypothesis for the Dedekind zeta function of
K
is not assumed, longer Euler products are required to approximate
\rho_{K}
. Depending on the length of these Euler products compared to the running time of the rest of the entire algorithm (which is already at least
L_{|\Delta_{K}|}(1/2)
), letting go of the Riemann hypothesis might or might not influence the running time of the algorithm.

\@tocwriteb

PART III

Provable lattice reduction techniques

22.
Introduction

Roughly, this last part of the current paper
can be divided into two main subjects: The post-processing part, which is about
processing the generators of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice into a basis thereof
(
Sections

23
,
24
and
25
);
and the BKZ-algorithm part on approximate bases, which is needed to find short
vectors in ideal lattices (
Sections

26
,
27
and
28
).

22.1.
Post-processing

The first subject is the post-processing of the generating
matrix of the log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice in order to get a basis
(and hence a fundamental set of
{\mathbb{S}}
-units) of this log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit
lattice, see also
Section

19
.

In this post-processing stage, one consecutively computes the
rank
,
the
determinant
and a well-conditioned
basis
of the lattice generated
by the (approximate) generating matrix. The rank and determinant are
computed first in order to check if the generating matrix indeed generates
the entire log-
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice (and not a sublattice thereof).

This post-processing makes use of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm, an algorithm
that, roughly said, LLL-reduces a real-valued basis of one which only
knows a sufficiently good approximation of. Though, in the work of Buchmann, Kessler
and Pohst
[
18
]
, it is assumed that the rank and the determinant
of the generated lattice is
known
. In our application, though, these
quantities are not known but instead need to be computed.

Hence, a slight modification of the analysis of Buchmann, Kessler and Pohst
is treated in
Section

23
in order to show that
one can indeed
compute
the rank and a ‘relatively well conditioned’ approximate basis
of the generated
lattice with use of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm. This relatively well conditioned approximate
basis can then be used to compute an approximation of the determinant (see
Section

24
)

The entire compound result with the exact precision needed, applied to our

{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation algorithm of
Section

11
, is then treated in
Section

25
.

22.2.
The BKZ-algorithm on ideal lattices

As we aim for a provable running time in this paper,
we use a variant of the BKZ-algorithm
[
39
]
with a proven upper
bound on the run time. The challenge with the lattices occurring
in this paper, is that only an approximate basis of them are known. Hence,
to make use of this variant of the BKZ-algorithm, we have to prove
that this variant behaves well on small changes of the input.

In order to do so, a preliminary section about lattices is required (
Section

26
).
After that, the running time of the variant of the BKZ-algorithm
[
39
]
on
integer
bases
is analyzed, following a sketch of
[
39
, Section 3, Cost of BKZ’]
(
Section

27
).
Lastly, this BKZ-variant
is applied on an approximate basis, with use of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm (
Section

28
).

23.
Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm

23.1.
Introduction

In this section, we will treat a slightly modified analysis
of the algorithm of Buchmann, Pohst and Kessler
[
18
,
19
]
.
The original algorithm allows to compute an approximate basis
of a lattice
\Lambda
from an approximate generating matrix of a lattice (i.e., a matrix
whose rows generate the lattice), provided that the
approximation of this generating matrix is good enough. In this original
algorithm the rank and the determinant of the lattice generated by the generating matrix are
assumed to be known.

In our case, computing (a fundamental system of)
{\mathbb{S}}
-units,
the rank and the determinant of the lattice of the exact generating matrix
are
not
known. Rather, we would like to approximately compute these quantities,
in order to deduce whether the generating matrix generates the entire
logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice or not.

Exactly this lack of knowing the rank and determinant of the lattice (spanned by the exact generating matrix)
requires us to slightly modify the analysis of Buchmann, Pohst and Kessler.
In this modified analysis, the algorithm of Buchmann, Pohst and Kessler is actually applied
twice (this was already suggested in
[
18
, p. 9, remark after Theorem 4.2]
). These two
applications of the same algorithm serve two different goals.

During the
first application of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm, one computes an (approximate)
basis
of the lattice at hand.
From this approximate basis one can directly obtain the
rank
of the lattice. This first (approximate) basis, though, might have bad
conditioning properties and too long vectors. It is exactly the purpose of the second application of the
Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm to transform this approximate basis in a
well-conditioned
approximate basis,
with reasonably short vectors (close to what LLL can achieve).

This well-conditioning of the (approximate) basis allows to approximate the
determinant

of this basis within reasonable precision. This will be the subject of
Section

24
.
Knowing the determinant and the rank of the resulting basis allows to verify whether
the basis is an actual basis of the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice or not (as there are
formulas to compute the rank and the covolume of the logarithmic
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit lattice).

23.2.
Preliminaries

23.2.1.
On the quasi-norm
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}

In order to phrase the results of
[
18
]
more
succinctly, we introduce the following norm-like function on
matrices. It is just the maximum over the
2
-norms of the rows of the matrix.

Definition 23.1
.

For any
k\times m
-matrix
\mathbf{A}
, we denote

\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\|\mathbf{a}_{j}\|,

where
\mathbf{a}_{j}
are the
rows
of
\mathbf{A}
, and where
\|\cdot\|
is the Euclidean norm on the row vectors.

This is not a matrix norm as it is not submultiplicative, but it is almost submultiplicative:

Lemma 23.2
.

For a real
m\times\ell
-matrix
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
and a real
\ell\times k
-matrix
\mathbf{M}
, we have

\lVert\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{\ell}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\rVert_{2,\infty}\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}

Proof.

Take the
2
-norm of the
t
-th row in
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}
, which equals

\|\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}m^{\prime}_{tj}\mathbf{M}_{j}\|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|m^{\prime}_{tj}|\|\mathbf{M}_{j}\|\leq\|\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{t}\|\cdot\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\|\mathbf{M}_{j}\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leq\|\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{t}\|\cdot\sqrt{\ell}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}

where
m^{\prime}_{tj}
is the
tj
-th entry in
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{M}_{j}
is the
j
-th row of
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}_{t}
is the
t
-th row of
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
. The first inequality is the triangle inequality, the second inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the third from the inequality between
2
-norms and infinity norms. As this holds for any row in
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}
, we conclude that

\lVert\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{\ell}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\rVert_{2,\infty}\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}.

∎

23.2.2.
Rounding in the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm

In the original analysis of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm
[
18
]
,
the input approximate generating matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}
(consisting of rows
\mathbf{a}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
) is scaled up by
2^{q}
and rounded entry-wise
to the nearest integer, thus obtaining an integer matrix
\hat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times n}
(consisting of rows
\hat{\mathbf{a}}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
)
[
18
, Section 2]
. This is done this way because then the LLL-algorithm can be applied to
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
(the LLL-algorithm is generally only applied on integer (or rational) matrices). As a result, such an integer matrix
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
satisfies
\lVert\hat{\mathbf{A}}-2^{q}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{n}/2

[
18
, Equation (1)]
.

In our analysis, we deviate from this. Going over the result of Buchmann and Kessler
[
18
]
, one
can verify that the only requirements on
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
for their algorithm to work is:

•

Closeness to
2^{q}\mathbf{A}
, i.e.,
\lVert\hat{\mathbf{A}}-2^{q}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{n}/2
for some sufficiently large
q\in\mathbb{N}
, see
[
18
, Equation (1)]
.

•

Being able to call LLL on
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
; so, in fact
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
is allowed be a rational matrix, as long as the denominators
not too large.

By choosing, for example,
\hat{\mathbf{A}}\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{k\times n}
, the inequality
\lVert\hat{\mathbf{A}}-2^{q}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{n}/2
can be easily achieved, using a sufficiently good approximation of
\mathbf{A}
to construct
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
.

Summarizing, for our purposes it is not required that
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
is integral (rationals with small common denominators will suffice, too) nor that
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
is obtained by rounding
2^{q}\mathbf{A}
. For this reason,
the following notation turns out to be useful.

Notation 23.3

(Binary approximation)
.

A matrix
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k\times n}
is called a binary approximation of
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times n}

with precision
\varepsilon_{0}
if

2^{q}\cdot\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times n}
for some
q\in\mathbb{N}
and

\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

Note that we can choose
q=\log_{2}(\varepsilon_{0}\cdot n)
in above notation, as
that precision is sufficient to obtain the approximation
\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.
As a result, there always exists such a matrix
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k\times n}
whose bit-size is bounded by
q^{2}\cdot k\cdot n\cdot\log(\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty})=\log_{2}(\varepsilon_{0}n)^{2}\cdot k\cdot n\cdot\log(\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty})
(each coefficient of
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
is a rational number with numerator bounded by
2^{q}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
and denominator bounded by
2^{q}
).
Moreover, from any rational matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
satisfying
\lVert\mathbf{A}-\tilde{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\varepsilon_{0}/2
, one can compute in polynomial time a matrix
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
with bit-size as above (polynomial in
n
,
k
,
\log(1/\varepsilon_{0})
and such that
\log\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
) and
\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

23.3.
The algorithm of Buchmann, Pohst and Kessler

In the following lemma, we start by updating slightly the analysis of
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
, to make it compatible with our matrix norm, and also take into account the fact that the rank and determinant of our lattice are not known. In the later theorem (
Theorem

23.5
) we will apply this lemma twice, first to obtain just any basis of the lattice, and the second time to obtain a well-conditioned basis consisting of reasonably short vectors.

Lemma 23.4
.

Let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
be a lattice
with
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)\geq\mu
and
\rank(\Lambda)\leq r_{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{A}
be a
k\times(n_{1}+n_{2})
-matrix whose rows generate the lattice
\Lambda
with
k\geq n_{2}\geq 1
, let

C:=2^{4k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}+1},

and let
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
be a binary approximation of
\mathbf{A}
satisfying
\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}<\tfrac{1}{4}\mu\cdot C^{-1}
.

Then there exists an algorithm that, on input
\mathbf{\tilde{A}},\mu,r_{0}
, computes a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times r}
with
r=\mbox{rank}(\Lambda)

in time polynomial in
k
,
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
,
\log\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty},\log(1/\mu)
and the bit-size of its input,
and such that

(i)

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}
is a basis of
\Lambda
.

(ii)

\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C/\sqrt{k}\leq C
,

(iii)

\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq 4^{k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
.

(iv)

Writing
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
, we have
\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C\cdot\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
.

Additionally, if
\mathbf{A}
happened itself to be a basis of
\Lambda
, i.e.,
k=r=\rank(\Lambda)
, we have, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
,

\lVert\mathbf{b}_{j}\rVert\leq(\sqrt{rn_{2}}+2)2^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\Lambda),

where
\mathbf{b}_{j}
are the rows of
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{r})
.

Proof.

Constructing the matrix
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
from
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
.

Using the discussion after
23.3
, we can also replace the matrix
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
by a matrix with bounded bit-size (in time polynomial in the bit-size of
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
). Hence, in the rest of the proof, we will assume without loss of generality that the bit-size of
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
is polynomial in
k
,
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
,
\log\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
,
\log(1/\mu)
, and
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
satisfies
\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq 1/4\cdot\mu\cdot C^{-1}
.

The algorithm first creates the matrix
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
defined by
\mathbf{\hat{A}}_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{2}\lfloor 2^{q+1}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}_{ij}\rceil
with
q=\lfloor\log_{2}(T)\rfloor
, where
\lfloor\cdot\rceil
denotes rounding to the nearest integer,
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
denotes rounding down, and

T=\frac{2^{3k}}{\mu}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}}=\frac{2^{-k}}{\mu}\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{-1}\cdot C\leq C/(2\mu).

(139)

Note that computing
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
can be done in time polynomial in
q
and in the bit-size of
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
.
The entries of
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
lie in
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, and the matrix
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
satisfies

\lVert\mathbf{\hat{A}}-2^{q}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\underbrace{\lVert\mathbf{\hat{A}}-2^{q}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}\rVert_{2,\infty}}_{\leq\sqrt{n_{2}}/4}+\underbrace{\lVert 2^{q}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-2^{q}\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}_{\leq 2^{q}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\mu/C\leq 1/4}\leq\sqrt{n_{2}}/2.

According to the discussion in
Section

23.2.2
this is sufficient to apply the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm.

Applying Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst to
[\mathbf{I}\mid\mathbf{\hat{A}}]
.

This Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
essentially consists of applying LLL-reduction to the matrix
[\mathbf{I}\mid\mathbf{\hat{A}}]
consisting of the
horizontal concatenation of
\mathbf{\hat{A}}
and
\mathbf{I}
, the
k\times k
identity matrix.

Put,
33
33

33

We replaced
\alpha
in
[
18
, Proposition 3.2]
here by
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
. As
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is required to be an upper bound on the
2
-norms of the rows of
\mathbf{A}
,
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}=\max_{j}\|\mathbf{g}_{j}\|
clearly suffices.

in order to satisfy the prerequisites of
[
18
, Proposition 3.2]
,

\lambda:=2^{k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}}\geq(\tfrac{1}{2}k\sqrt{n_{2}}+\sqrt{k})\cdot\frac{\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}^{r}}{\covol(\Lambda)}.

(140)

where
r=\rank(\Lambda)\leq r_{0}
; in the inequality we made use of Minkowski’s inequality
34
34

34

Minkowski’s inequality states that
1/\covol(\Lambda)\leq(r/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda))^{r}\leq(r/\mu)^{r}
with
r=\rank(\Lambda)
. By the fact that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}/\mu\geq 1
and
r_{0}\geq r
, we can conclude that

\left(\frac{r_{0}\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}}\geq\left(\frac{r\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r}\geq\frac{\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}^{r}}{\covol(\Lambda)}

and the fact that
2^{k}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}k\sqrt{n_{2}}+\sqrt{k}
(since we assumed
k\geq n_{2}
).

Note that
q=\lfloor\log_{2}(T)\rfloor
satisfies
[
18
, Equation 7]
, since we have

\displaystyle 2^{q}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2^{3k}}{\mu}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2^{2k}}{\mu}\cdot\lambda

\displaystyle>(\sqrt{n_{2}k}+2)\cdot 2^{\frac{k-3}{2}}\cdot\frac{\lambda}{\mu},

by
Equation

140
and because
\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 2^{2k}>(\sqrt{n_{2}k}+2)2^{\frac{k-3}{2}}
for all
k\geq n_{2}\geq 1
.

With these parameters
\lambda
and
q
, one can apply

[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
: LLL-reducing
[\mathbf{I}\penalty\ \penalty\ |\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{\hat{A}}]
yields an integral matrix
(\mathbf{R}^{\top},\mathbf{M}^{\top})^{\top}
such that

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{R}\\
\mathbf{M}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{\hat{A}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{R}&\mathbf{R}\mathbf{\hat{A}}\\
\mathbf{M}&\mathbf{M}\mathbf{\hat{A}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{R}&\hat{\mathbf{E}}\\
\mathbf{M}&\hat{\mathbf{B}}\end{bmatrix}

(141)

for which holds
\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
, and
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}
, a basis of
\Lambda
. Furthermore, every row
\hat{\mathbf{e}}
of
\hat{\mathbf{E}}
satisfies (
[
18
, Theorem 4.1, first equation on page 8]
)

\|\hat{\mathbf{e}}\|\leq 2^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\lambda

and every row
\hat{\mathbf{b}}
of
\hat{\mathbf{B}}
satisfies (
[
18
, Proposition 3.1]
)

\|\hat{\mathbf{b}}\|>2^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\lambda.

Identifying
\mathbf{M}
and
\hat{\mathbf{B}}
, proving (i).

This gives means to distinguish where in the output matrix of
Equation

141
the approximate basis elements
\hat{\mathbf{B}}
are. Also, this gives us a way to identify
\mathbf{M}
, as it shares the same rows as
\hat{\mathbf{B}}
. Therefore,
we can compute an
\mathbf{M}
that satisfies part (i) of the lemma.

Bounds on
\mathbf{M}
,
\mathbf{B}
and
\lVert\mathbf{B}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{2,\infty}
.

By
[
18
, Theorem 4.1, Equation 9, bottom inequality]
, we have,
35
35

35

In
[
18
, Eq. 10]
, the lattice successive minima
\lambda_{j}(L_{r})
of the lattice
L_{r}
are used; this lattice
L_{r}
is any lattice spanned by

rows of
\mathbf{A}
. So these successive minima

are trivially bounded by
\alpha
, the uniform bound on the lengths of the rows of
\mathbf{A}
. Additionally, we replaced
\alpha
by
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
again.
since
2^{q}\leq T
(see
Equation

139
),

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{m}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{k-1}{2}+q+1}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}=2^{\frac{k+1}{2}}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\cdot T

(142)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{k+1}{2}}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\cdot\frac{2^{3k}}{\mu}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}}

(143)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{4k}}{\sqrt{k}}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}+1}

(144)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}\leq C\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ for all rows $\mathbf{m}$ of $\mathbf{M}$,}

(145)

where we used

for all

. Additionally, by
[

, we have,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{kn_{2}}+2)2^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}

(146)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ for all rows $\mathbf{b}$ of $\mathbf{B}$,}

(147)

which prove (ii) and (iii), as
\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}
and
\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2,\infty}
are just the maximum

-norm of the rows of the respective matrices.

For part (iv) use the ‘almost submultiplicativity’ of
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2,\infty}
(
Lemma

) and

, to obtain

\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2,\infty}=\lVert\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A})\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C\cdot\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}.

LLL-alike bounds for
\mathbf{B}
when
\mathbf{A}
is itself a basis of
\Lambda
.

In the case that
\mathbf{A}
itself happens to be a basis, sharper bounds on
\mathbf{B}
can be established, as can be deduced
36
36

36

We substitute
k=r
(as
\mathbf{B}
consists of
r
rows) and
L_{r}=\Lambda
. Here
L_{r}
is defined as any lattice defined by any
r
row vectors from
\mathbf{B}
. As
\mathbf{B}
consists of only
r
row vectors and is a basis of
\Lambda
, we have
L_{r}=\lambda
.
from
[
18
, Theorem 4.1, Equation 10]
.
Applying this bound yields, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
,

\lVert\mathbf{b}_{j}\rVert\leq(\sqrt{kn_{2}}+2)2^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)

where
\mathbf{b}_{j}
is the
j
-th row of
\mathbf{B}
.

Running time.

For the running time, note that the most costly part of the algorithm is to run the LLL algorithm on the matrix
[\mathbf{I}\penalty\ \penalty\ |\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{\hat{A}}]
. This can be done in polynomial time in the bit-size of the matrix, which is polynomial in
k
,
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
,
\log\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
and
\log(1/\mu)
, as desired.
∎

Theorem 23.5

(Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst)
.

Let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
be a lattice
with
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)\geq\mu
and
\rank(\Lambda)\leq r_{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{A}
be a
k\times(n_{1}+n_{2})
-matrix whose rows generate the lattice
\Lambda
with
k\geq n_{2}\geq 1
, let

C_{0}=2^{8k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot 4^{k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{2(r_{0}+1)},

and let
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
be a binary approximation of
\mathbf{A}
that satisfies
\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}<\tfrac{1}{4}\cdot\mu\cdot C_{0}^{-1}
.

Then, there exists an algorithm that, on input
\mathbf{\tilde{A}},\mu,r_{0}
, computes in time polynomial in
k
,
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
,
\log\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
,
\log(1/\mu)
and the bit-size of its input,
a matrix
\mathbf{N}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times k}
with
r=\rank(\Lambda)
such that

(i)

\lVert\mathbf{N}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C_{0}

(ii)

\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{N}\mathbf{A}
is a basis of
\Lambda
, whose rows satisfy, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
,

\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|\leq(\sqrt{rn_{2}}+2)\cdot 2^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)

(iii)

The approximate basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=\mathbf{N}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
satisfies
\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}.

Proof.

We will apply the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm as in
Lemma

23.4
twice: the first time
we apply it to
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
to obtain an approximate basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}
of
\Lambda
, and the second time we apply it to
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}
to obtain an approximate
good
basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
of
\Lambda
. This basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
is called ‘good’ because it is an approximation of a basis
\mathbf{B}
with a guarantee on the shortness of its basis vectors.

First application of
Lemma

23.4
.

We have that
C_{0}>2^{4k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}+1}=C
from
Lemma

23.4
. Therefore, the approximation
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
of
\mathbf{A}
is sufficiently good to apply
Lemma

23.4
, and compute
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k\times r}
such that

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}_{0}
is a basis of
\Lambda
. Additionally, this
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{B}_{0}
satisfy

\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \lVert\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq 4^{k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty},

(148)

and
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}:=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
satisfies

\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}-\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C\cdot\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C\cdot\mu\cdot C_{0}^{-1}

(149)

Moreover, the bit-size of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}
is polynomially bounded by the bit-size of
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
, and the bit-size of
\mathbf{M}
, the later being polynomially bounded by
k
,
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
,
\log\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
and
\log(1/\mu)
.

Second application of
Lemma

23.4
.

By
Equation

149
we deduce that
\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}-\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}<\mu(C_{0}/C)^{-1}
; since
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
, this matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}
is a binary approximation of
\mathbf{B}_{0}
.
In order to apply
Lemma

23.4
, we need to show that

C_{0}/C\geq 2^{4r}\cdot\left(\frac{r\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r+1}=:C^{\prime}
, i.e., that the precision of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}
is good enough . By the choice of
C_{0}
, the bound
4^{k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\geq\lVert\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}
(
Equation

148
),
r_{0}\geq r
and
k\geq r
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{C_{0}}{C}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{8k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot 4^{k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{2(r_{0}+1)}}{2^{4k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}+1}}\geq 2^{4k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot 4^{k}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}+1}

(150)

\displaystyle\geq 2^{4k}\cdot\left(\frac{r_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r_{0}+1}\geq 2^{4r}\cdot\left(\frac{r\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{r+1}=:C^{\prime}

(151)

Therefore, we can apply
Lemma

23.4
again, but now with
k=r
and
C^{\prime}
as in
Equation

151
, and with input
(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0},\mu,r)
(note that
r
is now known, and we still have
\mu\leq\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)
).
This yields
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{B}_{0}=\mathbf{B}
is a basis of
\Lambda
,

\lVert\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C^{\prime}/\sqrt{r}

(152)

and for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
we have

\|\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{j}\|\leq(\sqrt{rn_{2}}+2)\cdot 2^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\Lambda),

which proves (ii), putting
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}
.

For (i), we apply the ‘almost submultiplicativity’ (
Lemma

23.2
) to
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\mathbf{M}
, which yields, by
Equation

152
and
Equation

151
(
C_{0}/C\geq C^{\prime}
),

\lVert\mathbf{N}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{r}\lVert\mathbf{M}^{\prime}\rVert_{2,\infty}\lVert\mathbf{M}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot C\leq C_{0}.

For (iii), we note that, by the results of
Lemma

23.4
,

\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{0}-\mathbf{B}_{0}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot C\cdot\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C_{0}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{\tilde{A}}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}

For the running time of the algorithm, note that we applied the algorithm of
Lemma

23.4
twice, once with
(\mathbf{A},\mu,r_{0})
and once with
(\mathbf{B}_{0},\mu,r)
.
Recall that the bit-size of
\mathbf{B}_{0}
is polynomially bounded by
k
,
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
,
\log\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2,\infty}
and
\log(1/\mu)
and by the bit-size of
\mathbf{\tilde{A}}
. Using
Lemma

23.4
gives the desired upper bound on the total running time of the algorithm.
∎

24.
Computing the determinant of an approximate basis

24.1.
Preliminaries

In this section we will use the induced
2
-norm
on matrices, which is defined (for
m\times n
-matrices
\mathbf{A}
) by the rule,

\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2}:=\sup\{\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mbox{ with }\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}.

It satisfies the following inequalities
