\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{F}:=(\sum_{i,j}|A_{ij}|^{2})^{1/2},

where
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{F}
is the Frobenius norm. As a result,
a
m\times n
matrix
\mathbf{E}
with
E_{ij}\leq\varepsilon
for all
i,j
,
satisfies

\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{F}\leq\sqrt{nm}\cdot\varepsilon.

We will use the fact that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2}
corresponds to the largest singular value of
\mathbf{A}
, which gives us

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{A}^{\top}\rVert_{2}=\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{2}.

(153)

24.2.
The main result of this section

This section is about the following problem. Suppose we have
a rank-
n
lattice
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
of which we can approximate
the determinant, say
D\approx\covol(\Lambda)
. Suppose furthermore
that we have an
approximate basis

\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\approx\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
of a
sub
lattice

\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
(with the same rank
n
). The task is to decide whether
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda
, using
only the approximations
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
and
D
.

A straightforward way is to compute
\sqrt{\det((\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})}\approx\covol(\Lambda^{\prime})
and compare
it with
D\approx\covol(\Lambda)
. If the approximations are sufficiently good and

D
and
\sqrt{\det((\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})}
are sufficiently close, we can conclude
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda
.
If they are not close, we can conclude
\Lambda^{\prime}\neq\Lambda
.

This section treats how well
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
needs to approximate
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
(the exact basis of
\Lambda^{\prime}
)
and how well
D
needs to approximate
\covol(\Lambda)
in order to make this reasoning correct.
The main result of this section is the following statement.

Theorem 24.1
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a rank-
n
lattice (for some
m\geq n\geq 1
). Let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a basis of a rank-
n
sublattice
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
and let
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}^{m\times n}
be an approximation of
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
where the entry-wise error is less than

\varepsilon=2^{-6}\cdot n^{-(n+4)}\cdot m^{-1}\cdot\left(\prod_{j}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}^{\prime}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda^{\prime})}\right)^{-2}\cdot\lambda_{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})^{2}/\lVert\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\rVert_{2},

and let
D\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]\covol(\Lambda)\cap\mathbb{Q}
.

Then
we can decide whether
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda
or not, using only
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
and
D
,
in time polynomial in the bit-sizes of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
and
D
.

24.3.
Some preliminary lemmas.

Before proving
Theorem

24.1
, we will prove some intermediary lemmas. The first lemma gives an upper bound on the size of the inverse of a square matrix
\mathbf{A}
that is the basis of a lattice (the upper bound depends on some quantities related to
\mathbf{A}
, which quantify how well-conditioned
\mathbf{A}
is). This will be used in the second lemma to prove that if two rectangular matrices
\mathbf{B}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
are close coefficient-wise, then the determinants of the square matrices
\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
are close too. This second lemma will be central in the proof of
Theorem

24.1
.

Lemma 24.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a square real matrix and a basis of a lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then

\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\leq n^{n/2+1}\cdot\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{-1}\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\|\mathbf{a}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)}\right).

Proof.

For
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, define
C_{j}=\frac{\|\mathbf{a}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)}
.
We have that
\mathbf{A}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\det\mathbf{A}}\mbox{adj}(\mathbf{A})
. We have that
\mbox{adj}(\mathbf{A})_{ij}
is
defined by the determinant of the minor of
\mathbf{A}
where the
i
-th row and
j
-th column are deleted. By the
Hadamard bound and subsequently by Minkowski’s second theorem (see, e.g.,
[
54
, Theorem 1.5]
),

\displaystyle|\mbox{adj}(\mathbf{A})_{ij}|

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{k\neq i}\|\mathbf{a}_{k}\|=\prod_{k\neq i}C_{k}\cdot\lambda_{k}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle\leq n^{n/2}\cdot(\prod_{k\neq i}C_{k})\cdot\covol(\Lambda)/\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)\leq n^{n/2}(\prod_{k=1}^{n}C_{k})\cdot\covol(\Lambda)/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda).

Therefore, by observing that
\det(\mathbf{A})=\covol(\Lambda)
, we obtain

\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{F}\leq\frac{1}{\det(\mathbf{A})}\cdot n\cdot\max_{ij}|\mbox{adj}(\mathbf{A})_{ij}|\leq n^{n/2+1}\cdot(\prod_{k=1}^{n}C_{k})/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)

∎

Lemma 24.3
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a basis of a rank-
n
lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
(for some
m\geq n\geq 1
),
and let
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
be an approximation of
\mathbf{B}
where the entry-wise error satisfies s

|\mathbf{B}_{ij}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{ij}|\leq\varepsilon
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, where

\varepsilon=2^{-6}\cdot n^{-(n+4)}\cdot m^{-1}\cdot\left(\prod_{j}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)}\right)^{-2}\cdot\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}/\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}.

(154)

Then

\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})\in[\tfrac{7}{8},\tfrac{9}{8}]\cdot\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}).

Proof.

We have, by
[
43
, Corollary 2.14]
, for
n\times n
complex matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E}
, where

\mathbf{A}
is non-singular,

\displaystyle\frac{|\det(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{E})-\det(\mathbf{A})|}{|\det(\mathbf{A})|}

\displaystyle\leq(\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}+1)^{n}-1

\displaystyle\leq 2n\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ if $\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}\leq 1/n$}.

(155)

The last inequality here easily follows from
(x+1)^{n}-1\leq(e^{x})^{n}-1\leq 2nx
for
nx\leq 1
.

We instantiate this inequality with
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{E}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}
, so that
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{E}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
(note that
\mathbf{A}
is non-singular since
\mathbf{B}
is of rank
n
, so the instantiation is valid). In order to obtain a meaningful upper bound, let us compute bounds on the two quantities
\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}
and
\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}
.

We can rewrite
\mathbf{E}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathbf{B}+(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B})\big)^{\top}\cdot\big(\mathbf{B}+(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B})\big)-\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B})+(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B})^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{B}+(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B})^{\top}\cdot(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}).

Using the sub-multiplicativity of the induced
2
-norm and
Equation

153
, we obtain

\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}\leq 2\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}+\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

By assumption on the entry-wise error, we know that
\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{F}\leq\sqrt{nm}\cdot\varepsilon
. Moreover, it holds that
\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\geq\lVert\mathbf{b}_{1}\rVert\geq\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)\geq\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}/\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\geq\varepsilon
(where
\mathbf{b}_{1}
is the first column vector of
\mathbf{B}
, and see
Equation

154
). This yields

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}\leq 3\cdot n\cdot m\cdot\varepsilon\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}.

(156)

Let us now compute an upper bound on
\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}=\lVert(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{-1}\rVert_{2}
. Let
(\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{R})
be the QR-decomposition of
\mathbf{B}
, with
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
orthogonal and
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
upper triangular, i.e.,
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{R}
. Since
m\geq n
, we have
\mathbf{R}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{C}\\
0\end{pmatrix}
for some invertible matrix
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. It then holds, by orthogonality of
\mathbf{Q}
, that
\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}
; that
\lVert\mathbf{b}_{i}\rVert=\lVert\mathbf{c}_{i}\rVert
for all
1\leq i\leq n
; and that
\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)=\lambda_{i}(\Lambda_{\mathbf{C}})
for all
i
’s, where
\Lambda_{\mathbf{C}}
is the lattice spanned by the columns of
\mathbf{C}
.
This gives us

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}=\lVert(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{-1}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{C})^{-1}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert(\mathbf{C}^{-1})^{\top}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{C}^{-1}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathbf{C}^{-1}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(n^{n/2+1}\cdot\lambda_{1}(\Lambda_{\mathbf{C}})^{-1}\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\|\mathbf{c}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda_{\mathbf{C}})}\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=n^{n+2}\cdot\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{-2}\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)}\right)^{2},

(157)

where we used
Equation

153
for the third line and
Lemma

24.2
on
\mathbf{C}
(which is
n
by
n
as required) for the fourth line.

Combining the bounds
Equations

155
,
156
and
157
and observing that
\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}\leq 1/n
by choice of
\varepsilon
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{|\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})-\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})|}{|\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})|}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\cdot\underbrace{n^{n+2}\cdot\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{-2}\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)}\right)^{2}}_{\lVert\mathbf{A}^{-1}\rVert_{2}}\cdot\underbrace{3\cdot n\cdot m\cdot\varepsilon\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}_{\lVert\mathbf{E}\rVert_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 1/8,

which precisely means that
\det(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}})\in[\tfrac{7}{8},\tfrac{9}{8}]\cdot\det(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})
.
∎

24.4.
Proving the main result.

We are now equipped to prove
Theorem

24.1
. The proof is quite short once we have
Lemma

24.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

24.1
.

Let
\ell=[\Lambda:\Lambda^{\prime}]
be the index of
\Lambda^{\prime}
in
\Lambda
. Then
\ell\geq 1
is an integer, and we know that
\det\big((\mathbf{B}^{\prime})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\big)/\covol(\Lambda)^{2}=\ell^{2}
. Our objective is to determine whether
\ell^{2}=1
(i.e.,
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda
) or
\ell^{2}\geq 4
(i.e.,
\Lambda^{\prime}\subsetneq\Lambda
).

By the choice of
\varepsilon
, we can apply
Lemma

24.3
to
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
, which proves that
\det\big((\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\big)\in[\tfrac{7}{8},\tfrac{9}{8}]\cdot\det\big((\mathbf{B}^{\prime})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\big)
. Recall that by assumption,
D\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]\cdot\covol(\Lambda)
. From these two inequalities, we obtain that

\frac{\det\big((\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\big)}{D^{2}}\in[\tfrac{16}{25}\cdot\tfrac{7}{8},\tfrac{16}{9}\cdot\tfrac{9}{8}]\cdot\frac{\det\big((\mathbf{B}^{\prime})^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\big)}{\covol(\Lambda)^{2}}=[\tfrac{14}{25},2]\cdot\ell^{2}.

If
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\prime}
, then
\ell=1
and we have
\frac{\det\big((\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\big)}{D^{2}}\leq 2
. Otherwise,
\ell\geq 2
and we have
\frac{\det\big((\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\big)}{D^{2}}>2.2
. We can then distinguish the two cases by computing the quantity
\det\big((\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}\big)
and checking whether is it
\leq 2\cdot D^{2}
or
>2\cdot D^{2}
.

For the running time, we only need to compute the determinant of the rational matrix
(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
, and then compare it with the rational number
2\cdot D^{2}
. This can be done in polynomial time in the bit-sizes of the
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
and
D
(see, e.g.,
[
45
]
for an algorithm that computes the determinant of integer matrices in polynomial time).
∎

25.
Post-processing in the
{\mathbb{S}}
-unit computation

See
19.1

Proof.

Part I: Setting up the log-matrix and assembling properties of the log-
S
-unit lattice.

Define
A=\max_{j}\lVert\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{j})\rVert
and
\mu=(1000\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\log(n)^{3})^{-1}
. We know from
Lemma

15.6
that
\mu\leq\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
. Note that this lower bound is automatically also a lower bound for the first minimum of any sublattice of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
.

Consider the exact matrix
\mathbf{G}
where the rows consist of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{j})
with
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
and compute an approximation
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{k\times(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)}
, with
\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{G}}_{j}-\mathbf{G}_{j}\rVert\leq\varepsilon

for each row, where we put

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\mu\cdot\underbrace{2^{-8k}\cdot\left(\frac{k\cdot 4^{k}\cdot A}{\mu}\right)^{-2(k+1)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{\normalsize{$\leq C_{0}^{-1}$}}\mbox{ as in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{theorem:buchmannpohstkessler}}\\
\mbox{(Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst)}\end{subarray}}\cdot\underbrace{2^{-6}\cdot(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-(k+6)}\cdot 2^{-(2k+1)(k+2)}\cdot A^{-1}\cdot\mu}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mbox{Determinant}\\
\mbox{ approximation}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{4}\cdot\mu\cdot C_{0}^{-1}

(158)

Note that from the exact representation of the
\eta_{i}
’s, one can compute approximations of the
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{i})
’s with arbitrary precision
\varepsilon
in time polynomial in
\size(\eta_{i})
, in
\log(\|\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{i})\|)
and in
\log(1/\varepsilon)
. From our choice of
\varepsilon
, this is polynomial in
k
,
\log|\Delta_{K}|
,
\log\big(\max_{j}\lVert\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{j})\rVert\big)=\log(A)
,
|{\mathbb{S}}|
, and
\max_{i}(\size(\eta_{i}))
as expected. Moreover, the bit-size of the resulting matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
is also polynomial in these quantities.

The quantity
\varepsilon
is chosen such that the left part of the product in
Equation

158
counteracts the loss in precision due to the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm (
Theorem

23.5
), whereas the right part is the minimum precision required to make the determinant approximation work (as in
Theorem

24.1
).

Part II: Applying the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm.

We apply
Theorem

23.5
to
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
with
r_{0}=k
,
\mu\leq\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))
(as defined above)
and we note that, by definition,
\lVert\mathbf{G}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq A
which implies
\varepsilon<\tfrac{1}{4}\cdot\mu\cdot C_{0}^{-1}
(for
C_{0}
as in
Theorem

23.5
), so the prerequisites of the theorem are satisfied.

So, the algorithm of
Theorem

23.5
outputs an
\mathbf{N}
such that
\mathbf{B}:=\mathbf{N}\mathbf{G}
is
a basis of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)
and
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}:=\mathbf{N}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
satisfies

\lVert\mathbf{B}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C_{0}\cdot\varepsilon\leq 2^{-6}\cdot(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-(k+6)}\cdot 2^{-(2k+1)(k+2)}\cdot A^{-1}\cdot\mu^{2}

(159)

Additionally, the rows
\mathbf{b}_{j}
of
\mathbf{B}
satisfy

\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|\leq(\sqrt{rn}+2)\cdot 2^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)),

(160)

where
r
is the rank of the lattice
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)
.

Note that the pair
(\mathbf{N},G)
encodes a fundamental system of units of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)
in compact representation.

The Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm computes the rank
r
of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)
; we proceed
only to Part III if this rank equals the rank of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, namely
{n_{\mathbb{R}}}+{n_{\mathbb{C}}}+|{\mathbb{S}}|-1
. It thus remains to decide whether
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)=\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
or not. This is done by approximating determinants.

Part III: Computing the determinant.

In order to apply
Theorem

24.1
to the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
obtained, we need upper bounds on the quantities
\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))}
and
\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}
, and a lower bound on the quantity
\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))
.
Since
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)
is a sublattice of
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
, we have already seen that
\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))\geq\mu
.

By
Equation

160
, the output basis of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm satisfies

\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))}\leq(\sqrt{rn}+2)\cdot 2^{\frac{r-1}{2}}\leq 2^{k+2},

where the last inequality holds because
k\geq r
and
r=n_{\mathbb{R}}+n_{\mathbb{C}}-1\geq n/2-1
(since we proceeded to Part III).
So,
\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))}\leq 2^{k(k+2)}
.

Finally, we observe that

\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\leq r\cdot\max_{j}\lVert\mathbf{b}_{j}\rVert\leq r\cdot\max_{j}\Big(\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))}\Big)\cdot\lambda_{r}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))\leq r\cdot 2^{k+2}\cdot A,

where for the last inequality we used the bound on
\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))}
computed above, and the fact that
A=\max_{j}\lVert\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\eta_{j})\rVert
is an upper bound on the last minimum of the lattice generated by the
\eta_{j}
’s.

Combining these bounds with
Equation

159
(and using the fact that
k\geq r
and
n+|{\mathbb{S}}|\geq r
), we obtain, using
(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-(k+6)}\cdot r\leq(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-1}\cdot(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-(k+5)}\cdot r\leq(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-1}\cdot r^{-(r+4)}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{B}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq 2^{-6}\cdot(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-(k+6)}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2^{-(2k+1)(k+2)}}\cdot A^{-1}\cdot\mu^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq 2^{-6}\cdot(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-(k+6)}\cdot\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2^{-2k(k+2)}}}_{\leq\left(\prod_{j}\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|/\lambda_{j}\right)^{-2}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\cdot\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r^{-1}\cdot 2^{-(k+2)}}\cdot A^{-1}}_{\leq\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}^{-1}}\cdot\underbrace{\mu^{2}}_{\leq\lambda_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle\ \ \leq 2^{-6}\cdot r^{-(r+4)}\cdot(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)^{-1}\cdot\left(\prod_{j}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))}\right)^{-2}\cdot\lambda_{1}(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle))^{2}/\lVert\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2},

as needed to apply
Theorem

24.1
to the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
. We conclude that from the knowledge of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
and a rational number
D\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]\cdot\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, we can decide whether
\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\langle G\rangle)=\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times})
or not.

Part IV: The running time.

For the running time, we go through each part. For Part I, we have already seen that
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
can be computed in time polynomial in the desired quantities, and that its bit-size is also polynomial.
For Part II, note that we applied the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm as in
Theorem

23.5
,
which takes time
polynomial in
k
,
(n+|{\mathbb{S}}|)
,
\log(\lVert\mathbf{G}\rVert_{2,\infty})
,
\log(1/\mu)
and the bit-size of
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
. By definition of
\mu
, this is polynomial in the desired quantities. Moreover, the bit-size of the output matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
is also polynomial in those same quantities.

For Part III, we first compute a rational
D\in[\tfrac{3}{4},\tfrac{5}{4}]\cdot\covol(\Log_{{\mathbb{S}}}(\mathcal{O}_{K,{\mathbb{S}}}^{\times}))
, which can be done in time polynomial in
\log|\Delta_{K}|
from
Proposition

2.9
and
Lemma

15.5
. We then apply
Theorem

24.1
to the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
and the rational number
D
. This takes time polynomial in the bit-sizes of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
and
D
, which is polynomial in all the desired quantities.
This concludes the proof.
∎

26.
Lattice preliminaries

In this section, we succinctly treat lattice preliminaries
required for understanding the next two sections (
Sections

27
and
28
)
about a BKZ-variant by Hanrot, Stehlé and Pujol. This includes
the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization of a basis, the potential
of a basis, HKZ-reduced bases, Banaszczyk’s transference theorem and
the definition of a dually exponentially reduced basis.

26.1.
Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization in
\mathbb{Z}
-bases

Notation 26.1
.

For a basis
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
we denote its Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization by
\mathbf{B}^{\star}=(\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{n})
. We denote the dual basis of
\mathbf{B}
by
\mathbf{D}=(\mathbf{B}^{-1})^{\top}=:\mathbf{B}^{-\top}=(\mathbf{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{d}_{n})=(\mathbf{b}^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}^{\vee}_{n})
. If
\mathbf{B}
is a basis of
\Lambda
, then
\mathbf{D}
is a basis of the dual lattice
\Lambda^{\vee}
of
\Lambda
.

Notation 26.2
.

By a tilde, we generally denote an
approximation
. In this section, often a real-valued
\mathbf{B}
is approximated by a basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
with rational coefficients. Likewise, an approximation of the dual basis
\mathbf{D}
is denoted by
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}
.

Notation 26.3
.

For a basis
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
we denote by
\mathbf{B}_{[j:k]}
the basis
(\pi_{j-1}(\mathbf{b}_{j}),\ldots,\pi_{j-1}(\mathbf{b}_{k}))\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times(k+1-j)}
, where
\pi_{j-1}=\pi_{(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1})^{\perp}}
is the projection orthogonal to the linear subspace generated by
(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1})
.
Note that
\mathbf{B}_{[1:k]}
is just
(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{k})
.

Definition 26.4

(Potential of a basis)
.

For a basis
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
we denote the potential by

P(\mathbf{B})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{n+1-j}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j})),

where
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j})
is the lattice generated by
(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j})
.

Lemma 26.5
.

Let
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
be a basis of the lattice
\Lambda
,
then
\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1}))^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{[j:n]}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
.
In particular,

\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1}))^{2}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and hence
P(\mathbf{B})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{\star}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
.

Proof.

By the determinant formula for orthonormal projection
[
34
, Vol. 1, Chapter IX, §4, Equation (21)]

we have that, for
D_{j}:=\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:j-1]}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{[1:j-1]})
,
D_{j}\mathbf{B}_{[j:n]}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
.
In particular
D_{j}\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. Note that
D_{j}=\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1}))^{2}
by definition.

As a result, we certainly deduce that for
P(\mathbf{B})^{2}=\left(\prod_{j=2}^{n}D_{j}\right)\cdot\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B}))^{2}
we have
P(\mathbf{B})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
(here, we use that
D_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
since
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
). That is,
P(\mathbf{B})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{\star}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
.
∎

Lemma 26.6
.

Let
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
be a basis of the lattice
\Lambda
,
then
\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|\leq\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j}))\leq P(\mathbf{B})
.

Proof.

We know that
\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j}))=\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1}))\cdot\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{B}
is integral, then
\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1}))^{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and so it is
\geq 1
(because
\mathbf{B}
is a basis, so its vectors are linearly independent). The inequality
\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|\leq\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j}))
follows.
∎

Definition 26.7
.

We call a basis
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}

size-reduced if for all
0<i<j\leq n

|\langle\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{j}\rangle|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{i}\|^{2}.

Lemma 26.8
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
be a size reduced basis of a lattice
\Lambda
. Then, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|\leq\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{2}\max_{i}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{i}\|.

Proof.

Size reduced means that any basis vector
\mathbf{b}_{j}
can be written as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{ij}\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{i}
for
|c_{ij}|\leq 1/2
. Hence, by the Pythagorean theorem, we have

\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{2}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{i}\|^{2}\leq\tfrac{n}{4}\max_{j}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{i}\|^{2},

from which the claim follows.
∎

Corollary 26.9
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
be a size reduced basis of a lattice
\Lambda
. Then each coefficient in
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{B}^{\star}
and
\mathbf{B}_{[j:k]}
(for
k>j\in\mathbb{N}
) can be represented in
5\log_{2}(n\cdot P(\mathbf{B}))+3
bits.

Proof.

Each vector in
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\star}
is bounded in Euclidean norm by
\sqrt{n}\cdot P(\mathbf{B})/2
, which is also an upper bound on the absolute value of each coefficient. Moreover,
\mathbf{B}
is integral and the denominators occurring in
\mathbf{B}^{\star}
can be maximally
P(\mathbf{B})^{2}
. Every coefficient of
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\star}
is then a rational number which can be represented by a fraction
a/b
with
a
,
b
integers,
|a|\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot P(\mathbf{B})^{3}/2
and
0<b\leq P(\mathbf{B})^{2}
. They can be represented by respectively by
\lceil\log_{2}(\sqrt{n}\cdot P(\mathbf{B})^{3}+1)\rceil
and
\lceil\log_{2}(P(\mathbf{B})^{2})\rceil
bits.

Precisely the same reasoning can be used for
\mathbf{B}_{[i:j]}
, since
\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1}))^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{[j:n]}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
by
Lemma

26.5
(and
\mathbf{B}_{[j:k]}
consists of just the first
k-j+1
vectors of
\mathbf{B}_{[j:n]}
).
∎

26.2.
HKZ reduction

Definition 26.10

(HKZ-reduced basis)
.

A basis
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
of a lattice
\Lambda
is called HKZ-reduced if it is size reduced and if

\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{[j:n]})\mbox{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

i.e., if the Gram-Schmidt vector
\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}
attains the minimum of the projected lattice
\pi_{(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{j-1})^{\perp}}(\Lambda)
for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Lemma 26.11
.

Let
\mathbf{B}
be a HKZ-reduced basis of
\Lambda
, then

P(\mathbf{B})\leq n^{2n^{2}}\min_{\mathbf{C}}P(\mathbf{C}),

where the minimum over the potential
P(\cdot)
is over all bases
\mathbf{C}
of
\Lambda
.

Proof.

The HKZ reduced basis
\mathbf{B}
satisfies
\tfrac{4}{i+3}\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|^{2}\leq\tfrac{i+3}{4}\cdot\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)^{2}

[
48
]
, hence (using
\tfrac{i+3}{4}\leq i
for
i\geq 1
),

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{B})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{1}\|\cdots\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|)\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\|\mathbf{b}_{1}\|\cdots\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|)\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}j^{j}(\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)\cdots\lambda_{j}(\Lambda))

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{n}j^{j}\cdot j^{j}\min_{\Lambda_{j}}\covol(\Lambda_{j})\leq n^{2n^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\min_{\Lambda_{j}}\covol(\Lambda_{j})\leq n^{2n^{2}}\min_{\mathbf{C}}P(\mathbf{C}).

(161)

where the minimum
\min_{\Lambda_{j}}
is over all
j
-dimensional sublattices
\Lambda_{j}\subseteq\Lambda
and where the minimum
\min_{\mathbf{C}}
is over all bases of
\Lambda
.

The third inequality of
Equation

161
might require some explanation. Any
i
-dimensional sublattice
\Lambda_{i}\subseteq\Lambda
satisfies
\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)\leq\lambda_{j}(\Lambda_{i})
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,i\}
. Therefore, by Minkowski’s second inequality,

\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)\cdots\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)\leq\lambda_{1}(\Lambda_{i})\cdots\lambda_{i}(\Lambda_{i})\leq i^{i}\covol(\Lambda_{i}).

∎

This lemma has as a consequence that applying the HKZ algorithm can only increase the potential of a basis by a factor
n^{2n^{2}}
.

Corollary 26.12
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a basis of the
n
-dimensional lattice
\Lambda
and let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\mathrm{HKZ}(\mathbf{B})
be a HKZ-reduced basis of
\Lambda
. Then

P(\mathbf{B}^{\prime})\leq n^{2n^{2}}P(\mathbf{B}).

Proof.

We have
P(\mathbf{B}^{\prime})\leq n^{2n^{2}}\cdot\min_{\mathbf{C}}P(\mathbf{C})\leq n^{2n^{2}}P(\mathbf{B})
.
∎

The following lemma is for when a
block
of
\mathbf{B}
is being HKZ-reduced,
which happens in the BKZ-algorithm.

Corollary 26.13
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a basis of a lattice
\Lambda
, let
\mathsf{b}\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
,
and let
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{B}_{[k:k+\mathsf{b}-1]}
be a projected sub-block of
\mathbf{B}
. Suppose
\mathbf{U}\in GL_{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbb{Z})
is such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{U}
is HKZ-reduced
and write
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}\bar{\mathbf{U}}
, where
\bar{\mathbf{U}}
acts as
\mathbf{U}
on the basis elements
(\mathbf{b}_{k},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{k+\mathsf{b}-1})
and leaves the
rest intact. Then

P(\mathbf{B}^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}}P(\mathbf{B}),

Proof.

The action of
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}\bar{\mathbf{U}}
only changes the vectors
(\mathbf{b}_{k},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{k+\mathsf{b}-1})
, but does
not
change the
space
spanned by
(\mathbf{b}_{k},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{k+\mathsf{b}-1})
. Hence, the Gram-Schmidt vectors
\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}
for
j\notin\{k,\ldots,k+\mathsf{b}-1\}
also remain intact, as well as the volume
\covol(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}_{k},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{k+\mathsf{b}-1}))=\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|=\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{j})^{\star}\|
. So,

\displaystyle\frac{P(\mathbf{B}^{\prime})}{P(\mathbf{B})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{j})^{\star}\|^{n+1-j}}{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{n+1-j}}=\frac{\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{j})^{\star}\|^{n+1-j}}{\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{n+1-j}}=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{\mathsf{b}-1}\|(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{j})^{\star}\|^{\mathsf{b}-j}}{\prod_{j=0}^{\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{\mathsf{b}-j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{P(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}{P(\mathbf{C})}\leq\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}}.

where the last inequality follows from
Corollary

26.12
and where the third equality follows from dividing by
\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{(n+1)-(\mathsf{b}+k)}
so that

\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{n+1-j}=\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{(\mathsf{b}+k)-j}=\prod_{j=0}^{\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|^{j},

and similarly for
(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{j})^{\star}
, where we use that
\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|=\prod_{j=k}^{k+\mathsf{b}-1}\|(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{j})^{\star}\|
.
∎

26.3.
Reduction of dual bases

Definition 26.14

(A dually exponentially reduced basis)
.

A square basis
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
of a lattice
\Lambda
is
T
-dually exponentially reduced (for
T\geq 1
) if the dual basis
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{B}^{-\top}
satisfies
\|\mathbf{d}_{j}\|\leq 2^{Tn}\lambda_{j}(\Lambda^{\vee})
, where
\Lambda^{\vee}
denotes the dual lattice of
\Lambda
.

26.4.
Transference theorems

Banaszczyk proved the following transference theorem, relating the successive minima of a lattice
\Lambda
with the ones of its dual lattice
\Lambda^{\vee}
.

Theorem 26.15

(
[
6
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
\Lambda
be an arbitrary lattice of rank
n
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, for some
m\geq n\geq 1
. Then, for all
1\leq i\leq n
it holds that

1\leq\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)\cdot\lambda_{n-i+1}(\Lambda^{\vee})\leq n.

Proof.

The upper bound is exactly
[
6
, Theorem 2.1]
. The lower bound comes from the following standard argument. Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
be
i
linearly independent vectors of
\Lambda
with euclidean norm
\leq\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)
, and
w_{1},\dots,w_{n-i+1}
be
(n-i+1)
linearly independent vectors of
\Lambda^{\vee}
with euclidean norm
\leq\lambda_{n-i+1}(\Lambda^{\vee})
. Let
V
be the vector space spanned by the
(v_{j})_{j}
and
W
be the vector space spanned by the
(w_{j})_{j}
. Both spaces live in
\Span(\Lambda)
, which has dimension
n
. Moreover,
\dim(V)+\dim(W)=n+1
. Hence,
V
and
W
cannot be orthogonal, which means that there should exist
v_{k}
and
w_{\ell}
such that
\langle v_{k},w_{\ell}\rangle\neq 0
. By definition of the dual, this implies that
|\langle v_{k},w_{\ell}\rangle|\geq 1
(because the inner product must be an integer). And so,
1\leq|\langle v_{k},w_{\ell}\rangle|\leq\|v_{k}\|\cdot\|w_{\ell}\|\leq\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)\cdot\lambda_{n-i+1}(\Lambda^{\vee})
.
∎

27.
The BKZ algorithm on integer bases

27.1.
Introduction

In this section, we show that the variant of BKZ
from Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé
[
39
, Algorithm 2]
(which we call BKZ’ in the rest of this section)
does not cause coefficient explosion in integer basis matrices.
Hence, BKZ’ is suitable for integer lattice bases and one can obtain a bound on the bit-complexity of the algorithm, and not only the number of tours needed. This was already discussed in
[
39
, Section 3, Cost of BKZ’]
, where the authors explained the big lines of the reasoning. In this section, we make this discussion fully formal.

Concretely, applying the
\mathsf{b}
-BKZ’ algorithm of Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé on an integer basis
\mathbf{B}
of a lattice
\Lambda
yields a new basis
\mathbf{C}=(\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
of
\Lambda
satisfying
\|\mathbf{c}_{1}\|\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\frac{n-1}{2(\mathsf{b}-1)}+\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\covol(\Lambda)^{1/n}
. Furthermore, this algorithm runs in time
\poly(n,\log\max_{j}\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|)\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
.

Approach.

In
[
39
]
, the authors give an upper bound on the
number of tours
needed for the BKZ’ algorithm, in order to have a provable upper bound on the short vector output by the algorithm.
To obtain a total and provable bit-complexity for the time of the
\mathsf{b}
-BKZ’ variant
for integer matrices, we need to show that no coefficient explosion occurs and that the
\mathsf{b}
-HKZ algorithm on the sub-blocks runs in time about
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
.

To show that no coefficient explosion occurs, we rely on standard techniques using the potential
P(\mathbf{B})
of a basis. Namely, it is a fact that the bit sizes of the coefficients occurring in a size-reduced basis
\mathbf{B}
and its Gram-Schmidt basis
\mathbf{B}^{\star}
are bounded by
O(\log_{2}(nP(\mathbf{B})))
(see
Corollary

26.9
). Hence, it is enough to sufficiently bound the potential.

In the BKZ’ algorithm of Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé, only size-reduction and HKZ-reduction in dimension
\mathsf{b}
occur (see
Algorithm

7
). As size-reduction does not change the potential, only the influence of HKZ-reduction on the potential needs to be examined. It can be shown that
\mathsf{b}
-HKZ-reduction can only increase the potential of a basis by a factor
\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}}
(see
Corollary

26.12
). Hence, all coefficients remain sufficiently bounded, whenever the number of HKZ-reductions is polynomially bounded. But the latter is true by the fact that the number of ‘BKZ tours’ (essentially
n
times a
\mathsf{b}
-HKZ-reduction) is bounded; this is what Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé show in their work
[
39
, Theorem 1]
.

For the HKZ-reduction algorithm in dimension
\mathsf{b}
, that is used as a subroutine in the BKZ-algorithm, we use the provable Kannan-algorithm
[
40
]
on integer matrices with a run time of about
\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
(disregarding the size of the basis matrix). Note that the projected sub-blocks of the matrix are rational, but can be scaled up to be integral. This does not significantly increase the bit-size, because the denominators can be shown to be bounded by the potential.

27.2.
The BKZ’ algorithm of Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé

We restate the algorithm of Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé
[
39
, Algorithm 2]
and their result on the upper bound
on the number of tours required to obtain a sufficiently short non-zero vector of the input lattice.

Algorithm 7

The BKZ’ algorithm of Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé, for integer bases

0:

•

A basis
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}

•

A block size
\mathsf{b}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

0:

A basis of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B})
.

1:

repeat

2:

for

k
from
1
to
n-\mathsf{b}+1

do

3:

Modify
(\mathbf{b}_{i})_{k\leq i<k+\mathsf{b}}
so that
(\pi_{k-1}(\mathbf{b}_{i}))_{k\leq i<k+\mathsf{b}}
is HKZ-reduced;

4:

Size-reduce
(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
.

5:

end

for

6:

until
no changes occur or termination is requested.

7:

return

(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
.

Theorem 27.1

(Theorem 1 of
[
39
]
)
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that the following holds for all
n
and
\mathsf{b}
. Let
\mathbf{B}=(\mathbf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{b}_{n})
be a basis of a lattice
\Lambda
, given as input to the modified BKZ algorithm (
Algorithm

7
) with block-size
\mathsf{b}
. If terminated after

\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}=C\frac{n^{3}}{\mathsf{b}^{2}}(\log n+\log\log\max_{i}\frac{\|\mathbf{b}_{i}\|}{\covol(\Lambda)^{1/n}})

calls to an HKZ-reduction in dimension
\mathsf{b}
, the output
\mathbf{C}=(\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
is a basis of
\Lambda
that satisfies

\|\mathbf{c}_{1}\|\leq 2\mathsf{b}^{\frac{n-1}{2(\mathsf{b}-1)}+\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\covol(\Lambda)^{1/n}.

Remark 27.2
.

In the original theorem statement
[
39
, Theorem 1]
, the last line reads:
“If
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, then the overall cost
37
37

37

Here,
\size(\mathbf{B})=\sum_{ij}\size(\mathbf{B}_{ij})
and
\size(a/b)=\log|a|+\log|b|
for reduced fractions
a/b
. The number
\mbox{Cost}_{\mathrm{HKZ}}(\mathsf{b})
is the cost of applying HKZ in dimension
\mathsf{b}
.
is
\leq\poly(n,\size(\mathbf{B}))\cdot\mathrm{Cost}_{\mathrm{HKZ}}(\mathsf{b})
.”

It is precisely this statement in the last line that we prove in this section. This, because the authors
[
39
]
only gave a sketch of how to show this (
[
39
, Section 3, Cost of BKZ’]
). In this section we will follow their sketch and prove this statement rigorously.

27.3.
Applying BKZ’ to integral bases

Proposition 27.3
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
be
a
basis of a lattice
\Lambda
, with
m\geq n\geq 2
. Let
\mathsf{b}\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
be a block-size parameter.
Then the BKZ’ algorithm of Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé on input
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathsf{b}
has a bit-complexity

\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}),m)\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}

and
outputs a basis
\mathbf{C}=(\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
satisfying

\|\mathbf{c}_{1}\|\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\frac{n-1}{2(\mathsf{b}-1)}+\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\covol(\Lambda)^{1/n}\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda).

(162)

Proof.

We instantiate
Theorem

27.1
with the integral basis
\mathbf{B}
, yielding the shortness bound
38
38

38

The additional shortness bound in terms of
\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
comes from the fact that
\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)^{n}\geq\prod_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)\geq\covol(\Lambda)
. For the simplification into
\mathsf{b}^{n/\mathsf{b}}
, one can observe that
(n-1)b+3(\mathsf{b}-1)b\leq 4n(\mathsf{b}-1)
for all
\mathsf{b}
satisfying
2\leq\mathsf{b}\leq n
. Dividing by
2(\mathsf{b}-1)\mathsf{b}
yields the simplification.
in
Equation

162
and an upper bound on the number of HKZ-reductions
\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}
in dimension
\mathsf{b}
. Note that
\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}
is
\poly(n,\size(\mathbf{B}))
, according to
Theorem

27.1
.

For the
\mathsf{b}
-HKZ reduction algorithm we use the provable Kannan-algorithm
[
40
, Theorem 2]
: on input an integral basis
\mathbf{M}
of a rank-
\mathsf{b}
lattice
L\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, this algorithm runs in time
\poly(\size(\mathbf{M}),m)\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}/(2e)+o(\mathsf{b})}=\poly(\size(\mathbf{M}),m)\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
and outputs an HKZ-reduced basis of
L
.
We will apply this algorithm to the projected bases
\mathbf{B}_{[k:k+\mathsf{b}-1]}
occurring in the BKZ’ algorithm. Even though these bases are rational, this is still possible just by multiplying out the denominators of the coefficients (and revert that process after the lattice reduction). For this to be feasible, we will of course need to show that these coefficients are sufficiently small in size (i.e., have sufficiently small numerator and denominator).

Concluding, if we can show that all sizes of the coefficients occurring in the subblocks of all intermediately computed matrices in
Algorithm

7
remain sufficiently bounded, say
\leq\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}).m)
, we can indeed conclude that
Algorithm

7
has bit-complexity
\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}),m)
.

So, indeed, it remains to show that all (rational) coefficients of the occurring bases, their Gram-Schmidt bases and the sub-blocks
\mathbf{B}_{[j:j+\mathsf{b}-1]}
remain bounded in bit-size by
\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}),m)
. We distinguish some cases

•

The very first time that a HKZ-reduction is applied is just on the basis
\mathbf{B}_{[1:\mathsf{b}]}
, whose coefficient sizes are clearly bounded by
\poly(\log P(\mathbf{B}))
.

•

After
this very first time, we can, by line
4
always assume that the basis at hand, which we call
\mathbf{B}^{(0)}
for now, is size-reduced right before line
3
. Hence, the coefficients of
(\pi_{k-1}((\mathbf{b}^{(0)}_{i}))_{k\leq i<k+\mathsf{b}}
are bounded in size by
\poly(\log P(\mathbf{B}^{(0)}))
,
by
Corollary

26.9
. Hence Kannan’s HKZ algorithm can reasonably applied in time
\poly(\log(P(\mathbf{B}^{(0)})),m)\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
.

•

Right after line
3
but
before
line
4
, the new matrix
\mathbf{B}^{(1)}=\mathbf{B}^{(0)}\mathbf{U}
(where
\mathbf{U}
only changes
(\mathbf{b}^{(0)}_{i})_{k\leq i<k+\mathsf{b}}
) is not size-reduced anymore. But we do know, by
Corollary

26.12
, that

P(\mathbf{B}^{(1)})\leq\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}}P(\mathbf{B}^{(0)})
. Since
(\pi_{k-1}(\mathbf{b}^{(1)}_{i}))_{k\leq i<k+\mathsf{b}}
is HKZ-reduced (and thus size-reduced by definition), we know that the coefficients of

\mathbf{B}^{(1)}_{[k:k+\mathsf{b}-1]}
are bounded by
\poly(\log(P(\mathbf{B}^{(1)})),m)
, by
Corollary

26.9
. Since for
i\notin\{k,\ldots,k+\mathsf{b}-1\}
,
\mathbf{b}^{(0)}_{i}
are unaffected by
\mathbf{U}
, we know that these still
satisfy
\size(\mathbf{b}^{(0)}_{i})\leq\poly(\log(P(\mathbf{B}^{(0)})),m)
. Hence, we can certainly find a lift (by using Babai’s nearest plane algorithm) of
\mathbf{B}^{(1)}_{[k:k+\mathsf{b}-1]}
to
\mathbf{B}^{(1)}

for which the coefficient sizes of
(\mathbf{b}^{(1)}_{i}))_{k\leq i<k+\mathsf{b}}
are bounded by

\poly(\log(P(\mathbf{B}^{(0)})),\log(P(\mathbf{B}^{(1)})),m)=\poly(\log(P(\mathbf{B}^{(0)}))+\mathsf{b},m).

(Note that this lift does
not
affect the potential of
\mathbf{B}^{(1)}
.

Note that in the previous reasoning we see that in each HKZ-reduction, the potential of the basis
the algorithm is working on is maximally increased by a factor
\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}}
. But we know
that the maximum number of HKZ reductions in the entire algorithm is at most
\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}\cdot n=\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}),m)

(where
\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}
is the number of tours). Hence, the bases occurring in the algorithm can maximally have log potential of

\log(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{occurring}})\leq\log(\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}})\cdot\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}\cdot n+\log(P(\mathbf{B}))\leq\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}),m).

Hence all occurring bases
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{occurring}}
have a logarithmic potential bounded by
\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}),m)
, which has
as an immediate consequence that all bases, Gram-Schmidt bases and projected bases have coefficient sizes bounded by
\poly(\size(\mathbf{B}),\allowbreak m)
, which
finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 27.4
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
be
a basis of a lattice
\Lambda
, with
m\geq n\geq 2
and let
\mathsf{b}\in\{2,\cdots,n\}
.
Then there exists an algorithm that takes as input
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathsf{b}
, has bit-complexity

\poly(m,\size(\mathbf{B}))\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}

and outputs a basis
\mathbf{C}=(\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
of
\Lambda
satisfying

\|\mathbf{c}_{j}\|\leq n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\penalty\ \mbox{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(163)

Proof.

We apply the provable BKZ’ algorithm of Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé as in
Proposition

27.3
,
recursively to

\mathbf{B},\penalty\ \det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:1]})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{2:n},\penalty\ \ldots,\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:j]})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{j+1:n}\penalty\ ,\ldots,\penalty\ \det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:n-\mathsf{b}]})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{n-\mathsf{b}+1:n}.

Here, we keep using
the notation
\mathbf{B}
after an application of BKZ’. After these applications,
we backwards recursively lift this sequence into a size-reduced basis of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B})
.

More precisely we apply the following steps:

•

Apply BKZ’ to
\mathbf{B}
, call the result
\mathbf{B}
.

•

Construct the basis
\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:1]})\mathbf{B}_{[2:n]}
(integral, by
Lemma

26.5
) and apply BKZ’ to it, call the result again
\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:1]})\mathbf{B}_{[2:n]}
.

•

\ldots

•

Construct the basis
\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:j]})\mathbf{B}_{[j+1:n]}
(integral) and apply BKZ’ to it, call the result again
\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:j]})\mathbf{B}_{[j+1:n]}
.

•

\ldots

•

Construct the basis
\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:n-\mathsf{b}]})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}_{n-\mathsf{b}+1:n}
(integral) and apply BKZ’ to it, call the result again
\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:j]})\mathbf{B}_{[j+1:n]}
.

•

Going backwards, add
c_{k}\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{n-\mathsf{b}}
with
c_{k}\in[-1/2,1/2)
to each vector in
\mathbf{B}_{n-\mathsf{b}+1:n}
to reconstruct a short basis
\mathbf{B}_{n-\mathsf{b}:n}
.

•

…

•

Add
c_{k}\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}
with
c_{k}\in[-1/2,1/2)
to each vector in
\mathbf{B}_{j+1:n}
to reconstruct a short basis
\mathbf{B}_{j:n}
.

•

…

•

Add
c_{k}\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}
with
c_{k}\in[-1/2,1/2)
to each vector in
\mathbf{B}_{2:n}
to reconstruct a short basis
\mathbf{B}
.

Since a BKZ’-reduction as in
Proposition

27.3
consists of
\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}
HKZ-reductions, each BKZ’ reduction can maximally increase the potential by a factor
\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}\cdot\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}}
(see
Corollary

26.12
). Hence the
n-\mathsf{b}
BKZ’-reductions in above procedure can at most result in intermediate potentials of size

\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}\cdot\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}\cdot n}\cdot P(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{init}}),

where
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{init}}
is the starting basis. Since
\det(\mathbf{B}_{[1:k]})
divide
P(\mathbf{B})
(the potential of the same basis), and since
\mathbf{B}_{[k+1:n]}
has size bounded in
O(\log(n\cdot P(\mathbf{B})))
(see
Corollary

26.9
), we can immediately deduce that in above procedure, all bit sizes remain within (for the bound on
\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}
see
Theorem

27.1
)

\log\left(n\mathsf{b}^{2\mathsf{b}^{2}\cdot\tau_{\mathrm{BKZ}}\cdot n}\cdot P(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{init}})\right)=\poly(m,\size(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{init}})).

In the resulting basis, we have, by construction,

\|\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}\|\leq 2\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\penalty\ \mbox{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

where we use that
\lambda_{n-j}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{B}_{j+1:n}))\leq\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)
and
\mathsf{b}^{2(n-j)/\mathsf{b}}\leq\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}
. For the last
\mathsf{b}
vectors, we have this shortness guarantee from the
\mathsf{b}
-HKZ reduction (see
[
48
]
or the proof of
Lemma

26.11
).

Hence, by the size-reduced-like way the basis
\mathbf{B}
is constructed, we have
\mathbf{b}_{j}=\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{j}+\sum_{i<j}c_{i}\mathbf{b}^{\star}_{i}
with
c_{i}\in[-1/2,1/2)
, and we obtain

\|\mathbf{b}_{j}\|\leq 2n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\penalty\ \mbox{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

as required.

∎

28.
The BKZ algorithm on approximate bases

28.1.
Introduction

In this section, we will show that there exists a provable algorithm that runs essentially in time
O(\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}})
that computes a basis of an ideal lattice
x\mathfrak{a}
whose Gram-Schmidt lengths are at most
\mathsf{b}^{O(n/\mathsf{b})}\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a})
, which is sufficient for the needs of this paper (see
Sections

8.3
and
1
). We assume throughout this entire section that an LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is given; in the end result,
Lemma

8.5
, this assumption is again explicitly mentioned.

In all this section, the ideal lattices
x\mathfrak{a}
we considered will be represented exactly. To do so, we will restrict ourselves to elements
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
that have all their complex coordinates of the form
a+ib
, with
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
. In other words, we consider the intersection of
K_{\mathbb{R}}
with the subset
(\mathbb{Q}+i\mathbb{Q})^{n}
of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The ideal
\mathfrak{a}
is a fractional ideal, and will be represented by a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis, consisting of
n
elements
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
of
K
(which can be exactly represented in a fixed basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
).

Even though we have a way to represent the ideal lattice
x\mathfrak{a}
exactly, we can only compute an
approximate basis
of the corresponding lattice, due to the (irrational) Minkowski embedding of
x\mathfrak{a}
into
K_{\mathbb{R}}
(even whenever
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
itself is rational). In this section
we settle these precision issues by applying the basis reduction algorithms (LLL, BKZ’) on rational approximations of the bases of
x\mathfrak{a}
.

The algorithm and its analysis consists of the following steps. These steps (i) - (v) will correspond with
Sections

28.2
,
28.3
,
28.4
,
28.5
and
28.6

(i)

Computing an (approximate) dual basis of the basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
.
For the algorithm to succeed, the basis that we start with is required to be
dually
reduced. For the purpose
of reducing it, we therefore first compute the dual basis of the input basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
(which we may assume to be in some Hermite Normal form).

(ii)

Apply the the Buchmann-Pohst-Kessler algorithm to this approximate dual basis.
To make the dual basis reduced, we apply the Buchmann-Pohst-Kessler algorithm
[
18
,
19
]
(see also
Section

23
). We apply the inverse-transposed transformation to the original basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
, to obtain a dually exponentially reduced basis (see
Definition

26.14
). This part is treated in
Section

28.3
.

(iii)

Apply the variant of BKZ by Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé
[
39
]
.
Applying a variant of BKZ with provable running time on this dually exponentially reduced (approximate) basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
yields an approximate basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
with BKZ-like shortness guarantees on the basis vectors.

(iv)

Apply the closeness-lemma.
By computing an initial approximate basis with sufficiently good precision, the same unimodular transformation that makes this approximate basis have BKZ-like qualities, will also make the exact basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
satisfy these shortness properties. Note that the unimodular transform can be actually exactly (that is, not approximately) applied to a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
which consists of elements
(x\cdot\alpha_{1},\ldots,x\cdot\alpha_{n})\in K_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\alpha_{j}\in K
.

(v)

Concluding.
Applying the compositions of the unimodular transformations in each step to the initial basis
(x\cdot\alpha_{1},\ldots,x\cdot\alpha_{n})
of
x\mathfrak{a}
yields a new basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
that has BKZ-like shortness guarantees.

28.2.
Computing an (approximate) dual basis of the basis of
x\mathfrak{a}

Introduction

Computing the dual basis of an approximate basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\approx\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
of
x\mathfrak{a}

is done by inverting and transposing the matrix:
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}
.
The precision loss in numerical approximation of inversion is proportional to
\|\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\|
, the matrix norm of the inverse of the basis. Hence, we would like to upper bound this matrix norm.

We do that by applying
Lemma

24.2
, which bounds this matrix norm in terms of how far the basis vectors
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
are from attaining the successive minima
\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})
. Since we generally assume that ideals are given in a Hermite Normal Form (HNF) in coordinates of an LLL-reduced
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
(see
Section

2.6
), a reasonable upper bound on the basis vectors
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
can be achieved. On the other hand, a lower bound on
\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})
can be achieved by using ideal lattice properties from
Lemma

2.13
.

To obtain these results, the following lemma will turn out useful.

Lemma 28.1
.

Let
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
and
\mathfrak{a}\in\ideals
. Let
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}=(x\alpha_{1},\ldots,x\alpha_{n})\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
a basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
, where the basis
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
is assumed to be in HNF form whenever written in terms of an LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
(see
Section

2.6
). Then

(i)

For all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we have
\|x\alpha_{j}\|\leq 2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})}\cdot n^{3/2}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
.

(ii)

N(x\mathfrak{a})\leq 2^{n\size(x\mathfrak{a})}
.

(iii)

N(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\leq 2^{n\size(x\mathfrak{a})}
.

(iv)

\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\leq 2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})}
.

where
\size
is defined in
Section

2.6
.

Proof.

For (
i
), we make use of the assumption that
\mathfrak{a}
is represented by a rational HNF basis in terms of an LLL-reduced
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})
of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
(for which thus holds
\|\beta_{j}\|\leq 2^{n}\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
by
Lemma

2.13
). More precisely,
\alpha_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{ij}\beta_{i}
,
where
(a_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n\times n}
is an HNF-reduced basis. By definition of the size of an ideal (see
Section

2.6
), we have that
\size(\mathfrak{a})\geq\size(a_{ij})
. Recall that the size of rational numbers
a/b
with
\gcd(a,b)=1
is equal to
\log_{2}|a|+\log_{2}|b|
. Hence, using
\size(x\mathfrak{a})=\size(x)+\size(\mathfrak{a})

\|x\alpha_{j}\|\leq n\cdot\max_{j}|x_{j}|\cdot\max_{ij}2^{\size(a_{ij})}\cdot\underbrace{\max_{j}\|\beta_{j}\|}_{\leq 2^{n}\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}}\leq 2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})}\cdot n^{3/2}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}.

For (
ii
) note that
N(x\mathfrak{a})
is equal to
\det((a_{ij})_{ij})\cdot\prod_{j}x_{j}
.
By Hadamard’s bound,
\det((a_{ij})_{ij})\leq\prod_{j}\|(a_{ij})_{i}\|_{2}\leq\prod_{j}(\sum_{i}|a_{ij}|)
(by the inequality between
1
and
2
-norms)
and the latter can be bounded by
(\sum_{i}|a_{ij}|)^{n}

and hence
\log_{2}N(x\mathfrak{a})\leq n\size(x)+n\log_{2}(\sum_{i}|a_{ij}|)\leq n\size(x)+n\size(\mathfrak{a})=n\size(x\mathfrak{a})
.

Item (
iii
) can be proven as follows. Because
\norm(x\mathfrak{a})=|\norm(x)|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{a})
and
|\norm(x)|^{-1}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}|x_{j}|^{-1}\leq 2^{\size(x)}
, we only need to show that
\norm(\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\leq 2^{n\size(\mathfrak{a})}
. Write
\mathfrak{a}=\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathfrak{b}
with
d
a minimal positive integer and
\mathfrak{b}
an integral ideal. Since
d
minimally clears the denominators of
\mathfrak{a}
, we certainly have
\size(d)\leq\size(\mathfrak{a})
. Now
\norm(\mathfrak{a})^{-1}=\norm(\mathfrak{a}^{-1})=|\norm(d)|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{b})^{-1}\leq|\norm(d)|=d^{n}\leq 2^{n\size(d)}\leq 2^{n\size(\mathfrak{a})}
.

Item (
iv
) holds because
x\mathfrak{a}
is an ideal lattice, which implies
\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\geq\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}
due to item (
i
) from
Lemma

2.13
. Hence
\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\leq\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1/n}
and the result follows from item (
iii
)
∎

Bounding the matrix norm of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}

We start by bounding the matrix norm of the exact inverse of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
.

Lemma 28.2
.

Let
x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
and
\mathfrak{a}\in\ideals
. Let
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}=(x\alpha_{1},\ldots,x\alpha_{n})\in K_{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
a basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
, where the basis
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
is assumed to be in HNF form whenever written in terms of an LLL-reduced basis of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
(see
Section

2.6
). Then

\lVert\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\leq n^{2n+1}\cdot 2^{n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/2}\cdot 2^{(2n+1)\size(x\mathfrak{a})}.

Proof.

We apply
Lemma

24.2
to obtain

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq n^{n/2+1}\cdot\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\cdot\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\|x\alpha_{j}\|}{\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq n^{n/2+1}\cdot\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{-1/2}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|x\alpha_{j}\|.

(164)

where we used
\prod_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})\geq\covol(x\mathfrak{a})=\norm(x\mathfrak{a})|\Delta_{K}|^{1/2}
in the last inequality.

Using that
\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\leq 2^{n\size(x\mathfrak{a})}
,
\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\leq 2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})}
and
\|x\alpha_{j}\|\leq 2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})}\cdot n^{3/2}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
(see
Lemma

28.1
(
iii
), (
iv
) and (
i
)), and applying it to
Equation

164
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq n^{n/2+1}\cdot\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{-1/2}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|x\alpha_{j}\|

\displaystyle\leq n^{n/2+1}\cdot 2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{-1/2}\cdot 2^{n\size(x\mathfrak{a})}\cdot\left(2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})}\cdot n^{3/2}\cdot 2^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle\leq n^{2n+1}\cdot 2^{n^{2}}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/2}\cdot 2^{(2n+1)\size(x\mathfrak{a})}.

∎

We can therefore conclude that the loss in precision by
inverting an approximate basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
can be reasonably
upper bounded.

Lemma 28.3
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{n\times n}
be an approximation of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
, then

\log_{2}\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}-\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\leq\log_{2}\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}-\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\rVert_{2}+5n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\log_{2}|\Delta_{K}|+3n\size(x\mathfrak{a}),

under the assumption that
\log_{2}\lVert\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\rVert_{2}\leq-(5n^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log_{2}|\Delta_{K}|+3n\size(x\mathfrak{a}))
.

Proof.

By
[
7
, Cor. 7.2, Eq. 7.46]
, we have that

\lVert\mathbf{B}^{-1}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\leq 2\lVert\mathbf{B}^{-1}\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{2},

as long as
2\lVert\mathbf{B}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{2}\leq 1
.
Instantiating with
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
and the bound of
Lemma

28.2
, we obtain the claim,
using the following computation:

\displaystyle\log_{2}(2\|\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1}\|)

\displaystyle\leq 1+(2n+1)\log_{2}(n)+n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|+(2n+1)\size(x\mathfrak{a})

\displaystyle\leq 2n+2n^{2}+n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|+(2n+1)\size(x\mathfrak{a})

\displaystyle\leq 5n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\log|\Delta_{K}|+3n\size(x\mathfrak{a})

using the fact that for any positive integer
n
it holds that
2n\leq 2n^{2}
,
\log_{2}(n)\leq n
and
\log_{2}(n)+1\leq 2n
. The condition
2\lVert\mathbf{B}^{-1}\rVert_{2}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{B}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{2}\leq 1
holds by the assumption in the lemma’s statement.
∎

Hence, the inversion of the approximate basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
only yields a polynomial (in the size of the input) loss of bit precision.

Lemma 28.4
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
be an approximation of a
T
-dually exponentially reduced basis
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
with
\log\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}-\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\rVert_{2}\leq-(9n^{2}+\tfrac{5}{2}\log_{2}|\Delta_{K}|+5n\size(x\mathfrak{a})+n(T+2))
. Then
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
is
(T+3)
-dually exponentially reduced.

Proof.

Let us write

(\mathbf{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{d}_{n})=\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}
and
(\tilde{\mathbf{d}}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbf{d}}_{n})=\tilde{\mathbf{D}}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}
. Let us also define
\varepsilon
such that
\log_{2}(1/\varepsilon)=4n^{2}+2\log_{2}|\Delta_{K}|+2n\size(x\mathfrak{a})
.
By
Lemma

28.3
(and since transposing does not change the
2
-norm of matrices), we have

\displaystyle\log_{2}\lVert\mathbf{D}-\tilde{\mathbf{D}}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\log_{2}\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}-\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\rVert_{2}+5n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\log_{2}|\Delta_{K}|+3n\size(x\mathfrak{a})

\displaystyle\leq-(4n^{2}+2\log_{2}|\Delta_{K}|+2n\size(x\mathfrak{a}))\leq\log(\varepsilon).

Then, we have, for all
j
that
\|\tilde{\mathbf{d}_{j}}-\mathbf{d}_{j}\|=\|(\mathbf{D}-\tilde{\mathbf{D}})\mathbf{e}_{j}\|\leq\lVert\mathbf{D}-\tilde{\mathbf{D}}\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon
, which implies that

\|\tilde{\mathbf{d}_{j}}\|=\|\mathbf{d}_{j}\|+\varepsilon\leq 2^{Tn}\lambda_{j}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})+\varepsilon\leq(2^{Tn}+1)\lambda_{j}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})\leq 2^{(T+1)n}\lambda_{j}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee}),

(165)

where, in the second inequality, we used the fact that
\varepsilon\leq\lambda_{1}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})
. This inequality comes from combining the transference bound (
Theorem

26.15
) with items (
ii
) and (
iv
) from
Lemma

2.13
and item (
ii
) of
Lemma

28.1

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\geq(\sqrt{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{3/2}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n})^{-1}

\displaystyle\geq(\sqrt{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{3/2}\cdot 2^{\size(x\mathfrak{a})})^{-1}\geq\varepsilon

Writing
\tilde{\Lambda}
for the lattice generated by the approximation
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
, it suffices to show that
\lambda_{j}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})\leq 2n\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda}^{\vee})
for all
j
, to show that
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
is
(T+2)
-dually exponentially reduced. Indeed, then, by
Equation

165
, we have
\|\tilde{\mathbf{d}_{j}}\|\leq 2^{(T+1)n}\lambda_{j}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})\leq 2^{(T+3)n}\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda}^{\vee})
for all
j
.

By transference (see
Theorem

26.15
), we have

\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda^{\vee})\cdot\lambda_{n-j}(\Lambda)}{\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda}^{\vee})\cdot\lambda_{n-j}(\tilde{\Lambda})}\leq n

hence
\lambda_{j}(\Lambda^{\vee})\leq n\frac{\lambda_{n-j}(\tilde{\Lambda})}{\lambda_{n-j}(\Lambda)}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda}^{\vee})\leq 2n\cdot\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda}^{\vee})
, if one shows that
\frac{\lambda_{n-j}(\tilde{\Lambda})}{\lambda_{n-j}(\Lambda)}\leq 2
, i.e.,
\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda})\leq 2\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)
for all
j
. This latter statement is what we finish the proof with.

Write
\mathbf{x}_{j}\in x\mathfrak{a}=\Lambda
for the vector attaining
\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})
. Then
\mathbf{x}_{j}=\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\mathbf{u}_{j}
for some
\mathbf{u}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. By the closeness lemma (
Lemma

28.6
), we must have
\|\mathbf{u}_{j}\|\leq 2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})/\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\leq 2^{(T+2)n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
(by
Lemma

2.13
). Hence

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\mathbf{u}_{j}=\mathbf{x}_{j}+(\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}})\mathbf{u}_{j}
satisfy the property that
\{\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{j}\}
span a
j
-dimensional space, and therefore surely

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})+\lVert\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\rVert_{2}\cdot 2^{(T+2)n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a})+\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\leq 2\cdot\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a}),

since

\lVert\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\rVert_{2}\leq 2^{-n\size(x\mathfrak{a})-\log|\Delta_{K}|-(T+2)n}\leq\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{-1/n}\cdot 2^{-(T+2)n}\leq\lambda_{1}(x\mathfrak{a})\cdot 2^{-(T+2)n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{-1/n}
(where we used item (
i
) from
Lemma

2.13
).
∎

28.3.
Applying Buchmann-Pohst-Kessler to the approximate dual basis

We apply
Theorem

23.5
with
k=r_{0}=n_{2}=[K:\mathbb{Q}]=:n
,
n_{1}=0
,
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}
. To satisfy the initial conditions, we must have
\log_{2}\|\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}\|_{2,\infty}<\log_{2}(1/4\cdot\mu\cdot C_{0}^{-1})
,
where
39
39

39

We have
\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a})\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{\tfrac{3}{2n}}\cdot\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{1/n}
by items (
ii
) and (
iv
) of
Lemma

2.13
.

\mu=\norm(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1/n}\cdot n^{-1/2}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|^{-\tfrac{3}{2n}}\leq\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a})^{-1}\leq\lambda_{1}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})
.
And

C_{0}=2^{8n}\cdot\left(\frac{n\cdot 4^{n}\cdot\|\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}\|_{2,\infty}}{\mu}\right)^{2(n+1)}

By
Lemma

28.2
, we have that
\|\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}\|_{2,\infty}\leq\|\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}\|_{2}\leq n^{2n+1}2^{n^{2}}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/2}\cdot 2^{2n\size(x\mathfrak{a})}
. Hence,

-\log_{2}(1/4\cdot\mu\cdot C_{0}^{-1})=O(n^{3}+n\log|\Delta_{K}|+n^{2}\size(x\mathfrak{a}))
,
and a precision polynomial in
n,\log|\Delta_{K}|
and
\size(x\mathfrak{a})
is sufficient to apply
Buchmann-Pohst-Kessler. Hence we obtain a unimodular transformation
\mathbf{U}
such that
the basis elements of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}\cdot\mathbf{U}
have an LLL-like shortness quality
compared to the successive minima of
(x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee}
. More precisely, the basis elements

(\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{n})=\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}\cdot\mathbf{U}
satisfy

\|\mathbf{c}_{j}\|\leq(n+2)2^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\cdot\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a}^{\vee})\leq 2^{3n}\cdot\lambda_{j}(x\mathfrak{a}^{\vee}),

hence, the basis
\mathbf{C}_{x\mathfrak{a}}:=(\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}\cdot\mathbf{U})^{-\top}=\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}\mathbf{U}^{-\top}

is
T
-dually exponentially reduced with
T:=3
(see
Definition

26.14
). For this last computation, note that both inverse and transpose switch positions, hence doing them both keeps the positions of the matrices.

We can conclude with the following proposition.

Proposition 28.5
.

There exists an algorithm that computes a
3
-dually exponentially reduced
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(x\alpha_{1},\ldots,x\alpha_{n})
of an ideal lattice
x\mathfrak{a}
in time polynomial in
n,\log|\Delta_{K}|
and
\size(x\mathfrak{a})
.

Proof.

Computing
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
with sufficient precision of order
O(n^{3}+n\log|\Delta_{K}|+n^{2}\size(x\mathfrak{a}))
allows for computing the dual basis
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-\top}=(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{-1})^{\top}
and applying the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm (see
Lemma

28.3
and the discussion above). Hence, by applying the inverse transpose
\mathbf{U}^{-\top}
of the unimodular matrix
\mathbf{U}
that is the output of the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm to the initial
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(x\alpha_{1},\ldots,x\alpha_{n})
of
x\mathfrak{a}
yields a
3
-dually exponentially reduced basis of
x\mathfrak{a}
.
∎

28.4.
Applying the variant of BKZ by Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé

By clearing the denominators in the (new, dually exponentially reduced) matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
,
we obtain an integer basis on which we can apply
Algorithm

7
, a variant of the BKZ algorithm from Hanrot, Pujol and Stehlé. By
Corollary

27.4
, this algorithm outputs in time
40
40

40

Clearing denominators in a basis
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{Q}^{m\times n}
at most increases the size of that basis by
2\cdot m\cdot n
. Since
d\mathbf{B}
for
d=\prod_{ij}d_{ij}
(where
d_{ij}
is the denominator of
\mathbf{B}_{ij}
) has size
mn\size(d)+\size(\mathbf{B})\leq(mn+1)\size(\mathbf{B})\leq 2mn\size(\mathbf{B})

\poly(n,\size(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}))\cdot\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}}
a basis
\tilde{\mathbf{C}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}=(\mathbf{c}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{c}_{n})
of
x\mathfrak{a}
that satisfies

\|\mathbf{c}_{j}\|\leq n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a}).

28.5.
The closeness lemma

Lemma 28.6
.

Let
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be basis of a rank-
n
lattice
\Lambda

that is
T
-dually exponentially reduced as in
Definition

26.14
and
let
\mathbf{v}\in\Lambda
be any lattice vector, with
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{u}
for some integral vector
\mathbf{u}
.
Then

\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}\leq n^{3}\cdot 2^{nT}\cdot\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}\leq 2^{n(T+2)}\cdot\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}.

Proof.

Write
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}
for the quadratic form associated to
\mathbf{B}
; then
\mathbf{Q}^{-1}=(\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B})^{-1}=\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-\top}=\mathbf{D}^{\top}\mathbf{D}
is the quadratic form associated to the dual basis
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{B}^{-\top}=(\mathbf{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{d}_{n})
of
\mathbf{B}
.

Denote
\Lambda^{\vee}
for the dual lattice of
\Lambda
.
By
\mathbf{B}
being
T
-dually exponentially reduced, we have
(\mathbf{Q}^{-1})_{jj}=\|\mathbf{d}_{j}\|^{2}\leq 2^{Tn}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\Lambda^{\vee})^{2}\leq n^{2}\cdot 2^{T\cdot n}/\lambda_{n-j+1}(\Lambda)^{2}

by Banaszczyk’s transference theorem (
Theorem

26.15
). Therefore

\Tr(\mathbf{Q}^{-1})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{Q}^{-1})_{ii}\leq n^{2}\cdot 2^{Tn}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}(\Lambda)^{2}}\leq n^{3}\cdot 2^{Tn}/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}.

Write
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n}>0
for the eigenvalues of the positive definite matrix
\mathbf{Q}
. Then, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
\frac{1}{\mu_{i}}\leq\Tr(\mathbf{Q}^{-1})\leq n^{3}\cdot 2^{Tn}/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}
, since all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{Q}
are positive.
Therefore, the inverse of any eigenvalue of
\mathbf{Q}
is upper bounded by
n^{3}\cdot 2^{Tn}/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}
,

Write
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{u}
, then

\|\mathbf{u}\|^{2}=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{u}\leq\frac{\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{u}}{\min_{i}\mu_{i}}\leq\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}\cdot\frac{n^{3}\cdot 2^{Tn}}{\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)^{2}}

which, together with the fact that
n^{3}\leq 2^{2n}
, proves the claim.
∎

Corollary 28.7
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be
T
-dually exponentially reduced bases of rank-
n
lattices
\tilde{\Lambda}
and
\Lambda
respectively.

Then

\left(1-\frac{2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}{\min(\lambda_{1}(\Lambda),\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda}))}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)}{\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda})}\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}{\min(\lambda_{1}(\Lambda),\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda}))}\right)

and
|\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda})-\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)|\leq 2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}\cap\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
for the (not necessarily unimodular) transformation such that
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{U}
attains the successive minima, i.e.,
\|\mathbf{c}_{j}\|=\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)
, and the vectors
\mathbf{c}_{j}
are linearly independent. By
Lemma

28.6
, we know that
\|\mathbf{u}_{j}\|\leq 2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)/\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)
.

Since
\mathbf{U}
is non-singular, then

\tilde{\mathbf{C}}:=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{U}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{n})
consists of
n
independent vectors. Hence
\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda})\leq\max_{1\leq k\leq j}\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{k}\|
, but we have

\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{k}\|\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{k}-\mathbf{c}_{k}\|+\|\mathbf{c}_{k}\|\leq\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{u}_{k}\|+\lambda_{k}(\Lambda)\leq\lambda_{k}(\Lambda)\left(1+\frac{2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}{\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)}\right),

hence, by applying the same reasoning but interchanging
\Lambda
and
\tilde{\Lambda}
,

\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda})\leq\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)\left(1+\frac{2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}{\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)}\right)\mbox{ and }\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)\leq\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda})\left(1+\frac{2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}{\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda})}\right).

Instantiating this with
j=1
, shows

|\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda})-\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)|\leq 2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}
. Applying the inequality
1-x\leq 1/(1+x)
(which holds for all
x\geq 0
) and replacing
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)
and
\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda})
by their minimum, we obtain the final claim

\left(1-\frac{2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}{\min(\lambda_{1}(\Lambda),\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda}))}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda_{j}(\Lambda)}{\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\Lambda})}\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{(T+2)n}\cdot\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}}{\min(\lambda_{1}(\Lambda),\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda}))}\right).

∎

Corollary 28.8
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be
T
-dually exponentially reduced bases of rank-
n
lattices
\tilde{\Lambda}
and
\Lambda
respectively.
Let
\tilde{\mathbf{C}}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\mathbf{U}
be a basis of
\tilde{\Lambda}
satisfying

\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{j}\|\leq q_{j}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Lambda})\penalty\ \mbox{ for all $j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$},

for
q_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
Suppose
\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\cdot 2^{-(T+2)n}\cdot\min(\lambda_{1}(\Lambda),\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda}))
and write
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{U}
. Then,

\|\mathbf{c}_{j}\|\leq 2\cdot q_{j}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\penalty\ \mbox{ for all $j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$}.

Proof.

We have
\|\mathbf{u}_{j}\|\leq 2^{(T+2)n}\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{j}\|/\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda})
by
Lemma

28.6
, and hence

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{c}_{j}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{c}_{j}-\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{j}\|+\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{j}\|\leq\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{u}_{j}\|+\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{j}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\mathbf{c}}_{j}\|\left(1+\frac{\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\cdot 2^{(T+2)n}}{\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda})}\right)\leq q_{j}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\tilde{\Lambda})\cdot\left(1+\frac{\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\cdot 2^{(T+2)n}}{\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq q_{j}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda)\cdot\left(1+\frac{\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}\cdot 2^{(T+2)n}}{\min(\lambda_{1}(\tilde{\Lambda}),\lambda_{1}(\Lambda))}\right)^{2}\leq 2\cdot q_{j}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\Lambda),

where in the penultimate inequality, we used
Corollary

28.7
and in the last inequality we used the assumed upper bound on
\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B}\rVert_{2}
.
∎

28.6.
Conclusion

See
8.5

Proof.

Using the HNF rational matrix
M_{\mathfrak{a}}
, we have a sequence of elements
(\alpha_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{(0)})
generating
\mathfrak{a}
. The exact Minkowski basis is given by
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}=(\sigma(\alpha_{j}^{(0)}))_{\sigma,j}
, i.e., the columns of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
are the Minkowski embeddings of
\alpha_{j}^{(0)}
into
K_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Due to the fact that these numbers are irrational in general, the basis
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
can only be approximated; we call this approximation
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
. Note that the values of
\sigma(\alpha_{j}^{(0)})
can reasonably be approximated by computing the roots of the defining polynomial of the number field
K
. This can be done efficiently by, for example, Newton iteration; this costs time polynomial in the desired bit precision. Hence, if the final required bit precision is polynomial in the size of the input (which we indeed will show to be the case), approximation is not going to have a significant influence on the running time.

Computing the dual.

So, suppose we have a rational approximation
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
within polynomial bits of precision. By
Lemma

28.3
, computing the dual basis of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
only looses
O(n^{2}+\log|\Delta_{K}|+(n+2)\size(x\mathfrak{a}))
bits of precision. Therefore we can assume that the dual basis of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
approximates the dual basis of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}
with polynomial bits of precision.

Applying Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst on the approximate dual basis.

Using the Buchmann-Kessler-Pohst algorithm on the approximate dual basis, we have
Proposition

28.5
, which states we can compute
efficiently a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(x\alpha_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,x\alpha_{n}^{(1)})

that is ‘
3
-dually exponentially reduced’ (see
Definition

26.14
). That means the dual basis

(\mathbf{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{d}_{n})
of this new basis satisfies
\|\mathbf{d}_{j}\|\leq 2^{3n}\cdot\lambda_{j}((x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee})
, where
(x\mathfrak{a})^{\vee}
is the dual lattice of
x\mathfrak{a}
.

This new basis
(x\alpha_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,x\alpha_{n}^{(1)})
is exact
and we can re-approximate the associated basis
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
by
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
. By
Lemma

28.4
, a sufficient (polynomial) approximation
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
can be shown to be
6
-dually exponentially reduced as well.

Applying Hanrot-Pujol-Stehlé.

Without loss of generality, we can clear the denominators in
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
. Then, we apply the BKZ version of Hanrot-Pujol-Stehlé with provable bounds on the number of tours. Using
Corollary

27.4
,
we conclude that there exists an algorithm using time
\poly(n,\size(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}),\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}})=\poly(\log|\Delta_{K}|,\allowbreak\size(x\mathfrak{a}),\mathsf{b}^{\mathsf{b}})

that outputs a
new basis
(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{1}^{(2)},\ldots,\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{n}^{(2)})=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(2)}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}\cdot\mathbf{U}
of the lattice
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)})
that satisfies

\|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{(2)}\|\leq n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{L}(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}))

But we would like the new
exact basis

(x\alpha_{1}^{(2)},\ldots,x\alpha_{n}^{(2)})
, obtained by applying
\mathbf{U}
to
(x\alpha_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,x\alpha_{n}^{(1)})
to satisfy above claim. For this we need the
closeness lemma
.

Applying the closeness lemma.

Note that
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(2)}=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}\mathbf{U}
is an approximation of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(2)}=\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}\mathbf{U}
, the exact Minkowski-basis of the
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(x\alpha_{1}^{(2)},\ldots,x\alpha_{n}^{(2)})
