. Since
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
are both
T
-dually exponentially reduced (with
T=6
and
3
respectively), we can apply
Corollary

28.8
. A sufficient (polynomial bit precision) approximation
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(1)}
then suffices to deduce that the vectors of
\mathbf{B}_{x\mathfrak{a}}^{(2)}
satisfy the same BKZ-like bounds (with a blow-up factor of
2
), hence,

\|x\alpha_{j}^{(2)}\|\leq 2n\cdot\mathsf{b}^{2n/\mathsf{b}}\cdot\lambda_{n}(x\mathfrak{a})\mbox{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

∎

\@tocwriteb

Appendix

A.1.
Proof of
Theorem

6.4
, the random walk theorem

This following proof of
Theorem

6.4
is put in the appendix because it is very similar to proofs in other work
[
14
, Section 3]

[
12
, Chapter 4]
, with the difference that it is here generalized to arbitrary moduli
\mathfrak{m}
and finite-index subgroups of
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, and a slight change of the definition of the log-space
H
.

See
6.4

We follow the proof of
[
14
, Section 3]
, with small adaptations.

The Hecke operator, characters and eigenvalues

Recall that
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
is a finite-index subgroup of
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
where we try to randomize over.
Putting
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime ideal of }K\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B,[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G\mbox{ and }\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}\}
, we define the Hecke operator
\mathcal{H}:L^{2}(G)\rightarrow L^{2}(G)
by the following rule, for
f\in L^{2}(G)
.

\mathcal{H}(f)(x):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}}f(x-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]).

This Hecke operator has the
characters

\chi\in\widehat{G}
as eigen functions, with eigenvalues
\lambda_{\chi}\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying
|\lambda_{\chi}|\leq 1

[
14
, Section 3.2]
. The trivial character
\mathbf{1}\in\widehat{G}
can be shown to have eigenvalue
\lambda_{\mathbf{1}}=1
.

\mathcal{H}(\chi)(\cdot):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}}\chi(\cdot-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])=\chi(\cdot)\cdot\underbrace{\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}}\chi(-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])}_{\lambda_{\chi}}

For a fixed
\chi\in\widehat{G}
, there are
[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]
extensions of this character
to
\theta\in\widehat{\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}
. These characters satisfy the following identity, which can be proved
by using standard character orthogonality techniques.

[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\widehat{\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}\\
\theta|_{G}=\chi\end{subarray}}\theta(-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])=\left\{\begin{matrix}\chi(-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])&\mbox{ if }[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise }\end{matrix}\right.

(166)

Putting
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{\mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime ideal of }K\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B\mbox{ and }\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}\}
(note the absence of the class condition on
\mathfrak{p}
), we thus have, for any fixed
\chi\in\widehat{G}
, using
Equation

166
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\chi}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{P}}\chi(-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\widehat{\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}\\
\theta|_{G}=\chi\end{subarray}}\theta(-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])

(167)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|}{|\mathcal{P}|}\cdot\frac{1}{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\widehat{\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}\\
\theta|_{G}=\chi\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}}\theta(-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])

(168)

Assuming the Extended Riemann Hypothesis (as formulated in
[
44
, §5.7]
), one can use results from analytic number theory
[
44
, Theorem 5.15]
to derive the following asymptotic bound for non-constant characters
\theta\in\widehat{\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}}
(see
[
14
, Section 3.3]
).

\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|}\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}}\theta(-[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})])=O\left(\frac{\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\theta))}{B^{1/2}}\right),

(169)

where
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi)
is the infinite part of the analytic conductor of the character
\chi
(cf.
[
44
, Eq. (5.6)]
), The proof of this result uses the Abel summation formula and is almost the same as in
[
14
, Section 3.3]
or
[
78
, Corollary 2.3.5]
(see also
[
12
, Section 4.3.3]
), with the sole difference that the analytic conductor gets an extra factor
\norm(\mathfrak{m})=\norm(\mathfrak{m}_{0})\cdot 2^{|\mathfrak{m}_{\mathbb{R}}|}
.

By applying
Equation

167
for
\chi=1\in\widehat{G}
, the trivial character, one can get an upper bound on the fraction
\overline{|\mathcal{P}|}/|\mathcal{P}|
. By the fact that
\lambda_{1}=1
,
we obtain, from
Equation

169

\frac{|\mathcal{P}|\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}{|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|}=1+O\left(\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(1))}{B^{1/2}}\right).

(170)

Hence, choosing
B
such that the value of the big-O of
Equation

170
is strictly bounded by a half
41
41

41

Notice that, in order the bound
Equation

172
to be non-trivial (i.e., smaller than
1
),
we already need
B
to satisfy this condition, so it does not add an extra requirement on
B
. In
Equation

169
, the analytic conductor satisfies
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\theta)\leq 4^{n}

for extensions
\theta
of the unit character on
G
, as
T^{\mathfrak{m}}\subseteq G
. This follows from the definition of the analytic conductor
[
44
, Eq. (5.7)]
(denoted
\mathfrak{q}(f)=\mathfrak{q}(f,0)
there), and the fact that the local parameters (denoted
\kappa_{j}
in
[
44
]
) are zero for characters trivial on
T^{\mathfrak{m}}
.
, we obtain

\frac{|\overline{\mathcal{P}}|}{|\mathcal{P}|}\cdot\frac{1}{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]}\leq 2

(171)

Combining
Equations

168
,
169
and
171
, we obtain that the eigen value
\lambda_{\chi}
for non-constant characters
\chi\in\widehat{G}
under the Hecke operator
\mathcal{H}
satisfies

\lambda_{\chi}=O\left(\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi))}{B^{1/2}}\right),

(172)

The infinite analytic conductor
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}

The infinite analytic conductor
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a number
quantifying the amount of oscillation of the character
\chi\in\widehat{G}
. By restricting a character
\chi\in\widehat{G}
to the ray unit torus
42
42

42

Due to the fact that
[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]
is finite and the ray unit torus satisfies
T^{\mathfrak{m}}\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, it is also included in
G
.

T^{\mathfrak{m}}=H/\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m},1})\subseteq G
, one gets a character on
T^{\mathfrak{m}}
, which can be uniquely associated with a dual lattice point
\ell^{*}\in\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}
, where
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})=\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}\cap K^{\mathfrak{m},1})
. The infinite part of the analytic conductor is in that case equal to
43
43

43

This definition of the infinite part of the analytic conductor is slightly different to that of Kowalski & Iwaniec
[
44
, Equation (5.7)]
(with
s=1
) which is due to the difference in definitions of the real and the complex
L
-functions (see also
[
12
, Remark 4.13]
). This difference is solved by putting
L_{\mathbb{R}}(s)L_{\mathbb{R}}(s+1)=L_{\mathbb{C}}(s)
, (see
[
58
, Chapter 7, Proposition 4.3(iv)]
).
(see
[
44
, Equation (5.3), (5.4), (5.7)]
and
[
57
, Equation (3.3.6), (3.3.12)]
or
[
14
, Section 3.4]
or
[
12
, Section 4.3.4]
)

\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi)=\prod_{\nu\mbox{\footnotesize{ real }}}(3+|2\pi\ell^{*}_{\nu}|)\prod_{\nu\mbox{\footnotesize{ complex }}}(3+|2\pi\ell^{*}_{\nu}|)\big(3+|i2\pi\ell^{*}_{\nu}+1|\big)

(173)

Here, the components of the dual lattice point in the ray logarithmic unit lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}
are parametrized by the places
\nu
. As a result, by applying the geometric and arithmetic inequality for vector norms, we obtain the following bound on the infinite analytic conductor.

\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi)\leq\left(4+4\pi\left\lVert\ell^{*}\right\rVert/\sqrt{n}\right)^{n},

where
\ell^{*}\in\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}
is the unique dual logarithmic ray unit lattice point associated with the character
\chi|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

Fourier analysis of the Gaussian

Since the initial distribution of the random walk is a Gaussian
g_{s}:H\rightarrow\mathbb{R},x\mapsto e^{-\pi\|x\|^{2}/s^{2}}
over the hyperplane
H=\log K_{\mathbb{R}}^{0}
,
we are particularly interested in the behavior of the distribution resulting from applying the Hecke operator
N
times, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}^{N}(g_{s})
. By standard Fourier computations, one can prove that periodized Gaussian function
44
44

44

The periodization is defined as follows:
g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}(x):=\sum_{\ell\in\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})}g_{s}(x+\ell)
.

s^{-\mathbbm{r}}g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\in L^{2}(T^{\mathfrak{m}})
satisfies

s^{-\mathbbm{r}}g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\sum_{\ell^{*}\in\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}}a_{\ell^{*}}\chi_{\ell^{*}}

(174)

where
a_{\ell^{*}}=\frac{1}{\vol(T^{\mathfrak{m}})}g_{1/s}(\ell^{*})
, where
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}
is the dual lattice of the log ray unit lattice
\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})
, and where
\chi_{\ell^{*}}(x)=e^{-2\pi i\left\langle x,\ell^{*}\right\rangle}
is a function on
T^{\mathfrak{m}}
, i.e.,
\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

By standard character arguments, one can simply write each
\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{G}
(which is zero everywhere except on
T^{\mathfrak{m}}\subseteq G
) as an average of all characters in
\widehat{G}
that restrict to
\chi_{\ell^{*}}
on
T^{\mathfrak{m}}
.
This results in the following identity (see also
[
14
, §3.6]
), where
\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{G}

ranges over the characters of
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

s^{-\mathbbm{r}}g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\frac{1}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}}g_{1/s}(\ell^{*})\sum_{\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}}\chi^{\prime}.

(175)

Splitting up the sum

By splitting up the sum of
Equation

175
into a ‘unit part’, a ‘low frequency part’ where
\|\ell^{*}\|<r
and a ‘high frequency part’ where
\|\ell^{*}\|\geq r
, we obtain the following decomposition, for any
r>0
, where
\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{G}

ranges over the characters of
G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.

\displaystyle\lvert G\rvert\cdot s^{-\mathbbm{r}}\cdot g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{1}_{G}}_{\mbox{\footnotesize{Unit character}}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\\
\|\ell^{*}\|<r\end{subarray}}g_{1/s}(\ell^{*})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}\\
\chi^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{1}\end{subarray}}\chi^{\prime}}_{\mbox{\footnotesize{Low frequency characters}}}+\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\\
\|\ell^{*}\|\geq r\end{subarray}}g_{1/s}(\ell^{*})\sum_{\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}}\chi^{\prime}}_{\mbox{\footnotesize{High frequency characters}}},

Now, applying the Hecke operator
\mathcal{H}^{N}
times to this equation, and taking the absolute value of
\lvert G\rvert\cdot\mathcal{H}^{N}(g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}})-\mathbf{1}_{G}
, we obtain, by the Pythagorean theorem (using that
\|\chi^{\prime}\overline{\chi^{\prime}}\|_{2}^{2}=|G|
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert\lvert G\rvert\cdot\mathcal{H}^{N}(s^{-\mathbbm{r}}g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}})-\mathbf{1}_{G}\right\rVert^{2}\!\!

\displaystyle=\underbrace{|G|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\\
\|\ell^{*}\|<r\end{subarray}}g_{1/s}^{2}(\ell^{*})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}\\
\chi^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{1}\end{subarray}}|\lambda_{\chi^{\prime}}|^{2N}}_{\mbox{Low frequency}}

(176)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{|G|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\\
\|\ell^{*}\|\geq r\end{subarray}}g_{1/s}^{2}(\ell^{*})\sum_{\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}}|\lambda_{\chi^{\prime}}|^{2N}}_{\mbox{High frequency}}.

(177)

We will bound the parts
Equation

176
and
Equation

177
separately. For the latter, we have

\displaystyle|G|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi_{\ell^{*}}\in\widehat{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}\\
\|\ell^{*}\|\geq r\end{subarray}}g_{1/s}^{2}(\ell^{*})\sum_{\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}}|\lambda_{\chi^{\prime}}|^{2N}

\displaystyle\leq|G|\cdot|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell^{*}\in\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}\\
\|\ell^{*}\|\geq r\end{subarray}}g_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}s}}(\ell^{*})

(178)

\displaystyle\leq|G|\cdot|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot\beta_{\sqrt{2}rs}^{(\mathbbm{r})}\cdot g_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}s}}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}),

(179)

where the last inequality follows from Banaszczyk’s tail bound
[
6
]
and the assumption that
rs>\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}/(4\pi)}
,
where
\beta_{z}^{(n)}:=\left(\frac{2\pi ez^{2}}{n}\right)^{n/2}e^{-\pi z^{2}}
, for which holds
\beta_{t}^{(n)}\leq e^{-t^{2}}
for all
t\geq\sqrt{n}
(see also
[
14
, §3.6]
).

To bound the share of the low-frequency characters,
we use the bound from
Equation

172
,
|\lambda_{\chi^{\prime}}|\leq\allowbreak O\left(\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi^{\prime}))}{B^{1/2}}\right)
. Since these characters have a ‘low frequency’, their analytic conductor
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi^{\prime})
is bounded, namely,
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}(\chi^{\prime})\leq(4+2\pi r/\sqrt{n})^{n}
for any
\chi^{\prime}\in\widehat{G}
such that
\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}
for some
\ell^{*}\in\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee}
with
\|\ell^{*}\|<r
. Therefore,

|\lambda_{\chi^{\prime}}|\leq c\allowbreak=O\left(\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot(4+4\pi r/\sqrt{n})^{n})}{B^{1/2}}\right).

We then obtain

|G|\sum_{\left\lVert\ell^{*}\right\rVert\leq r}g_{1/s}^{2}(\ell^{*})\underbrace{\sum_{\chi^{\prime}|_{T^{\mathfrak{m}}}=\chi_{\ell^{*}}}|\lambda_{\chi^{\prime}}|^{2N}}_{\leq|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot c^{2N}}\leq|G|\cdot|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot c^{2N}\cdot g_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}s}}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee})

(180)

We obtain the following bound by combining
Equations

178
and
180
, dividing by
\lvert G\rvert
(which accounts to dividing by
\lvert G\rvert^{2}
under the square
2
-norm)
and using the identity
\lvert G\rvert=|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot\vol(T^{\mathfrak{m}})
. Assuming the Extended Riemann Hypothesis for Hecke L-functions (e.g.,
[
44
, §5.7]
), and
for all
r,s>0
with
rs>\sqrt{\frac{\mathbbm{r}}{4\pi}}
, we have

\left\lVert\mathcal{H}^{N}(s^{-n}g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}})-\frac{1}{\lvert G\rvert}\mathbf{1}_{G}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{g_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}s}}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee})}{\vol(T^{\mathfrak{m}})}\left(c^{2N}+\beta_{\sqrt{2}rs}^{(\mathbbm{r})}\right)

(181)

with
c=O\left(\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot(4+4\pi r/\sqrt{n})^{n})}{B^{1/2}}\right)
.

Tuning parameters

Let
1>\varepsilon>0
,
s>0
and
k\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be given. We have
g_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}s}}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee})\leq g_{1/\tilde{s}}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee})\leq 2\covol(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times}))/\tilde{s}^{\mathbbm{r}}
by smoothing arguments (see
Lemma

2.20
).
Using that inequality and Hölder’s inequality (i.e.,
\|f\cdot 1\|_{1}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert 1\rVert_{2}
), noting that
\lVert 1_{G}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\lvert G\rvert
, we obtain, for each
r>\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}}/(\sqrt{2}s)
,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \left\|\mathcal{W}_{G}(B,N,s)-\mathcal{U}(G)\right\|_{1}^{2}

(182)

\displaystyle\leq\lvert G\rvert\cdot\left\lVert\mathcal{H}^{N}(s^{-\mathbbm{r}}g_{s}|^{T^{\mathfrak{m}}})-\frac{1}{|G|}\mathbf{1}_{G}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

(183)

\displaystyle\leq|G/T^{\mathfrak{m}}|\cdot g_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}s}}(\Log(\mathcal{O}_{K^{\mathfrak{m},1}}^{\times})^{\vee})\cdot(c^{2N}+\beta_{\sqrt{2}rs}^{(\mathbbm{r})})

(184)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot|G|\cdot\tilde{s}^{-\mathbbm{r}}\cdot(c^{2N}+\beta_{\sqrt{2}rs}^{(\mathbbm{r})})

(185)

Here,
c=O\left(\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot(4+2\pi r/\sqrt{n})^{n})}{B^{1/2}}\right)
. We proceed by bounding the two summands in
Equation

185
separately.

•

By putting
45
45

45

We use the bound
\beta_{\alpha}^{(\mathbbm{r})}\leq e^{-\alpha^{2}}
for
\alpha\geq\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}}

r
equal to

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}s}\cdot\max\left(\sqrt{\mathbbm{r}},\sqrt{2+\mathbbm{r}\log(1/\tilde{s})+2\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\lvert G\rvert}\right)

we deduce that

2\cdot\lvert G\rvert\cdot\tilde{s}^{-\mathbbm{r}}\cdot\beta_{\sqrt{2}rs}^{(\mathbbm{r})}\leq\varepsilon^{2}/2
.

•

Subsequently, choose
46
46

46

In this bound on
B
one would expect an additional
\log\log\lvert G\rvert\leq\log\log\lvert\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}\rvert
. But as it is bounded by
\log(\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m})))
(see
Lemma

5.1
), it can be put in the hidden polylogarithmic factors.

a
B=\tilde{O}([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]^{2}\cdot n^{2k}[\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m}))^{2}+n^{2}\log(r)^{2}])
, i.e.,

\displaystyle B=\tilde{O}\Big([\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]^{2}\cdot n^{2k}\cdot\big[\log(|\Delta_{K}|\norm(\mathfrak{m}))^{2}

\displaystyle+n^{2}\log(1/\tilde{s})^{2}

\displaystyle+n^{2}\log(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{2}\big]\Big)

such that
c\leq 1/n^{k}
,
where
c=O\left(\frac{[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot\log(B)\log(B^{n}\cdot|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\norm(\mathfrak{m})\cdot(4+4\pi r/\sqrt{n})^{n})}{B^{1/2}}\right)
.
Lastly, taking any integer
N\geq\frac{1}{2k\log n}\cdot(\mathbbm{r}\cdot\log(1/\tilde{s})+2\log(1/\varepsilon)+\log\lvert G\rvert+2)
and noting that
c^{\frac{1}{k\log n}}\leq 1/e
, we deduce that

2\cdot\lvert G\rvert\cdot\tilde{s}^{-\mathbbm{r}}\cdot c^{2N}\leq\varepsilon^{2}/2
.

Combining, we can bound the right-hand side of
Equation

185
by
\varepsilon^{2}
.
Taking square roots gives the final result.
∎

A.2.
Sampling of random prime ideals

In this section we show how to sample prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}
that
are coprime with
\mathfrak{m}
and whose class
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]
fall into

G\subseteq\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
, using an
oracle
that can check
whether an ideal
\mathfrak{a}
satisfies
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\in G
or not.
The algorithm doing this is a slight generalization of the algorithm
sampling prime ideals in
[
14
, Lemma 2.2]
.
Before giving this algorithm (together with its properties), we first
need the following result on the number of prime ideals that are coprime
with
\mathfrak{m}
.

Lemma A.2.1

(ERH)
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{K}
be an ideal modulus and denote

\pi_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}(x)=|\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{I}_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime and }\norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq x\}|

for the number of prime ideals not dividing
\mathfrak{m}
and having norm bounded by
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\omega(\mathfrak{m})
denote the number of different prime ideal divisors of
\mathfrak{m}
.

Then, there exists
x_{0}\in\widetilde{O}(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+\omega(\mathfrak{m}))
such that for all
x\geq x_{0}

we have

\pi_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}(x)\geq\frac{x}{4\log x}.

Proof.

Denote
\pi_{K}(x)=|\{\mathfrak{p}\in\ideals\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime and }\norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq x\}|
, i.e., whenever
\mathfrak{m}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
. We will prove the statement for this specific case first. By simplifying an explicit result of Grenié and Molteni
[
37
, Corollary 1.4]
, we obtain, under the Extended Riemann Hypothesis
47
47

47

In the paper of Grenié and Molteni
[
37
, Corollary 1.4]
, only the Dedekind zeta function
\zeta_{K}(s)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}}\norm(\mathfrak{a})^{-s}
needs to satisfy the condition that all of its non-trivial zeroes lie at the vertical line
\Re(s)=1/2
.
,

\left|\pi_{K}(x)-\pi_{K}(3)-\int_{3}^{x}\frac{du}{\log u}\right|\leq\sqrt{x}[6\log|\Delta_{K}|+4n\log x+14].

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{K}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{3}^{x}\frac{du}{\log u}-\sqrt{x}[6\log|\Delta_{K}|+4n\log x+14]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{x}{\log x}-\sqrt{x}\log(x)[6\log|\Delta_{K}|+4n+14]

\displaystyle=\frac{x}{\log x}\left(1-\frac{\log(x)^{2}(6\log|\Delta_{K}|+4n+14)}{\sqrt{x}}\right)\geq\frac{x}{2\log x}

where the first inequality follows from omitting
\pi_{K}(3)
and the second inequality from
\int_{3}^{x}\frac{du}{\log u}\geq\frac{x}{\log x}
and from the assumption that
x>x_{0}
, where
x_{0}\in\widetilde{O}(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|)
such that
\frac{\log(x_{0})^{2}(6\log|\Delta_{K}|+4n+14)}{\sqrt{x_{0}}}<1/2
.

For the general case of
\mathfrak{m}\neq\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we need to avoid
\mathfrak{m}
; so writing
\omega(\mathfrak{m})
for the
number of different prime ideals dividing
\mathfrak{m}
, we obtain

\pi_{K}^{\mathfrak{m}}(x)\geq\pi_{K}(x)-\omega(\mathfrak{m})\geq\frac{x}{2\log x}\left(1-\frac{2\cdot\omega(\mathfrak{m})\cdot\log x}{x}\right)\geq\frac{x}{4\log x}.

Where the last inequality can be deduced from the fact that
x>x_{0}
and where
x_{0}=\widetilde{O}(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|+\omega(\mathfrak{m}))
is chosen such that
\frac{2\cdot\omega(\mathfrak{m})\cdot\log x_{0}}{x_{0}}<1/2
.
∎

The following lemma is a slight generalization of
[
14
, Lemma 2.2]
,
where we demand the Arakelov ray class of the sampled prime ideals
to be lying in
G
, some subgroup of
\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}
.
See
5.4

Proof.

The algorithm can be described as follows. Sample a uniform integer from
[0,B]
and check if it is prime (if not, output ‘failure’). If it is, name the prime
p
, factor
p\mathcal{O}_{K}
into prime ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
and list the different prime factors
\{\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{k}\}
that have norm bounded by
B
, do not divide
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
and satisfy
[d^{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{j})]\in G
. If this set is empty, output ‘failure’; otherwise, choose one
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
uniformly at random in
\{\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{k}\}
and output it with probability
k/n
. Otherwise, output ‘failure’.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}_{B}
be arbitrary, and let
p=\mathfrak{q}\cap\mathbb{Z}
the prime ‘below’
\mathfrak{q}
. Then the probability of sampling this
\mathfrak{q}
equals
\frac{1}{nB}
, namely,
\frac{1}{n}
times the probability of sampling
p
. So, the probability of sampling successfully (that is, no failure) equals

\frac{|\mathcal{P}_{B}|}{nB}\geq\frac{1}{8\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot n\log B}

since, by
Equation

170
, denoting
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}=\{\mathfrak{p}\mbox{ prime ideal of K }\penalty\ |\penalty\ \norm(\mathfrak{p})\leq B,\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}\}
,

\frac{|\mathcal{P}_{B}|}{|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}|}\geq\frac{1}{2\cdot[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]},

and by
Lemma

A.2.1
, we have
|\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}|\geq\frac{B}{4\log B}
(by adequately increasing
B_{0}
so that
B_{0}\geq x_{0}
from
Lemma

A.2.1
).

The most costly part of the algorithm is the factorization of a prime
p\leq B
in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. This can be performed using Kummer-Dedekind algorithm, which essentially amounts to factoring a degree
n
polynomial modulo
p
.
Using Shoup’s algorithm
[
71
]
(which has complexity
O(n^{2}+n\log p)

[
77
, §4.1]
) and the fact that the algorithm needs to repeat
[\Pic_{K^{\mathfrak{m}}}^{0}:G]\cdot n\log B
times to get constant success probability, yields the complexity claim and the query complexity for
\mathbf{O}_{G}
.
∎

A.3.
Upper bound on the
n
th successive minimum of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
by Bhargava et al.

The following theorem and its proof is a copy of that of Bhargava et al.
[
8
, Theorem 3.1]
, with the difference that it is applied to the infinity norm
and has explicit constants everywhere. A similar result, but also without explicit constants, can be found in an article by Peikert and Rosen
[
62
, Lemma 5.4]
.

Theorem A.3.1

(Bhargava et al.
[
8
]
)
.

Let
K
be any number field of degree
n
and let
\mathcal{O}_{K}
be its ring of integers. Let
\mathcal{O}_{K}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{R}}

have the structure of a lattice via the Minkowski embedding (see
Section

2
), and denote

\lambda_{j}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
for the
j
-th successive minimum with respect to the infinity norm in
K_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then

\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\leq|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}.

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
attain the successive minima for the
infinity norm
\lambda_{j}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, with
\alpha_{1}=1
.
For any element
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we write
\beta=\sum_{j=1}^{n}[\beta]_{j}\alpha_{j}
,
i.e.,
[\beta]_{j}
are the coordinates of
\beta
with respect to
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
.

For
2\leq k,\ell\leq n-1
consider the
(n-2)\times(n-2)
-matrix
C=([\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\ell}]_{n})
, i.e., the matrix consisting of the
coordinates of
\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\ell}
with respect to
\alpha_{n}
. We will
show at the end of this proof that this is a non-degenerate matrix, implying that there are no
zero rows or columns. In other words, there exists a permutation
\pi:\{2,\ldots,n-1\}\rightarrow\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
[\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\pi(k)}]_{n}\neq 0
for all
k\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
.

So, the product
\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\pi(k)}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
extends
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1}\}

to a
n
-dimensional lattice; therefore we have
\|\alpha_{k}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha_{\pi(k)}\|_{\infty}\geq\|\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\pi(k)}\|_{\infty}\geq\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. Taking products over all
k\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
we obtain

\prod_{k=2}^{n-1}\|\alpha_{k}\|_{\infty}^{2}=\prod_{k=2}^{n-1}\|\alpha_{k}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha_{\pi(k)}\|_{\infty}\geq\big(\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\big)^{n-2}.

Multiplying above equation by
\|\alpha_{1}\|_{\infty}^{2}=1
and
\|\alpha_{n}\|_{\infty}^{2}=\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{2}
,
and using Minkowski’s second inequality
48
48

48

Note that Minkowski’s second inequality as stated in
[
21
, Chap. VIII, Thm. 5]
only states that
\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}^{\infty}(\Lambda)\cdot\vol(B)\leq 2^{n}\cdot\covol(\Lambda)
, where
B=\{x\in K_{\mathbb{R}}\,|\,\|x\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}
(
B
is a ball of dimension
n
, living in a real vector space of dimension
2n
since our lattice is not full rank). To compute the volume of
B
, observe that
B
consists in
n_{\mathbb{R}}
orthogonal copies of
B_{\mathbb{R}}=\{x\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R},|x|\leq 1\}
and
n_{\mathbb{C}}
orthogonal copies of
B_{\mathbb{C}}=\{(x,\bar{x})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\,|\,|x|\leq 1\}
. One can check that
\vol(B_{\mathbb{R}})=2
and
\vol(B_{\mathbb{C}})=2\pi
, leading to
\vol(B)=2^{r_{\mathbb{R}}+r_{\mathbb{C}}}\cdot\pi^{r_{\mathbb{C}}}
. Minkowski’s second theorem then implies that
\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}^{\infty}(\Lambda)\leq\covol(\Lambda)
as desired.

[
21
, Chap. VIII, Thm. 5]

\prod_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}^{\infty}(\Lambda)\leq\covol(\Lambda)
, we obtain

|\Delta_{K}|\geq\prod_{k=1}^{n}\|\alpha_{k}\|_{\infty}^{2}\geq\big(\lambda_{n}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\big)^{n}.

It remains to prove that
C=([\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\ell}]_{n})
is non-degenerate. Suppose it is not, and there
exists
d_{\ell}
for
\ell\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
(not all zero) such that

\Big[\sum_{\ell=2}^{n-1}d_{\ell}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\ell}\Big]_{n}=\sum_{\ell=2}^{n-1}d_{\ell}[\alpha_{k}\alpha_{\ell}]_{n}=0\mbox{ for all }k\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}

Writing
\beta=\sum_{\ell=2}^{n-1}d_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}
, this means that, for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
,
\alpha_{k}\beta
lies in the
span of
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1})
. In other words,
L=\mathbb{Q}\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\mathbb{Q}\alpha_{n-1}
is

\mathbb{Q}(\beta)
-invariant, i.e., a
\mathbb{Q}(\beta)
-vector (strict) subspace of
K
. That is,
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}(\beta)}(L)\leq\dim_{\mathbb{Q}(\beta)}(K)-1
.
But then

\displaystyle n-1

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(L)=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}(\beta)}(L)\cdot[\mathbb{Q}(\beta):\mathbb{Q}]

\displaystyle\leq(\dim_{\mathbb{Q}(\beta)}(K)-1)\cdot[\mathbb{Q}(\beta):\mathbb{Q}]=n-[\mathbb{Q}(\beta):\mathbb{Q}],

yielding
[\mathbb{Q}(\beta):\mathbb{Q}]=1
, i.e.,
\beta\in\mathbb{Q}
, which is impossible by the fact that

\beta=\sum_{\ell=2}^{n-1}d_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}
is assumed to be non-zero and has no
\alpha_{1}=1
part.

We conclude that
C
is non-degenerate, which finishes the proof.
∎

A.4.
Upper bound on a defining polynomial of a number field

The following two lemmas
show together that for any number field
K
there exists a
polynomial
f\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
such that
K=\mathbb{Q}[x]/(f(x))
and
so that
\size(f)\leq O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log^{2}(\log|\Delta_{K}|))
. That is, each field has a ‘small’ defining polynomial. The first lemma is just a variant of the
primitive element theorem
[
50
, V.§4, Theorem 4.6]
, whereas the second lemma uses this primitive element theorem inductively to bound such a primitive element.

Lemma A.4.1

(Primitive Element Theorem)
.

Let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,\beta)
be a separable extension. Then there exist an integer
\lambda\in\{0,1,\ldots,d_{\alpha}\cdot d_{\beta}\}
such that
K=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha+\lambda\beta)
,
where
d_{\alpha}=[\mathbb{Q}(\alpha):\mathbb{Q}]
and
d_{\beta}=[\mathbb{Q}(\beta):\mathbb{Q}]
.

Proof.

We write
\gamma=\alpha+\lambda\beta
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
. Our aim is to show that the number of
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
for which
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha+\lambda\beta)\neq\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,\beta)
is bounded above by
d_{\alpha}\cdot d_{\beta}
, hence, for a certain
\lambda\in\{0,1,\ldots,d_{\alpha}\cdot d_{\beta}\}
, it holds that
\mathbb{Q}(\alpha+\lambda\beta)=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,\beta)=K
; by the pigeon-hole principle.

Write
f_{\alpha},f_{\beta}
for the minimum polynomial of
\alpha
respectively
\beta
. Write
\gamma=\alpha+\lambda\beta
. Then we have
f_{\alpha}(\gamma-\lambda\beta)=0
, hence
\beta
is a zero of the polynomial
g=f_{\alpha}(\gamma-\lambda x)\in\mathbb{Q}(\gamma)[x]
.

So, the minimum polynomial
h_{\beta}^{\gamma}
of
\beta
over
\mathbb{Q}(\gamma)
divides
g\in\mathbb{Q}(\gamma)[x]
, but also
f_{\beta}\in\mathbb{Q}[x]
. So,

h_{\beta}^{\gamma}\mid\mbox{gcd}(g,f_{\beta}).

Suppose
\beta\notin\mathbb{Q}(\gamma)
, then the minimum polynomial
h_{\beta}^{\gamma}
has degree
d\geq 2
, and divides both
g
and
f_{\beta}
. By separability, the polynomial
h_{\beta}^{\gamma}
has
d
distinct roots (in the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}(\gamma)
), so in particular, there exists
\beta_{2}\neq\beta
such that
h_{\beta}^{\gamma}(\beta_{2})=0
. This
\beta_{2}
then satisfies
f_{\beta}(\beta_{2})=g(\beta_{2})=0
. Putting
\alpha_{2}=\gamma-\lambda\beta_{2}
and using the definition of
g
, one can see that
\alpha_{2}
is a root of
f_{\alpha}
.

Finally, combining the equations
\alpha=\gamma-\lambda\beta
and
\alpha_{2}=\gamma-\lambda\beta_{2}
and using the fact that
\beta\neq\beta_{2}
, we obtain that
\lambda=\frac{\alpha-\alpha_{2}}{\beta_{2}-\beta}
, where
\beta_{2}
is a root of
f_{\beta}
and
\alpha_{2}
is a root of
f_{\alpha}
. There are at most
\deg(f_{\alpha})\cdot\deg(f_{\beta})=d_{\alpha}d_{\beta}
possible
\lambda
with this shape.
Hence,
\mathbb{Q}(\gamma)=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha+\lambda\beta)=\mathbb{Q}(\alpha,\beta)
for all but
d_{\alpha}\cdot d_{\beta}
values
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}
. This was what was remained to be proven.
∎

Lemma A.4.2
.

Let
K
be a number field of degree
n
. Then there exists a monic integral irreducible polynomial
g=\sum_{i=0}^{n}g_{i}x^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
such that
K\simeq\mathbb{Q}[x]/g(x)
with

\max_{i}\log|g_{i}|=O(\log|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log\log|\Delta_{K}|)

and hence

\size(g):=\sum_{i}\log|g_{i}|=O(\log^{2}|\Delta_{K}|\cdot\log\log|\Delta_{K}|).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{K}
be the ring of integers of
K
. By definition of the last minimum of a lattice, we know that there exist
n
linearly independent elements
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
satisfying
\|\beta_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{i}^{(\infty)}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\leq|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
, where we used
Theorem

A.3.1
for the last inequality.

Since
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n}
span a
n
-dimensional space, then
K=\mathbb{Q}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n})
.
Applying the primitive element theorem (in the shape of
Lemma

A.4.1
) inductively, we can then deduce that there exists

\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i}\beta_{i}

with
n_{i}\in\{0,1,\dots,n^{2}\}
for all
i
, and such that
K=\mathbb{Q}(\beta)
. Indeed, assume by induction that
K_{j}:=\mathbb{Q}(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{j})=\mathbb{Q}(\gamma_{j})
for some
\gamma_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j}n_{i}\beta_{i}
as above. Then by
Lemma

A.4.1
, it holds that
K_{j+1}=\mathbb{Q}(\gamma_{j},\beta_{j+1})=\mathbb{Q}(\gamma_{j}+n_{j+1}\beta_{j+1})
for some
n_{j+1}\leq n^{2}
(since all the intermediate fields appearing have degree at most
n
).

Therefore,

\|\beta\|_{\infty}\leq n^{3}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}.

By definition of
\|\beta\|_{\infty}
, this implies that for all embeddings
\sigma_{j}:K\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, it holds that
|\sigma_{j}(\beta)|\leq n^{3}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}
.

Let
g=\sum_{i=0}^{n}g_{i}x^{i}
be the minimal polynomial of
\beta
. Since
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
(because the
\beta_{i}
’s are in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
and the
n_{i}
’s are in
\mathbb{Z}
), then
g
has integer coefficients. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle|g_{j}|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}\\
|J|=n-j\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in J}\sigma_{i}(\beta)\Big|\leq\binom{n}{j}(n^{3}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n})^{n-j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}(n^{3}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n})^{n-j}=(1+n^{3}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n})^{n}

\displaystyle=n^{3n}|\Delta_{K}|\left(1+\frac{1}{n^{3}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}}\right)^{n}\leq e\cdot n^{3n}|\Delta_{K}|,

since
n^{3}|\Delta_{K}|^{1/n}\geq n
.

Hence,
\max_{i}\log|g_{i}|=O(n\log(n)+\log|\Delta_{K}|)
. Using the fact that
n=O(\log|\Delta_{K}|)
concludes the proof.
∎
